## BOOK ONE.

- CHAP. I. *On the difference between worldly and divine theology, and on the demonstrations of each.*
- CHAP. II. *What is the subject-matter of the hierarchies, and their arrangement.*
- CHAP. III. *On the three hierarchies.*
- CHAP. IV. *Why Dionysius the Areopagite undertook to treat of theology, after he had received the catholic faith.*
- CHAP. V. *What a hierarchy is, and its arrangement and origin*



## CHAPTER ONE *On the difference between worldly and divine theology, and on the demonstrations of each.*

[0923B] *The Jews seek signs, and the Greeks wisdom [n: (I Cor. I)]*. For there was a certain wisdom, which seemed wisdom to those who did not know the true wisdom. And the world found that wisdom, and began to be puffed up, and swelled, esteeming itself great in it. And it presumed, and resolved to press on further to the highest wisdom, trusting in its own wisdom, as though this could have been a way to that. And it climbed, and was lifted up, that it might reach the heights in heart. And it made itself a ladder, the outward form of the creature, striving toward the invisible things of the Creator. Then those things of God which [0924B] were manifest came forth to give light; and they were made known so as to be tested by a heart that was not pure. For the things that were seen were known, and there were others that were not known; and through the things that are manifest they thought to pass into those that were hidden, and they fell in mind beyond the truth that was possible into the lies of their own fabrications, where there was found nothing further that they might grasp. Therefore God makes foolish the wisdom of this world, because in it the wisdom of God could not be found; and he showed [0925A] another wisdom, which seemed foolishness and was not, that the true wisdom might be found through it. Christ crucified was preached, that truth might be sought through humility. But the world despised the physician [modicum], and could not recognize the truth. For it wished to contemplate the works of God which he had made to be wondered at, yet the things he had set forth to be imitated it would not venerate. For it did not attend to its own disease, so as to seek the medicine with devout devotion; but presuming upon a false health, it gave itself over to investigating things not its own with empty curiosity. And it seemed to make progress outside itself, but it failed within itself, and did not find him who was above it. And it set forth opinions about him which it had conceived with a proud heart, lest it should seem to be ignorant of the truth; and it followed, in the way of error, one thing after another, very many against the truth, and at the last things unseemly [0925B] to the Godhead and misshapen to his surpassing majesty, so that the error became manifest. For at first it was true, and seemed great, and at the last falsehood followed, when the truth ought to have been brought to completion. And there was shown, as it were, a certain light in one part, so that there something might be seen; and over against it were dense shadows and the deep gloom of ignorance, where the snare of error was set for the taking of the proud. And they saw where the light was, and where the shadows were they could not see; and there they were caught in the snare because of the boldness of their presumption, by which they cast themselves headlong to go into it: these things are manifest. For how great are the monuments of surpassing genius that they left, wherein with so much investigation they pursued the secrets of nature and the hidden things of created things, so that we judge their very efficacy [0925C] to be preferred to every study. We read of the arts, and the studies, and the disciplines, and very many precepts of the reasonings, which they, by the sense and genius given to them, searched out for this very purpose, and found; and they wrote down what they had found, and handed it down to posterity to be read — logic, and ethics, and mathematics, and physics — concerning the form of reasonings, and of life, and of morals befitting the ordinance of nature, concerning the disposition and order, and the causes, and the outcomes of all things. And they grew strong in this part, so as to grasp the truth; since this truth too was to be dispensed through them — a truth that was not unto life, since they were not the sons of life. Therefore it was given to them for our sake — for whom the consummation was being reserved and the beginning prepared — that they might find that truth [0925D] which the sons of life had to take up for the service of the highest truth. The labor of it was laid upon them, the fruit reserved for these others. And in all these things they received the light of understanding, and the keenness of genius, and the power of sense for the foregoing lessons; in which they subtly examined the force of creatures and the measure of the lower nature according to the form of the reason implanted in them. But at the last they devoted themselves to theology, as the account of divine things and the searching out of the invisible, as though to bring wisdom to completion — as they themselves supposed, about to bring it to completion; but in truth about to lose it, and not to find the true wisdom. For there they began to fall into the lies of fabrications, and they took visible forms as the images of divine things, so as to see the invisible through the things that were seen; and there was there something similar, but showing from [0926A] afar what was sought, and not bringing light to eyes that were darkened. For nature, established for service, showed forth its Creator; but it was a likeness foreign to his surpassing and sovereign majesty. Nor could that nature, which had to be taught, find an evident declaration in all these things, since it too was not sound, so as to grow much clear unto contemplation. For it did not have those exemplars which through grace were being formed for the health of the inner vision; nor did it know the ark of wisdom and of the treasures laid up — the flesh of the eternal Word in the humanity of Jesus; but using the natural lesson alone, with bleary sight, it contemplated a clouded and ambiguous light, bringing it to the beholder in the outward form of created things. Therefore they went astray and became vain, [0926B] when they wished to pass in mind beyond the things which alone they had received to know; and groping with their estimations toward the things that could not be seen, they were found blind who thought that they saw. These are the images of errors, which their theology (for so they themselves called the study by which they believed they were searching out divine things) proclaims as things to be venerated — images of their vanity and deception; in which they lie in so many things so far from the true, and the right, and what accords with good nature, that they too are compelled to blush over them. For it was fitting that those who were proud over the knowledge of the lowest things should be confounded in the highest, and that those who despise the humility of faith in the death of the Savior should marvel at his loftiness in the knowledge of the Creator. For two images were set before man, in which he might have been able to see the invisible: [0926C] one of nature, and one of grace. The image of nature was the outward form of this world. But the image of grace was the humanity of the Word. And in each God was shown, but not in each was he understood; for nature indeed by its outward form showed forth the Maker, but could not illumine the eyes of the beholder. But the humanity of the Savior was both a medicine, that the blind might receive light, and likewise a teaching, that those who see might recognize the truth. He made clay from spittle, and smeared the eyes of the blind man, and he washed and saw [n: (Joan. IX)]. And what afterward? Then to the man who saw and did not yet know, he says: I am he; and he who speaks with thee, he it is, the Son of God [n: (ibid.)]. First, therefore, he gave light; afterward he showed. For nature could show, but could not [0926D] give light. And the world proclaimed its Creator by outward form, but did not pour the understanding of truth into the hearts of men. Through the images of nature, therefore, the Creator was only signified; but in the images of grace God was shown present, because those things he wrought that he might be understood to be; but these he wrought that he might be acknowledged to be present. This is the difference of the theology of this world from that which is called divine theology. For it is impossible that the invisible be shown except through the visible; and therefore every theology must needs use visible demonstrations in the declaration of the invisible. But worldly theology, as we have said, took up the works of creation, and the elements of this world created according to their outward form, that it might make its demonstration in [0927A] them. But divine theology chose the works of restoration according to the humanity of Jesus, and his sacraments which are from the beginning — with natural things too subjoined in due measure — that in them it might shape its instruction. But a greater declaration of the divinity, as we have said, is shown in the sacraments of grace, and in the flesh of the Word, and in his mystical working, than is proclaimed by the natural outward form of things. And therefore worldly theology, using a demonstration of little evidence, had not the power to draw forth the incomprehensible truth without the contagion of error, whereas divine theology is known to proclaim it with a simple and pure assertion.

Now it must be said what theology is — beginning indeed somewhat higher, for the clarity of the things to be said. All philosophy is divided into three principal parts: [0927B] logic, ethics, and the theoretical. For the fourth, which we have added in its own place, to enumerate here would be superfluous. Philosophy, then, contains three parts: the first, that is, logic, which takes up the force and manner of reasonings, the judgment of the true and the false; the second, that is, ethics, which orders the right manner of living and the form of discipline according to the ordinances of the virtues; the third, that is, the theoretical, which alone has chosen to search out the true in all that is and is not. Of this, that is, of the theoretical, there are three parts: the first, mathematics; the second, physics; the third, theology — in which the contemplation of truth ascends to the summit as though by certain steps of contemplation. For the first, that is, mathematics, beholds the visible forms of visible things. [0928A] But the second, that is, physics, searches out the invisible causes of visible things. But the third alone, that is, theology, contemplates invisible substances, and the invisible natures of invisible substances. And there is in these, as it were, a certain progression, and an advance of the mind ascending to know the truth. For through the visible forms of visible things one comes to the invisible causes of visible things; and through the invisible causes of visible things one ascends to invisible substances, and to the knowing of their natures. And here is the summit of philosophy, and the perfection of truth, than which nothing can be higher for the contemplating mind. In this the wise men of this world were therefore, as we have already said, made foolish; because, walking by the natural lesson alone according to the elements and the outward form of the world, they did not have the exemplars [0928B] of grace, in which, although the outward form was humble, yet a more manifest demonstration of truth was afforded. Here, therefore, God made foolish the wisdom of this world [n: (I Cor. I)]; because it could not recognize the truth; because in its own learning it scorned to hold the form of humility. Let it now suffice to have said these things about theology, for the sake of the hierarchy of Dionysius, upon which, for the sake of explanation, we have undertaken to say some things. For every hierarchy is subordinated to theology; and it was necessary, for those being introduced to the reading of the hierarchy, to set down beforehand some things about theology, in order to define its subject-matter, which consists wholly in invisible substances, and in their natures likewise invisible — using a visible lesson for the demonstration of itself.



## CHAPTER II. *What is the subject-matter of the hierarchies, and their arrangement.*

[0927]

[0927C] Dionysius the Areopagite, from a philosopher made a Christian theologian and describer of the hierarchies, shows the order of the divine arrangement in the governance of all things: how God made the rational creature a partaker of his power, appointing sacred magistracies, and powers, and principalities in heaven among the angels, and on earth among men, that they might have dominion over his creation. For it had been fitting that that part of his work should become a partaker of his power in its own arrangement — the part which in its own creation had received a participation of the likeness, so that, being the only one made to the likeness of its Maker, it alone should retain his image in its own ordering. God himself, therefore, the Maker of all things, whose ineffable majesty and unfailing power was able alone to govern [0927D] what it alone had created, willed to have partakers and fellow-workers in the governance of the things made by him — not that he himself might be helped by their ministry, but that they themselves might be made exalted by fellowship in his power. The Lord, therefore, alone and prince of all, established dominions and principalities beneath him and after him, for the accomplishing of the ministry which the order of the universe demanded, so that his works might be fulfilled through orders and arrangements, by a command running down from the highest into all things. The majesty, therefore, and the dominion, [0928C] which in the Lord and governor of all is adored universally, and most omnipotently, and super-excellently, and ineffably, has been distributed among those who have been made partakers of grace and glory, consorts and companions of the majesty, through parts, and divisions, and degrees, and orders — descending from him and looking back to him, and ordered beneath him who is the fount and cause of all things, and the first beginning: « One work, and one maker? one dominion, and one ruler, one prince, and one commonwealth: one Lord, and Father, from whom is all fatherhood in heaven and on earth [n: (Ephes. III)]: one in all, all things: and all things one in one. » For the highest good, by the participation of graces and the distribution of gifts, remaining one in itself, [0928D] is divided among all the partakers of its bounty, and all who partake of the one are gathered together into the form of its unity and its true simplicity. For they could not have been partakers of the power, unless first through grace they became consorts of the virtue. Nor could they, together with him, do what he himself can do, unless first they deserved to be, from him, what he himself is. But the omnipotent Maker is named governor of all things made by him, not by a foreign usurpation, nor by a perfunctory title; but by a power innate in him, and by an ingrown goodness cherishing and nourishing all things, [0929A] ruling and disposing all things, he has dispensed that same goodness and power, in manifold ways, to those who rule beneath him, according to the measure of participation and the reckoning of ministry, through orders and degrees — to the more excellent indeed, and those set above in dominion, imparting greater and super-excellent gifts; but to the lower, and to the subordinate degrees, granting lesser graces, and things freely serving, set over their subjects too without oppression. These are the distributions of lights, descending into all things, to which it has been given to partake of them from the Father of lights and the Sun of justice — made bright mirrors, that they may shine and give light. Subordinate indeed in that they shine, and set above in that they give light. And there is one light, and there is one good; and there are very many things that shine, and that partake of the one [0929B] good and the one light; and in that they partake, they are one, gathered together into the one, and led back to the one, and conformed to the one. These are the hierarchies, that is, the sacred principalities, which the highest hierarchy formed after its own likeness, and appointed beneath itself to have dominion and to preside over its works according to the orders marked out, [0930A] under one beginning and one power, from which is all power, and all virtue, and all light spiritually shining and spiritually giving light to all things that shine. This is the loftiness of the rational creature, and its sublimity, and its dignity to be marveled at, whereby it deserved to have dominion in the works of its Maker, having received virtue from him, and holding power together with him. Which virtue, since according to the measure of the bounty and the participation it is distributed variously and manifoldly, for the comeliness and beauty of those works which wisdom has adorned, from one virtue and one power, becomes many virtues and many powers. But, lest again the multitude beget schism and division, and the world war against itself with contrary rule, there is one beginning, and one moderator of all, [0930B] from whom they have what they are, and under whom they moderate what they can do, and they refer to him everything that they accomplish, so that unity may remain in all things, and peace endure in the kingdom of God, who creates all things and governs all.

## CHAPTER III. *On the three hierarchies*

[0929]

[0929C] There are three hierarchies, in the description of which Dionysius — theologian and narrator of the hierarchies and of the sacred powers that are in heaven and on earth — set forth a finished work. The first, chief of all, and the form and exemplar of the rest, is the highest and ineffable power of the Trinity — simple, and one, and uniform, without degree and difference and comparison, highest, and eternal, and perfect, and true, Omnipotent by its own virtue in creating and ruling all its works, receiving nothing external, losing nothing of its own. The second hierarchy is formed in the angelic nature, by adoption, and participation, and deigning — from the first, and beneath the first, and toward the first, second, sublime by likeness, having degree, and admitting difference, and allowing comparison, second after the highest Trinity in emulation, [0930C] distinguished by a threefold division. The third and last hierarchy is ordained in human nature, imitating the second in the first place and the first in the second, and receiving, through the middle one, the highest image and the super-excellent likeness in the things that partake, and referring it through that very thing, so that the whole may be from the one, and the whole toward the one, and the whole one. But theology took up the angelic hierarchy first, as the exemplar of the human hierarchy — to whose likeness the whole hierarchy that is among men has been made and formed — to be explained; and after it, the human, which stands according to its likeness; in the third place setting the highest, and ineffable, and super-exalted hierarchy, so that, from the illumination of the preceding ones — nearer to human minds — that which is exceedingly intelligible and obscure might grow clear.



## CHAPTER IV. *Why Dionysius the Areopagite undertook to treat of theology, after he had received the catholic faith.*

[0929]

[0929D] The theologian Dionysius, seeing the wisdom of the world puffed up against the humility of the Christian faith — and seeing the cross of Christ, and the works of the humanity of the Word which in the flesh were wrought as a remedy, despised as though unworthy of God, and incongruous with the highest majesty, and impossible to the truth; and the Word of preaching, unto the redemption of the human race, esteemed foolish; and those walking according to the elements of this world, out of the lesson of nature, judging false the things that were accomplished for the salvation of man in the death of the Redeemer — sets himself against it, to empty out the glory which is according to the sense of the wisdom of this age. And he shows that the wisdom of the Christian faith, humble indeed in the death of the Redeemer, is yet [0930D] sublime in the knowledge of the Creator; and that the sacraments of redemption, which seemed despised according to the outward form of this world and the estimation of those who had known only what is worldly, are exalted and to be venerated, and above the wisdom of this world, and above reason, and efficacious in doctrine for demonstrating the highest truth. Therefore he shows that divine theology, which used these exemplars in the demonstration of the invisible, proclaims things worthy of God and consonant with the truth; but that the theology of the world, which by the reasoning of the flesh followed the elements of creation, could not grasp the truth of God. And so indeed, that it may be said as in a riddle, Goliath is struck upon his own head by one despised and [0931A] small, and laid low; because the wisdom of this world, swelling on high, is convicted at its very summit of manifest error by the humility of Christian truth. And it is shown that God is found by the humble, and revealed to the humble, and has bestowed the cross of Christ upon believers — which the wisdom of the world could not bestow upon the proud, and upon those who presume of themselves. Therefore we set our theology, and the knowledge of things exalted, and sublime, and invisible, against those who deride and reprove the humble faith — that they themselves may glory, if they can, over things like these which they despise. And if they have found such things, and known the like, by their own wisdom, and their own reason, and their own sense — which they do not fear to prefer to our faith — let them look upon their own theology, and the things they have said about God, things to be blushed at, [0932A] and ridiculous, and incongruous, and false, that they may acknowledge the faith and take up the truth. For on this account God willed to dwell in the nature of man, that the dwelling of man might be in heaven; and for this that One endured human things, that this one might deserve to know divine things. This is the mystery of the humility of God, and the mystery of faith, and the mystery of truth — which the proud in heart did not know, puffed up by their own sense, and by their own wisdom, which was of the flesh, and could not find the wisdom of God, so that it was found to be foolishness which was thought to be wisdom, and was not. In this we have been made sublime beyond them; because the wisdom of God, which by us is believed in the flesh, is despised by them; in glory and majesty it is acknowledged by us, and unknown by them.

## CHAPTER V. *What a hierarchy is, and its arrangement and origin.*

[0931]

[0931B] The things that it behooved me to set down beforehand, in the theology of Dionysius, for the description of the hierarchies and for the understanding of what is to be said, I have set forth in the foregoing chapters, as far as for the time was suggested to my mind. Now it remains that we examine what has been said about these things by the theologian. And because the very surface of the reading is covered with a great depth of words, and hidden by a certain lofty ambiguity of speech that carries and lifts itself up, and is folded up according to the greatness and excellence of the most secret matters, it is necessary that it first be unlocked by a measured, and common, and easy explanation for the understanding. And this will perhaps be the more suitable explanation for those being introduced to the great beginning, since it is not fitting that minds, to be exercised in the meditation of matters so great and so sublime, be [0931C] occupied with the wrappings of words. Which wrappings, although they are set as veils worthy of the venerable secrets for those entering in, nevertheless, when the face is unveiled for the contemplation of the things that are within in the liberty of the spirit, are to be taken out of the way, for the disclosing of the mysteries. This, then, we now undertake, and this is enough for us in the things that we shall say. For as to the rest, if anything be added, let it be from a superabundant gift. Hierarchy, interpreted from the Greek, is called a *sacred* *principality:* and there are three hierarchies, that is, sacred principalities, which we shall name, as we have recalled above. The first and highest hierarchy is the power of the Godhead. The second and middle is the angelic power, made to the likeness of the first power, and established beneath the first power. The third and last hierarchy is [0931D] the human power, made to the likeness of the angelic, and established beneath it, and through that middle one beneath the first and highest. By these hierarchies, that is, sacred principalities, the whole world is governed: among which there is the highest power, which only commands, and the lowest, which is only commanded; and the middle, which commands the lower, and is commanded by the higher. The highest, therefore, and first power established the second and third power after itself, in the angels and in men, that they might be both conformed to it by participation in virtue, and fellow-workers by fellowship [0932B] in power. And he divided the gifts of the virtues, and according to the divisions of the gifts distributed the offices of the powers; and he gave very many gifts, and established many powers, and all the gifts from one, and all power under one; and one in all, and all things toward one, and in one. But the angelic hierarchy the theologian shows first. Second, he treats of the human. Third, as at the end and consummation, of the divine and highest. But the angelic itself he subdivides into three hierarchies, and each of the three he distinguishes by three orders, so that the number of the nine angelic orders may be completed. And every hierarchy is of one power and one office and one dignity, and in each hierarchy he establishes both first, and middle, and last orders. And the first indeed [0932C] give light; the last are given light; but the middle both are given light by the first, and give light to the last. After these things he pursues, concerning the last hierarchy likewise, the divisions of gifts, and the distributions of powers and dignities, and the offices, and the orders, and the operations, ordered after the likeness of the angelic hierarchy. At the last, ascending to the divine, and simple, and super-excellent hierarchy (so far as is possible for one contemplating amid human things), and bringing them to consummation in it, this consummated whole is the summit. But the beginning (since it is the highest good), being one, is wondrously multiplied and varied for the comeliness and beauty of division and outpouring into all who are worthy of participation in it, so that in it all may be one; since its gifts are lights, and the lights make [0932D] shining things, and the things illumined are shining, and themselves illuminating, and they become light — shining things, and illuminating lights. They are light, and one light everywhere, and the shining things are one in the one light; and the one is multiplied in the many, and the many are united in the one. From this, therefore, theology begins, because the shining and illuminating lights are divine mirrors; and in them is seen the light shining and illuminating, which remains incomprehensible and inaccessible in itself. And for this reason, that it may be seen, it goes out into them, and pours itself into them, that they may grasp it, and [0933A] receive it, and see in themselves, shining from it. For those that do not shine from it cannot see or grasp it, because without light not even the light itself can be seen. And these are indeed beautiful images of the light unto the invisible outpouring, so that through visible things the invisible may be seen. For that good, and the light itself, is not this light; and yet according to something it is light, and can truly be called light; and when it is called light, the truth is spoken, because it is light, and it is the image of that light. And what is in this light is in that good; since the light itself is good from that good, and is wholly in it, because it is wholly from it: for it does not, when it began to be from it, cease [0934A] to be in it; but it proceeded from it, and remained in it. Therefore the image serves, and the creature shows the Maker, since it was made for this, that he might be seen in it. Therefore the gifts are illuminating lights, and the lights that partake are illumined, and themselves illuminating, and like to the illuminating light. In that they are illumined, they become partakers of grace. But in that they give light, they are made consorts of power. And every hierarchy consists of these two, and is perfected by grace and by office, by virtue and by ministry — all of which are shown theologically by likenesses, and figures, and various riddles taken from visible things.



## BOOK TWO. There follow the titles of the fifteen chapters of the celestial or angelic hierarchy of the blessed Dionysius the Areopagite, to Timothy; after which the text of each several chapter will be set down according to John Scotus; and after the text, the exposition of our Hugh.

[0933]

- CHAP. I. *That the divine illumination, coming forth variously unforeseen according to goodness, remains simple; and not this only, but it also unifies the things illumined.*
- CHAP. II. *How beautifully the divine and heavenly things are manifested through dissimilar symbols.*
- CHAP. III. *What a hierarchy is, and what things are understood through a hierarchy.*
- CHAP. IV. *What the appellation of angels signifies.*
- CHAP. V. *Why all the heavenly essences are commonly called angels.*
- CHAP. VI. *Which is the first arrangement of the heavenly essences, which the middle, which the last.*
- CHAP. VII. *On the seraphim, and cherubim, and on the thrones, and on their first hierarchy.*
- CHAP. VIII. *On the dominions, and virtues, and powers, and on their middle hierarchy.*
- CHAP. IX. *On the princes, and archangels, and angels, and on their last hierarchy.*
- CHAP. X. *A recapitulation and gathering of the angelic ordering.*
- CHAP. XI. *Why all the heavenly essences are commonly called heavenly virtues.*
- CHAP. XII. *Why, in respect of men, the high priests are called angels,*
- CHAP. XIII. *Why the prophet Isaiah is said to have been purged by a Seraph.*
- CHAP. XIV. *What the angelic number that has been handed down signifies.*
- CHAP. XV. *What are the formative images of the angelic virtues, and what follows.*

## TITLE OF CHAPTER I. *That the divine illumination, coming forth variously unforeseen according to goodness, remains simple; and not this only, but it also unifies the things illumined.*



## THE TEXT.

[0933B]

« *Every best gift, and every perfect gift, is from above, coming down from the Father of lights [n: (Jac. I)]*. » *But also every procession of the manifestation of lights, the Father being moved, coming forth into us most excellently and abundantly: again, as a unifying power restoring us, fills us anew, and turns us back to the unity of the gathering Father, and to the deifying simplicity. For from him are all things, and unto him [n: (Rom. XI)]*, *as the divine word says. Therefore, calling upon Jesus, the paternal light, that which is, that which is true, that which « illumines every man coming into this world [n: (Joan. I)]* » *through whom we have had access to the principal light, the Father [n: (Ephes. II)]*: *upon the illuminations handed down in the Father of the most holy oracles, we shall look, as far as is possible. And the hierarchies of the heavenly minds, manifested to us from them symbolically and anagogically, [0933C] we shall consider, as far as we are able. And beholding [0934B] the principal, and super-principal brightness of the divine Father — which manifests to us in figured symbols the most blessed hierarchies of the angels — with immaterial and untrembling eyes of the mind, we are again restored from it into the single ray of that light. For neither is the ray itself anywhere, nor is it ever, forsaken by its own singular unity. But toward the anagogic and unifying tempering of the things that have been provided, it is most excellently and beautifully multiplied, and comes forth; and it remains within itself, securely, uniformly fixed in an unchangeable likeness; and toward those who look upon it, as far as is lawful, it extends itself proportionally to them, and unifies them according to the simple unity of itself. For neither is it possible for the divine ray to shine upon us otherwise than anagogically wrapped about with a variety of sacred veils, and connaturally and properly prepared, by the paternal providence, for those things that are according to [0934C] us. On account of which [sic: *quoae*], judging our most holy hierarchy [0935A] worthy of the celestial hierarchies by a supra-mundane imitation, the most perfect arrangement of sacred things both handed down the said immaterial hierarchies, varying them by material figures and formal compositions, so that we might proportionally ascend, for our very selves, from the most sacred formations into the simple and unfigured heights and likenesses. For neither is it possible for our mind to ascend to that immaterial imitation and contemplation of the celestial hierarchies, unless it use the material hand-leading that is according to itself. Deeming, indeed, the visible forms to be imaginations of the invisible beauty, and the sensible sweetnesses to be figures of the invisible distribution, and the material lights to be an image of the immaterial refulgence, and the sacred disciplines run through according to the intellect to be an image of the contemplative fullness, [0935B] and the ordered habits of the dispositions of the things that are here to be an image of things united and ordered toward the divine, and the very reception of the most divine eucharist to be an image of the participation of Jesus — and whatever other things are handed down to the heavenly essences in a supra-mundane way, but to us symbolically. On account, therefore, of this our co-rational deification, the merciful beginning of perfection, both manifesting to us the heavenly hierarchies, and making our hierarchy their fellow-minister unto virtue and unto our likeness, described the deiform sanctification of those super-celestial intellects in sensible images, in the sacred compositions of the oracles, that he might lead us back through sensible things to intelligible things, and from sacredly figured symbols to the simple summits of the celestial hierarchies.*



## EXPOSITION.

[0935C]

The first book of Dionysius the Areopagite the theologian, which is entitled On the Celestial Hierarchy, that is, on the celestial principate, is woven together of fifteen chapters: in which he carefully enumerates the gifts of the celestial spirits, and their offices, powers, and operations, through their several orders and grades and distributions and differences. And the title of the first chapter is: That every divine illumination, coming forth in various unforeseen ways according to goodness, remains simple, and not this alone, but also unifies the things illuminated. For grace itself is a divine illumination, and the gifts of grace themselves are lights illuminating those who partake of them; and all grace [0935D] descends from one fount, and every illumination from one light; and there are many rays, and one light: and the one light scatters itself, that it may illuminate many; and the many illuminated shine, and nothing is seen but one light, and they become one light in one light. By such a likeness theology shows how the one good offers itself to be partaken by many, that they may be one in it — they who draw one likeness from it. Then the theologian goes on, and shows that the invisible operations of grace, and the distributions of the invisible gifts, cannot be demonstrated or understood except by visible signs and likenesses; and that every visible appearance and sensible nature holds some likeness for the demonstration of invisible things. For as every good is from the [0936A] highest good, so in every good, according to the emulation of participation, the highest good can be contemplated. And therefore it is fitting for theology to conform, out of every appearance and form and sensible quality, to the signification of invisible things. And this is the sense of the first chapter. « Every best gift, and every perfect gift, is from above, descending from the Father of lights. » By apostolic authority, and that of divine theology, he first proves that every good is from the highest good; then from his own opinion he adds that every good looks toward and is poured back to the highest good; because, as it is divided among many by participation, so the many are united in one likeness and imitation. The best gifts are the gifts of nature; the perfect gifts are the gifts of grace; the Father of lights is the author and bestower of the gifts.

[0936B] « Every best gift, therefore, and every perfect gift, is from above, descending from the Father of lights, » since all goods — whether those which nature, first well established, received, or those which afterward, glorified through grace, it merited to obtain — are given by one author of nature and bestower of grace. For which to descend, as it were, was to come from the fount of highest fullness to the participation of lower things. For every creature is by nature inferior to the excellence of the Creator; and therefore every good which by the Creator's gift is poured into the creature is rightly said, as it were, to descend to lower things. For it descends, because it pours itself into a nature subject and inferior in dignity. It descends also, because from a perfect and consummate fullness it divides itself into that participation [0936C] which is in part. Yet in such a way that neither in descending is it enslaved, nor in being divided does it decrease; but, remaining in itself what it is, and in that from which it came, it offers itself, so far as it may, to those to whom it comes; and what being they receive, and how much being in that which they are, they have merited. Thus from one good all goods are; and in one good all goods are; and the good itself, from which all goods are, is good, and is light; and of the things which are from it, it is the author of goods and Father of lights; and the goods themselves are lights, and illuminating those things which are able to shine, and to become shining lights from the illuminating light. For this good leaves nothing established by it estranged from it; and yet it illuminates only those things which it created toward itself and formed according to itself. For those [0936D] alone receive light which can shine from the light — which the coming light finds not unlike itself; and, poured in, it raises them to a greater likeness of itself and a more perfect outward image. These alone, therefore, receive the best gifts and the perfect gifts; because every creature (except that one which has the image of its founder and is capable of likeness), if in its own kind it is a good — which it received from the Creator to be — was neither best when it was given, nor perfect and consummate. But the rational creature, which alone was made to the image of its founder, has received both the given things and the gifts. The best, indeed, while it was being established toward the lowest; and the perfect, while it was being lifted toward the highest. For nothing better was given to any other nature than what it first received, nor anything more perfect than what it afterward [0937A] merited to be, since it was both first established good before all the rest, and afterward perfected to the image and likeness of him by whom it was made. Therefore the best gifts and the perfect gifts, descending from the Father of lights, reach only the loftiness of the rational creature, which alone was made sublime. First, while it was being granted them, the best; and afterward, while it was being glorified, it is made perfect, in a certain manner conformed and coequal to the highest. For the best gifts themselves and the perfect gifts are lights, which descend from the Father of lights; and he from whom they descend, the Father of lights, is himself light, and those into whom they descend themselves become lights; because neither could the Father of light beget another than he himself is, nor can the receiver of light become other than light. If therefore he who begot light is light [0937B], and he who received light is light, now in a certain manner both he who begot and he who received are found to be the same. Yet in such a way that the one is believed to be this by nature, but the other is acknowledged to be this by grace. For this reason, therefore, the one light poured itself out into many lights by participation, that it might reform the many illuminated toward the one light, so that, while many received it by partaking of it, all might appear one in its form. Therefore the theologian, when he had shown from divine authority that every good is from the highest good, at once adds from his own opinion: That every good is toward the highest good, and that it is both poured forth from it by the division of participation, and united to it by the likeness of conformation.

[0937C] « Every best gift, and every perfect gift, is from above, descending from the Father of lights. » In this, therefore, he proves that every good is from the highest good. After this he adds that every good has its conversion, and its leading-back, and its end toward the highest good. « But also, the Father being moved, every procession of the manifestation of lights, coming forth into us most excellently and largely, again, as a unifying power, restoring us, fills us, and converts us to the unity of the gathering Father, and to the deifying simplicity. » The movement of the Father is the affection of fatherly kindness; for by kindness alone and by mercy alone the Father is moved to pour out his lights upon us. He is moved not by any disturbance of himself, but by compassion for us. He is moved, not shaking himself, but gathering us; not emptying himself, but filling us. [0937D] He is moved, therefore, that he may come to us, and he is not moved so as to depart from himself. He is moved, that he may begin to be to us what he was not, and he is not moved so as to cease being to himself what he was. Thus, therefore, the Father of lights is moved upon us, and, the Father being moved, his lights descend into us, and by the things proceeding into us they are manifested through us. First to us, after this to others from us; and all this procession of the manifestation of lights — that is, through which the lights are manifested (for they would not be manifested unless they proceeded) — goes forth from the Father when he is moved. That is, all this procession of the manifestation of lights, coming forth into us, works this: namely, that it fills us — by nothing other, indeed, than itself, out of that with which it fills us — again restoring us by repairing, as it established us by punishments in creating. It restores us, namely, inasmuch as it is a unifying [0938A] power, which gathers what is scattered, composes what is diverse, and out of many makes one; and thus, restoring and reforming, it converts us, who before had been turned away by unlikeness, made diverse by multitude, made perverse by depravity. It converts us, I say, to the unity of the gathering Father, and to the deifying simplicity. For the light of the Father, invisible in itself, proceeding into us and going forth to manifestation, finds us empty and void of the true good; and, pouring itself into us, fills us according to the power and capacity of each one of us; and when it has filled us, it converts us, that we may not be at variance with the Father, but in the same likeness and image may look toward him, by which we are not in discord with him. It fills, indeed, by illuminating; and it converts by making us lights. For we are filled in that we receive light; but we are converted [0938B] in that, from the light received, we too are lights. For in this we are one with the light, and in the light we are one, that we are light; and there is unity in one likeness, in one light, in one brightness, and one illuminating and shining light. Since, therefore, every good is from the highest good, and every good is toward the highest good; for from it we receive that we are one with it; nor would there be in us anything by which we might look toward it, unless first what is its own became ours through it. « For from him are all things, and unto him, as the divine word says. » That is, as the authority of the divine word testifies, all things proceeding from him, converted unto him; from him as beginning unto him as end. But we can also, moreover, not unfittingly take the movement of the Father as the beginning of our [0938C] contemplation. For the mind, accustomed to its own darkness, when it strives to contemplate the inner brightness, as though with trembling and groping eyes not enduring the force of the unwonted radiance, is beaten back by the very first rays of the sight; and the light appears to it as though quivering; and the light itself seems to be moved, when rather its own darkness alone is moved and flees the presence of the flashing light. And this seems to be the movement of the light, when it is the light of the fleeing darkness; and after this movement the lights are poured out, and proceed to manifestation — lights which, while the darkness stood, could not be seen — and they fill us with light, that we may shine, and be lights, just as the light itself is which illuminates us, so that from it are all things [0938D] and unto it; things which receive the beginning of their subsistence from it, and the end of their consummation in it, as the divine word through Paul the apostle testifies: « For from him, and through him, and in him are all things: to him be glory forever. Amen [n: (Rom. XI)]. »



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

« Therefore, invoking Jesus, the paternal light, which is, [and] true, which illuminates every man coming into this world [n: (Joan. I)]; through whom we have had access to the principal light, the Father; we shall look, as far as is possible, upon the illuminations handed down by the Father in the most sacred oracles, and we shall consider, as far as we are able, the hierarchies of the celestial spirits made manifest by them to us symbolically and anagogically. » In the preceding chapter he has briefly comprised the sum of the following work, namely [0939A] that every good is from the highest good, and every good is toward the highest good. Now, before he enters upon the text of his subject-matter, he makes an invocation to Jesus, who is the paternal light, by whose mediation all spiritual illuminations and gifts of graces are bestowed on those to be illuminated, so that, with him illuminating and helping, according to the traditions of the divine oracles — which themselves too were handed down by the Father for the illuminating of the hearts of men — he may be able both truly to recognize and worthily to narrate the invisible illuminations and sacred dispositions of the celestial spirits. « Therefore, invoking Jesus; » as if he said: Every illumination indeed is from the Father, but without Jesus the mediator no illumination can be had. « Therefore » we, who ask to be illuminated, « invoking Jesus, » namely that we may merit to be illuminated [0939B]. Then there follows, concerning Jesus Christ himself, that it is « the paternal light; » instead of what he ought to have said, "who is the paternal light," the reference has been made to the following expression, "which is the paternal light." Or, « invoking Jesus, the paternal light, » which, namely, is light; that is, it has true and eternal and unchangeable being, and is light from that which it is. For it is as with the illuminated lights, which are lights not from that which they are, but from that which they have received. For which phrase "true light" — the word "light" too is understood — because from that which it is, it shines, and it shines not only for itself, but also illuminates others. Which « illuminates every man coming into this world. » Not because all are illuminated; but because of all men [0939C] there is none who has of himself why he should merit to be illuminated rather than another. For this illumination is of grace, not of nature; a gift, not a debt; the benefit of the giver, not the reward of the receiver. For either this light illuminates every man coming into this world inasmuch as all were made by nature capable of reason, and received the light of understanding. Or this light illuminates every man coming into the world; not because all, as has been said, are illuminated; but because all who are either illuminated or not illuminated have no means of being illuminated from elsewhere. For to illuminate belongs to none but the light; as to be illuminated belongs to none but the one receiving the light. Wherefore, as it belongs to all to receive light, so it belongs to the light alone to illuminate all.

[0939D] There follows: « Through whom we have had access to the principal light, the Father. » Through the light Jesus we have had access to the light the Father. For Jesus is light, and the Father of Jesus is light; and the Son, light, was begotten of the Father, light; and the Father and the Son are one light. And the Father is called the principal light, not because he is a greater light or a better light, since he is the same light; but because he is light not from light, therefore the principal light. The Son is light from light, the Father is light not from light; and yet the Father and Son are one light, as the Father and Son are one God. And therefore the Father is the principal light, because from the Father, light, the Son, light. It can, moreover, be understood also in another way, that the Father is called the principal light. For I see that the Son himself too is light in a certain respect, in which he cannot be equal [0940A] to the Father. For the Father of Jesus is God, and God only; but the Son is God, and not only God, but also man and God; and Jesus is light in that he is God; and the same light which the Father, God, is. But in that he is man, he is light, since the very humanity of Jesus too was light in that it demonstrated the truth, and revealed the principal light, the Father. But the light of the humanity of Jesus was less than the light of the deity; and in the light of humanity indeed Jesus was inferior to the Father, but in the light of divinity the same as the Father. And we come through him — through the light of his humanity, in which was the Son alone — to the light of the divinity, in which was the Father and the Son. Therefore we have had access through the Son to the Father; and not to the Father alone, but both to the Son and to the Father. I see also another access [0940B] through Jesus to the principal light, the Father. For Jesus is the wisdom of the Father, and this wisdom revealed the Father, and the wisdom of the Father went forth, the Father remaining in the hidden place: and the Father remained invisible, and his wisdom was made visible, that it might lead through to the invisible Father; and when it was made visible, it did not cease to be invisible, because it came thither where it was not: thence, where it was, it did not withdraw. And it went forth first through the creature of the world, and manifested itself in its works: and, invisible, it began to be seen visibly in that which was visible, and a certain light was shown, that it might lead to a greater light: and the first light was of wisdom, and the second light was wisdom. And Jesus was made for us the way to the Father; from the inferior light to the principal light. Then wisdom came a second time [0940C], and went forth, as has been said, through the flesh, and became light in an earthen vessel, that it might illuminate us, and accustom us to the greater light, and lead us through to the principal light. But thus also we have had access through Jesus to the principal light, the Father. « Therefore, invoking Jesus, the paternal light, which is, which is true, which illuminates every man coming into this world: through whom we have had access to the principal light, the Father. » Up to this point the sentence hangs, until it is completed by what follows; and the sense is this. We, invoking Jesus, shall look, as far as is possible for us, upon the illuminations of the most sacred oracles — that is, upon the most sacred oracles, which illuminate us by the teaching of truth and the understanding of secrets — handed down [0940D] by the Father. For the illuminations of the divine oracles were handed down by the Father, that we might be illuminated by them, until we are made capable of the contemplation of the principal light; and, with them illuminating us, we now recognize the celestial secrets which we are not yet able to comprehend by sense. We can also take the "illuminations of the most sacred oracles" to mean the descriptions of the sacred oracle, and the configurations drawn from visible forms, in which it demonstrates to us, from the proposed likeness, the state of invisible things and the reason and manner of the celestial secrets. For by these very visible signs the human mind is more conveniently instructed and illuminated to the knowledge of invisible things. Therefore almost everywhere in the sacred oracle the appearances of visible things are employed for signification, [0941A] when the human mind is instructed concerning those things which are hidden from human understanding, so that through the things it knows it may be able to grasp and understand the things it does not know. These illuminations, set before us outwardly for instruction, we ought — invoking Jesus, who inwardly illuminates hearts — to examine, and according to their demonstration to learn the celestial secrets.

This is what follows: « And we shall consider, as far as we are able, the hierarchies of the celestial spirits made manifest by them to us symbolically and anagogically. » The order, then, is this: that first we examine the illuminations of the Holy Scriptures with the invocation of Jesus, through whom the inward illumination is given and without whom the outward is empty; and then, with Jesus illuminating, from these illuminations and demonstrations of Holy Scripture [0941B] we consider the sacred principates and powers of the celestial spirits, that is, of the celestial spirits (which, unless they were made manifest to us through the traditions of Holy Scripture, would be altogether invisible and unknown to us). For this is what he says: « We shall consider, as far as we are able, » or as far as we can. For our ability is small and scanty for the magnitude of such great things. « We shall consider the hierarchies, » that is, the sacred principates of the celestial spirits, that is, of the angelic spirits. The hierarchies, I say, made manifest to us, that is, demonstrated, or revealed by them, namely by the illuminations, that is, by the demonstrations of the sacred oracle, symbolically and anagogically. A symbol is the bringing-together of visible forms for the demonstration of invisible things. But anagogy [0941C] is the ascent, or elevation, of the mind for contemplating the things above. Here he notes a twofold mode of divine revelation, which was poured into the minds of the theologians and prophets through visions and demonstrations, which the Greeks call theophanies, that is, divine apparitions. Since sometimes invisible things were demonstrated through signs like to sensible things, sometimes through anagogy alone, that is, the ascent of the mind, purely contemplated in the things above. From these two kinds of visions, two kinds of descriptions also were formed in the sacred oracle. One, by which the truth of hidden things is shadowed forth by forms and figures and likenesses. The other, by which it is expressed nakedly and purely, as it is, without covering. When, therefore, [0941D] that which is hidden is manifested by forms and signs and likenesses, or that which is manifest is described, it is a symbolic demonstration. But when it is shown [d: by pure] by pure and naked revelation, or taught by plain and open narration, it is anagogic.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « And, looking with immaterial and untrembling eyes of the mind upon the principal and super-principal brightness of the divine Father, which manifests to us in figured symbols the most blessed hierarchies of the angels, again from it we are restored into the single ray of it. » Here he plainly teaches by what grades the procession of the divine illumination takes place down to us; and again by what advances our mind is led back to the contemplation of the highest brightness. For the divine light first descends into the angelic nature [0942A], and from it, by revelations and divine demonstrations and the mystical narration of the sacred oracle, transfuses itself even to our understanding and participation. But the human mind, again ascending by the same grades to the things above, by the sacred inspection of the divine oracle weighs the celestial secrets and that illumination of the divine brightness which is in the angels: from which, gradually growing up into the recognition of invisible things, it at last becomes strong enough to contemplate the very splendor of the highest light; and there comes about what was said at the beginning, that the one light both divides itself for the illuminating of many, so that it may reform all the illuminated to the sight and likeness of one brightness. Therefore, the brightness of the celestial spirits being known from the sacred traditions of the divine oracle, he shows how the contemplation [0942B] of the highest light follows upon this knowledge, saying: « And, looking with immaterial and untrembling eyes of the mind upon the principal and super-principal brightness of the divine Father, which manifests to us in figured symbols the most blessed hierarchies of the angels, again from it we are restored into the single ray of it. » The order of the words is: We, looking upon the principal and super-principal brightness of the divine Father with immaterial and untrembling eyes of the mind — which brightness manifests to us the most blessed hierarchies of the angels in figured symbols — again from it we are restored into its single ray. For, looking upon the brightness of the Father, we are restored, that is, reformed again — that is, being turned back reductively to that whence we came — into his single ray, [0942C] that in one light we may be one, we who shine from one light, and that there may be no discrepancy of the shining ones, just as there is no diversity of the lights. But the brightness of the Father is called principal and super-principal, because it is superior to all shining things not only in dignity or in being their cause, but also loftier in nature. And this brightness, because it is not corporeal but spiritual, and shines spiritually: therefore it can be seen only by immaterial, that is, incorporeal, eyes. Which eyes must also be untrembling, that is, sound and clear-sighted and unbeaten-back, lest, gazing upon so great a light with bleary keenness, they bring darkness upon themselves out of the very brightness. And rightly, after the brightness of the angels has been contemplated through the sacred oracles, [0942D] we are illuminated to the brightness of the divine Father; because the brightness of the angels itself too is made manifest to us, from the revelation of the sacred oracle, by the brightness of the Father descending through them into us. This is what he says: « *Which, in figured symbols,* » that is, in signs and conformations figured according to visible appearances, manifests to us the most blessed hierarchies of the angels, that is, the principates, dignities, and powers. The orders of the angels, and their powers, and their divine operations are altogether invisible: and unless they are demonstrated by visible signs and by likenesses drawn from visible things, they can in no way be understood by those who have known only visible things. Therefore the brightness of the Father, wishing to restore us to his principal light, first arouses us by the illuminations of the sacred oracle to contemplate the brightness of the angels [0943A], and, illuminated from that brightness, at last reforms us to the recognition of his own brightness and light, that we may be one in it, we who have received one thing from it. But this brightness of the Father, by the emission and illumination of one simple ray, diffuses itself through all things, and penetrates all things, since the Father begot one wisdom, through which he made all his works. For the Word of the Father is light from light; one Word, and one ray, and the Word itself is wisdom, and this wisdom is a proceeding light, by which it is known: one from one, and therefore one ray from one brightness, by which they are illuminated who are reformed toward it, that they may shine from it, and be light with it, just as he is light. And although there are many illuminated lights, shining variously and dissimilarly, yet there is one [0943B] light illuminating in all the illuminated.

This is what follows: « For neither anywhere, nor ever, is the ray itself forsaken by its own singular unity. » For the wisdom of God, although it pours itself variously and dissimilarly into the minds to be spiritually illuminated according to the capacity and possibility of those receiving it, yet in itself remains one and simple. Neither greater in itself in those who abound from it, nor less in those who, according to a lower grace of participation, receive to be united in it; but whole in the whole, and whole in each, transcending the highest and condescending to the lowest, offering itself to be partaken manifoldly, and remaining one in itself. Therefore it divides itself among many partakers, but by the participation of itself it restores the many partakers to unity. For we [0943C] can be gathered in it; but it cannot be divided in us. For the ray itself, that is, the illuminating wisdom itself, going forth from the principal light, the Father, and proceeding even to all things to be illuminated, is never forsaken by its own proper unity, since it is always with it; nor anywhere forsaken, since it is everywhere in it. For even when, through diverse times, it offers itself dissimilarly to the minds to be illuminated, it is the same and undissimilar. And when it shows itself present at once in diverse places and to diverse partners of participation, it is not manifold.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « But for the anagogic and unifying tempering-together of the things that have been provided, it is multiplied and comes forth most excellently and beautifully: and it remains within [0943D] itself securely, uniformly fixed in the unchangeable likeness of its nature: and, to those looking upon it, as far as is right, it extends itself proportionally to them, and unifies them according to the simple unity of itself. » As if he said: The ray of divine brightness, which illuminates those who shine spiritually, although it remains one in itself, is nevertheless variously multiplied by the participation and distribution of gifts, since it is distributed and multiplied in many and diverse ways. But this multiplication and variation is the beauty of all things; since, unless the several things were beautiful dissimilarly, all things together would not be supremely beautiful. For no one thing out of all the diverse things could contain what was the whole of beauty: and therefore the highest beauty was distributed by varied participation among the several things, that [0944A] it might be able to be perfect in all things together. But this distribution itself is multiplied most excellently and beautifully: most excellently in all things, and beautifully in the several, or, most excellently according to the disposition of the partakers. For it is multiplied most excellently, that the good which is perceived by many may be greater; and it is multiplied beautifully, that the comeliness of the whole may be greater — in that it is poured into the several partakers in varied and diverse ways, so that from the numerous multitude in the partakers there may come about the consummation of good, and from the dissimilar distribution the beauty of the partakers. Or the multiplication of the simple ray, by which we are illuminated, can suitably be understood according to the many and diverse figures and likenesses of the divine oracles: by which, taken according to the measure of us, the divine [0944B] wisdom — which in its purity and simplicity is altogether incomprehensible — is declared to human minds variously and manifoldly, although it is itself always one and the same. It is multiplied, therefore, because it is declared in many ways: most excellently indeed as regards itself, and beautifully as regards us. It is multiplied most excellently, because from the very multiplication of declarations it is more perfectly recognized; it is declared beautifully, because it is manifested by the beautiful and seemly appearance and dissimilarity of the forms that declare it. And this multiplication takes place for the tempering-together of those things that have been provided — that is, of the elect and of those provided for life — that they may be tempered together and united by the concord of participation, like many members in one body, so that the very diversity of gifts may confirm among them the fellowship [0944C] of unity and peace: so that each single member may not presume to divide itself from the framework of the whole body, since the fullness of good, which it has less in itself, it possesses in the fellowship of the others. For because each one has what another does not have, therefore each one has in the other what it does not have in itself, or has more in the other what it has less in itself. And therefore the several members tend toward one another and keep concord and peace among themselves: and this concord and peace is the tempering-together by which they agree with one another, that they may stand as one and be reformed to one. For this reason, therefore, he calls the tempering-together of those provided-for anagogic and unifying. Unifying indeed, that they may stand as one; anagogic, that they may be reformed [0944D] to one. Unifying, because it gathers the dispersed into one; anagogic, because it leads back the cast-down to higher things. Unifying, through the love of neighbor; anagogic, through the love of God. Therefore it is beautiful in congregation, best in elevation. Therefore the ray of divine illustration itself, multiplied whether through the distribution of spiritual gifts, by which it is partaken inwardly, or through the variety of mystical demonstrations, by which it is declared outwardly in the sacred oracle, comes forth — that is, proceeds into the hearts to be illuminated — whether from within filling them by inspiration, or from without pouring itself into them by the instruction of the sacred word: and yet it remains within itself securely, so that, while it offers itself to be partaken variously and manifoldly by the minds to be illuminated, it nevertheless does not flow out beyond the simplicity of its [0945A] unity. For its unity itself therefore always remains within itself, because a coessential unchangeableness guards and keeps it, so that, although a plurality of partakers approaches to the communication of it, yet the diversity of participation does not prevail to the division of it. It remains, therefore, within itself securely, that it may always be the same, and may not go out from that which it is into that which it is not: but remaining uniformly fixed in its unchangeable likeness, so that, namely, it may not differ from itself, as though made unlike itself. Nor does it alone remain one, but also to those looking upon it and turned toward it — as far as is right, that is, permitted and possible for them — it extends itself to them, that is, it reaches out so that it may come even to those who are far off, and yet does not withdraw from itself. It extends itself, namely, proportionally, [0945B] that is, so that some receive more, others less [sic: *miuus*], according to the dispensation of the distribution of gifts, or the possibility of the capacity of the partakers. For those who are nearest receive more abundantly from it: and so then each one, the more they approach, the more they receive; and the more they withdraw, the less they receive. Yet all receive; each one according to his part. This is what he says: « It extends itself proportionally, because nevertheless it extends itself to all, and pours itself into all, illuminates all, and makes them to be lights from its own light, and through this makes them to be one, just as it itself is one. And this is what he says: « It unifies them according to the simple unity of itself. » For the one illumination of all is one likeness and one form, in which all are one from it — they who [0945C] are illuminated by it — and one with it, since the illuminated are lights, just as it itself is light. Yet those who are one from it are one by unification, not by unity, because what they are, one out of many, they are one in one. For they themselves are many, and therefore they are not one in that which they are; but, because they are many in one, from one the many are one, and they are one by unification, not by unity. But it itself is one not by unification but by unity, because it is one from that which it is, which is one. And therefore he said "simple unity," because it is one from one, and the same one from which it is, it itself is. Therefore unity in one is simple; but unification is manifold in one, because those are one from one, and one in one: but here [0945D] one out of many, because they are many in one, and one in many. Then he adds the reason why the divine ray, that is, the divine wisdom, which is one from the Father and simple with the Father, is variously multiplied through the likenesses and forms of demonstrations in the sacred oracle: namely, because the invisible wisdom of God could not otherwise become known to us, unless it conformed itself unto likeness to those forms of visible things which we know, and through them expressed to us, by signifying, its invisible things which we do not know. Therefore the divine ray, which in itself is one and simple, coming to us, is multiplied by the variety of significations and demonstrations, because the human mind is not able to grasp it in its purity and simplicity.

[0946A] « For neither is it possible for the divine ray to shine upon us otherwise, unless, by the variety of sacred things, or the mind, [it be] anagogically veiled about, and prepared, connaturally and properly, by the fatherly providence, for those things which are according to us. » The sacred veils, in which the divine ray shines for us, are the mystical descriptions in the sacred oracle, which bring forward visible forms and likenesses of invisible things for their declaration. By which veils, namely, the divine ray itself is anagogically veiled about. For anagogy, as has been said, is called the ascent of the mind, or elevation, into the contemplation of the things above. It is therefore anagogically veiled about, because it is veiled for this end, that it may grow more fully bright; it is covered for this, that it may appear the more. Its overshadowing, therefore, is our illumination; and its veiling-about [0946B] is our elevation. Just as weak eyes freely behold the sun covered by a cloud, which cannot gaze upon its flashing light: so too it is impossible for the divine ray to shine upon the bleary eyes of the mind, unless it be veiled about by the variety of sacred veils and prepared, connaturally and properly, by fatherly providence, for those things which are according to us. For unless fatherly providence had provided for us in this with ineffable goodness, the light of it could not shine at all to our eyes: and therefore it provided for us fatherly and devoutly, that it might prepare that light for us and fit it to those things which are according to us — to things and likenesses and forms: and so, connaturally and properly, that is, according to our nature and the property of those things which belong to our nature, it might show it to us prepared [0946C] and fitted. It conforms itself to our things, that through our things it may become known to us, so that those things which are connatural and proper to us, set forth in demonstration, may more easily be understood by us.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « For which reason also the most perfect disposition of sacred things, judging our most holy hierarchy worthy of the supermundane imitation of the celestial hierarchies, handed down the said immaterial hierarchies too, varying them by material figures and formal compositions. » As if he said: Because invisible things are suitably demonstrated through visible things, therefore the divine Wisdom, which disposes all things to the likeness of the angelic hierarchy, which had been invisibly [0946D] ordered in the heavens, formed the human hierarchy visibly on earth, so that in men too, as in the angels, there might be sacred powers and principates, by which human conduct, proceeding in time, might be governed, so that from the visible disposition of men the invisible disposition of the angels might become known. For this is what he says: For which reason the most perfect disposition of sacred things — that is, the highest hierarchy, the ineffable Trinity itself, from which, and according to which, all sacred things, that is, all sacred ordinations, are disposed — judging our most holy hierarchy, that is, the human, worthy of the supermundane, that is, spiritual or intellectual, imitation of the celestial hierarchies, so that it might imitate them spiritually and be disposed according to them. And therefore "varying," that is, variously and manifoldly [0947A] declaring those same already-mentioned immaterial, that is, spiritual and angelic hierarchies by material, that is, corporeal and visible, or figures taken according to visible things, and by formal compositions, that is, fittingly and competently expressed according to the plan of the formations, "he hands down," that is, institutes, namely our most holy hierarchy itself, so that in this too the reason of visible demonstrations to the invisible truth might be preserved, that we may ascend from these most sacred formations, which are set forth outwardly for demonstration, to the unfigured heights and likenesses, that is, the simple and spiritual ones. Let us ascend, I say, proportionally to our very selves, that is, according to the proportion and measure of spiritual gifts, illuminated [0947B] through the things that are outward, into the recognition of invisible things, and to the sublime and simple truth that ever remains the same. For the heights and likenesses themselves are the invisible powers. Heights indeed by dignity, likenesses by unchangeableness, unfigured by simplicity. To the knowledge of which we ascend proportionally to our very selves, according to the difference of progressions and the increments of illuminations, by the grades of spiritual advancements in us, by the difference of gifts among us. For according to that proportion by which we now perceive the gifts of graces differently, we deservedly afterward pass over into the number of the angels — either here through the contemplation of the mind, or there through retribution. For thus we ascend proportionally to our very selves, when, according to [0947C] the fact that in the present we are disposed differently through the gifts of graces and the grades of dignities, we are lifted up to the participation of invisible truth by a secret and invisible promotion. But the truth itself, in us indeed, is figured figuratively and materially through the offices of spiritual dignities and the grades and distributions of the orders, and is formed according to habits and actions and visible signs, which are set forth in our hierarchy, as it were, for declaration — all which things, in the celestial and angelic hierarchy, according to the simple truth, in a manner sublime and superexcelling our nature, subsist purely and uniformly without signs and figures: according to which excellence theology calls the angelic orders themselves "heights" [0947D] and "likenesses," either because through no mutability do they differ from themselves, or because those things which are in them according to the simple truth seem to be, as it were, certain likenesses and exemplars of the things which subsist through figures and images. For unless there were some likeness between these and those, through these there would be no declaration of those. But now, since the divine wisdom formed these visible things, for the emulation of invisible things, in the first creation, in the second disposition it also established from these same things, for the rational soul ascending to the recognition of invisible things, signs and examples of demonstrations.

For this is what follows: « Since it is not possible for our soul to ascend to that non-material imitation and [0948A] contemplation of the celestial hierarchies, unless by that, » which is according to it, it make use of material hand-leading. By "non-material" he means the spiritual imitation, or contemplation, and the incorporeal; since everything that is corporeal is from matter. But the spiritual nature is rightly called immaterial, since it neither is from matter, so as to be from another, nor can be matter, so that something else be from it. But by "material hand-leading" he understands corporeal signs, by whose hand-leading, as it were, the human mind makes use, so that from visible things it may be directed to the imitation and contemplation of invisible things. To imitation indeed through the exercise of virtue; but to contemplation through the knowledge of truth. But note a new composition and a new likeness in the fact that he says "hand-leading." For the mind [0948B] of man, wrapped about by the darkness of its own ignorance, cannot go out to the light of truth unless it be directed and, like a blind man using a hand to lead him, walk on where he does not see. But these hand-leadings and directions, which the mind uses in tending toward invisible things, are taken from visible signs and from demonstrations formed according to visible things.
