And all this was disposed and ordered by the most perfect disposition of sacred things, which in Greek is called hierothesia, that is, the placing of sacred things — namely the highest Trinity, from which and according to which all sacred things are disposed and ordered in heaven and on earth — and by another name is called teletarche, that is, the beginning of purgation, since from it is every cleansing and beginning of purgation — not only that the things which are evil may be made good; but also of illustration [0948C] and deification, that the things which were good may be made better. Therefore this highest priesthood, or highest fatherhood, cleansing and sanctifying and illustrating all things, instituted and ordained this: that in the first place, according to it, the angelic nature might be invisibly formed, and in the second place the human nature, aroused through visible things, might be reformed to the same image; because the human soul cannot be led back to the knowledge or imitation of invisible things unless instructed through visible demonstrations, since the visible things themselves too were so made by God that, according to that likeness and emulation which they received toward those invisible things, they can suitably declare the same, so that our soul, using the leading of these, may be directed to those [0948D] according to these, estimating and weighing those things by the likeness of the demonstration.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

As it follows: « judging the visible forms to be imaginations, or images, of invisible beauty, » namely our own mind; « and the sensible sweetnesses figures of the invisible distribution, » — understand, our own mind judging; « and the material lights an image of the immaterial brightness, » so that again it is understood, our own mind judging; « the sacred disciplines run through according to the understanding of contemplative fullness, » here more is to be understood, our mind judging them to be images; « and the ordered dispositions, gathered unto divine things and set in order, of those things which are here, the orders of dispositions, » again understand, our mind judging [0949A] them to be images; « the very reception of the Eucharist of the participation of Jesus, that most divine reception, » again understand it, our mind judging it to be an image. The sum, then, of the whole chapter is this: Since our mind cannot ascend to the imitation and contemplation of invisible things unless it be directed through visible things — in such a way, namely, that from the visible things themselves it may know how to judge and estimate the invisible, according as they bear a likeness to those and effect a signification. But which likenesses of visible things unto invisible things our own mind ought to judge and estimate, he shows by subjoining certain distinct examples, as if he were saying: For this reason the truth of invisible things has been demonstrated through visible things: because our mind cannot ascend to the truth of those invisible things themselves unless it be instructed through the [0949B] consideration of visible things — in such a way, namely, that it may judge the visible forms to be imaginations of invisible beauty. For since in the forms of visible things their beauty consists, he fittingly says that invisible beauty is demonstrated from visible forms, since visible beauty is an image of invisible beauty. Yet because in visible things form is one thing and essence another, therefore whatever things are visible are mutably beautiful, since whatever things are diverse in number and by nature mutable do not inseparably subsist together. But invisible things, in which form is not one thing and essence another — since everything that is, is one and simple, and the same being — are beautiful from the very fact that they are; and their beauty is not compacted out of [0949C] many things running together into one, as the visible nature is seen to be, whose form is unfolded according to the spaces of places and is arranged through figures fitted together out of many. Therefore the beauty of the visible nature is one thing, and that of the invisible nature another, since the latter is simple and uniform, but the former manifold and led together by a varied proportion. There is nevertheless some likeness of visible beauty to invisible beauty, according to the emulation which the invisible Artificer established between the two, in which, as it were, certain reflections of diverse proportions fashion one image. According to this, then, the human mind, fittingly roused, ascends from visible beauty to invisible beauty; as though led [0949D] from like to like, easily understanding within itself, invisibly, what kinship there is to invisible things in the things which it comprehends outwardly and visibly. For according to the invisible light implanted in it, our mind, looking to invisible things, easily judges the visible forms to be images of invisible beauty, corresponding by a certain friendly likeness to that which it itself has invisibly within, finding them according to its approval and affection. For what is in the mind is invisible, just as the mind itself is invisible; and yet it, which is invisible, conceives from those things which are visible joy, and love, and affection; and it loves out of these certain things as though like, and friendly, and akin, and yields itself to them willingly, and exults in them. But others it spurns, and hates, and shuns, and puts itself far [0950A] from them, in love and delight; and it judges them foreign to itself, and discordant, and having no likeness with itself. And in this manner our mind is taught, from its own nature, that visible things have kinship and likeness to invisible things; and that the visible things themselves are images and likenesses of those things which cannot be seen visibly, since from these are understood those things which are not seen; and because, according to something, the whole of that which the mutable nature has here received visibly and sensibly subsists there invisibly in the immutable nature, so that it may lead to invisible things. For here there is a shape and form which delights the sight; there is also the pleasantness of melody, which soothes the hearing; there is the sweetness of odor, which restores the smell; there is the sweetness of savor, which pours into [0950B] the taste; and the softness of bodies, which cherishes and gently receives the touch. But there the shape is virtue, and the form justice, the sweetness love, and the odor desire; the song indeed joy and exultation; and the touch the finding of the loved, and desired, and sought good. For all these things are there, and are true there, and have with these things, which are not true, some likeness according to which they are understood by us. For from these our mind ascends to the knowledge and imitation of those things, judging the visible forms — which either nature shows implanted according to the first condition, or the sacred word sets forth dispensatively as a signification, to make a declaration — to be images of invisible beauty; and the sensible sweetnesses, that is, the sensible delights, to be figures and likenesses [0950C] of the invisible distribution, that is, of the sweetness which is distributed invisibly, that is, bestowed in diverse ways: namely, that one may receive more, another less, according to measure, and the gifts of the bounteous Giver. And likewise, that the material, that is, corporeal lights are an image of the immaterial brightness, that is, of spiritual light; and again, that the sacred disciplines, that is, the things investigated and searched out by the intellect, are images of contemplative fullness, that is, of full and perfect contemplation, which is both perceived and ministered according to the understanding, that is, intellectually only and invisibly. For all that knowledge which we now learn through the sacred word by the study of reading or meditation is, as it were, only an image of that full [0950D] and perfect knowledge which we shall afterward draw from the contemplation that is to come. Whence the Apostle also says: « We see now through a glass, in a riddle; but then face to face [n: (I Cor. XIII)]. » What wonder is it, then, if those sensible things which appear outwardly are said to be images of invisible things, when our very knowledge, which in comparison with these is believed to be spiritual and invisible, is reckoned to hold the place of an image and likeness in relation to those things?

There follows: « and the ordered dispositions, gathered unto divine things, of those things which are here, the orders of dispositions. » Again understand, our mind judging the orders of dispositions: « which dispositions are here, » that is, exteriorly, to be images of a state gathered unto divine things. For there is a certain state of the good mind gathered [0951A] unto divine things, and, as it were, collected into one out of the various rents of desires, and set in order, so that it may go forward rightly. Gathered, namely, through love, and set in order through discretion. Gathered unto one, and ordered in one. Of which state, invisibly ordered and subsisting, the orders of dispositions are images, which are dispensed outwardly in the grades, and offices, and ministries in the holy Church. For just as in one Church diverse orders and dispositions effect one peace and concord in the whole, by ministering and cooperating, so in one soul many virtues, by cooperating and ministering to one another, compose one form of perfection. But also, in each one of us, the disposition of the outer man, and the order of living, and the manner of acting, demonstrate the form [0951B] and state of the inner man.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « and the very reception of the most divine Eucharist of the participation of Jesus. » Again understand, as above, our mind judging the reception itself of the most divine Eucharist to be an image of the participation of Jesus. For the very reception of the most divine Eucharist, that is, of the most holy partaking of the body and blood of Jesus Christ, which we now handle sacramentally and visibly upon the altar, is an image and form of that participation of Jesus by which either now we are joined to him in spirit through love, or afterward, appearing in the same form of glory, we shall be united to him in full likeness. Now here it is to be noted that certain men have thought to allege from this passage a bulwark for their error, saying that in the sacrament of the altar there is not the truth [0951C] of the body and blood of Christ, but only an image and figure of it: for this reason, because the Scripture says that what is taken in the Eucharist of the altar is an image of that which will be received in the participation of Jesus. Who assuredly would not fall into this snare of error, if they either received the sacraments of God with a right and humble faith, or handled the sacred Scriptures with a fitting understanding. But now, because in the sacraments of God they prefer their own sense to faith, and in the sacred Scriptures scorn to hold the sound form of interpretation, it comes about that the very word of truth makes them grow yet more blind, while, not rightly understood, it ministers error in place of truth. Which, however, is not the fault of Scripture, but the blindness of those who read and do not understand; [0951D] nor a confusion of the sacraments of God, but the depravity of the presumptuous. Here, however, they have erred perilously, preferring one ambiguous thing to so many manifest sentences and undoubted assertions, and choosing in it the lie rather than the truth — not because this was more said there, but because this was more believed by them. For what? Is the sacrament of the altar therefore not truth, because it is a figure? Then neither is the death of Christ truth, because it is a figure; and the resurrection of Christ is not truth, because it is a figure. For that both the death of Christ and the resurrection are a figure, and are an image, and a likeness, and a sacrament, and an example, the Apostle plainly declares, saying: « Christ died for our sins, and rose again for our justification [0952A]: that, dead to sins, we might live to justice [n: (I Cor. XV)]. » And the apostle Peter said: « Christ suffered for us, leaving you an example, that you should follow his steps [n: (I Petr. II)]. » Therefore the death of Christ was an example, that we might die to sin, and his resurrection was an example, that we might live to justice. Was it therefore not truth? Then Christ did not truly die, and did not truly rise again, if his death, or resurrection, was not true. God forbid! For of him it is written: « Surely he himself hath borne our infirmities, and himself carried our sorrows » [n: (Isa. LIII)]. Therefore the death of Christ was true, and yet was an example; and the resurrection of Christ was true, and yet was an example. Why then can the sacrament of the altar not be both a likeness and a truth? In one thing indeed [0952B] a likeness, and in another a truth. For, since the sacrament is one, three distinct things are there set forth, namely: the visible appearance, and the truth of the body, and the virtue of spiritual grace. For the visible appearance, which is visibly discerned, is one thing. The truth of the body and blood, which under the visible appearance is invisibly believed, is another. And the spiritual grace, which is invisibly and spiritually received together with the body and blood, is yet another. For what we see is the appearance of bread and wine; but what we believe under that appearance is the true body and true blood of Christ Jesus, which hung upon the cross, and which flowed from his side. Nor is only the body and blood signified through the bread and wine; but under the appearance of bread and wine the true body and true blood is consecrated. And indeed the visible appearance [0952C] is a sacrament of the true body and true blood; but the body and blood is a sacrament of spiritual grace. And just as there the appearance is discerned, whose reality or substance is not believed to be there, so there the reality is believed to be truly and substantially present, whose appearance is not discerned. For the appearance of bread and wine is seen, and the substance of bread and wine is not believed. But the substance of the body and blood of Christ is believed, and yet its appearance is not discerned.

What, then, is seen according to the appearance is a sacrament, and an image of that which is believed according to the truth of the body; and what is believed according to the truth of the body is a sacrament of that which is received according to spiritual grace. The sacrament, then, [0952D] of the altar and the divine Eucharist is, in the true body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, both an image according to the appearance of bread and wine, in which it is discerned, and a reality according to the truth of its own substance, in which it is there believed and received. And again, that we now visibly, according to the appearance of the sacrament, and corporeally, according to the truth of flesh and blood, take Christ upon the altar, is a sacrament and image of the fact that we ought to take that same one invisibly and spiritually, according to the infusion of grace and the participation of his Spirit, in the heart. Therefore the most divine Eucharist, which upon the altar is handled visibly and corporeally both according to the appearance of bread and wine and according to the truth of the body and blood of Christ, is a sacrament, and a sign, and an image [0953A] of the invisible and spiritual participation of Jesus, which is accomplished within, in the heart, through faith and love. For the Wisdom of God, which manifests itself through the visible, willed to show that it is itself the food and refreshment of minds; and therefore it set forth the assumed flesh as food, that through the food of the flesh it might invite to the taste of the Godhead. But, lest again human weakness should shrink from the touch of flesh in the receiving, it veiled it with the appearance of the accustomed and principal food, and thus set it forth to be taken, so that the sense might be cherished in the one, and faith built up in the other. For the sense is cherished in the one, while it perceives only the usual and accustomed; but faith is built up in the other, while in that which it sees it recognizes what manner of thing that is which it does not see. The appearance of bread and wine is therefore set forth, that it may be taught [0953B] that there is full and perfect refreshment in the taking of the body and blood of Christ from the Godhead of Christ. Now full refreshment is food and drink; but of food and drink, bread and wine is the principal substance. And the appearance is set forth from the principal substance of refreshment, that in it may be taken, and through it may be signified, the truth of the body and blood, as he himself testifies, saying: « My flesh is meat indeed, and my blood is drink indeed [n: (Joan. VI)]. » Yet that this taking of the body and blood, alone and without a spiritual effect, does not confer salvation, the same Savior himself manifests, saying: « The flesh profiteth nothing; it is the spirit that quickeneth [n: (ibid.)]. » Therefore the virtue and fullness of the spiritual refreshment which is in the body and blood of Christ is signified indeed through the appearance of bread and wine; [0953C] but it is accomplished in the receiving of grace, and in the infusion of the inward and eternal refreshment. And thus indeed, since three things are there in one, in the first is found a sign of the second; in the second the cause of the third; but in the third the virtue of the second, and the truth of the first. And these three are in one, and are one sacrament. It is clear, therefore, that the reception of the most divine Eucharist is a sacrament, and an image of the participation of Jesus; because this, that we visibly receive the sacrament of it, is a sign that we ought to be united to him spiritually and invisibly. But the Eucharist itself, that is, the good grace, that very sacred host, is called most divine, [0953D] since it makes divine, and partakers of the Godhead, those who partake of it. And because it is a sign, and a truth in which the true flesh of Christ is taken under the appearance of bread, and in his flesh, worthily taken, the reception too, and participation, and fellowship of his very Godhead is granted. Therefore it is most worthy, and most holy, and sanctifying all things that sanctify, and holy.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « And whatever other things have been handed down to the heavenly essences in a supramundane manner, but to us symbolically. » After he had set forth certain things, by way of example, to show the likeness of visible things to invisible, now he concludes generally about the whole, saying: « And whatever other things have been handed down to the heavenly essences in a supramundane manner, but to us symbolically, » as if he were saying: These things above mentioned, the visible things, our mind ought to judge [0954A] to have a likeness to invisible things according to the examples set forth; and not these alone, but whatever other things have been handed down to us symbolically, or to the heavenly essences supramundanely. For not only these visible things which have been set down — that is, the forms, the sweetnesses, the lights, the disciplines, the orders, the sacred Eucharist — have the manifestation and demonstration of invisible things, even if a multitude of them; but also all other visible things whatsoever, which have been handed down to us — who are to be instructed visibly — symbolically, that is, figuratively, have been set forth for the signification and declaration of invisible things. And not only these visible things which have been handed down to us symbolically have a demonstration of invisible things; but those too which have been handed down to the heavenly essences, that is, to the angelic spirits, supramundanely, that is, invisibly and spiritually, and not [0954B] according to the appearances of this world, are signs of invisible things, and images of those things which, in the excellent and incomprehensible nature of the Godhead, subsist above all understanding and sense. For this is what he says: that not only those things which have been handed down to us symbolically are signs of invisible things; but those too which have been handed down to the heavenly essences supramundanely. For the angelic spirits themselves also have their signs, and demonstrations, through which they are instructed concerning the invisible things of God, and things exceedingly hidden, and secrets concealed within, immaterially, and invisibly, and simply. Which signs indeed, as far as concerns us and those things which are visible among us, are reckoned altogether invisible; but as far as concerns that light, much invisible, and inaccessible, and incomprehensible, [0954C] of the Deity, they are as it were outside, and proceed from within into demonstration. On which account those very signs, which come upon minds, or upon souls divinely illuminated, are called theophanies, that is, divine apparitions; because in them, coming to manifestation, that which is altogether hidden of God is demonstrated. These, then, are the things which are handed down to the heavenly ones supramundanely, not according to that demonstration which is among us, but breathed in invisibly and simply. Many things indeed were here to be said about this kind of contemplation, by which theophanies, that is, divine apparitions divinely breathed in, come upon minds to be illuminated, and, by instructing them concerning the hidden and invisible things of God in a wondrous, and concealed, and secret, and [0954D] singular manner, make them wise: especially since here too certain men are found to have become vain in their thoughts, proclaiming God to be altogether incomprehensible and inaccessible to the rational mind, except that it is instructed concerning him by certain theophanies, that is, divine apparitions, or divine likenesses set forth for contemplation. But these they place, as it were, certain images of the hidden Godhead, as means between rational souls and God, higher indeed than the mind, but lower than the Godhead. And this alone, they say, is seen of God, and in this alone is God seen — inasmuch as he in himself can be seen by no mind or soul. But these are likenesses of them, and phantasms of vanity: in which, while they try to assert that only the light of the Godhead is visible and perceptible, [0955A] they are proved to take away from holy minds the true knowledge and vision of the Deity. For what is it, that God is seen in those things alone, and outside them is not seen, save that he is never truly seen, and the truth is never seen? For if the image alone is always seen, and the truth is never seen, since the image is not the truth, even when it is of the truth. Let them therefore take away their phantasies, by which they strive to overshadow the light of our minds; nor let them fence off from us our God by the likenesses of their own conjectures; because, just as nothing besides himself can satisfy us, so neither can anything come to a stand short of himself. Let us therefore reckon these theophanies in another manner, and one consonant with truth. For just as there are two things, the light and the body that receives the light — and out of these two is made one thing that [0955B] shines, and that shining thing is in a manner an image and likeness of the light, in that it shines as the light itself does — so also our God is light, and is true light, and rational souls, clean and pure, conceive that very light: and from it they become shining, and they themselves are not an image of the light in that they are; but in that they shine from the light, as the light itself shines; and those shining things are theophanies of the light, in which the light is seen, since by none would the light be seen, if none were illuminated by the light. For he too who sees the light in himself sees himself shining; who assuredly would not see, if he did not shine, and would not see himself shining. Thus, then, we set no other between our God and us, but make a way immediately, both for us to him, and for him even to us, that we may be in him, and [0955C] he in us: so that there be nothing outside himself in which we may be beatified, just as there could be nothing besides himself by which we might be created. On account of this, therefore, let us interpret the aforesaid sentence of theology to a more convenient understanding. For because he had said that certain invisible things are demonstrated through those visible images which are determinate, now he subjoins a general sentence concerning the invisible things themselves, saying: « And whatever other things, etc. » As if he were saying: Not only those invisible things, of which these signs have been set forth, came into manifestation; but also whatever other invisible things which have been handed down to us — namely to men — symbolically, that is, figuratively and through sensible demonstrations, that is, set forth and made manifest; [0956A] but to the heavenly essences, that is, to the angelic spirits, supramundanely and spiritually, revealed through a bare and simple truth impressed.

There follows: « On account of this, then, our co-rational theosis, » that is, divinity, which is to us co-rational, that is, congruous or apt. For it is congruous that through those things which are known to us we should be made partakers of divine things: « On account of this, then, etc., » as if he were saying: Because the human mind cannot be instructed concerning invisible things save through visible things, and things known and akin to it, therefore « the merciful Principle of perfection, » that is, God, from whom all perfection has its beginning, as in him it takes its consummation and end — by grace and mercy alone, that he might call us back to his own knowledge, and reform his likeness; [0956B] « manifesting to us the heavenly hierarchies, » that is, the angelic powers; « and perfecting also our hierarchy as fellow-minister of them, » namely of the heavenly hierarchies, that it might be both conformable to them in dignity, and like them in divine ministry, the deiform likeness of their sanctification being preserved in us, that we may be like the sanctification of them who are conformed to God, « according to our virtue, » that is, according to our possibility, that is, as far as we are able to be like — we who are men, and mortal, and still liable to sin; « he described the supercelestial intellects, » the invisible spirits, who were unknown to us, by sensible images, that through the known we might learn the unknown. And this description he made [0956C] « in the sacred compositions of the words, » that is, in the descriptions of the sacred word: which compositions set forth to us both figures and likenesses for the demonstration of invisible things. And this he did for this reason, « that he might lead us back through sensible things to intellectual things, » that is, through visible things to invisible things. And that he might also lead us back and « from sacredly figured symbols, » that is, from sacred figures and the figures of sacred things; « into the simple heights, » that is, into the simple and spiritual excellences to be known « of the heavenly hierarchies, » that is, of the angelic powers. For this reason he described them to us by visible signs, that he might make them intelligible to us, and conform us to their imitation.

## THIRD BOOK.

[0955]

## TITLE OF CHAPTER II: *That divine and heavenly things are beautifully manifested even through dissimilar symbols.*



## THE TEXT.

[0955C]

*It behooves us, then, as I judge, first to set forth what indeed we reckon the speculation of every hierarchy to be, and what each of them has profited the divine praisers of it. Then to praise the heavenly hierarchies according to their manifestation in the words, and, in what follows these, to say by what divine formations the sacred [0956C] descriptions of the words figure the heavenly orders, and to what truth one ought to ascend through the forms. That we too may not, in the same manner as the many, uncleanly reckon the heavenly and deiform minds to be certain many-footed things, and of many faces, and formed to oxen and to a cattle-like beastliness [pecuinitatem], or to the bestial imagination of lions, or figured to the appearance of eagles with curved beak, or to the tripartite motion of the wings of flying things: and imagine certain fiery [0957A] wheels above the heaven, and material thrones necessary for the Divinity to recline upon; and certain many-colored horses, and arms-bearing chief-commanders; and whatever other things have been handed down to us from the sacred words, and formatively, in the variety of manifesting symbols [n: (Ezech. I; Apoc. IV; Isa. VI; Dan. VII; Zach. I; Apoc. VI; Ezech. XXIII; Job. XVI; Sap. V; Josue V; II Machab. III)]*. *For indeed most artfully has theology used poetic sacred formations in un-figured intellects: revealing, as has been said, our mind, and providing for it by a proper and connatural reduction, and reforming unto itself the anagogical holy Scriptures. But if to anyone it seems that the sacred compositions ought indeed to be received as of things simple in themselves and unknown to us, and of things incontemplable [0957B] in their subsisting: but he reckons unfitting the descriptions of the holy intellects in the sacred words, and all such speaking hard — this theater of angelic names; and that the theologians ought, he says, coming universally to a corporeal fashioning among corporeal things, to form and manifest these by their proper figurations, and, as far as possible, akin, from the most precious things among us, and in any way immaterial, and supereminent essences — and not to set the earthly, most base, manifold forms round about the heavenly and deiform simplicities. This indeed would be both more sublime for us, and would not lead down the supramundane manifestations into unfitting dissimilitudes. This too would at once not unjustly do injury to the divine virtues, and likewise would not seduce our [0957C] mind, thrusting itself into unclean compositions. And perhaps also the supercelestial things will be reckoned to be filled with certain leonine and equine multitudes; and with a bellowing utterance of praises, and with the flying headlong rush of angels, and with other animals, and with more ignoble matters, as though shut up unto the inconsequent, and ignoble, and passible, while there are described throughout as deiform the clear likenesses of the manifesting words. But the inquiry of truth, as I judge, shows that the most sacred wisdom of the words [n: (Ezech. XXVIII; Job. XXXVIII)] has in the formations of the heavenly minds provided exceedingly for both: so that neither would it (so perhaps someone might say) do injury to the divine virtues; nor would it fix you, in a passible manner, into the vile lownesses of images. For because indeed there have been beautifully procured [0957D] forms of the formless, and figures of things lacking figures, one might say that it is not one single cause — that our analogy, not being able to be extended immediately into invisible contemplations, and desiring its proper and connatural reductions: which hold out to us the passible formations of formless and supernatural speculations. But because this too is most seemly for mystical words, to conceal by incomprehensible divine [sic: *aenigmat*], and to set trackless to the many the sacred and hidden truth of the supramundane intellects. For not every one is holy, nor is knowledge, as the words say, of all [n: (Matth. VII; I Cor. II)]*. *But if anyone should reckon the deformed causes of the description of images dishonorable, saying that base [0958A] formations are thus referred to deiform and most holy dispositions, it suffices to say to him: How is the mode of holy manifestation twofold? One indeed as it were consequent, on account of the like images of the sacred figures that come forth; but the other, on account of the dissimilar makings of forms, conformed to what is altogether inconsequent and unseemly. And so the mystical traditions of the manifesting words sometimes praise the blessedness of the Godhead, which is to be venerated above essence, as reason, and intellect, and essence; declaring the divine rationality and its wisdom, and the truly existing substance, and the true cause of those things which are substance; and they form it as though light, and call it life, so many wondrous, more chaste formations abiding, and the material formations [0958B] being proved in some way to fall short of excelling; and thus by a likeness of the divine unto truth. For it is above all essence and life, no light indeed characterizing it, all reason and intellect being incomparably left behind by any likeness of it. But sometimes it is praised supramundanely by dissimilar manifestations from the very words [n: (Rom. XI; I Tim. VI; Psalm. CXL)], which call it invisible, and infinite, and incomprehensible: and by which is signified not what it is, but what it is not. For this, as I judge, is more powerful in its case. Since indeed, as the hidden and priestly tradition has introduced, we truly say that it is not according to anything of those things which are. But we do not know its superessential, and invisible, and ineffable infinitude. [0958C] If, therefore, negations in divine matters are true, but affirmations incompact, the manifestation through dissimilar formations is more apt to the obscurity of the arcane things. And so now the sacred descriptions of the words do not fill up the heavenly ornaments with base things, manifesting them by dissimilar makings of forms, and through these showing, together with all material things, the excellences of the supramundane things. Nay rather, that dissimilar likenesses lead our mind back more — I do not think any of the well-wise contradict. For through the more precious sacred formations it is consequent to be seduced, reckoning the heavenly essences to be certain gold-formed things, and certain resplendent men clothed in comely garments, sprinkling harmlessly a white and fiery brightness, and by whatever other like imaginal forms theology has [0958D] figured the heavenly intellects. And lest they who understand nothing higher than visible goods should suffer this, the restorative wisdom of the holy theologians has wondrously descended to unseemly dissimilitudes: not granting our material part, remaining in base images, to rest; but purging, and bearing upward, and suggesting to the soul by the deformity of the compositions — as neither the just nor the true approving — that the supercelestial and divine spectacles are by no means, according to truth, like to things very material, because thus base. But so this too it behooves to understand: That nothing of those things which are, is universally deprived of the participation of the good: since indeed, as the Truth of the words says: « All things very good [n: (Gen. I)]*. » *It is possible, then, from all things to understand good [0959A] speculations, and, of both invisible and intellectual things, to form from material things the said dissimilar likenesses: the intellectual things having in another way those things which are otherwise distributed to sensible things. For fury indeed in irrational things is from a passible motion: and the furious motion of them is filled with all irrationality; but in intellectual things one must understand the irascible in another way, declaring, as I judge, their manly reasonableness, and their immense [immanentem] rest in divine and immutable foundations. In the same way we say that concupiscence indeed is in irrational things a certain inconsiderate and material thing, from a natural motion, or from custom incontinently engendered in mutable things — an impassibility, and an irrational continuity of bodily pleasure; at once compelling every animal [0959B] according to sense to what is unfit to be desired. But when, setting dissimilar likenesses about intellectual things, we form concupiscence about them, we must understand it to be divine love — above reason, and above the intellect of immateriality, and the inflexible and needless desire of a superessentially chaste and impassible contemplation, and that pure and most sublime brightness, and the invisible and form-making beauty of eternal, true, and invisible fellowship. And as it were it receives power in sufficiency and in convertibility; and it is afflicted by no virtue, through the unconfused and immutable love of the divine beauty, and the universal recalling into that which is truly to be sought. But that very irrationality and insensibility [0959C], in irrational animals indeed, or in animate matters, we properly call a defect of reason and sense; but in immaterial and intellectual essences we confess it, holily and fittingly, to be their supereminences, as of supramundane things, excelling our transitory and corporeal reason, and the material sense of the mind, alienated from incorporeal things. And so it is possible to form for the heavenly things forms not dissonant, even from vile parts of matter. Since it too, possessing subsistence from the truly good, has through all its material disposition certain images of intellectual beauty; and it is possible through them to be led back to the immaterial first forms — the likenesses being received dissimilarly, as has been said, and not in like manner with them: but compactly and beautifully, the intellectual [0959D] and sensible properties being defined. These mystical theologians we shall find wondrously conforming not only to the declarations of the heavenly dispositions, but sometimes to the divine manifestations themselves. And sometimes indeed they praise it from precious lights, as the Sun of justice, as the morning star holily rising in the mind, and as a light shining unenveloped and invisibly [n: (Malach. I; Apoc. II)]*. *But sometimes from middle things, as fire shining harmlessly, as water the giver of vital fullness, and, as it must be said symbolically, entering into the belly, and pouring back rivers that flow again immeasurably. But sometimes from the lowest things, as a sweet ointment, as a corner-stone [n: (Exod. III; Joan. I)]*. » *But they set about it also a bestial form; and fit to it the special property of a lion and of a panther [0960A], and clothe it with a leopard's skin, and a raging she-bear [sic: ur sam] [n: (Cant. II; Isa, XXVIII; Osee V)]*. *I will add [sic: Ad dam] indeed that which seemed to be the vilest of all, and to signify more; since the divinely wise [divine wisdom] handed it down as forming itself even into the appearance of a worm [n: (Psal. XXI)]*. *So too all the theosophers, and the prophets by hidden inspiration, distinguish the Holy of Holies from the undefiled saints, and honor the dissimilar holy figuration, so that neither may divine things be rightly received by the unclean, nor may the students of wondrous images remain in the figures of contemplation as though in true things. And so they honor divine things by true negations, and by diverse likenesses of images compacted unto the lowest things. Nothing, therefore, is inconsequent, if they form the heavenly essences too from unfitting, dissimilar likenesses [0960B] according to the said causes. For perhaps we would not indeed come to the question, out of need, unto the anagogy, through the diligent scrutiny of divine things, unless the deformity of the manifesting deformation of the angels forced us out: not suffering our mind to remain in the dissimilar makings of forms, but making it struggle to refuse the material passibilities, and accustoming it to extend itself purely through visible things into the supramundane heights. So much, then, has been said by us on account of the material and unfitting angelic descriptions of the images of the divine words. But then it behooves to set apart what indeed we reckon the hierarchy itself to be, and what those who obtain a hierarchy profit from the hierarchy itself. But let Christ be my leader (if indeed it is lawful for me to say), the [0960C] inspiration of the whole hierarchical manifestation. But thou, O boy, according to the holy legislation of our priestly tradition, do thou holily and fittingly hearken, made divine in the divine doctrine of the things wondrously said, covering round in the secret of the mind the things that are holy, and, from the unclean multitude, guard them as uniform. For it is not lawful, as the words say, to cast to swine the unconfused, and light-formed, and beneficent ornament of the invisible pearls [n: (Matth. VII)]*.



## EXPOSITION.

First I said, and I say now — that I may not detain you in expectation — that I undertook your petition upon the Hierarchy of Dionysius, not that I should pursue the searching-out of the deep things of the matters, but that I should only uncover, and bring into the light [0960D], the coverings of the words. For this is more fitting, first, for those who are to be introduced: especially because those things which we judged were to be discussed we recognize to be exceedingly great, and above our possibility. The title of the second chapter is this: « That divine and heavenly things are beautifully manifested even through dissimilar symbols. » Above we have already said what a symbol is: namely a collation, that is, a fitting-together of visible forms set forth for the demonstration of an invisible thing. For example, when we wish to express visibly the natures of the heavenly spirits, we fit human faces indeed, but the wings of a bird, into one appearance of composition, so that, by the face of a man — who alone among visible things uses reason — the invisible spirits themselves too may be understood to be rational and wise; but by the wings the agility [0961A] of their nature, and their swift motion toward all things, may be expressed. Since, then, in the first chapter he discussed generally concerning every hierarchy, as though briefly foretasting the sum of the following work in order to make instruction — first, that every good is multiplied from the highest good by participation, and every good is united to the highest good by likeness and conversion; then, that Scripture fittingly took up visible signs for the declaration of invisible things: now in the following chapter he shows, as the title itself declares beforehand, that beautifully — that is, aptly and fittingly — are manifested, that is, represented and signified, that they may be made manifest: divine things, namely those which are in the highest hierarchy, and heavenly things, which are in the angelic hierarchy. Both these are fittingly manifested, [0961B] not only through like symbols, that is, not only through beautiful and comely figures and forms, congruous or like to their majesty and purity; but also through dissimilar symbols, that is, through such forms and descriptions as seem foreign to their excellence and unworthy of their purity. Which indeed may seem to some to be less fitting. But to those who consider well, it is found to be ordered by a great reason and a necessary dispensation. By reason indeed, so that while we discern these foreign things taken up for demonstration, we may recognize that those things too, which seemed to be proper according to something, are foreign and dissimilar from the highest truth. But by dispensation, so that while the pious mind sees those things, which it does not endure to be placed among divine things according to their propriety, attributed by the signification [0961C] of them, it may in no way be able to doubt that other things too, which seemed worthy, and through this could be believed to be true and proper, are figurative and led to truth by likeness. Therefore the like symbols are set forth for demonstration to this end, that they might show the truth of invisible things by a like appearance; but the dissimilar ones, that by signifying they might demonstrate that one must go out from the figure to the truth, and not remain in those things which could not be true. Therefore, as much as the like symbols excel in appearance, yet the dissimilar symbols transcend in signification; since, even if the former have a more beautiful appearance, yet the latter hold a more manifest signification. The former show the truth; the latter compel us to go out from falsity. The former so signify the truth that [0961D] the untrained mind can easily be detained in them; the latter so instruct that they do not suffer the minds of those who consider to be deceived in their veneration. The former, though they are signs of the truth, would yet perhaps sometimes cause themselves to be received in place of the truth on account of their excellent appearance, unless these were signs in which the truth is not believed, even though through them the truth is signified. For the truth is one thing, and the sign of the truth another; because the sign is not the truth, even when it is a sign of the truth, and is true. Those signs, therefore, have a more evident demonstration, which both, through the likeness by which they approach the truth, manifest the truth itself; and, through the dissimilitude by which they are far removed from the truth, demonstrate that they are not the truth, but signs nevertheless, and an image of the truth. [0962A] This, then, is what he intends to demonstrate in this chapter, as is expressed through its title: that divine and heavenly things are beautifully and fittingly demonstrated not only through like, but also through dissimilar forms and figurations. And that no injury, as some have reckoned, is done to those divine and heavenly things, if they are sometimes figured in the sacred Scriptures through humble formations, and, as it were, removed from their excellence, and unworthy — as in Scripture God is compared to a wagon, and the angels to oxen: and the rest in this manner.

« It behooves us, then, as I judge, first to set forth what indeed we reckon the speculation of every hierarchy to be, etc. » Modestly he tempers his assertion, as befits a prudent man; nor in excellent matters, [0962B] and remote from the human sense, does he seem to presume beyond man's possibility, saying: « As I judge. » For he himself knew this which he said, since he had been taught by him who had seen and knew. But he kept the modesty of speech, that he might show that these things are to be sought and found by humility, not by elation. It behooves us, then, as I judge, first to set forth what indeed we reckon the speculation « of every hierarchy to be. » Five things here he first sets forth, generally, by way of introduction to the readers: which through the following chapters he will set forth one by one. First, the general definition of every hierarchy. Second, the utility of each hierarchy. Third, the praise of the heavenly hierarchies according to the visible formations which are found in the sacred word. [0962C] Fourth, the quality of those very formations and descriptions by which the heavenly virtues are signified. Fifth, what truth the human mind ought to conceive, in those very invisible things, from those things which are visibly set forth for the declaration and praise of invisible things. That is: to consider, first, what a hierarchy is. Secondly, what it profits, that is, what utility consists in each one. Thirdly, how the excellence of the invisible hierarchies is shown through visible signs. Fourthly, to know the quality of the mystical signs and demonstrations. Fifthly, to separate the thing signified from the sign, the truth from the figure. It behooves us first to set forth what speculation, that is, what definition we reckon to be of every hierarchy, that is, how generally [0962D] or universally a hierarchy is to be defined. But he calls the definition a speculation for this reason; since the definition of a thing is as it were a mirror, in which the nature of the thing itself is discerned, just as in a mirror the image of the nature of the body placed before it is seen. A general definition is that which fits universally, and is found in the whole. For he who says that the angelic principalities are a hierarchy, says truly; but he does not define universally, since a hierarchy is found among men also. Again, he who says that a hierarchy is an order, says truly; since where there is power, there is order. But he does not say the whole; since not every order is a hierarchy, because not every order is a power. And so a general definition is that which fits every one and contains the whole. [0963A] This general definition of a hierarchy, therefore, the author named the speculation of every hierarchy: which both fits every one, and contains the whole. But because he himself is about to place and set forth this in what follows, we ought not to anticipate it at present. Then he subjoins another of those things which one ought to search out or set forth, namely, what each hierarchy has profited its divine praisers, that is, what or how much each hierarchy, that is, each sacred power, has profited its divine praisers, that is, those who, established and ordained in it, praise God, or celebrate and frequent the divine praises. For all the hierarchies are ordained and instituted unto the divine praise, whether the higher or the lower, so that by all God may be praised: by whom and through whom are all things, and [0963B] in all things all things [n: (I Cor. XV)]. And those who are more excellent praise more; and those who praise more are more sublimely rewarded. And so every hierarchy profits its own divine praisers, in proportion to what it has received according to the distribution of grace and the gifts of bounty, so that it may be either more sublime in knowledge, or more fervent in love. For according to grace are the gifts, and according to the gifts are the merits, and according to the diversity of the merits the diversity of the rewards is established. Every hierarchy, therefore, has profited its praisers of God, in reward, in proportion to what it conferred in gift; because every dignity and power, according to the grace of ministry and office by which it was able to proclaim and amplify the praise, and honor, and glory of God, grows greater in the fruit of retribution. But the heavenly virtues themselves also, [0963C] to which this very thing is a reward — to praise God — receive, each according to their orders and dignities in their principalities and hierarchies, so much of utility as they rise up, more or less, in the praise of the Creator through the gift of cooperating grace. Then, he says, « it behooves, » that is, after the divine definition of every hierarchy and its utility has been set forth, it behooves to praise the heavenly hierarchies, that is, the angelic principalities, that is, to describe and demonstrate their praises according to the manifestation of them, namely of the hierarchies, in the words, that is, according to this, that their praises are manifested in the words, that is, in the divine Scriptures. For there are set forth in the sacred word figures and demonstrations by which the virtues and praises [0963D] of the angelic powers are declared: which the author says are to be inspected and considered, unto the manifestation of the angelic hierarchy.

There follows: « And in what follows these, » that is, consequently after those things aforesaid, or in those things which follow: It behooves « to say by what divine formations the sacred descriptions of the words, namely the divine words, figure the heavenly orders. » As if he were saying: First it behooves to consider how the divine words proclaim the heavenly virtues as praiseworthy through the formations of the descriptions. Then, of what quality these very descriptions and formations are, which by a certain divine speech they figure for the declaration of those heavenly virtues. But last of all it behooves to consider to what truth one ought to ascend through [0964A] the forms. For it is not enough to attend to this alone, that it is visible, unless we also know of what quality we ought, from that which is visibly demonstrated, invisibly to think the truth. For if we think that this alone is what is visibly discerned in the demonstration, we are never made partakers of the truth. Let us therefore discern the sign from the truth, and let us know that one thing is that which is set outwardly for the instructing of the sense; but another that which is reserved within for the beatifying of the mind. Therefore it behooves to say to what truth we ought to ascend from these forms of the visible figurations, lest we too, like many, should reckon all these things, which are set forth figuratively in the Scriptures as signs of the heavenly ones, to be there just as here they are mystically figured in likenesses and forms [0964B] and figures pertaining nevertheless to corporeal and sensible things: which by no means fall into the divine and spiritual things.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

For this is what follows: « So that neither may we too, in the same fashion as the many, impurely suppose the heavenly and deiform minds to be certain many-footed and many-faced things, and formed after the herd-nature of oxen, or after the bestial imagination of lions, and shaped after the appearance of eagles with a curved beak, or after the threefold beating of the wings of flying things. And that we should imagine certain fiery wheels above the heaven; and material thrones necessary to the Divinity for its reclining, and certain horses of many colors, and arms-bearing supreme commanders; and whatever other things have been handed down to us out of the sacred words, sacredly and in a form-giving way, in the variety of manifesting [0964C] symbols. » So that neither may we too, then, in the same fashion as the many — that is, in the way that the many suppose — suppose the heavenly and deiform minds, that is, the invisible, incorporeal spirits, which have the likeness and form of God, not of bodies, to be many-footed; namely, in the way in which they are figuratively described in the sacred word under the figures and forms of animals, as in Ezekiel, in Isaiah, and Zechariah, and Micah, and other prophets, the angelic spirits are figured by four-footed animals, and flying things, and the rest in this manner. And lest we also suppose certain of those spirits to be many-faced, that is, to have many countenances or faces, as it is written in Ezekiel concerning the holy living creatures: « Four [0964D] faces had one of them [n: (Ezech. I)]: » which, although it may be fittingly referred to the holy evangelists, or the apostles, or any of the just, is nevertheless also not inconveniently taken of the holy angels, who announce the incarnation of the Word, and the passion, and the resurrection, and the ascension, and in all these things attend upon the incarnate Word in the service of ministry.

There follows: « And after the herd-nature of oxen, » — it is to be understood, and lest we suppose certain of them formed after the herd-nature of oxen, that is, after the likeness and form of oxen, which is that of the herd, not angelic, as in Ezekiel the form of an ox, or of a calf, is expressed in the figuration of the living creatures. Or again, lest we suppose the same spirits formed after the bestial imagination of lions, that is, after the imagination or [0965A] image of lions, which is bestial, and unworthy of a spiritual and rational nature, as in Ezekiel the face of a man and the face of a lion is mentioned in the figuration of the living creatures. And after the appearance of eagles with a curved beak, again it is understood, let us not suppose them formed. But in that he says « with a curved beak, » he plainly stirs a fitting derision, as in the rest, against those who think these things assigned to a spiritual nature according to property. It is known, moreover, that in Ezekiel's distinction the face of an eagle also is mentioned.

There follows: « Or shaped after the threefold beating of the wings of flying things, » — it is understood, let us not suppose. For as it is written in Isaiah of the throne of the one sitting and of the temple: « The seraphim stood above it: [0965B] six wings to the one, and six wings to the other. With two they veiled the head, with two they veiled the feet; and with two they flew, the one to the other [n: (Isa. VI)]. » And elsewhere: « For with two they covered their bodies [n: (Ezech. I)]. » Where indeed, in the six wings, by twos and twos in their distinctions, a threefold or tripartite beating is found. For six, joined two and two together, make three pairs of wings.

There follows: « And let us imagine certain fiery wheels above the heaven. » The negative particle is supplied from above, namely, let us not imagine certain fiery wheels above the heaven, and let us not imagine also material thrones as though necessary to the Divinity for its reclining, since the divine nature, which alone bears all things, has no need of a support. But of the fiery wheels and thrones [0965C] we have testimony in the book of Daniel [n: (Dan. VII)], and in Isaiah [n: (Isa. VI)], and Micah, concerning the one sitting upon a lofty and high throne.

There follows: « And certain horses of many colors » (as in Zechariah there are read white horses, and black, and black, and dappled [n: (Zach. VI)]; and in the Apocalypse likewise [n: (Apoc. VI)]) — it is understood, let us not imagine. « And arms-bearing supreme commanders, » likewise let us not imagine, that is, princes and leaders of the army bearing arms, or clad in arms, as we read in Zechariah, and in Micah. Likewise the Lord sitting upon a lofty throne, and the host of heaven at his right hand and his left; and in the book of Kings the chariots, and horses, and horsemen of fire shown upon the mountains to Elisha, and coming to the aid of his servant [n: (IV Reg. VI)]. They are called supreme commanders [0965D] as it were leaders, or conductors of the princes of the army. It is a compound name, from that which is ἀρχός *archos*, that is, prince, and στρατηγος *strategos*, which is leader and commander of an army; and so supreme commanders are understood as the leaders of the princes of the army, who rule over the princes themselves, or, being superior, afford them their leadership. All these things, therefore, taken by likeness from those things which are according to us, and attributed to those heavenly spirits for the signifying of those things which are invisible to themselves, are so to be received by us that we do not suppose these things to be in them in this way, but that through these things we may study to weigh other things, which are in them and are invisible to us, visibly set forth. For no corruptible appearance attains to their purity; and therefore in a certain manner [0966A] it is an impure supposition which, without discretion, attributes things so lowly and unworthy to that excellent nature according to property. Whether therefore these things, or whatever other things out of the words, that is, the Scriptures, have been handed down to us sacredly and in a form-giving way — that is, by a sacred fashioning or figuring, or the figuring of a sacred thing — in the variety of manifesting symbols, that is, of sacred figures and descriptions, by which secret and hidden things are made manifest: let us so receive them all that, according to their likeness and signification, we believe all those things of which they are made to be true; and yet let us think none of these things to be in them according to property.

There follows: « For very artfully theology has used poetic sacred formations in unfigured intellects [0966B] — revealing, as has been said, our mind, and providing for it its own and connatural leading-back, and reforming unto it the anagogical holy Scriptures. » To this he responds to what he had said above, that it is to be considered with what sort of formations sacred Scripture would figure the heavenly orders. As if he said: For this reason the very formations of the heavenly orders in the sacred word are diligently to be inspected; because they were made for this end, that they might reveal, that is, illuminate, our mind unto the knowledge of invisible things through these visible and known things. For very artfully, or prudently, or with consideration, theology, that is, the divine Scripture, has used poetic sacred formations, that is, descriptions of forms or figures set forth for the representing of sacred or holy things — [0966C] poetic formations, that is, ones devised, or invented by reason and set forth through the likeness of mystical tradition — for the signifying of unfigured intellects, that is, of spirits subsisting without figure and corporeal form in their own simplicity. « Revealing, as has been said, » namely in the foregoing chapter, « our mind » from the deep veil of ignorance, so that with bared and open face it may contemplate heavenly things: « and providing for it, namely for the mind, its own and connatural leading-back, » so that, instructed, it may be led back to the knowing of invisible things through those things which are connatural and its own; and reforming, namely, or adapting, or tempering unto the mind itself the anagogical holy Scriptures, so that first, being conformed to us, they might descend to us, and afterward, by leading back to higher things, [0966D] might lift up the illumined. This, then, theology did very artfully, that, speaking to us, it might take up our things, and, through our things, might lift up those admonished and instructed unto its own. But if perchance it seem to anyone that this is to be approved insofar as theology itself, for the signifying of visible things, took beautiful and seemly formations from those things which are ours; but that it is reprehensible and unfitting that it also appended likenesses unseemly and unworthy of so great a purity and majesty: to him it is to be answered that theology took up not only like, but also unlike, forms for the demonstration of divine things for this reason — that through that which is unlike and, as it were, alien, said manifestly of them by figure, even that which [0967A] could seem proper might be recognized as figured and alien to property:



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

This is what follows: « But if to anyone it seems that the sacred compositions ought indeed to be received, as of things simple in themselves, and unknown to us, and uncontemplable in their subsistence. » This is, that it is worthy that the sacred compositions be received, that is, the sacred figurations or formations which are made concerning the divine and invisible things themselves, inasmuch as they are of those things which are simple and uncompounded, without corporeal figuration and form, and thereby, to us who know only how to contemplate and think corporeal things, subsist unknown and uncontemplable, and cannot be shown, nor perceived by the human heart, save through visible signs and visible demonstrations. If, then, it seems to anyone worthy [0967B] and necessary, on account of causes of this sort, that the sacred compositions be received — provided only that they be formed beautifully and seemly, and befitting so great a purity — but now judges them to be unseemly « and unfitting, » namely whatever, « of the holy intellects, » that is, the descriptions of spirits, that are made « in the sacred words »; and he judges it also to be unfitting, all this figment of angelic names, as it seems to him theatrical — namely, that angels, oxen, and lions, and eagles, and horses, and wheels, and chariots, and thrones, and the rest of this sort should be introduced and named: which according to his supposition seems theatrical, and worthy of theatrical recitation and derision. [0967C] But to us, thinking piously and believing rightly, to speak thus is hard, or, every such speaking is hard, that is, all such hard and unfitting sayings and descriptions, which is as it were a certain theater of angelic names; or, if he supposes that to speak thus, that is, that it must so be spoken, that which is feigned in Scripture, is as it were hard, that is, an inconsonant and unfitting theater of angelic names to be: and if he also says that the theologians, coming or descending to a corporeal fashioning, that is, a material figuration, of things universally, that is, altogether incorporeal — namely the heavenly and invisible — ought to have formed them, namely the invisible things, and to have made them manifest, as far as was possible for them, by proper and cognate figurations, that is, by unfitting and like figurations [0967D] taken from among us, that is, from among these visible things, out of the most precious ones — or taken out of the most precious things among us, that is, out of those things which are most precious among us; and out of things in some way immaterial, that is, out of those things which in any, that is, in some, way seem to be immaterial and incorporeal, such namely as light, and fire, and splendor, and heat, and the rest of this sort: which, though they be material and corporeal, nevertheless approach closely to a spiritual nature by their subtlety and purity. If then he say that out of these most precious things, and in some way immaterial and supereminent essences, the theologians ought to have taken the figures of those things which are altogether incorporeal, so that through those things which are almost immaterial and incorporeal, truly incorporeal things might be signified. And if he say further that there ought not to be set round about [0968A] earthly and lowest, that is, last or least, or abject multiformities, that is, figures composed of many and various forms, upon the deiform simplicities, that is, the spiritual natures: which, because they are deiform in glory, earthly and lowest things, and because they are simple in essence, multiformities ought by no means to have been attributed to them. And if he say further: This indeed — that is, if out of precious and supereminent essences the figures of the invisible things were taken, and earthly and lowest formednesses were not set round about, or fitted to, the deiform simplicities — this indeed both would be our own more sublimely, that is, would advance us more sublimely to the knowledge of spiritual things. Or, this indeed both would be ours, because it would be corporeal and material; and would be more sublime, [0968B] that is, nearer to divine and spiritual things, so that on either part it would become reasonable: so that through that which was ours, that is, familiar and known to us, it could instruct; and through that which was more sublime and more worthy, it would befit the excellence and dignity of spiritual things. And it would also not lead the supermundane, that is, the heavenly and spiritual manifestations into unfitting unlikenesses, as these descriptions do; because out of earthly and lowest essences they bring likenesses unfitting and unlike to spiritual things. This also — that is, if only out of beautiful and seemly forms the figures of spiritual things were formed — would do no injury to the divine powers by attributing to them unfitting forms: [0968C] which to do is unjust. And equally, that is, likewise, it would not seduce our mind, as this base and unseemly formation seduces it, into thinking things alien and unworthy of those powers, while it inserts, or intrudes, itself into these impure compositions: which have been made unseemly concerning them. And perhaps yet another evil may come from these base compositions, namely, that the supercelestial things will be supposed to be filled, or to have been filled, with certain leonine and equine multitudes, that is, of horses and lions, and with a lowing utterance of praises, and with a flying headlong of angels; because the figures of lions, and horses, and oxen, and birds, whose office is to roar, and to neigh, and to fly, and which cannot praise God save by lowing, or roaring, are ascribed to the angels. And the heavenly things themselves will be supposed [0968D] to be filled not only with these, but also with other animals and baser materials, such as worms, and coals, and other things of this sort, which are found said by figure of spiritual things — as though the heavenly things themselves were laid open, that is, disclosed and opened, unto that which is inconsequent, that is, unfitting and ignoble, and it be possible above that things of this sort be admitted into them, or even passible, that is, corruptible. And all these things indeed will be supposed, while they are described, likenesses of the manifesting words: which the words set forth for manifestation clearly, that is, manifestly, as deformed. If then to anyone this whole thing seem so (so that up to this point the sentence hangs, and at last it is thus inferred) — to him indeed it so seems, namely that only beautiful and excellent appearances are to be set to the signification of spiritual [0969A] and divine things. But nevertheless, if anyone diligently inquire into the truth, he recognizes that the wisdom of the divine words has fittingly set forth both.

This is what follows: « But the inquiry of truth, as I suppose, shows that the most sacred wisdom of the words, in the formation of the minds, or spirits, of the heavenly ones, has provided for both exceedingly, so that neither (as perhaps someone would say) should it do injury to the divine powers, » that is, so that neither through those lowly figurations should it do injury to the divine powers, that is, to the spirits, who are divine and sacred and incorporeal: which nevertheless perhaps someone would call injury, and would think ought to be called so, when yet it is no injury, « neither should it fix us » through the same figurations « passibly [0969B] in the vile lownesses of images, » that is, in the vileness of lowly, or in the lowness of vile, images. For the wisdom of the divine word provided and took care of both: that through these lowly figurations it should neither do injury to the divine things, nor inform our mind to vile thoughts or false suppositions. And first, indeed, why corporeal and visible figures and forms have been set to the manifesting of divine and invisible things, a necessary and fitting cause is demonstrated — which perhaps someone would say is not one, but twofold, that is, not only because those things could not otherwise be made manifest to our mind save through these, but also because in the figures and enigmas of mystical descriptions the divine secrets were to be covered and concealed from impure minds and from the malevolent. [0969C] Thus, he says, someone will state the cause of the mystical descriptions: which, though he himself could have said it indubitably and truly, he modestly attributes to another, lest perchance he should seem to commend his own authority.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

This, then, is what he says: « For because indeed the forms of the formless, and the figures of things lacking figures, have been beautifully provided: one would not say the cause thereof to be one, our analogy. For because indeed. » Contrary to the usage of Latinity, according to the idiom of the Greek tongue, he has heaped up conjunctions, or *quia* is to be read for *quod*, so that this is the sense: That indeed one, that is, someone, would say the cause not to be one — namely this our analogy, that is, that not only our [0969D] analogy is the cause of this thing, namely that the forms of the formless and the figures of things lacking figures have been beautifully, that is, fittingly, provided by the sacred word — that is, that in the sacred word forms and figures have been attributed to those heavenly spirits, which in their own nature have neither corporeal forms nor figures. Of this thing, I say, one would say the cause to be not only the analogy, that is, our condition, otherwise unable to attain to the knowledge of invisible things, nor able immediately, that is, without some intermediary, to extend itself through the intellect into the invisible contemplations, that is, into the contemplations of invisible things. Our analogy, I say, which also desires its own and connatural leadings-back, this is, to be led back to the knowledge and contemplation of invisible things [0970A] through those things which are its own and connatural, that is, visible and corporeal — which corporeal things, namely, put forth, that is, set forth, the formations of the formless, that is, of spiritual speculations, and the supernatural, that is, of speculations exceeding our nature, and altogether incomprehensible to us, unless through these passible formations they were demonstrated and insinuated to us. He calls analogy the human condition; because analogy is a property, according to reason and the agreement of several like things in one, just as grammarians too are wont to assign analogies of words according to the likeness of several falling under one property. The analogy, then, of human nature, that is, the condition or property, or [0970B] agreement, is this: to be able and to know those things which pertain to man, and which man received to be and to be able. But above our analogy, that is, above our agreement and equality, is it to scrutinize heavenly things, unless we be instructed through those things which are with us visible and known to us. This analogy, then, that is, the human condition, was the cause why the sacred word, for the instructing of human minds, set forth visible signs of invisible things. And not this cause only, but also « because this also is most seemly for the mystical words, » namely to hide, « and to set as trackless to the many the sacred and hidden, » that is, the concealed truth « of the supermundane intellects, » that is, of the invisible spirits, « through incomprehensible divine enigmas. » [0970C] For enigmas, and parables, and figures are set to the mystical word of the Scriptures for this reason: that the truth of spiritual things be not laid open to carnal and impure spirits, and that at once it may exercise the studious and devout by its very profundity. Which cause, however, the author here judges to mention out of superabundance. Therefore, then, the divine sacraments are covered lest they lie open to all, because not all are worthy of the recognition of truth. « For not everyone is holy, neither is knowledge of all, as the words say. » For because not every man is holy, therefore the truth is not to be made manifest to all; since, if it were made manifest to all, many would gnaw at it by contradicting through malice, or would defile it by living impurely. Whence it is said: « Give not the holy thing [0970D] to dogs [n: (Matth. VII)], » namely to those who gnaw the truth set forth with the tooth of malice; « neither cast pearls before swine [n: (ibid.)], » namely before those who, as far as in them lies, pollute what is offered by living wickedly. For these are the unholy, that is, unworthy of holy things, whose knowledge it is not, « as the words say, » of the sacred Scripture. For the Apostle says that « faith is not of all [n: (II Thess. III)], » since that knowledge of truth, by which God is piously believed by the holy and just, is either not received by any of the perverse, or not worthily held.

« But if anyone should suppose the causes of the deformed descriptions of images to be dishonorable, saying that such base formations are thus referred to the deiform and most holy dispositions, it suffices to say to him: How [0971A] is the mode of holy manifestation twofold? » Now at length he responds to the question objected above, in which it was contained that vile and abject formations are unfittingly set to sacred and divine things in the sacred word, saying: « If anyone should suppose the causes of the description of images to be deformed, » that is, if anyone should suppose the causes of describing images to be deformed, that is, unfitting, saying it to be dishonorable that such base formations be referred, that is, fitted, to the deiform and most holy dispositions, that is, orders. If anyone, I say, should so suppose, and so say: « It suffices to say to him how the mode of holy manifestation is twofold: » that is, to a supposition and objection of this sort it is sufficiently answered in this, that the holy manifestation which is made [0971B] through the Scriptures is shown to have a twofold mode. « One indeed as it were consequent, » that is, fitting and seemly, in which the signs follow the signified truth by a like property: on account of like images of sacred figures agreeing, that is, proceeding to make the manifestation, or coming forth, that is, apt and concordant with that which they signify. « But the other mode is conformed unto the altogether inconsequent, » that is, discrepant, and unfitting, and unseemly, for the reason that the very figures and the very signs manifesting the truth seem not to say it. This is what he says: « On account of unlike fashionings of forms. »

There follows: « And so the mystical traditions of the manifesting words praise the venerable [0971C] beatitude of the superessential divinity — sometimes indeed as reason, and intellect, and essence, declaring the divine rationality and its wisdom, and the truly existing subsistence, and the true cause of those things which are subsistences. » As if he said: Since the mode of manifestation in the sacred word is twofold — one, namely, formed through like signs, the other through unlike signs — and so the sacred words themselves also sometimes represent the majesty of the divine nature through like, sometimes through unlike formations. Through like ones, sometimes indeed taken from corporeal things, sometimes from incorporeal. From incorporeal things, as when they name it reason and intellect; from corporeal, as when they name it light and [0971D] splendor, and the rest that are done in this manner. But sometimes through unlike formations, likewise taken from incorporeal, or from corporeal things, they manifest the divine nature. From incorporeal indeed, as when they attribute to it anger, zeal, repentance; but from corporeal, when they set to it, in signification, the forms or figures of beasts, or of any other corporeal things whatsoever. This, then, is what he says: « And so the mystical traditions of the manifesting words praise the venerable beatitude of the superessential divinity; sometimes indeed as reason, and intellect, and essence, declaring its divine subsistence truly existing, and the true cause of the subsistence of all those things which are; » since it both truly subsists in itself, [0972A] and makes all subsistences to subsist, in that they call it essence — although it itself nevertheless, above all reason, and intellect, and essence, subsists in its majesty, neither intelligible nor comprehensible, above all subsistences.

There follows: « And they form it as light, and call it life. » Namely, the venerable beatitude the mystical traditions form, although it itself is light above all light and subsists as life above all life.
