By so many, then, and so great modes do the sacred words form the divine majesty in figuration. « These so great and wondrous formations remaining chaster than the rest, which seem incongruous, and unworthy, and these so great and wondrous formations being approved in some way to excel the other material formations. » As if he said: [0972B] Although unto that excellence every formation, or representation, is found inferior, yet in comparison with the other formations such beautiful and such seemly formations are proved to excel. When, namely, the nature of the divinity is named reason, and intellect, and essence, and light, and life — although even in these formations too the likeness fails in manifestation for the expressing of the ineffable truth. Whence he says: « These divine things also thus failing of likeness unto the truth. » Namely, even these more excellent likenesses thus failing, that is, even in so great excellence failing of the divine likeness — failing, namely, unto the truth, it is understood, to be expressed. For everything that here is in the sign is less than that which there is in the truth. « For it is, [0972C] « namely the divine nature, » above all essence and life, no light indeed characterizing, that is, figuring, or expressing, it: and it is excellent, all reason and intellect being incomparably left behind, or beneath the likeness of itself. Whence it appears that if its likeness is so far superior to reason and intellect, that neither reason nor intellect can advance to equal its likeness.

There follows: « But sometimes it is praised supermundanely by the words themselves through unlike manifestations, they calling it invisible, and infinite, and incomprehensible, and by those things from which is signified not what it is, but what it is not. » As if he said: Not only is it manifested by like and excellent manifestations, [0972D] and by those approaching to its image: but sometimes also by unlike manifestations, and ones foreign to its nature, it is praised by the words « supermundanely, » that is, mystically and spiritually, and above the appearance of this world. For when it is praised through beautiful forms, it is praised according to the appearance of this world, that is, it is said according to something which is the very thing through which it is praised. But when it is praised through unlike formations, and ones alien from itself, it is praised supermundanely; since it is neither said to be the same, nor according to that, but above that, wholly other, through which it is praised. Therefore, then, it is praised supermundanely by the words themselves through unlike formations. The words, I say, or the formations themselves, calling it, namely the divine nature, invisible, and [0973A] infinite, and incomprehended: first, infinite in itself; then, invisible to us; after, incomprehended by us; and many other things also the words themselves calling the divine nature, which are such that from them is signified not what it is, but what it is not. For when God is said to be invisible, and infinite, and incomprehended: it is not said what he is, but what he is not — for he is not visible, because he cannot be seen, nor is he bounded, because he is not enclosed by place, nor terminated by time; nor is he comprehensible, because, even if it is believed what he is, how great he is is not grasped. Whoever, then, says him invisible, does not say to be that which is, but not to be that which is not. Likewise also whoever says him infinite, and incomprehended, does not say to be that which is, because he affirms nothing to be. But he says not to be that which is not, [0973B] because he denies something to be; since both what he does not affirm is that which cannot be said, and what he denies is that which can be understood.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « For this, as I suppose, is more powerful in it. » This, namely, from which is signified not what it is, but what it is not, is more powerful, that is, more efficacious, and more proper, and more expressive in it; since he who says what it is not, says that which in some manner can be understood; but he who says what it is, says that which in no manner can be comprehended. But it is more powerful and more excellent, as regards the expression of truth, to say what God is not, than what he is. « Since indeed, as the hidden and priestly tradition has introduced, we truly say this to be it — not according to any of those things which are; but we are ignorant [0973C] of its superessential, and invisible, and ineffable infinitude. » As if he said: As the authority of sacred Scripture testifies, introduced from the hidden unto manifestation, and handed down for correction and information. As, then, that tradition, that is, that authority which is handed down, hidden as regards the mysteries of hidden sacraments, and priestly as regards the dignity and holiness of the divine writings themselves, and its own sanctification, because it was both handed down by divine priests and prophets, and divinely sanctified for the sake of those to be sanctified through it. As, then, that hidden and priestly tradition introduced, we learned that this, that is, such a thing according to which [0973D] we truly say it, that is, the divine nature, to be, is not of all those things which are; because no species of created things so approaches to its likeness as to show expressly and according to property that which truly is in it. Or thus we learned that it is not truly what we say it to be according to something, that is, according to any of those things which are: for that which truly is according to any of those things which are, cannot be said to be the whole; and therefore when we say it to be something according to those things which are, we do not yet manifest by expression that which it truly is. But we are ignorant of its superessential, and invisible, and ineffable infinitude. For that which is infinite cannot be estimated by human knowledge: which, because it is ineffable, is not said; and because it is invisible, is not known; and [0974A] because it is superessential, is not comprehended. Of it, then, the human mind can grasp something, itself it cannot; and the human tongue can say something of it, itself it cannot; nor for that reason, however, is what is said of it to be supposed false; since it is only of it, and it itself is not this which is said; nor is what is thought of it vain, since it is only of it, and it itself is not this which is thought; since what is said is true, and what is thought is truth: which so leads unto it, although it subsists more sublimely and loftily in itself.

There follows: « If, then, the negations in divine things are true, but the affirmations incompact: the manifestation through unlike formations is more apt to the obscurity of hidden things. » As if he said: Since more expressly and more [0974B] properly we say God to be nothing that is, when we say both to be something, and not to be, truly, it is manifest that in divine things, that is, in those things which are said of God and attributed to God, the negations are true, that is, proper; but the affirmations incompact, that is, improper and not cohering, since they try to join and fit together unlike things, according to that mode of speaking by which human speech cannot otherwise be formed concerning God. But if the negations in divine things are true, that is, proper, and the affirmations incompact, that is, improper, it is manifest that the manifestation made through unlike formations is more apt to the obscurity of the hidden things to be revealed than that made through like ones; since the former strives to demonstrate, as it were by negation, by removing, what God is not, [0974C] but the latter tries to show, as it were by affirmation, by positing, what he is. « And now, therefore, the sacred descriptions of the words do not, as base, fill up the heavenly adornments, manifesting them by unlike fashionings of forms; and through these showing the excellences of the supermundane things above all material things together. And now therefore, » — since unlike figurations stand more properly in divine things, according to that mode by which, out of all things which are, nothing is properly named of God; according to this mode, therefore, they do not fill up, that is, assert to be filled up, the heavenly adornments, that is, the heavenly orders or dispositions, the base, that is, deformed, formations themselves which are set forth in the sacred word; manifesting [0974D] them, namely the adornments, by unlike fashionings, that is, by compositions of forms unlike and alien from their excellence; and through these fashionings showing the excellences of the supermundane, that is, of the heavenly and invisible things, above all material things together; this is, their excellences in relation to all material things; that is, showing that the supermundane and spiritual things themselves excel all material things. For in this, that they attribute unlike figures to them, they show that even those things too, which seem to be said of them according to likeness, do not rise up to their property.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « But that unlike likenesses lead our mind back more, I do not suppose that any of those who are well-wise contradicts. » As if he said: Not only for this reason are the unlike figurations approvable, [0975A] that they show the excellences of the supermundane things; but also for this reason, that they lead our mind back from material and corporeal things more than like figurations do, nor suffer it to rest in itself. Hear a great mystery. That which God is, is above all; and when it is asked what he is, this cannot be said, because it cannot be thought. For that which can be thought ascends into the heart of man, and is grasped by the heart of man, either in those things which are seen according to appearance, or according to those things which are through imagination, or in those things which are perceived within by experience and truth; and the heart of man grasps only what it knows, or according to those things which it knows. But it knows those things which it conceives without by sense, and those things which it feels within by experience; and everything that it grasps, it either grasps in these, or conjectures according to [0975B] these. But that which is neither in these, nor according to these, the human heart cannot grasp. But that which God is, is neither any of these, because he is not a creature; nor is it according to these, because he is the Creator. That which God is, then, can neither be found in these, nor understood according to these what sort it is. For if it were understood according to these, it would be led down into the same likeness unto these, and there would be this in these which is in him. But whatever things are in creatures are more near and akin to one another, because they are made; than the work is to the artificer, and the thing fashioned to the fashioner. For all time compared to eternity, and all space composed to immensity, is found to have less, than any length of time set beside a moment, or an extension of quantity [0975C] however much increasing, referred to the proportion of an atom. So that which God is, compared to a creature, is found to excel more amply, than that which is highest among created things, compared to those things which are lowest, or made most extreme. God, then, cannot be thought according to these things, what he is; since he is other, and is otherwise, and far, and remotely, and unlikely; and what he is cannot be said. For if any of these things is said, he is other. If according to any of these things it is said, he is otherwise. What then is to be said, which God is? If he is called heaven, he is other. If he is called earth, he is other; and whatever is in heaven, and is on earth, is not this which God is. Therefore this which God is, is other. And what is this? Only this can be said, that he is other, [0975D] and what he is cannot be said. We have, then, what we may say, God is not this; but we have not what we may say, this is God; because everything that we have, this is not God, and we have not in all these things, nor do we find, what God is. For all this is other than God; because God is not everything that is made by God, and the eye does not see, nor does the mind grasp, save this, or according to this, which is not God, but from God. For man has the sense of man, and feels according to the sense of man, either that which is without according to the flesh, or that which is within according to the mind, and man has no more. The eye of flesh those things which are unto the flesh, the eye of the mind those things which are unto the mind. What more? « No man knows the things which are of a man, save the spirit of a man, which is in the man. » [0976A] [n: (I Cor. II)]. » So the things which are of God no one knows, save the Spirit of God; and he who has the Spirit of God knows through the Spirit of God the things which are of God. But the eye is threefold: the eye of flesh, the eye of reason, the eye of contemplation. The eye of flesh is open, the eye of reason bleary, the eye of contemplation closed and blind. By the eye of flesh is seen the world, and the things which are in the world. By the eye of reason the mind, and the things which are in the mind. By the eye of contemplation God, and the things which are in God. By the eye of flesh man sees those things which are outside himself; by the eye of reason those things which are in himself; by the eye of contemplation those things which are within himself and above himself. Therefore God, that which he is, is unthinkable, but to men, and to human reason: which perceives nothing save what it knows, or according to that which it knows, which is in itself or outside itself. But those who [0976B] have the Spirit of God in themselves, have God also: these see God, because they have an illumined eye by which God can be seen, and they feel him not in another, or according to another which he himself is not, but himself and in himself that which he is, which is present. Nor yet can it be said, because it is ineffable, because it is unthinkable; and it is felt, and not expressed. Therefore, everything that is said of God, that he is, is said according to that which can be said and thought, since it cannot be said otherwise; and everything that can be said and thought is less and is below than that which God is. This very thing which is said, is said as less, and this very thing is not said which God is, because he who says something says according to something, and thinks that which he says, and according to that which he says. You name [0976C] God, and you form two syllables; and you think you have said the whole of that which he is. What have you thought? For that which you have thought, this you have said. I have thought, you say, that which is above all things: what is this? If you can think what it is, this you can say. But if you cannot think it, you cannot say it; because that which cannot be thought, cannot be said. You have said God: and what is God? What do you think, or of what sort do you think, when you say God? For that which sounds is this, either one beholding or running, or fear, or whatever else you shall have supposed of him as you are able. Therefore when you say God, you say one beholding and contemplating, and considering all things. And what is this? How does God behold, and how does he see? What is his seeing, save his being? And of what sort is this? But if you understand one running, because he penetrates [0976D] all things, and apprehends, and contains everything that is, for him to run is this: to stand. And who may grasp this? But if you interpret it fear; and you assert that this itself is to be thought under this name when it is said God: who could explain how fear is God? But if for this reason you suppose fear to be said, because he is feared, how is that feared which is not seen? how can that be seen which cannot be thought? and how can that be feared which cannot be known? See therefore what you say, when you say God; or what you think, when you say God. The Creator, you say, of all things I think, when I say God, who made all things, and himself was not made. Therefore when you say God, you think that which made all things. You think that which he made, and you do not think that which he himself is who made. Not yet have you attained that which you had promised, that you should think, and understand [0977A] what God is. Less is this whole which you say; and this whole is not himself of whom you say it; and yet of him you say this, not that you may reach unto him, but that you may draw near to him. For it is a great thing for man now to go unto him, even if it be not granted to arrive. But it will be granted afterward, when that which is perfect shall have come; and man shall have begun to see as he is seen, not the image through a glass, but the truth face to face. But now meanwhile the whole is image, and the image itself is far from the truth; and yet it does what it can as an image; and it turns the mind, but does not lead it through. For this alone it can do in us, and we in it this alone, because neither can it show more, nor we grasp another, and yet there is a certain image more sublime, and more approaching to the truth, [0977B] so that it can be more called truth by us, because there is nothing else above it, by which truth could be more expressly demonstrated. For it is said that God is fire; and the figure is manifest; since God, according to property, is not fire; since fire is a body, God is not a body. It is said also that God is light; and here likewise appears an image of the truth, other than the truth, since God is not light according to property, which is named according to figure. For all these things are visible, and are far from God by the property of nature, even if they are fitted to him according to likeness alone, which itself too is scanty in relation to the excellence of his majesty. But there is another incorporeal nature more near to God, between which and God there is no other intermediary nature; and this approaches more to likeness, [0977C] although it too is far from the truth. According to this, then, the highest nature to us, a sublime likeness to God is formed for us, when God is called spirit, and wisdom, and reason, and love; because the soul is spirit, and the angel is spirit, and in the spirit itself is reason, wisdom, and love. And we know what spirit is, as far as we know the soul, and know the angel; and through the soul we know the angel, as far as we know our own selves; although even this is little, and can scarcely be called knowledge. When therefore we hear that God is spirit, we think the soul, and the angel, and we suppose a likeness, since God is some such thing as the soul is, and the angel, because the soul and the angel [0977D] are spirit. And we know not how far this is from the truth of the incomprehensible excellence. For whoever should call a body spirit, would say a falsehood, since a body is not spirit nor spirit a body. Whoever, then, should say this, would rightly be reprehended; and would be judged contrary to the truth; and yet whoever says God to be spirit is supposed to have said a truth. No one accuses him of falsity, although yet a body and a spirit are more near in nature and condition, than a spirit and God. For here each is a creature, and each is comprehensible, and each mutable, and finite. But there the one indeed is eternal, the other temporal; the one immense, the other comprehensible; the one always remaining the same, the other mutable; the one falling under knowledge, the other [0978A] unthinkable. And yet, because another cannot be said, this is said; lest nothing be said, where another is to be said, and what is cannot be said; or if it can be said, it cannot be understood. This, then, is said, and the truth tolerates this of itself, and commends this to us for truth — to us who cannot yet grasp the truth itself, until the figure pass away, and the truth be manifested, above all this, and outside all this, nakedly and openly as it itself is. Now therefore up to this point the figures remain, and of these some are far, and appear to be likeness only; but some indeed are proper, and are received as it were for truth, though they be only signs of the truth and not the truth, in which indeed, if nothing higher shall have been unto it, the truth concedes these to us, and does not reckon the impossibility. But if [0978B] they shall have been near and closely alike, yet shall not have been nearest, and something more sublime appear for the manifesting of the truth; the truth does not suffer itself to be led down to them according to property, since in the other it demonstrates itself more perfectly, in which it proves itself here to be only through likeness. But in that supreme thing, than which nothing is higher, no other appears unto it by which the figure may be proved; and therefore it behooves to receive it so as it is, since no other is given, until that which is perfect shall come. Every figure, then, the more evidently demonstrates the truth, the more openly it proves itself, through an unlike likeness, to be a figure, and not the truth; and in this the unlike likenesses lead our mind back more unto the truth, in that they do not permit it to remain in likeness alone. [0978C]



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

Wherefore, he says, « I do not think that any of the well-wise would contradict » this — that dissimilar likenesses lead our mind back to the truth. For indeed « it follows, that men are led astray by the more precious sacred formations, » that is, it can follow, or come about, or easily happen, that through those formations of sacred things which are represented as more precious in the sacred discourse, the thoughts of men are led astray, « thinking certain celestial essences to be gold-formed, » just as in certain places of the Scriptures they are represented by likeness; and thinking also that there are in heaven certain flashing men clothed in comely garments, just as [0978D] angels are read to have appeared in splendid garments and with flashing countenances, « sprinkling forth, harmlessly, what is white and fiery, » that is, emitting, or pouring out, brightness and light; the white indeed as to the garments, and the fiery as to the flaming and burning countenances; harmlessly, that is, without injury — this itself holding firm in the very divine miracle, because in the divine and celestial natures, which are here shown by appearance, they are otherwise there according to truth: in which the appearance of visible nature is discerned, but the effect is not found.

There follows: « And by whatever other similar imagined forms, » that is, expressed according to imaginable things, « Theology has figured the celestial intellects, » that is, has represented the spiritual natures — so that from the things above [0979A] (understand) here it follows that men are led astray, thinking the celestial essences to exist in their own nature such as these. « Now that they might not suffer this — those who understand nothing higher than visible goods, » that is, that those might not think this who do not suppose there to be other goods higher, or better, than these visible goods; therefore « the restorative wisdom of the holy theologians wondrously descended to unseemly likenesses, » that is, therefore the holy theologians, who by their wisdom — which restores and reforms us unto the knowledge of truth — handed down to us the divine discourses, by a wondrous consideration descended to the taking up of unseemly likenesses, that they might fit them to the divine and celestial natures; and through these they might show that the other things too, which seem to be said of them magnificently and becomingly, are to be referred to likeness, and not to [0979B] property. This, then, the wisdom of the holy theologians did, « not granting that our material part, remaining in base images, should rest; but purging, bearing upward the powers of the soul, and suggesting by the deformity of the compositions that these are approved by neither the just nor the true; and that the super-celestial and divine spectacles are not, according to truth, so very like to things material and thus base. » As if he were saying: The wisdom of the theologians, descending to unseemly likenesses, in this very thing wondrously provided for our restoration, not granting that our material part — that is, our carnal sense, which cleaves to material things — should rest; the material part, I say, which remains in base images, that is, which so far as in it lies wills to remain, so that it may think only these things, [0979C] and accept only these as though true, if it were permitted to rest in them, and were not compelled by the very baseness of the images to go out to seek other things beautiful and true; or, not granting that our material part should rest, remaining in base images — that is, not granting that it either rest or remain, but by the very baseness of the images expelling it, and advancing it to higher things; but purging, bearing upward, namely, the power of the soul, that is, the intellectual force of the soul, which bears it upward and strains toward higher things; purging it from the admixture of images, that it may grow accustomed to contemplate spiritual and invisible things purely and simply; and suggesting, that is, admonishing and persuading the soul from the very deformity of the compositions, [0979D] that the super-celestial and divine spectacles are not, according to truth, very like to material things — especially to things so base — as neither the just nor the true approves, namely that those are like to these; or, suggesting by the deformity of the compositions that it is approved by neither the just nor the true — understand, in that manner, in the property of spiritual and invisible natures, as it is shown in the appearance and image of visible things; and suggesting also, that the super-celestial and divine spectacles are not very — that is, not much — like, according to their own truth, to material things thus base.

There follows: « But so too this must be understood: that none of the things which are is universally deprived of the participation of the good. For indeed, as the truth of the discourses [0980A] says, all things are very good. » Above he demonstrated that dissimilar representations are for this reason fittingly brought forward for the manifestation of divine things: that from those which evidently appear dissimilar, those too which seemed similar may be recognized to be outside the property of the thing. But now he demonstrates that for this reason also dissimilarities are not unfittingly taken up; because those too which seem to be dissimilar from the truth of divine and spiritual things have something in common with them, in which they can be called similar, since there is nothing in the universe which does not draw participation from the highest good; and through this — in that it participates with it — it fittingly represents its image and likeness. And so it behooves us not only to consider the former cause, but [0980B] also to understand this: that none of the things which universally are is deprived of the participation of the good. For « as the truth of the discourses says, » that is, the true discourses say, « all things are very good. » For thus it is written in Genesis: « God saw all the things that he had made, and they were very good [n: (Gen. I)]. » If therefore all things were good, in all things there was the good, and all things participated in the good, and from the good all things had something similar to the good; from which the good itself may be understood and known in them. Whence there follows: « It is possible, then, » that is, it happens, « from all » namely things « to understand good speculations; and to form, » or fit, « to invisible and intellectual things the likenesses called dissimilar; » namely those which above we called dissimilar: « to form, » I say, « from these very [0980C] material things, » that is, corporeal and visible ones. Yet in such a way that those things which, according to the likeness of visible things, are attributed to invisible things, be recognized to subsist otherwise in the visible things themselves, and otherwise in the invisible. For this indeed he says: « the intellectual things having them in another manner, » namely those things which are distributed otherwise to sensible things.

There follows: « For indeed anger is present in irrational beings from a passible motion; and their raging motion is full of all irrationality. » Now, certain examples having been set forth, he proves that those things which are referred from the nature of visible things to invisible things subsist otherwise here and otherwise there; as anger, and concupiscence, and the rest, which are referred from visible things to invisible things [0980D] by likeness; as if he were saying: Those things which are fitted from visible things to invisible things must be believed to be otherwise in the visible things themselves, and otherwise in the invisible; as can be plainly understood in the things which follow. For « anger » « in irrational beings, » that is, in those proceeding and acting irrationally — whether they have no reason, or, having reason, do not proceed according to reason. In these, indeed, anger is present from a passible motion, that is, one impetuous and fervent, and born according to a dominating passion; and their raging motion is full of all irrationality. For that motion is named anger which, seething wholly outside reason, is borne by the impulse of passion alone. « But in intellectual beings the irascible must be understood in another manner; » [0981A] in this manner, namely, that the irascible itself be understood in them as declaring their manly rationality, and their vast quiet in the divine and immutable foundations. For when anger is named in the spiritual and celestial nature, it is not an impulse or a motion that is named, but quiet and immutability that is understood. By this reasoning — these things being considered among the dissimilar and contrary: that just as in these beings anger strives by impulse and vehemence to drive off a supervening annoyance, so there an immovable quiet, persisting, is disturbed by no supervening shock — for this reason he calls that quiet itself "vast," that is, strong, and robust, and imperturbable; repelling every motion without motion, and suppressing every violence without shock and disturbance of itself; which quiet, however, [0981B] is not from themselves, but from the divine and immutable foundations to which they cleave, that is, from divine love and contemplation, by which they are made firm unto eternity, that they can in no way henceforth be moved. But this quiet, since it is not of necessity — as though they were held unwilling — but of will — as though, loving indefatigably, they are not forsaken — therefore he joined manly rationality to the quiet, showing that through illuminating reason they manfully reject the evils which they discern, and through affecting love they are at rest in the goods which they perceive, so that the quiet may be fortified by reason, lest it take on an affection for evils; and reason may be guarded by the quiet, lest, setting itself in opposition in hatred of evil, it overstep the bounds of tranquillity. This manly reason, then, and vast quiet, free and [0981C] absolute, and receiving no perturbation, is what anger — when fitted to divine things — signifies; because, as we have said, just as here anger repels an assailing annoyance through madness, so there a rational and voluntary quiet receives no shock, through its immutability.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « In the same manner we say that concupiscence in irrational beings is a certain ill-considered and material passibility, engendered incontinently from a natural motion or from custom in mutable things, and an irrational continuity of bodily pleasure; at once compelling every animal toward the concupiscible according to sense. » After he demonstrated what anger signifies when attributed to sensible and material [0981D] things, and likewise what it signifies when fitted to immaterial and invisible natures, now he consequently shows the difference of concupiscence — whether when it is said of corporeal things, or when it is named in spiritual and divine things. Defining the concupiscence of corporeal beings, indeed, to be a certain passibility, or passion, that is, a dominating affection — ill-considered indeed, because it is not borne by reason, but is drawn by rash appetite into those things by whose delight it is affected; and material, that is, rising from the flesh and from the carnal sense, and showing forth things carnal and sensible — a passibility, I say, engendered either from a natural motion, namely when its appetite is according to nature; or from custom incontinently held in those mutable things, when its desire is borne outside nature, [0982A] or against it: and an irrational continuity of bodily pleasure; understand, we say that concupiscence itself is an irrational continuity, that is, a prolongation, or straining, or effusion of bodily pleasure — that is, of pleasure rising from the body, and tending toward corporeal things, and even compelling every animal, namely by the violence of its appetite, toward that which is concupiscible according to sense. And such indeed we understand the concupiscence of corporeal things to be. « But when, placing dissimilar likenesses about the intellectual and spiritual things, » or clothing them: « being formed about them, » that is, we adapt « to them concupiscence; » then we must understand that concupiscence itself not such as before, but rather divine love, [0982B] and a desire of immateriality, or incorporeality, and of divinity, existing above reason and intellect; since it can be loved more than investigated or understood; and inflexible, since it is always toward the one; and not needy, since what is loved is present. A desire, I say, of contemplation superessentially chaste and impassible — that is, of that thing which they contemplate superessentially, passing over in mind, namely, every essence and nature — the love of which thing is both chaste, since it brings no corruption upon the lover, and impassible, since, sweetly refreshing the one who desires, it does not afflict him. « And it must also be understood that concupiscence itself is a desire, tending toward that pure and most sublime brightness; and toward the invisible [0982C] and form-making beauty of the eternal, true, and invisible fellowship » — which beauty, namely, is called form-making, because it conforms to itself those turned toward it, that they may become beautiful, loving the true beauty, not as in the flesh and according to the flesh, where a lover of beauty can be base, and a possessor of beauty be found not good; but there he who loves, possesses; and he who loves to have, begins to be it. Such a love, then, and such a desire, we must understand in the concupiscence of spiritual and divine things.

There follows: « And it receives, as it were, a power in its sufficiency and in its convertibility; and is afflicted by none; by virtue, through the unconfused and immutable love of divine [0982D] beauty, and the universal recalling unto that which is truly to be desired. » As if he were saying: Concupiscence itself, or the chaste desire of eternal and invisible goods, because it is inflexible, as was said, and not needy, therefore has received, or receives, in its sufficiency and its convertibility toward God and toward divine things, as it were a certain power, or excellence and firmness, so that it cannot at all be afflicted by any contrary power, or violence, or strength. For no one can be hurt or afflicted, except in that which he loves; and therefore he who loves that alone which cannot be taken away can in no way be hurt, because he neither desires anything else outside it, having his sufficiency in it, nor is he anxious about its loss, holding it immutably. Because, then, [0983A] it is sufficient, it suffers no need; and because it is inflexible, it can undergo no violence. And this indeed is conferred on it through the unconfused and immutable love of divine beauty, and the universal recalling unto that which is truly to be desired. For because in them the love of God is unconfused, or, to say it otherwise, unmingled and pure, therefore their desire is not split toward diverse things. And because it is immutable, it is not bent away from the same object. And because they are universally recalled and gathered together unto that which is truly to be desired, it is disturbed by no exterior violence. For if with their whole desire they were not recalled to loving the truth, nor gathered together, without doubt they could be hurt and afflicted, wherever they cleaved in mind to mutable and transitory things. But now, because they are wholly there where all things [0983B] immutably stand fast, it comes about that they too, through the cohesion of love, cannot feel the mutability of affliction.

There follows: « But irrationality itself too, and insensibility, in irrational animals or in animated matters, we properly call a defect of reason and sense; but in immaterial and intellectual essences we confess, holily and becomingly, their supereminences as of super-mundane beings, surpassing our transitory and corporeal and material reason, and the mind's sense that is estranged from incorporeal things. » Other examples being set forth, he shows that the same things which are said of visible and invisible things are not said similarly, nor in the same manner. As if he were saying: Not only anger and concupiscence must be understood otherwise [0983C] here and otherwise there. For irrationality, which signifies the loftiness of reason; and insensibility, which demonstrates the excess of sensibility, when they are named here, that is, in irrational things and things not having sense, show a defect of reason and sensibility; but when they are said there, that is, in divine and intellectual natures, something above reason and sense is signified — not by defect, but by advance. For an excess of reason and sensibility happens either upward or downward: namely when something, either falling short of reason and sense, sinks down, so that it has not this; or, advancing beyond, transcends it, so that it has more. Whence also in Holy Scripture we find that holy men, breathed upon by the Spirit of God, at times suffered ecstasy, that is, an excess of mind [0983D]; since, led above human reason and sense, they went beyond reason and sense in this: that, reaching to that which was higher than reason, they began to be vivified in it and illumined by it. For them, then, the excess was in that they received more, just as for these others the excess came about in that they lost that very thing. Therefore, then, irrationality and insensibility in irrational animals and in inanimate matters we properly call a defect of reason and sense; but in immaterial and intellectual essences, as often as we name irrationality and insensibility, we confess not a defect of them, but rather their supereminences, inasmuch as they are super-mundane. We confess, I say, holily and becomingly, [0984A] that is, attributing nothing to them irreverently or unbecomingly; but, as befits the supereminences or excellences of super-mundane beings, that is, of spiritual essences, which surpass our transitory and corporeal reason — that is, a reason which can neither ascend above transitory things, nor comprehend anything at all outside corporeal things; and surpassing the material sense of our mind, estranged among corporeal things — that is, a sense flourishing only toward material things, and, through material and corporeal things, estranged from immaterial and incorporeal things, unable to perceive the same. Because, then, they are established above such reason, above such sense, through the excellence of contemplation, they are rightly reported to be estranged from such reason and sense — so that this very insensibility and irrationality of theirs [0984B] be understood not as stupor or ignorance, but to be something other — both a manly knowledge and a perception of the good; and not to be such as it is with us, or according to us.

There follows: « It is possible, therefore, to form for celestial things forms not discordant, even from the base parts of matter; since matter itself too, possessing its subsistence from the truly good, has, throughout its every material disposition, certain images of intellectual beauty; and it is possible through them to be led back to the immaterial first forms — the likenesses being taken, as was said, dissimilarly, and not similarly for the same things; yet compactly and beautifully, the intellectual and sensible properties being defined. » He subjoins another cause to the former, for which likenesses are fittingly drawn from visible things to invisible ones [0984C], even according to those things which seem contrary; namely not only because the things which are said are understood to subsist otherwise here and otherwise there, but for this reason too: because whatever things are here have, according to something, a likeness to those which subsist there, since both these and those are, whatever they are, from the one good; and according to the image which these possess toward that good, they also put forth the figure and likeness of those there. This is what he says: « It is possible, » that is, it happens, « to form for celestial things, » namely for the essences, forms not discordant, even « from the base parts of matter, » that is, of corporeal substance, since matter itself too, namely, possessing its subsistence from the truly good, has certain [0984D] images of intellectual beauty throughout its every material disposition: and through them, namely the images, it is possible for the mind to be led back to those first immaterial forms, that is, to the forms of immaterial and spiritual essences, which are prior to these — because these exist according to those, and these tend toward those, and these exist on account of those. It is possible, I say, to be led back from these — yet to those — the likenesses themselves being taken dissimilarly, so that, namely, the things which are here, and are said of those, be understood to be and subsist otherwise there. And the same properties being not similarly defined. But yet compactly, that is, fittingly, and aptly, and beautifully, that is, becomingly — understand, the same properties being defined, on both sides namely, both intellectual and insensible. He speaks of likeness [0985A] when, through extrinsic forms or figures or qualities, descriptions of invisible things are taken from visible ones; but of property, when the likeness is drawn from the inner nature. Whether, then, likenesses from visible things be set forth to be transferred extrinsically to the nature of invisible things, or properties be taken up intrinsically from those same visible and material things for the demonstration of invisible ones, the comparison and adaptation on both sides must be so tempered, that the likenesses themselves, which seem to be set forth similarly on both sides, be nevertheless taken dissimilarly, and be understood to be otherwise here, otherwise there; and the properties themselves, which seem to be attributed the same to both, be defined otherwise in these, and otherwise in those, and be understood to be different — so that to each may be attributed what is [0985B] fitting and apt to it according to its nature.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « We shall find the mystical theologians wondrously conforming these things not only to the declarations of the celestial dispositions, but sometimes also to the very divine manifestations. » He recalled the same thing above: namely that the mystical theologians — that is, theologians who narrate mystical and secret things — conform and fit these things, that is, these likenesses and properties taken from visible things, not only to the declarations of the celestial dispositions, that is, of the celestial orders, namely of the angelic spirits — that is, they set forth these likenesses and properties of visible things not only to declare or demonstrate the celestial dispositions and angelic orderings; [0985C] but they sometimes bring them also to the very divine manifestations, that is, to manifesting and indicating the divinity itself. « And sometimes indeed they praise it » namely the divinity, « from precious lights, » by comparing it to precious lights, and by demonstrating its praise through that thing which, among the other visible things, is precious and comely, as, for example, when they call it the sun — not this visible one, which illumines even the bodily eyes for perpetrating iniquity; but the sun of justice, which enlightens the spiritual eyes for knowing truth and virtue. And as when they call it the « morning star, » whose rising puts darkness to flight — not rising to illumine this world visibly, [0985D] but rising into the rational mind, which alone can receive this light, holily, that is, for making sanctification. And as also when they call it « light shining without being wrapped about, and invisibly; » not as this visible light, which can be both darkened by shadows, and wrapped about, and enclosed in place, and constrained by a bound. If, then, mystical theology praises the excellence of the divine majesty through the appearances and forms of the highest and most precious things. « But sometimes from the middle things, » understand, which in the order of creation are neither highest nor lowest, that theology praises the divine majesty, namely by calling it fire; not such as this corporeal fire is, which, though it profit by illumining, harms by burning and consuming; but « fire harmlessly shining; » [0986A] namely illumining, and not burning up; kindling, and not consuming. And as when it calls it « water, giver of vital fullness; » which gives, namely, the fullness of life, and fills with life those who draw and carry it; and water also, « that it be so said symbolically, » that is, figuratively, « entering into the belly; and overflowing with rivers immeasurably flowing back. » For all these things are said of it not in property, but in figure alone. In all these things mystical theology praises the majesty and goodness of the divinity. « But sometimes also from the lowest things » — as from the inferior and earthly ones — it praises it, and manifests its praise figuratively, as when it names it « sweet ointment, » and likewise when it calls it « corner [0986B] stone. » And to such a degree does it fit the appearances of lowly things to it by likeness, that sometimes it seems even to descend, in describing it, to unfitting and contrary forms. Which nevertheless, according to the cause mentioned above, is carried through by a fitting and necessary dispensation. « But they even set a bestial form about it. » As if he were saying: Not only in the declaration of the divinity do the theologians take up likenesses from inferior things, but (what seems marvelous!) they even descend to contrary and dissimilar formations — ones which, if they were understood according to property, would be wholly unworthy of its majesty. « For they even set a bestial form about it; and to it the particularity of a lion and of a panther, » that is, the appearance, or form, or figure, « they fit; [0986C] and they clothe it, » namely the divinity: « with a leopard-like form, » understand form, that is, the form of a leopard. « And they clothe it, » understand again the divinity; a raging she-bear, that is, with the appearance of a raging she-bear; or they call it « a raging she-bear, » so that in both the incongruity may appear, since they attribute even deformity to the beautiful, and cruelty to the mild.

There follows: « But I will add also that which has seemed to be, of all things, among the base, and to signify more. » As if he were saying: Although in the preceding things which have been mentioned, and in the rest of this kind, a great unworthiness may seem to appear, nevertheless in this which I subjoin a much greater affront appears. « But I will add, » I will indeed presume, and I shall seem to speak boldly, [0986D] I who add this too to the divine significations, « what has seemed to be the basest of all; » and through this, according to the aforesaid reasoning by which dissimilar symbols make the declaration greater, it is proved to signify more than the rest. Which nevertheless perhaps a man would not dare to fit to the divine majesty, if the wisdom of God itself did not take these things to itself by spontaneous condescension. « For those wise in divine things have handed it down as forming itself even under the appearance of a worm. » As it is written: « I am a worm, and no man; the reproach of men, and the outcast of the people [n: (Psal. XXI)]. » For unless it had first said this of itself, who would dare to say this of it? For since nothing seems to be baser and lowlier than a worm, who would presume to bring the highest majesty down to this abasement, [0987A] unless it had first, by its own dispensation, formed itself about with such an appearance, signifying itself thereby. Therefore it deigned first to say this of itself, because it knew that human conscience would not, of itself, dare this against its own Creator. « Thus all the theosophists, and the prophets by hidden inspiration, distinguish the Holy of Holies from the undefiled holy things. » Theosophists, that is, those wise in divine things and who have been made prophets by hidden inspiration. All these, when they wish to designate invisible things through visible appearances and the properties of visible things, distinguish, as we demonstrated above, « the Holy of Holies from the undefiled holy things. » The undefiled holy things are the symbols of divine things taken from beautiful and becoming forms; the Holy of Holies are the divine things themselves, which [0987B] are figured through these. The theosophists, then, by the fact that they attribute dissimilar figures to divine things, also separate the similar formations — even those which seemed worthy — from the excellence and majesty of those divine things. For because they fit even those figures which are not doubted to be foreign to the signification of them, they show that the others too, which could seem true, are not, according to property, similar. Thus, then, in one and the same thing, both in the similar figures they separate the figure from the truth, and in the dissimilar ones, by the fellowship of truth, they honor the figure of truth — since those figures, when they are shown to be foreign, are distinguished, and these, when they are fitted to likeness, are honored. For thus it was fitting that all that was made, compared to the excellence of the Creator, should be shown both in sublime things not [0987C] to be equal, and in the lowest things not to be dissimilar. For because it was made, it cannot be comparable unto equality; and because it was made by him, it cannot be wholly dissimilar to him by whom it was made.

There follows: « So that neither may divine things be rightly received by the unclean, nor may the zealous of the contemplation of marvelous images remain in the figures as though they were true. » This, then, is effected through dissimilar figurations: that in the consideration of them the unclean of heart, and those unworthy of the knowledge of truth, may be the more blinded; and those who are zealous, in the contemplation of marvelous images — that is, of sacred representations marvelously made — may be the more exercised. For thus on both sides [0987D] a just design is accomplished: that while the truth, in its manifestation, covers itself as with a base garment, it both provokes the unworthy to contempt of it, and invites the worthy to desire and to seek that appearance of its own which lies hidden, so that they be not content with what they look upon outwardly, but, repelled by the very deformity of the exterior demonstration, do not remain in the figures as though they were true — since a sign cannot be the truth, even when it is a sign of the truth.

There follows: « They therefore honor divine things by true negations, and by the diverse likenesses of imaginations compacted unto the lowest things. » As if he were saying: Since negations made concerning divine things signify their truth more expressly, and dissimilar formations of images demonstrate their purity more evidently, [0988A] therefore the holy theologians do no affront to the divine things themselves, both by making true negations concerning them, and by attributing dissimilar formations to them, but rather they honor the divine things themselves by true negations, by which they show their excellence to be so great that what they are can in no way be expressed, even if in some way what they are not can be said; and they honor them also by likenesses diverse from their sublimity through a humble formation; and by the likenesses of imaginations compacted unto the lowest things — that is, composed and joined together out of the lowest things, that is, out of the basest and vile appearances of corruptible things.

There follows: « Nothing therefore is inconsequent, if they also form the celestial essences from unfitting, dissimilar [0988B] likenesses, according to the causes stated. » As if he says: Since divine things are honored by true negations and by diverse formations, nothing is inconsequent — that is, unfitting — if the theologians form, that is, represent, the celestial essences from dissimilar and unfitting likenesses; that is, through representations of figures dissimilar from their nature, and in a certain way unfitting. It is not inconsequent, I say, according to the causes stated above.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « For perhaps indeed we would not, from need, come into inquiry, and into anagogy through a diligent scrutiny of divine things, unless the deformity of the manifesting formation of angels wrested us thereto. » As if he were saying: From our need [0988C] into this inquiry — that is, to seeking these things as regards the composition of visible images — or into anagogy as regards the investigation of invisible truth; from our need, I say — we who, knowing the truth, do not need these inquiries — into this inquiry, I say, we would not come, unless (on account of others who might be scandalized thereby, if they were not instructed) the deformity wrested us to it. First, note the doubling of the negation, by which the expression is made: or in this it is manifest that deformed formations are useful in divine things, because we too would not scrutinize the truth of divine things so diligently, unless the deformity of the manifestations wrested us, that is, compelled us. This is what he says: « Perhaps we would not indeed [0988D] come into an inquiry leading into anagogy, » that is, the contemplation of things above; from need, namely, of the understanding of truth: we would not come, I say, through a diligent scrutiny of divine things, « unless it wrested us, » that is, compelled us « to come into inquiry from need, and through inquiry into anagogy, » — the very deformity of the manifesting deformation of the angels, that is, unless the deformity of the formation through which the angels are manifested in Holy Scripture should compel us; I say: « Not allowing our mind to remain in the dissimilar makings of forms, » that is, in the deformed compositions of representations, which are dissimilar from the truth of spiritual things. Unless, then, the very deformity of the representations should compel our mind from the visible figures to [0989A] seek the truth, our mind itself would remain in those things which are set forth outwardly, as things only to be believed and venerated, and would not recognize its own need, so as to think that other things, beyond these, were to be desired and sought by it. But now the very deformity, intervening, compels the mind to go forth from the figure to the truth — the mind, I say, unable to remain in those things which are in themselves unworthy, and incongruous with the truth of divine things. « But the mind struggling to deny » of the divine things themselves those « material possibilities » which are set forth outwardly in signs; and through the fact that it thus holily abhors them, « growing accustomed purely to extend itself through visible things, » roused « into the super-mundane heights »: that is, the angelic excellences, which are supereminent over and excel all worldly things. For unless, roused from these [0989B] by the advancement of contemplation, it should wholly leave these very things behind, it would not be able purely to behold those things which it strives to contemplate.

There follows: « So many things indeed have been said by us, » etc. He himself connects the preceding matters to the following narration. « So many things indeed, » as many as we have said thus far, « have been said by us on account of the angelic descriptions of images, » that is, on account of the descriptions of angels, which are made through images and visible forms, and forms taken from visible things — which descriptions are « of the divine discourses, » that is, made through the divine discourses, and set forth in the divine discourses, which descriptions are also material, that is, formed according to the nature and likeness of material and corporeal things; and they are also unfitting [0989C] on account of the incongruous and base figurations of those things to which excellences have been attributed. For on account of such a thing the inquiry which has been ventilated thus far was set forth. « But next it behooves us to set apart what we consider the hierarchy itself to be, and what benefit those who obtain the hierarchy receive from the hierarchy itself. It behooves us, he says, next, » that is, after the aforesaid, to set apart, that is, to distinguish what we consider the hierarchy itself, taken generally, to be, its definition being set forth. « Next » also « it behooves us to set apart what benefit they receive, » that is, what of usefulness those who obtain the hierarchy receive from the hierarchy itself, namely, which each one obtains. And in saying this, he says, I pray that « my Christ » may be the « leader » of my discourse [0989D]; yet only if it is lawful for me, that is, permitted, to say « my. » For this is a great thing, and seems as it were close to presumption, that a sinner should receive the reward of the just, and one cast down should be confident of the familiarity of the Most High. Therefore I say [0990A] let « Christ » (and if I dare more, I say « my ») be the leader of my discourse. Without whom neither can discourse be right, because he is the Word; nor understanding be true, because he is Wisdom. For all who savor the true, savor through him; and he himself is the inspiration « of every hierarchical manifestation, » since he himself, being Wisdom breathed into the minds of the holy theologians, manifests the whole account of the hierarchical disposition — whether that which is in heaven, or that which is on earth — to those to whom he wills, in the ways he wills. « But you, O boy, » etc. He speaks to Timothy, the disciple of Paul, to whom he is said to have written these things — whom he also calls a boy for this reason: either because he preceded him in age, or because he held toward him the place and dignity of a teacher and master. « But you, O boy, hearken. » As if he says: [0990B] Because the things which are to be said are great, therefore hearken holily and becomingly, as befits the very holy things which are to be said. « Hearken, » I say; according to the holy legislation of our priestly tradition, which bids us not to contend, nor to resist in doctrine, but reverently and humbly to hearken. Or hearken to these things which have been said « according to the holy legislation of our priestly tradition, » that is, which have been said according to the holy law, that is, the Holy Scripture, which has been brought to us by God through the priestly tradition, that is, through the tradition of the holy and the sanctified. Thus, then, « hearken to the wondrously said things, » that is, those things which have been wondrously said: « you, » I say, who have been made divine in the divine doctrine, which makes those who hear and do it divine; [0990C] or, having been made divine in the divine doctrine of the wondrously said things, hearken to the things I propose to be said. And not only hearken, so as to receive the things said reverently, but also « in the secret of your mind covering about the things which are holy, keeping them from the unclean multitude, » that is, from those who are both unclean in conduct and divided in desires: « as uniform things, » that is, undivided and untouched, « guard them, » lest you imprudently expose them to be torn and defiled. « For it is not lawful, as the discourses say, to cast to swine the unconfused, and light-formed, and beneficent adornment of the invisible pearls. » For those are that unclean multitude who are signified by swine in Holy Scripture, who defile the Word of God by living wickedly, and defile the invisible pearls, that [0990D] is, the spiritual understandings — which adorn the understandings with morals, shine through pure truth, are unconfused through the affluence of grace, and are beneficent toward those who touch them with reverence and honor.

## BOOK FOUR.

[0989]

## TITLE OF CHAPTER III. *What the hierarchy is, and what the benefit is through the hierarchy.*



## THE TEXT.

[0989D]

*The hierarchy is, according to me, a divine order, and a knowledge, and an activity assimilating itself, as far as possible, to the deiform, [0990D] and, proportionally to the illuminations divinely implanted in it, ascending into the likeness of God. The divine beauty, as simple, and best, as consummative. It is pure [0991A] indeed, universally, from all dissimilarity; distributive according to the worth of each one's proper light; and perfective, in the most divine sacrifice, according to the compactly immutable formation of those perfected unto it. The interpretation, therefore, of the hierarchy is, unto God, as far as is possible, a likeness and unity, having Him as the leader of every holy action and knowledge; and unto its own most divine comeliness immutably defining them; but, as far as possible, it reforms, and perfects its own praisers as divine statues, mirrors most clear, and pure, receptive of the principal light and of the divine ray, and filled indeed with the implanted sacred brightness: and pouring it out again copiously into the things which follow, declaring the divine laws. For it is not lawful for the perfecters of the holy, and for the holily perfected, to do anything at all [0991B] apart from the + mysteries of the sacrificial victims, or the sacred ordinations; but neither to subsist otherwise, if they seek His divine brightness; and they look unto it holily and becomingly, and are reformed, according to the anagogy of each of the holy intellects. Does not, therefore, he who says "hierarchy" declare universally a certain sacred disposition, an image of the divine beauty, in the hierarchical orders and knowledges celebrating the mysteries of its own illumination, and assimilated to its own principle, as is permitted? For to each of those who obtain the hierarchy this is the perfection: to ascend, according to one's own analogy, into the imitation of God, and — most divine of all, as the discourses say — to become a fellow-worker of God [n: (I Cor. III; III Joan. I; Matth. V)]*, *and to show forth the divine action shining again in oneself, according [0991C] as is possible. Inasmuch as the order of the hierarchy is that some be purged, but some purge; and some be illumined, but some illumine; and some be perfected, but some perfect; for each in a deiform union, in whatever manner. The divine blessedness — as far as it is to be spoken of among men — is indeed pure, without any dissimilarity; full of eternal light; perfect and not needy, at once of all perfection; purging, and illumining, and perfecting; but a great purgation, and holy illumination, and perfection, above purgation, above light, perfect beforehand; through itself perfect, the principle of perfection; and the cause indeed of every hierarchy, and of everything sacred, according to its supereminent loftiness. [0991D] It behooves, therefore, as I consider, that those to be purged be wholly perfected by the pure, and be freed from all confusion of dissimilarity. But that those to be illumined be filled with divine light, and be led back unto a contemplative habit and power in the most chaste eyes of the mind. That from the imperfect, being restored, they become partakers of the perfective knowledge of the sacred things searched out. But that the purgers, by the greatness of purgation, impart to others out of their own chastity. But that the illuminators — having more luminous minds, and being properly suited both for the participation of light and for its distribution, and most richly filled with holy charity — convey their own super-excellent light entirely upon those who are worthy of light. But that the perfecters, as teachers of the perfective tradition, perfect those to be perfected by a most sacred doctrine, through the knowledge of the sacred things beheld. Is not, therefore, [0992A] each order of the hierarchy's disposition led back, according to its own analogy, to divine cooperation — accomplishing those things by grace and by the power given by God, which are present in the divinity naturally and supernaturally, and, by it superessentially enacted, are manifested unto the imitation of the hierarchy, as far as is possible, by minds loving God.*



## EXPOSITION.

Up to this point the theologian has discoursed on the things that had to be said in a general way for those being introduced into the knowledge of the hierarchies, in proportion to the visible demonstrations set forth for the signifying of visible things. Then now, entering upon the principal exposition, he first defines what a hierarchy is; not universally, however, but with a restricted meaning, only according to that hierarchy which consists in angels and [0992B] men. « A hierarchy, » he says, « is, in my view, a divine order, and knowledge, and activity, likening the deiform as far as is possible; and ascending, in proportion to the illuminations divinely implanted in it, into the likeness of God. » What he says, « in my view, » is to be taken as though he had said, according to my own estimation. For he modestly tempers his assertion, lest he seem to presume upon his own estimation more than is right. Then he sets forth three principal things in the definition of a hierarchy, which complete the very definition of a hierarchy. And these are: order, knowledge, and activity. If any one of all these three be lacking, there is no hierarchy. The first is a divine order; because there is no power, if it is not ordained [0992C] by God; for this reason he says, a divine order. For « all » « power is from God; and the things that are from God are all good and ordained; therefore he who resists the power ordained by God, resists God [n: (Rom. XIII)]. » For this reason, then, a hierarchy is a divine order, that is, a power ordained by God and disposed according to God. For it is from God by ordination; and according to God by imitation; and therefore a divine order, because it is from God, that it may be; and it is according to God, as to what kind and how great it is. Then, because every power that is ordained by God is ordained for the accomplishing and completing of something, there follows in the definition, after order, « knowledge and activity. » Knowledge, indeed, by which they may understand what is to be done; [0992D] but activity, by which they may accomplish what they have understood must be done. In order, duty; in knowledge, discernment; in activity, ministry. Without order, activity is presumption; without activity, order is negligence; but without knowledge, both activity is blameworthy and order is useless. For these reasons, then, a hierarchy is a divine order, and knowledge, and activity. A hierarchy, I say, both in order and in knowledge and in activity, likening the deiform, that is, imitating conformity to God as far as is possible for it; and ascending into the likeness of God in proportion to the illuminations, that is, according to the illuminations divinely implanted in it: each one, namely, according to the manner and measure of the grace divinely infused into it, accomplishing its task in its own order, and ascending to the imitation of God, so that by discerning rightly and working well [0993A] it may imitate him. For in both respects every hierarchy emulates the divine likeness: namely, both in that it is disposed by him, and in that it works according to him. But this definition, as we have said, embraces only the angelic and human hierarchy, which were made after the likeness of the supreme and eternal hierarchy; and, being disposed according to it, they imitate it.

There follows: « The divine beauty, as simple, and as best because consummative; it is pure, indeed, universally from all unlikeness; but distributive, according to the dignity of each, of its proper light; and perfective in the most divine sacrifice, according to the unchangeable formation of those made perfect, closely joined unto it. » The sense is this. That the divine beauty, [0993B] which we name the supreme hierarchy, according to which the other hierarchies were made, is universally pure, that is, wholly so, inasmuch as it is always simple in unity, best in goodness, consummative in perfection. For where there is unity, there is no diversity; and where there is no diversity, there can be no unlikeness. Likewise, where there is perfection, there is no degree; where there is no degree, there is no difference; where there is no difference, there is no unlikeness. Therefore the divine beauty, which is the form and exemplar of all things well and beautifully disposed — because it is one, it does not admit plurality; and because it is best, and consummate, and not only consummate but also consummative of all things to be consummated, and the cause of consummation, it does not admit diversity, and thereby [0993C] knows no unlikeness at all; it that is both one in simplicity and the same in perfection. And although in itself it is such that it is neither divided by plurality nor made lesser by diversity, yet in those who become partakers of grace it is distributive of its proper light, bestowing its proper light in diverse ways, according to the dignity, namely, of each of the participants — granting to the higher greater, but to the lower lesser, gifts of graces, so that it may be beautifully multiplied in them, it which in itself truly abides one. The divine beauty itself is also perfective, since it perfects and makes the partakers of its lights consummate; perfective, I say, in the most divine sacrifice, by which it perfects those to be perfected according to the unchangeable formation of those closely joined and made perfect unto it. By the most divine [0993D] sacrifice he means the divine illumination itself, and grace, and propitiation; by which all who are to be purged and to be saved are purged and cleansed, not only from the corruption of evil, that they may become good, but they are purged also from the defect of good, that they may stand present as better. It is therefore the supreme offering, and the *teletargis*, that is, the victim of the principal purgation; namely, the divine grace itself which is offered to us and offered for us. It is offered to us for purgation; it is offered for us for propitiation. It is offered to us, that we may have it; it is offered for us, that through it we may be pleasing. It is offered to us by infusion; it is offered for us by cleansing. It is offered to us while we begin to be what we were not; it is offered by us, while we exhibit and present [0994A] what we are. It is therefore the *teletargis* itself, that is, the victim of the principal purgation, and the most divine sacrifice, without which all victims and all sacrifices can have neither effect nor profit; it is that by which the divine beauty perfects, and makes perfect those who are perfect unto it — that is, reformed unto its likeness, that is, harmoniously, so that, namely, they may not diverge from it — according to the unchangeable formation of those very ones made perfect, unto it, that is, according to its likeness, which, once received, they keep unchangeably, so that they do not flow away from it. For the divine beauty, which in itself is one and perfect, purges and perfects those to be perfected unto itself through the victim of the principal purgation — that is, the infusion of its grace, which from its own fullness it poured out into participation for the sake of those to be purged and [0994B] perfected — according to the manner and measure and capacity of each, which they received by the gift of that same grace, according to the unchangeable formation of them unto it; which formation, namely, is called unchangeable, as we have said, either because it turns those who are formed and reformed toward unchangeability; or because it abides unchangeable in him from whom it is, even if it be changed for those for whom it is and in whom it is.

There follows: « The interpretation [ *intentio* ], therefore, of a hierarchy is likeness and unity with God, as far as is possible. » What in Greek is called σκοπός *scopos,* and what the translator calls interpretation, is more properly named *intentio* or *directio*. For the intention [0994C] or direction which is called *scopos* is a fixed aiming toward some end. For every action tends toward some end, and tends by some direction. But *scopas*, that is, the direction or aim, is that by which it tends. Every hierarchy, therefore, has a *scopon*, that is, a direction according to which it may proceed in fulfilling its ministry; namely, the imitation and divine likeness, so that, just as it has been ordained by him in office and dignity, so according to him it may proceed in ministry and operation, so that it presume to do nothing beyond the manner and measure assigned by him and owed to its own order. The interpretation, then — that is, the definition, or rather the direction and contemplation of a hierarchy, toward which every hierarchy ought to aim and does direct itself — is likeness and unity, that is, [0994D] imitation of and identity with God, so that in nothing it may deviate or decline from his likeness, in which it has been placed, but may emulate him in all things as far as is possible. Likeness to God is to imitate him; but unity is to follow him alone. And this is the *scopos*, that is, the direction or aim of every hierarchy: that according to him it may proceed toward him, having him as the leader of all holy knowledge and activity, following him in judgment and in action — illumined by him unto the knowledge of truth, and aided unto the carrying out of good work. « Having him as leader, » presiding within in the demonstration of truth, and going before without in the example of good action. « And unto its own most divine comeliness defining unchangeably indeed; [0995A] but, as far as is possible, it reforms, and perfects its own praisers as divine images. » The hierarchy itself, namely, defining unchangeably — that is, invariably or inflexibly turned, or aiming through its *scopos* toward the most divine comeliness of God himself, by imitating and following him, so that it may be conformed to his beauty and most divine comeliness, according to which every hierarchy is beautifully and becomingly disposed in its own order and rank: as far as is possible it reforms its own praisers, that is, those who in it praise God, and are disposed and ordained unto the praise of God: it reforms them, I say, in this very thing, that it makes them imitators of God, and turns them unto his likeness in their ministry; and by turning and reforming them it perfects them as divine *agalmata*, so that they themselves may be divine [0995B] *agalmata*, that is, holy images and receptacles of divinity, and most bright mirrors; so that they themselves may be divine *agalmata*, indeed, by perfecting the divine light, but most bright mirrors, to shine from the light received. He also perfects those praisers of his as « pure receptacles, » or vessels « of the principal light, and of the divine ray » — that is, of the light immediately illuminating, and descending to illuminate those first things — so that, after the light is received, they may be filled with the sacred brightness implanted in them, that is, infused; and so that they may also be things declaring it, namely the brightness — « again, » that is, in the second place, « in those things which follow, declaring it » abundantly, according to « the divine laws. » The sense is this: Since a hierarchy, according to that disposition by which it imitates the divine beauty, [0995C] makes its praisers universally — that is, whether the higher or the lower — such that they are worthy both to receive the divine light and to shine from the light: but in particular it makes certain ones so pure that they are capable of the principal light, and are immediately illumined by God, and then transfuse their light to those who follow after them and are set beneath them in dignity: administering, indeed, abundantly, out of the abundance of the first reception, the second participation — provided only that the divine law is observed, in which it is prescribed for each one what, or how much, or to whom he ought to impart out of the gift of grace granted to him. For that it is not without the divine law — that is, that divine dispensation by which the gifts of grace are bestowed upon the partakers of the largess according to [0995D] a fixed measure and proportion, and duties are assigned, so that each one may know how much is permitted to him according to the ministry assigned, or is permitted to be done — he makes clear when he adds:



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

« For it is not lawful for the perfecters of holy things, and for the holily perfected, to work anything at all beyond their proper mysteries of victims, and their sacred ordinations. » As if he should say: For this reason those who in the order of the hierarchies are superior transfuse their lights to the inferiors, according to the divine laws; because it is not lawful, that is, not at all permitted, to work anything either for the perfecters of holy things, that is, for those who perfect others in holiness, or for the holily perfected, that is, for those who are perfected by others in holiness, beyond the proper mysteries of victims, that is, the proper gifts of graces, and the proper [0996A] sacred ordinations, that is, the sacred orders proper and assigned to each one. For to work without grace is vain; to work beyond order, or against it, is perverse.
