For individuals have their proper gifts, according to which they are able to work; and their proper orders, according to which they ought to moderate their working. Therefore it is necessary that each one strive to recognize the grace which he has received, lest he begin to presume in that which he cannot do; and to attend to his proper office and order, lest he dare to transgress in that which he ought not. For that this order of the divine disposition must be diligently observed, the Apostle Peter admonishes, saying: « Each one, as he has received grace, ministering it one to another » [n: (I Petr. IV)]. And the Apostle Paul reproves those who were unwilling to hold this divine ordination [0996B] and dispensation, saying: « Are all apostles? Are all prophets? Are all doctors? Are all workers of powers? Have all the grace of healings? Do all speak with tongues? Do all interpret? Be zealous for the better charisms. » [n: (I Cor. XII.)] Therefore, then, it is not lawful for those who have been made partakers of divine grace, whether they have received it immediately from God, or through man, to work anything beyond their proper gifts and offices, so that the divine beauty may be conserved in all, and the order of the supreme dispensation may persevere. He calls perfecters of holy things those who have received so great a grace from God that, by illuminating and instructing, they perfect others also unto holiness. The holily perfected are those who, [0996C] illumined and instructed by those very superiors, are perfected in holiness. By mysteries of victims he wishes gifts of graces to be understood: which are for this reason called mysteries, because they are secretly inspired; but victims, because they are bestowed for the cleansing and expiation of those who receive them. Or by ministries of victims he means the exhibitions of holy works, and the administrations of the divine sacraments: which are both victims, because they are offered through the exhibition of action, and mysteries through the sacraments of signification; because through that which is done visibly without in the sacrament, the invisible power of truth is signified. So, then, beyond the proper mysteries of victims, that is, the proper gifts or ministries, it is not lawful to work anything, either for the perfecters of holy things, that is, for those who administer the grace they have received to others to be perfected, [0996D] or for the perfected themselves, that is, for those who, through the grace received, are perfected unto the perfection of holiness. Nor is it lawful either for these or for those to work anything beyond their sacred ordinations, that is, beyond that which pertains to their sacred ordinations, so that, namely, each one may presume to work that alone which is proved to pertain to the order and office assigned to him.

There follows: « But neither can they subsist otherwise, if they desire his divine brightness, and look toward it sacredly and becomingly, and are reformed, according to the analogy of each of the holy intellects. » As if he should say: Not only is it a debt for them to keep the sacred mysteries and the sacred ordinations, but it is also necessary, [0997A] since they cannot subsist otherwise, namely in that state in which they imitate the divine beauty, unless, by keeping their orders, they proceed according to the divine laws. If, therefore, they desire the divine brightness of their Creator himself, and look toward the receiving of it by imitating and desiring — sacredly by affection, becomingly by habit — and we are reformed unto his likeness by imitating and following it: each of the holy intellects, namely, that is, of the angels, « according to the analogy of each, » that is, according to the manner and measure of his possibility, which each has according to his order, and rank, and property; otherwise they can in no way subsist in that good which they desire, unless they proceed according to the divine laws, [0997B] keeping their ordinations, and executing their proper ministries.

There follows: « Does not, therefore, he who says a hierarchy universally declare a certain sacred disposition, an image of the divine beauty, in the hierarchical orders and knowledges sacrificing the mysteries of its proper illumination, and, as far as is allowed, assimilated unto its proper principle? » Since, he says, it is necessary that every hierarchy desire the divine likeness, nor can it subsist otherwise, unless it persevere in his imitation. Therefore he who says a hierarchy declares a certain sacred disposition, which is an image of the divine beauty, that is, comeliness. A disposition, I say, sacrificing the mysteries, that is, exercising or exhibiting the mystical works, or the divine ministries [0997C] of its proper illumination, that is, according to its proper illumination, in the hierarchical orders and knowledges — as far, namely, as is given to each for the work, according to his order, and rank, and the gift of illuminating grace; and, by working, assimilated, as far as is allowed, that is, as far as is possible for the creature according to its manner and dignity, unto its proper principle, from which also it was made that it might be something, and according to which it was disposed that it might be such. « Does not, therefore, he who says a hierarchy declare a certain sacred disposition universally, » that is, so holding itself in every order and rank of it; because, namely, it is disposed and ordained after the image of the divine beauty in the hierarchical orders and knowledges, and sacrifices [0997D] the mysteries of its proper illumination, so that in all its disposition, and order of dignity, and knowledge of discernment, and imitation of work, it may emulate its principle. He sacrifices the mysteries of his proper illumination who, out of the hidden gift of inspiration, represents good work; he also sacrifices the mysteries of his proper illumination who, transfusing the grace he has received to others, multiplies the talent committed to him; he also sacrifices the mysteries of his proper illumination who administers those things alone which befit his order and office. « For the perfection of each of those who are allotted a hierarchy is this: namely, according to his proper analogy, to ascend into the imitation of God, and to become, most divinely of all [divinissime], as the words say, a fellow-worker of God, and to show forth the divine action in himself, shining, as far as is possible, [0998A] relucent. » For this reason he says: He who says a hierarchy signifies a certain general disposition, imitating the divine beauty in its ordination, because this is the perfection of each order, namely, or person, of all who are allotted a hierarchy: to ascend, namely, according to his proper analogy, that is, manner and measure, into the imitation of God, and to become a fellow-worker of God, as the words say, most divinely of all, that is, than which the words say nothing more divine, or, most divinely of all, to become a fellow-worker of God, that is, than which nothing makes men more divine than, namely, to become a fellow-worker of God, and to show forth in himself the divine action relucent: so that, namely, by being relucent to others, he may transfuse through the example of work that which he first merited to receive through the gift of hidden inspiration. Thus, then, [0998B] the perfection of a hierarchy stands, that those who are purged may purge, and those who are illumined may illumine, and those who are perfected may perfect. « Inasmuch as the order of a hierarchy is thus, namely: that some indeed are purged, but some purge; and some indeed are illumined, but some illumine; and some indeed are perfected, but some perfect: for each one a deiform union in whatever mode. » First they are purged, afterward they are illumined, then they are perfected. For unless purgation preceded, illumination would not follow; and unless there were illumination, consummation would not come. For just as he cannot be illumined who is not purged, so he cannot be consummated who is not illumined; because the knowledge of truth illumines none but the pure, and the perfection of virtue [0998C] approaches none but those illumined by truth. But there are the superior and sublime, who themselves also, approaching the divinity, receive immediately from it both purgation, that they may be pure, and illumination, that they may be clear, and perfection, that they may be holy. And from those in turn, according to the order of the divine disposition, those who follow, and are subject in order, are both purged, and illumined, and perfected. And thus, according to this mode, for each union — that is, order and distribution — in whatever mode the deiform, that is, deiformity or likeness, is: namely, this very being purged, illumined, and perfected, so that in this their mode and measure the individuals may have deiformity and the likeness of God: they who are pure by purgation, illumined by truth, perfected by goodness.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

[0998D]There follows: « The divine beatitude (as far as it must be said among men) is indeed pure at once from all unlikeness; but full of eternal light: perfect, and at once not lacking any perfection; purging, and illuminating, and perfecting. » Now he wishes to demonstrate that the good which, in the creature — whether purged, or illumined, or perfected — stands by grace, in the Creator himself, from whom it is, subsists by nature: whose divine beatitude is both pure without purgation, and shining without illumination, and perfect without reception. For it is not purged in order that it may be pure; nor is it illumined in order that it may shine; nor does it receive in order that it may be perfected. But having the whole in itself, it does not lack what is another's; and, administering its own, it fills those things which of themselves are in need. It is indeed pure, he says, from all unlikeness equally; [0999A] this, then, is the reason for which it is rightly named pure, and has no unlikeness whatsoever. For where there is purity, there is no unlikeness; because it is the very same throughout, and a concordant one. For corruption introduces unlikeness, and makes other that which withdraws from its own being, and fails as though it were other than it was. That, therefore, which is always the same admits no unlikeness; and that which perseveres unchangeable admits no corruption. Therefore the divine beatitude is pure, and incorrupt, and foreign to all unlikeness, abiding in that which it is, and keeping incorrupt what it has. It is also full of eternal light, yet not as if illumined, since it is itself light; nor beginning to shine, since it is eternal; nor growing in light, since it is [0999B] full. It is also perfect, and at once not lacking any perfection, that is, lacking nothing that pertains to perfection; because it has and possesses the whole, and it is itself the whole which it possesses: and therefore it can be neither greater, because it has the whole; nor lesser, since it has it unchangeably and eternally. And although in itself it is such that it needs neither to be purged, being pure; nor to be illumined, being full of light; nor to be perfected, being consummate: yet it purges, and illumines, and perfects all who merit to be purged, and illumined, and perfected — whether receiving grace immediately in the first place, or partaking mediately through those who first merited to receive it. But this whole, namely that the divine beatitude is called full, and full of light, and perfect: is to be said as far as among men, that is, [0999C] according to that mode by which that which is ineffable can be said by men. For as regards that ineffable purity of the supreme truth, the divine nature itself is rather to be called holy purgation, and illumination, and perfection, so that it may be understood to be little that it is called pure, but rather purgation itself; that it is called illumined, but rather illumination itself; that it is called perfect, but rather perfection itself: which itself, however, is still found to be too little, unless there be thought purgation above all purgation, illumination above all illumination, perfection above all perfection. For this reason he says: « Purgation, illumination, perfection, above purgation, above light, before perfect, » or more than perfect, that is, above perfection; because all this that is said is said according to [0999D] something, from which he who supremely is is far removed, and therefore he is above all this, which is. Therefore it is purgation, since it receives nothing defiled into itself, and takes away defilement or corruption from those receiving and suffering corruption: and yet above purgation, since it touches no corruption. And it is illumination, since it shines in itself, and makes dark things grow bright from itself, and above illumination, since, irradiating and penetrating all things, it does not go out from itself; and it is perfection, since it has nothing less in itself, and to those having less it grants what is lacking, and supplies it out of itself; and yet before perfect, or more than perfect, since it stands wholly in the singularity of good, and undivided in participation, so that its good is neither less [1000A] because it is one, nor manifold because it is participated. For this reason, « it is perfect through itself, » because what it has it did not receive from elsewhere; and « through itself the principle of perfection, » because, by granting to those to be perfected that which it has, it itself does not lose it. And it is « the cause of every hierarchy, and of every holy thing, according to its supereminent loftiness. » It is a principle, since it is from it; and a cause, since it is through it, and on account of it; and a form, since according to it every hierarchy in the order of dignity, and every holy thing in the ministry of action, is dispensed. From it, by predestination; through it, by creation; on account of it, by glorification; unto it, by conversion, that it may imitate it and be beatified in it.

There follows: « It behooves, therefore, as I suppose, etc. » [1000B] After he has demonstrated that the divine beatitude is the form and cause of every sacred power and disposition; and that it is both pure, and shining, and perfect: pure indeed and clean in that it lacks all unlikeness and confusion; shining in that it contains in itself the fullness of eternal light; but perfect in that, having all things, it lacks nothing. Now he infers, proving that those too who are ordained unto its likeness and imitation in the sacred dispositions ought likewise to be pure from all contagion and confusion, and shining with truth, and perfect in goodness, so that they too may be able to cleanse, and illumine, and perfect others by doctrine and by example, so that in both respects they may rise up unto the image and likeness of their Creator, [1000C] namely, both in that they themselves are pure, and clear, and perfect, and in that they cleanse, and illumine, and perfect others. « It behooves, » he says, « as I suppose, that those to be purged indeed » — namely, whether those who in the first place, or those who, with others mediating, receive purgation — « be made altogether pure, and be freed from all confusion of unlikeness, » which either ignorance of the true, or concupiscence of evil, has introduced. « But those to be illumined it behooves to be filled with the divine light, » inasmuch as they are to be led back, in the most chaste eyes of the mind, « unto a contemplative habitude and power — that is, so that, the eyes of the mind being chaste and pure from the preceding purgation of sin and of error, they « may have a contemplative habitude and power. » A habitude, namely, through purity of mind; but a power through [1000D] the stability of contemplation, so that they may be able to contemplate divine things, which they both truly apprehend, and retain perseveringly. « But those to be restored from the imperfect it behooves to become partakers of the perfective knowledge of the explored holy things, » so that, namely, they may apprehend the true goods, and those holy things which they explore through perfect knowledge, by following them with love and partaking of them with holiness; so that, just as through perfect knowledge they are perfected in cognition, so through perfect goodness they may be perfected in participation. « But the purgers, by the greatness of purgation, to hand on to others out of their proper chastity, » that is, the purgers themselves, whose ministry it is, namely, that through them others be purged from error and fault: it behooves them to be such that, by the greatness [1001A] of their own purgation, that is, of their own cleanness which they have in themselves, they may hand on purgation to others by their proper chastity, chastening the life of those to be purged by their own, not another's, example. « But the illuminators it behooves to convey their supereminent light upon those who are worthy of light, » inasmuch as they have more luminous souls for the participation of light, by which they themselves receive light, and for the distribution of light, by which they transfuse the light received to others to be illumined: « having » I say « properly, » that is, singularly and excellently, because those who ought to illumine others ought to shine more than others: « and most richly, » that is, they ought to be abundantly filled with the sacred brightness, so that out of the abundance of reception they may be able to bestow upon individuals what is needful. For he who ought to teach all ought to have the knowledge of all; [1001B] because he cannot administer to each one what is expedient, who does not know the causes of all things, and their utilities. And they ought to convey their light aloft, so that what they teach by word they may commend by the excellence of life, as though shining on high, and pouring lights upon those who remain lower in their manner of life. But the perfecters it behooves to perfect those to be perfected by most sacred doctrine, through the knowledge of the holy things beheld, as teachers of the perfective knowledge. He truly beholds holy things who knows the habit through experience; he truly beholds holy things who has perceived the interior goods by tasting. He therefore has most sacred doctrine who teaches what he savors; who instructs in what he feels; who teaches not only to know the true, but to apprehend the good [1001C] and to love the just. Which handing-on is for this reason called perfective, because this alone leads a man to the perfect, when he apprehends, through the pursuit of good work, the goods which he could not know through the understanding. By such doctrine, therefore, the perfecters themselves ought to perfect those to be perfected, so that they may be holy and perfect in goodness, imitating him unto whose form and likeness they are reformed — the superiors by bestowing, and the inferiors by receiving, the participation of divine grace.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

Whence there follows: « Is not, therefore, every order of the hierarchic disposition, according to its proper analogy, that is, » manner, and measure, and order, « led back unto the divine cooperation: doing and perfecting those things through the grace and power given by God, [1001D] which are in the divinity naturally and supernaturally, and, having been » — namely by the divinity — « superessentially wrought by it, were afterward manifested to the hierarchy for the possible imitation of the souls, that is, » of the spirits « that love God? » For unless those goods which are in God by nature descended to these by grace, they would not be like him; and unless they themselves, by acting, obtained those things which he possesses not by acting but by having, they would not be his imitators. Finally, unless those goods had been led forth from his invisible secret unto manifestation, they would in no way have been imitable by the possibility of the creature; nor would there be dignity according to him, unless grace had gone forth from him. Behold what charity does. To those souls alone that love God are the hidden [1002A] divine things said to be made manifest, and possible to imitate. For rational souls perceive the interior and eternal goods through charity alone: those goods, through love, both by tasting that they may understand, and by following that they may apprehend. For unless they loved they would not understand, because they are not understood except when they are loved; and again, unless they loved they would not seek, and unless they sought they would not find, because they are not found if they are not sought. For hence it is written: « Now I will not call you servants, because the servant knows not what his Lord does. But I have called you friends, because all things whatsoever I have heard from my Father, I have made known to you [n: (Joan. XV)]. » And again: « Father, I give thee thanks, because thou hast hidden these things from the wise and prudent, and hast revealed them to little ones [n: (Luc. X)]. » Behold, [1002B] therefore, how charity alone reveals the things of God that are hidden, and likewise it too leads unto possibility the things that are ineffable, and superessential, and supernatural to every creature, according to the incomprehensible sublimity of God. « If anyone, he says, loves me, he will keep my word; and my Father will love him, and we will come to him, and will make our abode with him [n: (Joan. XIV)]. » Thus, therefore, every order of the disposition of the hierarchy, the grace and power having been received from God, is perfected through charity alone, by acting and imitating God, so that by action and imitation there may begin to be present in them those things which are in the divinity naturally, since they are from it; and supernaturally, since they are the same with it: naturally, since coeternal; supernaturally, since [1002C] coessential: for that which is always present is natural; but that which is the same is supernatural, since it is nature itself, and it itself is nature.

There follows: « And superessentially wrought by it; and manifested to the hierarchy for the possible imitation of the souls that love God. » What are those things which are supernaturally in the divinity, and were superessentially wrought by it, and finally were manifested to the hierarchy for the possible imitation of the souls that love God. He wishes certain goods to be understood, invisible and ineffable, which were with God, and were in God, and had not yet come into knowledge through participation, so as to be known and possessed except by him alone whose they were, in whom they were: and afterward they were made by him, when it was brought about that they should come to be in [1002D] us; and they were superessentially made, since first they were made by him, for us, so that afterward through him they might come to be in us. Made for us by predestination, that they might come to be in us by reception; made for us when they are bestowed, that they might come to be in us when they are received. Made above us, when they begin to come to us; made in us, when they begin to be had by us. Made unto us, when they descend from him; made in us, when they are bestowed through him. These, therefore, are the things which are supernaturally in the divinity before us, and were superessentially made from it unto us: finally manifested in us through it. « They were manifested, he says, to the hierarchy, » that is, to the sacred disposition which is ordained according to God, and proceeds: and this was done [1003A] « for the possible imitation of the souls that love God, » that is, so that the souls that love God might be able to imitate him; because, unless they had been manifested, they would not be imitable. For unless they were known they would not be sought, and unless they were sought they would not be known. But this is to be diligently attended to, that those goods are said to be manifested not to any single individuals, but to the hierarchy, that is, to the whole; yet in such a way that they are exercised by the individuals [1004A] within the whole by the zeal of imitation, because grace is poured out upon all and works in individuals. Outside unity none can receive it, and, given to another within unity, it can suffice for no one. For this reason they are manifested to the hierarchy, to bring forth the possible imitation of the souls that love God.

## BOOK FIVE.

[1003]

## TITLE OF CHAPTER IV. *What the appellation of the angels signifies.*



## THE TEXT.

[1003B]

*What a hierarchy is, therefore, having been, as I suppose, well defined by us — the angelic hierarchy — is then to be praised, and its wondrous makings of forms in the words are to be beheld with supermundane eyes, that we may ascend into their most deiform simplicity through the mystical formations. And at the same time we shall praise the principle of all hierarchic knowledge in divine religiosity, and in the most perfect givings of thanks: first of all, at once, to say this true thing — that by universal goodness the superessential divinity, setting up the essences of the things that are unto being, led them forth. For this is proper to the cause of all things, and to the goodness that is above all: to call unto its own communion the things that are, as for each one of the things that are it is defined out of its proper analogy. All things, therefore, [1003C] that are participate the providence flowing from the superessential and most causal divinity. For perhaps they would not be, save by the assumption of the principle of the essences of the things that are. All existing things, therefore, participate its being. For the being of all things is the superbeing of the divinity: but living things participate the same vivific power above all life. Rational and intellectual things participate the same wisdom, above all reason and intellect, perfect through itself, and before perfect. And it is clear that about it are those of the essences which have innumerably received from it. The holy dispositions, therefore, of the heavenly essences, above the things which merely are and live irrationally (according to which we are rational), have been made in the participation of the hierarchic tradition. For, invisibly looking supermundanely [1003D] into the divine likeness, and desiring to form their intellectual appearance, they beautifully have the more copious communions toward it. For they are attentive to all life. They, therefore, are first, and manifoldly, and ever to the utmost, as far as is lawful, intent upon the conformation of the divine and inflexible love, and receiving the principal illuminations immaterially and purely, and ordained unto them, and having all intellectual life. They, therefore, are first, and manifoldly made in the participation of God: and first, and manifoldly the makers-manifest of the divine hiddenness. For this reason also, beyond [1004B] all things, they have been made worthy of the angelic appellation, and by selection: in that they first bring forth into themselves the divine illumination, and through themselves convey to us the manifestations which are above us. Thus indeed, as theology says, they were given to us through angels. And our glorious fathers also, both before the law and after the law, the angels led back unto the divine: introducing what was to be done, and leading them back unto the right way of truth from error and from an unclean life; or revealing prophetically the sacred orders of the supermundane mysteries, or the hidden visions, or certain divine prior proclamations [praedictiones] [n: (Gen. XIX, XXII, XXXI; Judith. VI; Dan. VII; Matth. II; Act. X; Apoc. IV)]*. *But if anyone should say that from thence also there were, immediately, theophanies to certain of the holy ones, let him learn this too wisely from the most sacred [1004C] words: how this indeed is a certain thing — no one sees the hidden of God, nor will see [n: (Num. XII; I Tim. VI)]*. *But theophanies were made to the holy ones, [sed] according to what befits God, and through certain sacred manifestations of proportional visions to those who see. Therefore the most wise theology itself revealed that vision which is described in it to be divine, so that, as it were, a likeness in the form of the formless, from the leading-back of the seers into the divine, is beautifully called a theophany, as though through it divine things were made to the seers by illumination, the divine ones themselves indeed holily perfecting them. But these divine visions our glorious fathers accomplished through the mediating heavenly powers. Does not also the tradition of the words say that the sacred legislation was given, as it were of itself indeed, from God to Moses, so that it may truly teach us also [1004D] that it and the sacred characters are divine? [n: (Deut. IX.)] But theology also wisely teaches that it came unto us through angels, as though the divine legal order legally set that down — that is, that the second things are led back into the divine through the first. For not only in the superposed and subject souls, but also in the equally powerful, is the law itself defined out of the superessential principle of the ordination of all things. This is: through each single hierarchy there are the first, and the middle, and the last, and ordinations, and powers, and the teachers of the least are the more divine, and the guides-by-hand unto the divine leading, and illumination, [1005A] and communication. But I see that the angels first taught the divine mystery of the humanity of Christ, and then through them the grace of knowledge descended unto us [n: (Matth. I; Luc. I, II)]*. *Thus, therefore, the most divine Gabriel taught the mysteries to Zacharias, the high priest [n: (Luc. I)]*, *that is, that from him a prophet should come to be, against hope — a boy to be born divinely by divine grace — and, for the salutary manifestation to the world of the manly divine operation of Jesus. And Mary too, how in her there should be the divine mystery of the ineffable divine formation [n: (ibid.)]*. *But another of the angels was instructing Joseph, how the things divinely promised to the forefather David were truly to be fulfilled [n: (Matth. I; Luc. II)]*. *But another evangelized the shepherds, as those purged, as it were, by the return of the many and by silence, and with him a multitude of the heavenly host [1005B] was delivering to those who are on earth that greatly laudable doxology. And let us look also to the most excellent apparitions of the light of the words. For I see that Jesus himself, the superessential essence of the supercelestial essences, coming unchangeably unto that which is according to us, does not recoil from the human ordination ordained and assumed by himself, but, obedient, is made subject to the dispositions of the Father, and of God, through angels. And through those very mediators there is announced to Joseph the withdrawal of the Son to Egypt, disposed by the Father, and again the leading-across into Judaea out of Egypt [n: (Matth. II)]*. *And through angels we see him ordained under the paternal legislations [n: (Luc. XXII)]*. *For I press on to say (as to one who knows the things expressed in our priestly traditions) also concerning the angel strengthening Jesus [sic: Vesum] himself; or because Jesus himself too, coming through our [1005C] salutary, beneficent, and manifesting ordination, is called the Angel of great counsel. For indeed, as the angel himself said, whatsoever things he heard from the Father, he announced to us [n: (Joan. XV; Isai. IX)]*.



## EXPOSITION.

After he has shown — the general definition having been given and set forth — how the hierarchy is to be understood according to the angelic and human ordering (for the divine hierarchy, which is infinite, cannot be defined), he now begins to treat especially of the angelic hierarchy, first showing how the names of the angels, which have been given by men and after the manner of men, are to be understood in that spiritual and heavenly nature. He links what precedes to the narrative that follows, saying: « Therefore, what the hierarchy is, as [1005D] I think, the angelic hierarchy having been well defined by us, is next to be praised; and its wondrous fashionings of forms in the sacred words are to be beheld with supramundane eyes. » As if he should say: After we have, according to our estimation, well shown what the hierarchy is from the definition of it set forth, now it follows that we praise the angelic hierarchy — that is, that we show how greatly it has been praised in the Holy Scriptures, or that we demonstrate how praiseworthy it appears from the things that have been said about it. And to demonstrate this it is fitting that we behold with supramundane eyes, that is, with spiritual eyes and eyes that see spiritually, the wondrous fashionings, that is, the compositions or adaptations of forms, which in the sacred words are [1006A] attributed to it, in order to demonstrate its invisible nature. For the things that are set forth visibly are to be beheld with spiritual eyes for this reason: lest what is seen be thought to be the only thing that is, lest the mind remain in that which it looks upon outwardly. But through that which is set forth outwardly for demonstration, it is invited to contemplate that which is truer and more sublime.

Therefore there follows: « That we may ascend to the most deiform » — namely of the angels — « simplicity through the mystical formations. » Therefore, he says, « the wondrous fashionings of forms are to be beheld with supramundane eyes, so that through those very mystical formations » — considered outwardly — being roused, we may inwardly « ascend to the most deiform » « simplicity » of the angels themselves. For it is as though a certain multiplicity belongs to the angels [1006B], through which the truth of the mystical formations is demonstrated outwardly. But their simplicity is the unity of their spiritual and unvarying nature. Therefore in their multiplicity they do not have deiformity, but in their simplicity; because in that part in which they are shown through visible forms, they take on the likeness of corporeal nature. But in that part in which they themselves are understood as spiritual and as an incorporeal nature, they look toward the image and likeness of God. Which likeness is called not only deiform but even most deiform; because, although the very same is found in men too, yet in the angels it is believed to be more excellent.

There follows: « And at the same time we shall praise the principle of the knowledge of every hierarchy in divine religiousness, and [1006C] in most perfect acts of thanksgiving. » In this, he says, that we shall praise the angelic hierarchy, at the same time we shall also praise the divine and highest hierarchy, which is the principle of all the knowledge of the hierarchy — that is, the knowledge which every hierarchy has, and through which every hierarchy is ordered, because every hierarchy is both ordered through it and illumined by it. « We shall praise, I say, the principle of every hierarchy, » of knowledge — yet not by definition and demonstration as we praise the angelic hierarchy, nor by definition and comprehension as we praise the human, of which the one, invisible, is demonstrated through visible things, the other, visible, is known in itself. But we shall praise it with divine religiousness and most perfect acts of thanksgiving. [1006D] For that which is unthinkable is not praised by demonstration, nor is that which is incomprehensible praised by comprehension. Therefore God, who cannot be searched out and comprehended, can be praised by religion and thanksgiving alone. If, then, you cannot praise by speaking, praise by living. What the tongue does not unfold, a good life commends; he who is not searched out through knowledge is attained by good will. If, then, you do not comprehend him, by living according to him, reach toward him; this is to praise with divine religion. Again, if you cannot speak him, you can speak the things that have been given by him; to commend his best gifts, to proclaim him ineffably good — this is to praise with a most perfect act of thanksgiving. Thus, then, we shall praise our principle with divine religiousness [1007A] and most perfect acts of thanksgiving. But in the very praising of our principle, first of all this must be called to mind: how the divine goodness, which is poured out upon all things, first brought into being the things to be created; then established beneath itself the things to be governed, and reformed unto itself the things to be nourished. This is what he says: « First of all together, » that is, first before all things, one must « speak that true thing, » that is, speak that truth, « namely how, » that is in what manner, the superessential divinity « by its universal goodness brought into being the essences of the things that are; substituting, » after creation, namely disposing and ordering, or establishing beneath itself, and ordering according to itself. « For it is, » etc. As if he should say: For this reason the divine goodness recalls to itself and reforms the things which it created, [1007B] because it is proper to it, out of its innate kindness, to call to its own communion the things which have received being from it, so that they may be able to be blessed — namely, as far as each one, according to the order of its condition and its measure, can be capable of that participation. « For this, he says, is proper to the cause of all things, and to the goodness that is above all things, » that is, to the goodness which is the cause of all things, because through it all things were made, and is above all things, because it draws to itself the things made by it; this, namely, is proper to it: to call the things that are to its own communion — that is, as it is defined or dispensed to each one of the things that are according to its proper analogy, that is, its measure, and mode, and order. For in this very thing the beauty [1007C] of the universe is perfected: that not all things in one and the same way, but each single thing according to its own order and grade, are variously and manifoldly recalled to the communion of the divine goodness — so that, in that they are not forsaken, the work of goodness may be completed; but in that they are variously disposed unto the comeliness and beauty of all, the work of wisdom may be perfected. « All things, therefore, that are, participate in the providence flowing from the superessential and most causal divinity. » Since, he says, this is proper to the divine goodness — that it recall all things to participation in itself, so that, turned back to it, the things which proceeded from it in order to receive being may subsist — therefore « all things that are participate in providence, » that is, in the provided good and the provided grace flowing from [1007D] the divinity itself, as from a fountain and the first principle of all goodness, which is superessential, because in the excellence of its nature it is supereminent over all essences and subsisting natures; and is most causal, that is, the cause of all causes and the first cause, since from its goodness proceeds every good which it offers to be participated by all the things founded by it, that they may subsist. « For perhaps they would not be, except by the assumption of the essences of the things that are, and of their principle. » From this, he says, it can be proved that all things that are participate in divine providence, because they could not otherwise subsist, unless they subsisted by participation in the divine goodness itself, from which all things received being, and in which all things subsist. For all things that are, unless they had received their principle from the divine goodness, [1008A] would not have begun; and unless they had their essence in it, they would not remain in that which they are. Therefore it is the principle of all things, by assuming and participating in which they begin; and it is the essence of all things, by assuming and participating in which they subsist.

Whence there follows: « All existing things, therefore, participate in its being. » As if he should say: Because without it nothing can subsist, it is manifest that all things which subsist participate in its being. Therefore the divine nature, being one and the same in itself, as regards effect and power and operation, is, to all subsisting essences and natures created by it, both the principle from which they receive being, and the essence of all in which they subsist; and it is the life, not [1008B] of all indeed (because not all things live), but is the life of all living things, from which and through which they are made alive and live; and it is the wisdom, not of all existing or living things — because not all existing or living things are wise — but is the wisdom of all wise things; and it is the intellect and reason, from which and through which they are illumined and are wise and understand and discern.

This is what he says: « For the being of all things is the super-being of the divinity, » because through the being of the divinity, which is above all being, whatever is has being and subsists. « But living things, » which now receive from it not only being but also life, by participating in that same life which is above all life, and [1008C] by participating in that same life-giving power, are made alive and live. « But rational and intellectual things, » by participating in « that same » wisdom itself, existing « above all reason and intellect, perfect through itself and pre-perfect, » that is, by participating in the wisdom perfect above all things, reason and understand and are wise. They reason indeed by searching out, they understand by knowing, they are wise by participating — so that one and the same divine goodness is both essence to subsisting things, and life to living things, and wisdom to wise things. From which it is clear that those created things are nearer to the creating nature in likeness and in truth which have received more from it as a gift.

[1008D]This is what follows: « And it is clear that those of the essences are around it which have received innumerably from it. » Around it, namely the divine nature — that is, they are near and neighboring to it, and immediately joined to it, those of the essences, that is those essences or natures, whichever, after being itself was given, have received innumerably or manifoldly from it the gifts of powers. Among which in the first place are reckoned those heavenly and spiritual natures of the angels, which are rational not only through the subtlety of wisdom, because they discern by intellect; but are also intellectual through the subtlety of their spiritual nature, because they are perceived in their nature by intellect alone, and do not touch the sense of the body; and through this they have been made above all things that are, because they live; and above [1009A] all things that live irrationally, because they discern.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

This is what he says: « The holy dispositions, therefore, of the heavenly essences, » that is, the holy orders of the heavenly spirits, « have been made by participation in the tradition of the hierarchy, » that is, by participation in the grace which is handed on to the hierarchy ordered according to God, « above those things which only are, » because they live; « and above those things » which « are living irrationally, » because they discern; in comparison with which irrationally living things we men are called, and are, rational animals. Nor is it any wonder, he says, if the angelic spirits have been made superior in dignity not only to existing and non-living things, and to living and non-discerning things, but even to rational and corporeal things; because rational [1009B] corporeal things, that is, men, although they are reformed unto the divine likeness, nevertheless look toward it only when instructed and roused by the mediation of corporeal things. But the angelic sublimity, established above all corporeal things, invisibly and immediately, no other creature set between it and God, is conformed to the divine likeness more copiously, drawing grace from it more manifoldly, and contemplating it more nearly, as being close at hand. « Invisibly, » he says, that is, conforming themselves unto the divine imitation without material and corporeal instruments and visible signs — namely the dispositions themselves of the heavenly essences. « And unto the divine likeness, » through imitation and conformation, « supramundanely, » that is, spiritually, « looking, they have beautifully more copious [1009C] communions with it, » that is, they take more manifold perceptions of spiritual gifts from it, namely from the divine likeness, in which they have communion with it; because, by perceiving and sharing its good in those very spiritual gifts, they possess it in it and with it. Beautifully indeed, and purely, and without corporeal contagion and material contact; being simply taken up into it. « They are attending » indeed « unto all life, » that is, unto the highest life, in which is all life; which is wholly life, not living from elsewhere, but is itself life; and through this, perceiving all life from the highest life, so that, by the very fact that they receive the fullness of life immediately in themselves, in them life may never fail.

[1009D]There follows: « They themselves, therefore, are — firstly, and manifoldly, and always, unto the highest, as far as is lawful — intent upon the conformation of divine and inflexible love; and receiving the principal illuminations immaterially and purely, and ordered unto them, and having all intellectual life. » They themselves, he says, are intent — namely upon the highest good — firstly because immediately, and manifoldly because perfectly, and always because intent without intermission, « namely as far as is lawful, » that is, permitted or possible to a creature so placed, « in the conformation of divine and inflexible love, » that is, of a creature loving God inflexibly or immutably, and turning itself and reforming itself toward God through inflexible and divine love. « They themselves also are, » namely the heavenly powers, « receiving the principal [1010A] illuminations, » that is, those given firstly and principally — immaterially, without body, and purely, without contagion. Immaterially, without visible signification, and purely, without the contagion of error. « They are also ordered unto them, » namely unto the illuminations to be received, so that according to the difference of order there may be a difference of perception; or, ordered unto them, they are by justice subjecting themselves and fitting themselves to the divine will in all things, so that in this very thing they may perceive the illumination of grace without impediment, in that they do not disagree from its truth through desire of iniquity. « They are » also « having all intellectual life, » because the very thing that they are, they are life, and their spiritual substances have this very same thing — to live — that they have to be. Because, therefore, in them there is not one thing which gives life [1010B] and another which is given life, but the whole is one and the same; they are said to have all life, or to be all life, because the whole of what they are, they are life — just as the highest life itself also, in which they are and in which they live, is all life; because it lives of itself, and is life, and the whole of what it is, is life.

There follows: « They themselves, therefore, have been made — firstly, and manifoldly — in the participation of God, and are — firstly, and manifoldly — the manifesters of the divine hiddenness. » They themselves, he says, the heavenly dispositions, « have been made in the participation of God, » that is, so that they may participate in God and in the divine grace; firstly, because no creature is before them; « and manifoldly, » because no creature is above them. « Firstly, » because before all; « and manifoldly, » because more than all. And they are « manifesters of the divine hiddenness, » [1010C] that is, of the divine grace invisibly and secretly breathed into them, while that which they inwardly perceive from a hidden inspiration they afterward transfuse to others by manifesting it. Or « of the divine hiddenness, » that is, they are manifesters of the hidden and invisible divinity; because in them and through them the invisible things of God come forth unto manifestation, when the divine brightness both first shines in them and afterward, through them, illumines the subject orders of those provided for. Therefore, he says, « they have been made worthy, by selection, » that is, specially or singularly, « beyond all things, of the angelic appellation, in that they first put forth into themselves the divine illumination, and through themselves bring down to us the manifestations which are above us. » Therefore, because the divine light pours itself into [1010D] them in the first place, and afterward descends through them to illumine us, they have been made worthy, beyond all things, of the angelic appellation. For 'angel' is interpreted *messenger*. Those, therefore, who bring down the grace they have received by ministering it to others, what else are they than messengers of the author and bestower of that same grace? But there are messengers — some prior, some posterior. For the angels, who, perceiving the divine grace in the first place, afterward bring it down to the knowledge of men, are, as it were, the first messengers of that same grace. But men themselves, when they bring down the grace they have received to others by preaching and announcing it, are indeed called messengers, but are not the first messengers, because they have perceived what they announce from others first illumined and first announcing. Therefore those heavenly and invisible [1011A] spirits are named angels both by a singular dignity and by a proper appellation; because to them first, through hidden inspiration, is manifested that which they behold in themselves of the invisible light of the divinity, and through them in the first place is transfused to us that which they themselves carry to be manifested to our knowledge. For in the first place, as though giving birth out of the hidden womb of divine conception, they put forth into themselves the divine illumination — not by drawing it from without, but by conceiving from within the light of eternal brightness — so that there may come forth from within, to themselves, that which they see in themselves, and that they may transfuse to us through themselves. For thus, by first perceiving the divine illuminations in themselves through themselves, they afterward bring down to us the manifestations of those very illuminations, which are above us. For « thus indeed the manifestations, » namely the divine ones, « have been given [1011B] to us through the angels, as theology, » that is, Holy Scripture, « testifies. And the angels led back also our glorious fathers, both before the law and after the law, unto the divine, introducing that which was to be done, and leading them back from error and unclean life unto the right way of truth; either revealing the sacred orders of the supramundane mysteries, or the hidden visions, or certain divine fore-preachings prophetically. » Thus, he says, as theology, that is, Holy Scripture, testifies and manifests, the divine manifestations have been given to us through the angels; because both before the law, namely before it was given, and after the law was given, that is, under law and under grace, the angels led back our glorious fathers, that is, the elect and just, by imitating whose life we are sons, unto the divine, that is, [1011C] unto the divine knowledge: introducing, namely, that which was to be done, that is, leading them to live justly and piously, and leading them back from error and unclean life unto the right way of truth, that is, teaching them to believe rightly, so that both their faith might be right and their life clean. Or also revealing the sacred orders of the supramundane mysteries, that is, of the heavenly secrets — namely concerning the blessed life, and the joys of the heavenly fatherland, and the invisible ordination of the blessed spirits: how all are happy in the one good, yet not co-equal; and how the superior are set above without pride, the inferior subjected without misery; or revealing the sacred orders of the supramundane mysteries, that is, of the divine secrets: namely how it must be fulfilled visibly [1011D] and temporally by men, which God disposed to be done by an invisible and secret dispensation; or also revealing certain hidden visions, signifying something concerning things past, or present, or future; or revealing certain divine fore-preachings made prophetically, that is, divine preachings in which something, before it came to pass, was prophetically foretold — ministering, namely, to those who spoke them the knowledge of things to come, or to those who heard them the understanding of what was said, and in all these things, by instructing holy men either unto right faith or unto good working, leading them back unto divine knowledge and participation.

There follows: « But if anyone should say that there have also been theophanies to certain persons immediately [1012A] from thence, let him learn this too wisely from the most sacred words: how this indeed is a certain thing: No one has seen, nor will see, the hidden thing of God. » If anyone, he says, against what I have said — that divine revelations come to the knowledge of men through the angels — should say that sometimes, without the angels mediating, certain of the holy Fathers received revelations from the divinity itself, let him learn from the most sacred words this: that the hidden thing of God itself no one has seen, nor will see. If, then, anyone — that is, some person — should say that from thence, that is, from the divine secret, theophanies were made to certain of the holy ones, that is, divine apparitions immediately, that is, descending into them in the first place with no other creature mediating; if anyone, I say, should say this, let him [1012B] also learn to understand this wisely from the most sacred words: how « no one » ever « has seen, nor will see, the hidden thing of God, » that is, the divine nature, which is hidden and removed from every sense. He has seen, I say — namely what this very hidden thing is; that is: even if he has seen it in a figure, he has not seen it in essence; he has not seen it in its proper form, even if he has seen it in signification. And when he has come to know this from the most sacred words, let him understand that the human mind cannot by itself approach immediately unto the contemplation of the inaccessible light, unless, roused by theophanies, that is, by divine apparitions, it be lifted up. « But theophanies have been made to the holy ones [but] according to what befits God: and through certain sacred manifestations of proportional visions to those who see. » Theophanies, he says, that [1012C] is, divine revelations, or revelations of the divinity, have been made to the holy ones — yet not in this manner, that the human mind, placed in mortal flesh and established within human sense, should approach immediately to touch the incomprehensible nature; but they were made according to certain sacred manifestations, whether of visible forms applied outwardly to the sense, or of imaginations brought inwardly into the mind according to visible things, or impressed spiritually upon human reason in some other, higher and more excellent way whatsoever — fitted with such suitability that they both befitted God himself, concerning whom they were made, and were congruous to those very seers to whom they were made, according to the proportion and capacity of each.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

[1012D]This is what he says: To the holy ones there have been made theophanies according to certain sacred manifestations befitting God, and through manifestations of proportional visions to those who see. Which, namely, were so proportionally tempered to the capacity of those seeing, that the more imperfect indeed perceived the truth of heavenly things by an inferior and more imperfect kind of vision; but the more excellent came to know the divine secrets in a more sublime manner. And yet, whether in these or in those, whatever could come from God unto human knowledge was less than himself, and in a certain manner beneath himself. For hence it is written: « The things that were under him filled the temple [n: (Isai. VI)]: » because everything that the human mind in this life can grasp of the knowledge [1013A] of him cannot be made equal to his ineffable majesty. Nevertheless Holy Scripture has been accustomed to call those manifestations, by which God reveals himself to human minds, [sic: *theopbanias*], that is, divine apparitions; because, although that which is discerned is not God by nature, according to demonstration it is, since through it and in it God is manifested.

This is what follows: « That most wise theology, therefore, revealed that vision which is described in it to be a divine one, and to be beautifully called a theophany, as a likeness of formless things in a form, from the leading-back of those who see unto the divine. » « That most wise theology itself, he says, » that is, Holy Scripture, which wisely brings forth the divine secrets unto human knowledge, « revealed that that vision which is described in it is beautifully, » that is, fittingly, called [1013B] a theophany — inasmuch as it is « a divine likeness of formless things, » that is, of spiritual natures, and of natures not having corporeal form, expressed in a visible form, or taken according to visible things. It revealed, I say, that it is beautifully called a theophany from the leading-back of those who see unto divine things — that is, because through it those who see are led back to knowing divine things, as though, through it, divine things were made illumination to those who see, and indeed the divine things themselves holily perfecting them; that is, not only because it manifested divine things to those who see, but because it also made the seers themselves divine.

There follows: « But these divine visions our glorious fathers accomplished, through the mediating heavenly powers, » [1013C] that is, by the heavenly powers mediating, through which they were brought down to the knowledge of men: our glorious fathers attained, and obtained, and perfectly comprehended those visions.

There follows: « Does not the tradition of the words also say that the sacred legislation was given to Moses by God, as it were through himself, so that it may also truly teach us that they are divine and sacred characters? » Does not, he says, « the tradition of the words, » that is, the authority of the Scriptures, « say that the sacred legislation, » that is, the giving of the sacred law, « was given to Moses by God through himself, » namely commemorating no mediator in the giving of the law between God and Moses. But testifying that God himself, through himself, spoke to Moses: so that it may also teach us that it, namely the law, are sacred and divine [1013D] characters, that is, signs of sacred and divine things, so that through the things which were done visibly in the legislation it may show certain other invisible things signified and demonstrated, as it is written: « Thou shalt therefore make the tabernacle according to the pattern which was shown thee in the mount [n: (Exod. XXVI)]. » According to this manner, therefore, the visible manifestation itself and the material address, by which the invisible God willed to show himself to men, is said to be God, or to be of God, because in it the invisible God came forth unto manifestation, and brought forth to knowledge the things of his own which were hidden. In which visible manifestation, nevertheless, he worked all things by the mediating ministry of the angels, so that that which was made visible might be said to be both of God, by the first [1014A] authority, and of the angels, by the subordinate operation.

For hence it is that there follows: « But theology also teaches this wisely: that it came unto us through the angels. » Not only, he says, does theology testify that the legislation was given to Moses by God immediately, but it also teaches that it, namely the law, came unto us — that is, was set forth to our knowledge through the angels — so that it may be openly demonstrated that that legislation was both of God, from whose authority it proceeded, and of the angels, whose ministry too it employed. « As though, he says, the divine legal order itself was setting that down legally, that is, that through the first things the second are led back unto the divine. » Thus, he says, Scripture shows the legislation to have been ordained by God through the angels among men, as though [1014B] the legal order itself, divinely made, established that legally, that is, instituted or sanctioned it — namely, that the second things are led back unto the divine through the first. For when the divine law is declared to have proceeded first from God unto the angels, and then through the angels unto men, it is manifestly shown that through the first and superior things, the second and inferior things are led back unto the divine knowledge: and this must be understood not only between God and the angels, or between the angels and men, but also among the angels themselves, since the superior lead back the inferior unto the divine knowledge. Since among the angels themselves too there are orders, some superior, some inferior; and those who are superior draw more copiously the light of the divinity, and transfuse it to those who follow after them, to be illumined. And in those very orders too, [1014C] in which, according to an equal disposition, many are equal, this definition of the law is kept: that in the perception of divine grace some are first, some second, some last; and even those who are equal in order are not equal in the perception of grace. This is what he says: « For indeed not only among the placed-above and among the subject minds, that is, spirits, but even among the equally powerful, the very law is defined from the superessential principle of the ordering of all things, that is, » namely this, « that through each hierarchy, » that is, sacred ordination, « there are both first, and middle, and last ordinations and powers: » and that in those very ordinations and powers, the more divine are always the teachers « and hand-leaders » of divine things, [1014D] so that they may teach them and lead them « into the divine leading, and illumination, and communication. Into the leading » by conversion, « into illumination » by knowledge, « into communication » by perception.

There follows: « But I see that the angels first taught the divine mystery of Christ's humanity. Then through them the grace of that knowledge descended unto us. » From this, he says, it is clear that the things which are commanded by God to men are announced through the angels, since the angels taught even the divine mystery of Christ's humanity itself — both those who foretold and believed, and those who saw and received. And then, that is, consequently, through those very angels the grace of this knowledge descended unto us. Thus, therefore, just as [1015A] Christ's nativity was announced through the angels, so too the nativity of his forerunner is read to have been announced through the angel Gabriel. « Thus, he says, the most divine Gabriel taught Zachariah, the high priest, the mysteries, that is, the boy to be born of him against hope, » because of a barren mother and an aged father: by divine grace « that he would be a Prophet, to manifest to the world the manly divine operation of Jesus, divinely and salvifically. » As if he should say: The most divine Gabriel taught Zachariah the high priest that the boy to be born of him would be a prophet of the operation of Jesus — a manly operation, because it was displayed in the nature of humanity, in which he took on the sex of flesh; and a divine operation, because it was accomplished through the power of the divinity, in which also he founded his own flesh. An operation, I say, to be manifested to the world through him [1015B] divinely and salvifically, that is, one to come forth divinely unto the salvation of the world. For the same most divine Gabriel taught « Mary how, » namely, « in her the divine mystery of the ineffable divine formation was to be completed. » Namely, that the divine operation, ineffably, above the use and law of nature, would take flesh from the flesh of the Virgin herself without the admixture of male seed, and would wondrously form it as the garment of the eternal Word. « But another of the angels was instructing Joseph, how the things divinely promised to his forefather David were truly fulfilled. » That is, how those things were fulfilled which had been promised divinely, that is, by God, to his forefather David.

« But another evangelized the shepherds, as those purged by the withdrawal of the many and by silence: and with him the multitude [1015C] of the heavenly host were delivering to those who are on earth that most praiseworthy doxology. Another angel again, he says, evangelized the shepherds, as those purged and made pure by the withdrawal of the many, » that is, by the separation or segregation from the multitude and the tumult and the din; and purged also by silence, that is, by quiet of mind and inner peace, so that in this very thing they might become worthy of the Gospel and of the evangelic address, in that, returning apart from the multitude unto themselves, established in silence and quiet of mind, they had shown themselves capable of the spiritual announcement. Whence, the grace gradually growing, after the evangelization of the one, the multitude of the heavenly host is heard, who were delivering that most praiseworthy doxology, that is, hymn of glory, to those who are on earth, namely to men of good will.

[1015D]There follows: « And let me look also to the most excellent apparitions of the light of the words. » As if he should say: Not only in the things which were announced to men through the angels concerning the incarnation of the Word does it appear that the knowledge of divine things descends to men by the mediation and ministry of the angels themselves; but the same can also be seen in the very person of the incarnate Word: who, although by the majesty of his divinity he commanded the angels, nevertheless willed that the things which had to be done temporally about his humanity should be dispensed through the angels: in this very thing deigning to take up the condition of humanity, in that, lacking all necessity, by dispensation alone, for an example of humanity and a lesson of truth, [1016A] he inclined himself to the angelic dispositions. « I shall look, he says, also to the most excellent apparitions of the light of the words, » that is, to the apparitions of Jesus Christ himself, namely when Jesus himself, who is the true light, appeared visibly in the flesh he had assumed. Which apparitions of the light are the most excellent, beyond all the other apparitions [sic: *eloquiorom*], that is, beyond all the other apparitions which are reported in the sacred words; because never before did God manifest himself to the world so excellently as when he himself appeared visibly in the flesh he had assumed. To these apparitions of the light, therefore, he says, the most lofty above all the apparitions of the words, I shall look, so that in them too I may prove this: that the divine secrets are dispensed to men through the angels. For if, about that [1016B] man who is above men, the angelic dispensation was not repudiated, how much more is it proved to be necessary in those who are subject to sin and established in the darkness of ignorance. In that man, therefore, who is the head of men, we can recognize what we ought to think about other men, his members.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

« For I see, he says, that Jesus himself too, coming immutably — in the superessential essence of the supercelestial essences — unto that which is according to us, does not recoil from the human ordination ordained and assumed by himself. » Jesus himself, he says, who according to his divinity, by which all things subsist, is also the essence of the supercelestial essences, that is, of the angelic spirits, who transcend not only earthly things by purity, but also [1016C] the heavenly things by subtlety; and not only their essence, because in him they subsist, but the superessential essence, because they do not attain to his equality. Jesus himself, whose ineffable majesty and power of divinity both bears up the angels themselves through grace and transcends them through nature, coming unto that which is according to us, that is, unto the taking-on of flesh, which was of our nature and was like unto us; and coming immutably, that is, without diminution or change of his divinity, does not recoil from the human ordination, that is, from the ordination or law owed to humanity. An ordination, I say, first ordained by him, when he earlier instructed man; afterward assumed by him, when he took on the form and nature of humanity. But if he had assumed the human [1016D] nature and yet spurned the human condition, he would recoil from the human ordination. For it would be, as it were, a recoiling for him to be unwilling to endure by condition that which he had assumed by nature. But now, coming unto the human nature through the assumption of flesh, he does not recoil from the human ordination, by the keeping of humility. « But, obedient, he is made subject to the dispositions of God the Father through the angels, » administered about his humanity: « and through those very » angels as intermediaries, that is, by the mediation of the angels themselves, « there is announced to Joseph, » as to the guardian of his infancy, « the withdrawal » of the « Son » of God himself « into Egypt, » disposed by God the Father, « and again the leading-across, or leading-back, from Egypt into Judea. » « But we see him too, he says, [1017A] ordained through the angels under the paternal legislations. » We see him ordained through the angels, that is, disposed, « under the paternal legislations, » that is, under the legal commandments, which commandments those whom he had as fathers and forefathers according to the flesh had received to be observed. Under these legal commandments, therefore, he was ordained through the angels, when, according to the precepts of the paternal law, he was both circumcised on the eighth day, and on the fortieth day presented in the temple with gifts and victims — the Lord of the angels observing the law given through the angels.

There follows: « For I am urgent to say, as to you who know the things expressed in our priestly traditions, also concerning the angel comforting Jesus himself. » It is a small thing, he says, that I say, that Jesus in his humanity was ordained by the angels under the paternal [1017B] laws: he who, in the same humanity, drawing near to the passion, was even comforted by an angel. I am urgent to say what perhaps, if it had not been said before, would not be credible at all. I am urgent to say, also concerning the angel comforting Jesus himself. I am urgent to say what I myself too cannot utter without wonder — what I certainly believe and pursue: which, if I were about to say it to one who did not know, I would not presume to say at all. I am urgent to say what is so great that it cannot be comprehended by the little in knowledge; so wondrous that it cannot be believed by the little in faith — concerning the angel comforting him. I am urgent to say what, if it were thought to have been said by me, would not be believed. But to you, who know the things which are expressed, that is, manifestly set forth by our priestly traditions, that is, by the Scriptures, [1017C] which were handed on to us by priestly men who minister sacred things and are sanctified, to you I boldly am urgent to say the truth, hesitating nothing about your faith, which is firm and admits no scandal, having discernment of what was done, what was said. To you, therefore, O Timothy, son, as to one who knows the things expressed in our priestly traditions, to you confidently « I am urgent to say, also concerning the angel comforting Jesus, » so that in this it may become plain without doubt how greatly the humanity [1018A] was subjected, according to the divine ordination, to the angelic dispensation — the humanity which, in the man, at the time of the passion, willed to be comforted by an angel; yet not seeking aid, but forming an example; not having necessity, and nevertheless showing the condition. Therefore, then, « I am urgent » to « say to you, also concerning the angel comforting Jesus himself. » Or also I « am urgent » to « say this to you: that Jesus himself, coming through our salvific, beneficent, and manifestative ordination, is called the angel of great counsel. » This is that Jesus himself is called « the angel of great counsel, coming, » that is, in that he came to us, through our ordination, that is, according to what he had ordained, or what had been ordained — to come in our nature: which ordination was salvific, and beneficent, and manifestative. [1018B] Salvific in redemption, beneficent in the bestowal of grace, manifestative in glorification. Salvific, because he redeemed from death; beneficent, because to the redeemed he conferred the gifts of graces unto justification; manifestative, because he first set forth God, in his humanity, to be seen visibly by those to be justified, and afterward leads the justified, through the vision of his humanity, unto the contemplation of the divinity. According to this « manifestative ordination, therefore, Jesus, coming, is called the angel of great counsel. » Because, « as the angel himself says, whatsoever he heard from the Father, he announced to us. » If, then, an angel is called a messenger, deservedly the Savior himself too is called a messenger. Who announces to us the will of God the Father, and reveals the inward goods to our minds by breathing them in through his Spirit. In [1018C] which the angelic dignity is also manifestly shown: that the Savior himself too, bringing down to us the commands of the Father, is surnamed an angel. For since the sum of our salvation through redemption is accomplished not without the angelic appellation, it is clear that the rest too, which pertain to that same salvation, are dispensed by angelic administration. And these things indeed have been said concerning the angelic appellation.

## TITLE OF CHAPTER V *Why all the heavenly essences are commonly called angels, and specially have their own proper appellations, except those of the first and second orders.*



## THE TEXT.

[1017D]

*This indeed is, as far as concerns us, the cause of the angelic surname in the oracles. But it is fitting, as I think, to search out for what cause the theologians call all the heavenly essences at once angels, yet, coming to the manifestation of the supramundane dispositions themselves, they specially name the angelic order, which completively terminates the divine and heavenly things; but before it they set in order, placed above, the archangelic adornments, and the princes, and the powers, and the virtues, and whatever essences the manifestative traditions of the oracles know to be established above these. But we say that, throughout every holy disposition, the excelling orders have the illuminations and virtues of the inferior dispositions, but they that do not participate [1018D] in the things that excel them are last. Therefore the theologians call the most holy orders of the most excellent essences angels; for these too are manifesters of the divine illumination [n: (Hebr. I)]*. *But the lowest order of the heavenly minds have no ground for being named principalities, or thrones, or seraphim. For it is not in the participation of the most lofty virtues. But just as he himself leads back our divine high priests to the brightnesses of the divinity known to him, so also the wholly sacred virtues of the essences [still unclean] altogether lead back that which is before it, and they are ordered unto the divine, unto the disposition that consummates the angelic hierarchies. Unless indeed someone should say this too: that all the angelic namings are common, according to the subjection of all the heavenly virtues unto the deiform and God-given light, and their supereminent [1019A] communication. But that the matter may be the more discerned by us, we shall consider the holy and comely properties of each heavenly disposition, expressed in the sacred oracles.*



## EXPOSITION.

« This indeed is, » etc. In this fifth chapter it is inquired why all the heavenly essences are surnamed angels. But this question seems to have arisen from this: that in the sacred word certain of the heavenly spirits are declared [sic: *sempe*] to stand by God and always to see his face; but certain others are said to be sent forth outward, to fulfill exterior ministries. For hence it seems to follow that those who always stand by the divine countenance do not go forth to announce exterior things; but those [1019B] who are sent forth do not stand by the divine sight immovably, or immutably. For it is written in the book of the prophet Daniel: « Thousands of thousands ministered to him, and ten thousand times a hundred thousand stood by him [n: (Dan. VII)]. » In which distinction, by those who stand by seem to be signified those who do not go forth to exterior things; but by those who minister, those who, by going forth to exterior things, do not always stand by. On which account, because the inferior and lowest orders together go forth to exterior things, while only the superior and more excellent stand by the divine contemplation without intermission, those who stand by are declared to be fewer, but those who minister more. For since there are nine orders of angels, only two, that is, angels and archangels — inasmuch as they specially, from the distribution of their office, [1019C] are appointed to be sent — have received their surname from the very thing. Angels, namely, are called messengers, but archangels chief messengers; since the former announce lesser things, the latter greater, as it were from the dignity of their ministry. But because again, in Holy Scripture, we read that certain ones of the superior orders were sent — as in Isaiah one of the seraphim is recorded to have flown to the prophet and to have touched his lips with a coal which he had taken with tongs from the altar [n: (Isa. VI)]; and in the Epistle to the Hebrews the Apostle testifies that all are ministering spirits, and sent into ministry, for the sake of those who receive the inheritance of salvation [n: (Hebr. I)]: a great difficulty of ambiguity arises for us, and what is rather to be asserted here is not easily found. The author solves this question in this manner.
