He testifies that the name of angels belongs [1019D] only to the inferior orders, and to those which, from their office, are properly appointed to announce exterior things. But because the divine secrets, which by those inferior ones are brought down outwardly to the knowledge of men, are announced to those same ones by the superior: the name of angels too is communicable to the superior: who, although they do not go forth to announce exterior things, nevertheless — themselves receiving from above the things which are to be announced outwardly, according to the law of the divine ordination, and by announcing carrying them across to the inferior orders that follow after them — and the superior participate in every virtue, and grace, and property of the inferior: and therefore the surnames of the inferior also pass over to the superior; but the virtue, and grace, and [1020A] property of the superior are not all communicable to the inferior, and therefore their surnames too, by a like reasoning, cannot universally be brought down to the inferior. Yet sometimes the inferior, when they take up the property of the superior from the quality of an office, assume their name too in the execution of that same office. Hence is that which we commemorated above, that the angel who had come to kindle and purge the lips of the prophet is called a seraph, because in the quality of this work he was carrying out the property of the one who kindles or inflames. But that which the Apostle says, that all are ministering spirits and sent into ministry, they think is to be understood in this way: that, as we said above, those too are not unfittingly said to be sent; [1020B] because, although they do not go forth to exterior things, nevertheless they bring the things which are to be announced outwardly, by announcing them to the inferior and following orders. Others think that all the heavenly orders, both superior and inferior, are directed to exterior things according to time, and place, and cause; yet that those which have this as proper from their office are specially surnamed angels or archangels. For that all are sometimes sent, the Apostle seems to assert by testimony, in that which we commemorated above, saying: « Are they not all ministering spirits, sent into ministry, for the sake of those who receive the inheritance of salvation? » But that even certain superior ones are sometimes directed to exterior things not from office, but from an accidental cause, the Psalmist seems to assert, when he says: « Who [1020C] makes his angels spirits, and his ministers a burning fire [n: (Psal. CIII)]." Thus, then, there are some who stand by from office; and others who go forth to fulfill exterior things; and yet those who stand by, being sometimes sent into ministry, while they take up the property of the inferior, assume their name too in signification. Who, nevertheless, when they go forth to exterior things, do not withdraw from the interior contemplation, because they behold him who is present to them wherever they go. And in this way indeed, before we should examine the words of the book, we thought that mention of a question of this kind should be made briefly, for the clearness of the things to be said — defining nothing rashly, but venerating the secret, of which perhaps to have been ignorant is a venial infirmity, [1020D] but to assert presumptuously is a damnable rashness. This, however, must be known: that the author seems rather to lend his assent to that part which holds that only certain, not all, of the heavenly orders are sent to exterior things; and yet that, on account of the participation of the same property, by which the superior share the grace and virtue of the inferior, the names of the inferior too are assumed by the superior. Just as also sometimes the inferior, when they take up the property of the superior from the quality of an office, assume their name too in signification. Now to the text: « This indeed is, as far as concerns us, the cause of the angelic surname in the words. » « This, he says, is » — which we have said above — « the cause of the angelic surname; » namely because the divine secrets are announced to us through them, [1021A] and therefore, because they bring down to us the divine precepts by announcing them, as far as concerns us — that is, as far as regards that, or on account of that, which they work toward us — they assume the name of angels. But because this — that is, carry ye the messages of the interior teacher outward to the knowledge of men — whether it befits all is not yet proved by manifest reason or authority, it must now be inquired, he says, why in the sacred word he attributes the name of angels commonly to all the heavenly virtues.

This is what he says: « It is fitting, as I think, to search out for what cause the theologians call all the heavenly essences at once angels; but, uniting unto the manifestation, they specially name the angelic order of the supramundane dispositions themselves, [1021B] which completively terminates the divine and heavenly things; » as if he should say: This now remains to be inquired: why the theologians universally name all those heavenly natures angels, when nevertheless they specially name one order of them — namely of the spirits who come to us to manifest the supramundane dispositions themselves, that is, the spiritual, and divine, and invisible ordinations — the angelic order; « completively terminating the divine and heavenly things, » that is, leading through, unto completion and end, the things divinely disposed in a heavenly and secret ordination. For the divine precept, running down from the highest, is brought through the superior orders to the inferior, until at last, coming to the knowledge of men, it is completed in work: on which account [1021C] that heavenly order too, to which the command of the divinity descends last of all through the powers placed above, and then through it is announced outwardly, is said to terminate it completively, or unto completion, because through its announcement it is brought to that place where it is last to be completed by visible operation. This, therefore, must be inquired: why, namely, although this last order is specially marked with the angelic surname, all the orders of the heavenly essences are named angels. « But before it, those placed above set in order the archangelic adornments, and the princes, and the powers, and the virtues, and whatever essences established above these the manifestative traditions of the words know. » As if he should say: The theologians indeed establish the angelic order itself [1021D] as the last and latest, and nearest to human things, and as carrying forth the divine visions by a final announcement, and determining them; but before it they set in order those placed above, that is, they describe those placed above in the order. « The archangelic adornments, » that is, the choirs of the archangels, ordered and beautifully disposed, who in order are both superior to the angels themselves in dignity, and prior in announcement. Then the theologians themselves also establish — not only above the angels, but even above the archangels — the princes, that is, the principalities, and the powers, and the virtues, and other essences, that is, « whatever spiritual natures the manifestative traditions of the sacred words know, » that is, set forth to knowledge. Essences, I say, established above these, namely [1022A] the angels, or whatever others established above all these aforesaid, so that they are not only more sublime in dignity, but also stronger in firmness of perfection, and more stable unto eternity and immutability.

There follows: « But we say that, throughout every holy disposition, the excelling orders have the illuminations and virtues of the inferior dispositions; but they that do not participate in the things that excel them are last. » As if he should say: Since in that disposition of the heavenly spirits some are superior, others inferior, the superior indeed have every illumination and virtue of the inferior; but the inferior do not have all the illuminations and virtues of the superior orders. And therefore the names of the inferior [1022B] are sometimes transferred to the superior orders, inasmuch as these participate with them in the same properties of names. But the names of the superior cannot be so assumed by the inferior, since the properties of the names do not universally pass over to them: this is what he says: « But we say that, throughout every holy disposition » — namely of the heavenly spirits — « the excelling, » or superior, « orders have » all the illuminations and virtues « of the inferior dispositions, » that is, of the subject orders. But the last, that is, the inferior, and those following in order, do not participate — "in all," it is understood, the illuminations and virtues of those that excel them, that is, of those who are more excellent than they. We understand the illuminations [1022C] as being in the knowledge of truth; but the virtues as being in the love of goodness and the perfection of work.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « Therefore the theologians call the most holy orders of the most excellent essences angels. For they too are manifesters of the divine illumination. » Since he says that the higher orders have all the properties of the lower ones: therefore the theologians call the most holy orders of the most excellent essences — that is, of the most excellent spirits — angels; because they too partake of the angelic property, in this respect, that although they do not go out to announce outward things, nevertheless to those orders which follow after them they manifest, by transfusing and as it were by announcing, the divine illumination which they receive concerning themselves. « But the lowest order of the heavenly minds does not have [1022D] the ground for being named archas, or thrones, or seraphim. » — « They do not have, he says, the ground, » those same theologians, « for naming the lowest order of the heavenly minds, » that is, the last order of the heavenly spirits, namely the angels, « archas, » that is princes, or thrones, or seraphim, because that lowest order of the angels is not in participation of the most exalted powers. For this reason the angels cannot be named princes, or thrones, or seraphim, as princes and thrones and seraphim are named angels; because the higher ones partake of the angelic property universally, whereas the angels do not partake universally of the illuminations and powers of the higher ones; and those very ones especially, for which the higher ones received their special surnames, the angels possess only in part [1023A], and yet they do not have the dignity and the property of that surname. For not everyone who has some power or property by partaking of it ought at once to be surnamed according to that property, unless he holds it either singularly among the rest or excellently above the rest. For not everyone who knows something is at once called wise; nor is he who has done something right forthwith called just. But he alone who holds wisdom and justice either singularly or excellently ought to be called wise and just by a proper and express surname. So then those heavenly orders of the holy spirits have proper surnames, in which is signified not what they have received singularly, but what they possess excellently. For the seraphim [1023B] — because out of love of their Creator, as neighbors and those nearest, they both are burning in themselves and set others alight from themselves — are interpreted *burning* or *kindling*, not because they alone among the rest have this singularly, but because with the rest, and above the rest, excellently. For all burn with the love of God, and yet they especially ought to be called burning, who both first conceive the fire of that love and, burning more strongly, send out the flame of love to set the rest also alight. So too the cherubim (which name is interpreted *fullness of knowledge*) — because they have a greater knowledge of God than the rest — take their name from that alone which, possessing it with the rest, they merited to perceive more excellently than all the rest. The thrones too are so called, not because in them alone does God sit and discern [1023C] his judgments, but because they received this in their office more excellently than the rest, and on that account they draw their surname specially from it. And in this manner indeed the other orders too must be understood, namely, that each is believed to receive its proper surnames from that which it is proved to possess more excellently by the gift of grace.

But there arises a question not to be despised, and one which would bring great confusion to the mind if it were not examined by applying reason. For if, as has been said, in that heavenly arrangement each and every order draws its own surnames from that property in which they are proved to be more excellent than the rest, since the higher ones possess universally and excellently all the illuminations and [1023D] powers of the subject orders, nothing singular is left to the orders placed beneath and following, from which they might obtain a surname proper to them and distinct among the rest. Whence it behooves us to consider more diligently in what way both are at once true: namely, that the higher ones possess universally and excellently the illuminations and gifts of the subject orders, and yet that each and every one retains something proper and special from which it can be marked by a proper and distinct appellation. For the seraphim by their name express a singular love. The cherubim, however, intimate a more excellent knowledge. But the thrones show a greater force of discernment. Yet it is established that those who love more ardently look more deeply, and discern more subtly. [1024A] For those who look from nearer at hand undoubtedly recognize more evidently. How then is the order of the cherubim said to be surnamed from the privilege of a singular grace, if another retains this more excellently in its office, from which the cherubim obtains its appellation? But this question is perhaps not unfittingly solved by this reasoning: yet only so, if the secret of truth be violated by no rashness of prejudgment. For it can come to pass that, although the higher orders possess more excellently all the gifts of the powers, nevertheless they draw their surname only from those which among the gifts of the powers themselves are the more sublime; and they leave the others, which follow in the order of dignity, to those following after them for their denomination, so that the first may obtain its name from the first, the second from the second, the third from the third. For we know, [1024B] Scripture bearing witness, that among all the gifts of the powers charity excels; wherefore it was consequent that that order which is the most eminent of all should take its singular appellation, in its own distinction, from charity alone, although it might possess the other gifts of the powers too more excellently, from which it could have taken its appellation and name. But because the knowledge of truth is recognized as next in dignity after the love of virtue: therefore from that same dignity those who are second are, after the first, deservedly surnamed angelic spirits. But the judgment of discerning truth, because it seems to have a verdict still as it were doubtful, and appears in a certain manner suspended by a less perfect knowledge; and therefore because it, after full contemplation, in which truth is not sought but [1024C] possessed, looks back to that same knowledge of the highest truth, gave its name to the third order after the two former: this, however, must be understood according to man, for whose sake the names were given to those spirits themselves. For there judgment is not the settling of ambiguity, but the discernment of truth; nor there, where all things are manifest, is something that lies hidden discussed, but what is certain is esteemed according to its merit. Wherefore in this judgment too we hold that the knowledge of truth is to be preferred, since wisdom judges simplicity and unity; but judgment proves itself, through the force of discernment, to look toward diverse and contrary things. Whence the more excellent surname was to be taken from wisdom itself, by which the more excellent order was to be designated [1024D]: which order, although, as more sublime and more perfect, it possessed both wisdom and judgment more fully, yet, taking its name from wisdom alone, left judgment as the surname of the order following after it. According to this consideration, therefore, whoever should wish to interpret the surnames of the angels, there will perhaps be no unfittingness, if the higher orders possess more perfectly all the gifts of the powers, and yet the lower orders have their proper surnames from certain gifts specially. But concerning the orders of the angels themselves, in the first and the last at least, the same opinion of all is established. For that the seraphim are placed in the highest place, and after them the cherubim, and then the thrones, can be unknown to no one who knows the testimonies of the holy Scriptures. [1025A] It is manifest too that, for the lower ones ascending from the bottom upward, the angels and archangels are placed first. The four following orders certain men arrange in this manner, that from the thrones downward they set first the dominations, then the principalities, then the powers, then the virtues, so that in this manner the nine orders are distinguished by three triads: of which the first and highest contains the seraphim, cherubim, thrones; the second and middle comprises the dominations, principalities, and powers; the third and lowest arranges together the virtues, archangels, and angels. And so from the bottom upward the angels are numbered first; then the archangels, then the virtues, then the powers, then the principalities, then the dominations, then the thrones, then the cherubim, then the seraphim. But the Theologian [1025B] places first the angels, then the archangels, then the principalities, and these he assigns to the first triad. But in the second he sets first the powers, then the virtues, then the dominations. And in the third he judges that first the thrones, afterward the cherubim, afterward the seraphim are to be placed, the progression being made from the lower to the higher. But in this threefold triple distinction it especially behooves us to consider this: that the highest three orders, that is, the seraphim, and the cherubim, and the thrones, by the power of their singular excellence and the force of their denomination, seem to look only toward the interior. For to love and to know and to judge is proper to those presiding within, and having their conversion toward the interior. But the last three, and lowest orders, by the property of their surname [1025C], are proved to be arranged toward outward things only according to the office of their ministry; whether the angels and archangels, in that they announce outwardly whatever is to be done and manifested to men; or the principalities, in that they dispense by invisible power whatever things are to be administered and arranged concerning men. But the middle orders, as in arrangement, so also in office, seem to be borne between the invisible and the visible things; and to administer, according to their dignity and office, the things which are to be brought down from the higher ones to the lower. And among these the dominations are first, which by a singular excellence form by command alone the invisible announcement in the virtues; but the virtues are second, which, executing the enjoined command, publish it in [1025D] the powers. But the powers are third, which perfect the mandate conceived in the principalities, archangels, and angels, subjected to them for the operation. But those who set the virtues after the angels and archangels understand thereby, of course, those spirits through whom signs and miracles are more frequently done. In this too let the property of the last arrangement be preserved, whose ministry we hold to have been ordained for dispensing outward things alone. But the powers, they say, are those spirits so called who have the adverse virtues subjected to them, and freely restrain them according to the power given, lest they be able to harm as much as they will. But the principalities, they hold, are those spirits so named who are set over even the good spirits of the angels themselves: to whom, while they command whatever is to be done, [1026A] they act as princes over their subjects for the fulfilling of the divine ministries, and stand as superiors. But the dominations are those so called who surpass even the principalities by a more excellent power, so that they have as subjects even those who merited to command others for the fulfilling of the ministry. These things briefly concerning the orders of the angels, and their names, we thought had to be tasted beforehand as necessary for the coming narration, so that the reader, once these things are said, may take up the several matters — as reason and cause shall demand, to be recalled in what follows — that concern these things, and perhaps, without these, the obscure things that are to be said. Now let us return to explaining the sequence of the text itself. Above he said that the theologians do not have the ground for naming the angelic order archas, or thrones, or seraphim; because that angelic order [1026B] is not in participation of those most exalted powers, so as to partake with them in name, when it does not partake in the property of the name. Now he proves this same thing by the following words, namely, that the angelic order does not partake of the most exalted powers. It does not, he says, partake with them; but what it partakes, it partakes from them. For it is one thing to partake in the fullness, and another to receive participation from the fullness. And so the angelic order is subjected to the most exalted powers in such a way that it does not partake with them, but from them; because, just as it, by illuminating and instructing, leads back those men who are led back to divine knowledge, so also it receives divine illumination from those powers which are before it.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

[1026C]This is what he says: « As it, » namely the angelic order, « leads back our divine highest priests, » that is, the holy men who received the sacred divine things immediately from the angels and handed them on to us, « to the splendors of the divinity known to it, » that is, the divine knowledge which it received, and, having received, bestowed, « so also it is led back by the virtues of the essences which are before it, » that is, before it, or above it, « the virtues, I say, pure, » that is, exceedingly sacred, or wholly sacred, so that by the excellence of holiness they can be a form of perfection to those subject to them. And these same « virtues are also sacred toward the divine, » namely, toward what is to be conferred; that is, toward the knowledge of divinity to be given, « to the arrangement consummating the angelic hierarchies, » [1026D] that is, to the arrangement which perfects, or contains as perfected, or terminates the angelic hierarchies; this is, namely, the last arrangement, in which both the angelic order, in that it is perfected, is consummated, and, in that it is last, the hierarchies of all spirits are terminated. Indeed, here the execrable error of the interpreter must be guarded against, who supposed that the celestial virtues are called unclean. For the Greek πανιερὸν panieron, which is interpreted *exceedingly sacred,* or *wholly sacred,* or *universally sacred,* he thought was here to be understood as ἀνιερὸν anieron, that is, *unsacred,* or *not sacred,* or *without the sacred.*

There follows: « Unless indeed someone should also say this: that all the angelic namings are common, according to the subjection of all the celestial virtues to the deiform, and [1027A] God-given light, and their supereminent communication. » The lower orders cannot, he says, take up the names of the higher, because they do not partake in the same excellence of virtues with them, unless one should wish to say that, for this reason, all the namings of the angels ought to be common, because all alike, as subjects, perceive the one light given by God, and from that same perceived light all are made conformed to God, and exist as partakers of one supereminent communication in one light. Unless, he says, someone should also say this: that all the angelic surnames are common, according to the subjection of all the celestial virtues into the light, or toward the deiform and God-given light to be perceived, and according to the supereminent communication of spiritual or special grace: so that, just as one [1028A] grace diffuses itself for the participation of all, so one surname may reasonably be attributed to all. « But, that the reason may be more judged by us, we shall consider the holy and comely properties, expressed in the sacred oracles, of each celestial arrangement. » That we may be more able, he says, to judge this reason — namely, whether the angelic surnames ought to be common or not — we shall consider the holy and comely properties of each celestial arrangement, expressed in the sacred oracles. For from the properties of each, diligently considered, it will perhaps be able to be recognized whether the communication of the naming can be the same: to whom, even if one grace is infused, yet it is not given in one measure and proportion.

## TITLE OF CHAPTER VI. *What is the first arrangement of the celestial essences: what the middle, and what the last.*



## THE TEXT.

[1027B]

*How many indeed and of what sort are the adornments of the supercelestial essences, and how according to them the Hierarchies are perfected, I say that she alone diligently knows, the contemplative sovereign of their perfection; and moreover that they themselves are ignorant of their own proper virtues and illuminations, and of their own sacred and super-adorned ordination. For it is impossible for us to know the ministries of the supercelestial minds, and their most holy perfections, unless one should tell to himself whatever mysteries the divinity taught us through them, as through those who well know their own. Not therefore do we ourselves say anything of our own motion. But whatever of the angelic contemplations have been contemplated by the holy theologians, these, teaching us, as much as we are able, we shall expound. All theology [1027C] called the celestial essences by nine manifesting surnames [n: (Colos. I)]*. *These our divine holy perfecter separates into three ternary arrangements. And the first indeed he says to be that existing about God always, and attentively united to him, and, before the others, immediately — so it has been handed down. For the most holy thrones, and the eyed and winged orders, named cherubim in the voice of the Hebrews, and seraphim, according to a nearness set above all things, are placed immediately about God, he says, as the manifestation of the divine oracles hands down. This threefold adornment therefore, as one and equally ordered and truly the first hierarchy, our common master calls it: than which [n: (Ephes. III; Coloss. I)] there is no other more deiform, and, of itself, more immediately intent upon the pre-operating illuminations of the divinity. [1027D] But the second he says to be completed from the powers, and dominations, and virtues. And the arrangement, last of the celestial hierarchies, of the threefold company of the angels, archangels, and principalities [n: (I Thess. IV)].*



## EXPOSITION

The title of the sixth chapter is: « What is the first arrangement of the celestial essences, what the middle, and what [1028B] the last. » For in this chapter he treats of the threefold arrangement of the nine orders: of which the first, which is also the highest, contains three orders, the seraphim, cherubim, and thrones; the second, which is also the middle, likewise three, the dominations, virtues, and powers; the third, which is also the last, likewise three, the principalities, archangels, and angels: in which the arrangement of the nine orders is consummated. But being about to treat of things so sublime and remote from human sense, he first modestly confesses his own ignorance, showing that those celestial secrets are not only unknown to men, but cannot be wholly comprehended perfectly, as they are, even by the angelic spirits themselves, and that the divine power alone, from which they are, perfectly knows what they are.

[1028C]This is what he says: « How many indeed, and of what sort, are the adornments of the supercelestial essences, and how according to them the hierarchies are perfected: I say that she alone diligently knows, the contemplative sovereign of their perfection, » that is, the divine wisdom alone, which they have both as principle that they may be, and toward which they look through contemplation that they may be perfect. That divine wisdom therefore, he says, I say alone knows how many and of what sort are the adornments of the supercelestial essences, that is, of the invisible natures: because, of course, not even they themselves can perfectly comprehend of what sort they were created by God. For this reason, he says, I say moreover that they too are ignorant of their own proper virtues, and illuminations, and their own sacred and super-adorned ordination. [1028D] Therefore the divine wisdom alone, which made them, perfectly comprehends both of what sort it made them and how it arranged them, so that in this it may be openly shown how much the immensity of the Creator transcends all the possibility of the creature, since the creature does not at all suffice even to comprehend itself.

There follows: « For it is impossible for us to know the ministries of the supercelestial minds, and their most holy [1029A] perfections. » For it is not greatly to be wondered at, if we cannot know this concerning them, which they themselves are unable to comprehend concerning themselves, since we can know even that which we know concerning them only through what is given and ministered by God through them. For this reason it is impossible for us to know anything concerning them, except what the divinity taught us through them. « Unless, he says, one should there say, whatever mysteries the divinity taught, as through those well knowing their own. » It is impossible for us to know, unless one should say there, that we know only there where we have been taught through them whatever mysteries, that is, secrets, the divinity taught us through them, as through those well knowing their own ministries. Well therefore do they know their own ministries, as much as suffices for our instruction; and well are they ignorant, [1029B] as much as suffices for full and perfect comprehension.

There follows: « Not therefore do we ourselves say anything of our own motion: but whatever of the angelic contemplations have been contemplated by the holy theologians, these, teaching us, as much as we are able, we shall expound. » Since, he says, we of ourselves can know nothing concerning them, which we have not learned from them: therefore in those things which we wish to say concerning them, we do not say of ourselves, that is, of our own motion, or sense, or thought — we say nothing. But whatever the holy theologians have contemplated through contemplation concerning the angels themselves, we, following their authority as much as we can, teach this. « All theology called the celestial essences by nine [1029C] manifesting surnames. » All the celestial essences, he says, that is, the spirits, theology called by nine manifesting surnames, that is, distinguished them by nine manifest and evident surnames, namely angels, archangels, principalities, powers, virtues, dominations, thrones, cherubim, and seraphim, marking them off by their proper names. « These, he says, our divine holy perfecter separates into three ternary arrangements. » It is believed that no one is here better signified as his divine and holy perfecter, or teacher, than the apostle Paul, by whom he had been baptized and instructed in the catholic faith; who, caught up as far as the third heaven into the paradise of God, and there hearing secret things which it is not lawful for a man to speak, is thought to have revealed — as much [1029D] as it was either useful or possible to know of these things for this mortal life — by transmitting them to the memory of posterity, through this holy man as well as through others who by the Spirit of God had passed beyond human understanding. Relying therefore on his authority, and supported by the testimonies of the holy Scriptures, he brought down the knowledge of the celestial things to earth. These, he says, namely the essences, that is, these nine orders, « our divine holy perfecter separates, » that is, distinguishes, « into three ternary, » that is, threefold, « arrangements. And the first indeed he says to be that existing about God, always, and attentively united to him, and, before the others, immediately, as has been handed down. » He says the first arrangement to be about God, always [1030A] existing, and never at any time going out to outward things; and handed down, that is, ordained and arranged, to be united, namely, to God himself, that is, that it may be attentively united to him, being always intent, of course, upon him; and that it may be united to him before the other celestial virtues, which are after it, and united immediately, so that there are none before it. « For the most holy thrones, and the eyed and winged orders, named cherubim in the voice of the Hebrews, and seraphim, according to a nearness set above all, are placed immediately about God, he says, as the manifestation of the divine oracles hands down. » He himself therefore, our perfecter and teacher, says that the manifestation, or authority, of the divine oracles hands down or attests the most holy thrones, and the eyed and winged orders, named in the voice of the Hebrews [1030B] cherubim and seraphim — the eyed, namely, the cherubim, on account of contemplation, the winged, namely the seraphim, on account of love. These orders, I say, that is the thrones, cherubim, and seraphim, he himself says to be placed immediately about God according to the nearness which they have set above toward God, and more excellent than all the other orders. « This threefold adornment therefore, as one, and equally ordered, and truly the first hierarchy, our common master calls it. » These three orders therefore he says to constitute one hierarchy, first in relation to the others, equally ordered in itself. In which hierarchy, namely, although they are more exalted than the others in singular grace, yet all are in a certain manner equal in one arrangement: and from this, according to something, they [1030C] exist as equal, that all look immediately upon God: the first in love, the second in knowledge, the third in discernment. « Than which, » namely hierarchy, « there is no other more deiform, and, of itself, more immediately intent upon the pre-operating illuminations of the divinity. » No other hierarchy, he says, is more deiform than this one, nor more nearly approaching the likeness of God, nor more intent upon the illuminations of the divinity pre-operating in it alone, because before the others; and operating of itself, because not through others. For the divine illuminations operate in this hierarchy alone before the others, because all after this hierarchy are illuminated by those same divine illuminations; and of themselves the divine illuminations operate in this one alone, because through it all the other hierarchies [1030D] are consequently illuminated by the divine illuminations. « But the second he says to be, » namely hierarchy, « completed from the powers, and dominations, and virtues: » the order however being changed, so that the dominations are understood as first, the virtues as second, the powers as third. « And the arrangement of the threefold, last, of the celestial hierarchies, of the angels, archangels, and principalities. » It must be supplied from what precedes, our master himself says, to have known the arranged arrangement of the threefold celestial hierarchies, of the angels, archangels, and principalities. Where, if it be asked why Paul in his Epistles, when he was enumerating the arrangement of the angels by distinguishing it, did not preserve this order, since [1031A] he had asserted to others that such was the order among them. It can be said that there he wished to explain not so much the order of the celestial arrangements as their number, especially since in one place he enumerates only certain ones; but in another he explains those which he had there passed over in silence, without [1032A] repetition of the others. In which it is manifest that, if he were attending to order and number, he would have been zealous to explain all of them at once in one series of narration. And if there is any other reason which can be fittingly answered to this objection.

## THE SIXTH BOOK.

[1031]

## TITLE OF CHAPTER VII. *On the seraphim and thrones, that is, on the first hierarchy of the angels.*



## THE TEXT.

[1031B]

*We, being about to receive this order of the holy hierarchies, say that all the surnames of the celestial intellects have a declaration of the deiform property of each one. And indeed the holy naming of the seraphim — which are of the Hebrews — [we, ] knowing, [know it] to signify either the intent ones, or the heating ones; but that of the cherubim, a multitude of knowledge, or a fusion of wisdom. Beautifully, therefore, the first of the celestial hierarchies is sanctified by the most excellent essences, having an order higher than all, that is, it is placed immediately about God; and the first-operating theophanies and perfections are borne down into it, as into the nearest, more principally. Heating ones therefore are they named, both the thrones, and a fusion of wisdom, by a name manifesting their deiform [1031C] dispositions. For their ever-moving about the divine things, and unceasing, and hot, and sharp, and super-fervid — of an intent, and perhaps innermost, and ever-inflexible motion, and reductive of the things placed beneath, and active — the exemplative [property], as it were re-heating those things, and resuscitating them into a like heat, and fiery from heaven, and purgative by whole-burnt-offering, and unveiled, and inextinguishable, and having thus ever a light-form and illuminative property, persecutrix of every dark obscuring, and manifestatrix — this the manifestation, or surname, of the seraphim teaches. But that of the cherubim [teaches] their knowable, and bared [property], and receptive of the highest giving of lights, and contemplative, filled in the first operatrix power of the divine beauty, and of the wisdom-making tradition, and communicative [1031D] copiously to the second things, of the wisdom given for outpouring. But that [naming] of the highest and compacted seats [teaches] the being diligently exalted above every ignominy of subjection, and bearing upward to the summit in a supermundane way, and, by every extremity ineffably, into the most sublime, and, about the truly exalted, placed with all its powers unchangeably and stably, and receptive of the divine super-advent in all impassibility and immateriality, and God-bearing, and, in a servant-like manner, open unto the divine receivings. This indeed is the declaration of their names, as far as pertains to us. But it must be said what hierarchy of theirs we deem it. That the speculation of every hierarchy hangs ineffably upon a deiformity imitating God, and that every hierarchic action is divided into a sacred participation, and a tradition [1032B] of pure purgation, and of the divine light, and of perfective knowledge, I judge to have been already sufficiently said by us. But now let us worthily proceed to speak of the most excellent intellects, how now, according to them, the hierarchy is manifested by the oracles. In the first essences, which, placed after their substance-making divinity, and, as it were, ordained in its vestibules, super-excel every made power visible and invisible, it must be deemed that there is a proper, and wholly equiform, hierarchy. It must therefore be deemed that they are pure, not as freed from unclean stains and defilements, nor as receptive of material phantasies, but as clean by every diminishment, and higher, and, above every super-firmed temple, according to the most exalted chastity, super-placed above all the most deiform powers [1032C], and, by their own motion of themselves according to the unconvertible order of one loving God, ineffably received, and knowing wholly no contumely among the things subjected, but having a non-casual, as also an unchangeable, most pure placing of their proper deiform specialty. And [they are] again contemplative of the sensible symbols, or speculative of the intellectual ones, not as led back into the divine by the variety of a sacred-writing theory, but as filled with the higher light of all immaterial knowledge, and stuffed, as much as is lawful, with the contemplation of the form-making, and principal beauty, and superessential, and thrice-shining; and made worthy likewise of the communion of Jesus. Not in sacredly feigned images do they formatively figure the deific likeness, but as truly approaching him in the first participation [1032D] of knowledge [they figure] the deific [thing] of his lights, and because what is like God has been substantially given to them. But he communicates such a thing, as far as possible, in the pre-operatrix power, to his deific and human virtues. But [they are] perfect likewise, not as illuminated by a sacred variety in an analectic [analyticum] knowledge, but as filled with a first and supereminent deification, according to the most excellent knowledge of the divine works, as far as is in angels. For not through other holy essences, but sanctified by the divinity itself, they are extended immediately into it, by a virtue and order supereminent to all, and are placed unto a wholly most chaste fortitude, and are led into contemplation of the immaterial and invisible beauty, as much as is lawful, and are bent toward the knowable reasons of the divine works, as first, and about [1033A] God, essences, and are most excellently [excelsissime] sanctified by the very sovereign of perfection. This therefore the theologians also openly declare: that the subordinate arrangements of the celestial essences are ornately instructed in the deific sciences by their super-firmed [superiors], but that the higher of all are illuminated in doctrine by the divinity itself, as much as is lawful. For they introduce certain of them, sacredly instructed by the former ones, [saying] that the Lord is [Lord] of the celestial powers, and the King of glory received into the heavens in a human manner [n: (Psal. XXIII)]*. *But certain [others] inquiring with Jesus himself, and learning for us the knowledge of his divine action, and [they introduce] Jesus himself immediately teaching them, and, lavishing upon them, manifesting his human benignity. For I, he says, dispute justice, and the judgment of salvation [n: (Isa. LXIII)]*. *But I wonder that even [1033B] the first of the celestial essences, and — so many all together — supereminent by divine illuminations, reverently seek, as it were, mediated questionings. For indeed they do not thereupon ask: Why are thy [tibi] garments red? But among themselves they deliberate before asking, showing indeed that they learn, and seek the deific knowledge, yet not leaping before the illumination imparted through the divine procession. Is not, then, the first hierarchy of the celestial intellects, sanctified by the very sovereign of perfection, in that it is extended immediately into it — by a most holy purgation, by much light, by a perfect consummation beforehand, filling it proportionally — purged, and illuminated, and perfected; pure indeed by every diminution, but full of the first light, and partaking of the knowledge first given, and of the perfect knowledge [1033C]? But comprehending (and this I would say perhaps not undeservedly) that both purgation, and illumination, and perfection, is an assumption of the divine knowledge. Purging, namely, ignorance, inasmuch as the knowledge of the perfect doctrines is imparted according to order; but illuminating by that same divine knowledge through which it also purges the one not first contemplating, before it manifests through the higher illumination; and again perfecting, by that same light, according to habit, by the knowledge of the most lucid doctrines. This therefore is, as far as pertains to our knowledge, the first arrangement of the celestial essences, standing in the circuit of God, and immediately about God, and simply and unceasingly circling his eternal knowledge, according to the most excellent, ever-moving placing, as far as is in angels. Seeing indeed many [1033D] and blessed contemplations purely, and illuminated by the simple and immediate splendors, and filled with divine nourishment; by much given at first in outpouring, and by the sole domestic and unific unity of the divine refection, and made worthy of much communion of God and of cooperation toward it, by a likeness, as far as possible, of good dispositions and actions; and knowing many of the divine things in a super-placed manner, and made a partaker of the divine knowledge and cognition, according to what is lawful. Therefore theology handed down its praises also to those who are on earth, in which the eminence of its most excellent illumination is wonderfully manifested. For certain of them, speaking sensibly, re-echo as the voice of waters: Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place. [1034A] But others cry back that very laudable and most devout theology: Holy, holy, holy, the Lord God of Sabaoth; full is all the earth of his glory [n: (Ezech. I; Ezech. III; Isa. VI; Apoc. IV)]*. *But these most exalted hymnologies of the celestial minds we have already, indeed, opened as much as possible in those [treatises] which are on the divine praises, and it has been said of them in those [places], as far as pertains to us, sufficiently. From which, for remembrance, it suffices to say only, according to the present time, that the first arrangement itself was illuminated in the theological [sic: *seientiam*], as far as is lawful, by the divine goodness, through which it then handed itself down also to others as a deiform hierarchy, introducing that [truth] by way of brevity in saying: that it is lawful for that most devout, and supremely blessed, and wholly blessed divinity to be blessed [1034B] — as far as is possible — to be known and praised by the intellects that receive God. For they themselves are, as it were, deiform divine places, of divine, as the oracles say [n: (Psal. IX, XLVI, LXXIX; Isa. LXVI)]*, *rest. And because it is a monad and unity, three substantially, and, from the supercelestial essences even to the last things of the earth, extending its goodness into all things that are, [and its] providence, as the super-principal principle of every essence, and cause, and binding all things around by a super-essentially immeasurable containment.*



## EXPOSITION.

The title of the seventh chapter is: On the interpretation of the seraphim, and cherubim, and thrones; and on the first hierarchy, which is theirs. For after he enumerated [1034C] the orders of the celestial hierarchies, he now begins to treat of the interpretation and signification of the surnames. And first concerning those which are counted in the first hierarchy, that is, concerning the seraphim, and cherubim, and thrones, demonstrating their virtue according to the property of the appellation.

« We, being about to receive this order of the holy hierarchies. » We, he says, being about to receive and approve this order of the holy hierarchies, which we spoke of above: « We say » consequently, « that all the surnames of the celestial intellects have a declaration of the deiform property of each one. » For all the surnames of the holy intellects, that is, of the spirits, have a declaration [1034D] of the deiform property of each order. The surname declares what is proper to it, and singular, conferred through the excellence of the gift in deiformity. For every grace reforms the mind to the likeness of God; and yet what is conferred as proper to each in that one form, just as it is distinguished in the office, so was to be distinguished in the appellation.

There follows: « And indeed the holy naming of the seraphim — which are of the Hebrews — knowing [it] to manifest either the kindling ones, or the heating ones; but that of the cherubim, a multitude of knowledge, or a fusion of wisdom. We say, he says, that all the surnames of the celestial intellects have a declaration [1035A] of the deiform property of each one. » We, I say, knowing the naming of the seraphim: which seraphim are of the Hebrews, that is, are so called in Hebrew, [know it] to manifest either the kindling ones, or the heating ones; but that naming — supply it — which is of the cherubim, [we, ] knowing, [know it] to manifest a multitude of knowledge, or a fusion of wisdom, so that in the very expression of the spiritual names may be declared the grace of the gifts, which, even if they are not singular, yet are established as excellent. « Beautifully, therefore, the first of the celestial hierarchies is sanctified by the most excellent essences, having an order higher than all, that is, it is placed immediately about God: and the first-operating theophanies and perfections are borne down into it, as into the nearest, more principally. » This, he says, is beautiful [1035B] and fitting, that the first hierarchy should have more excellent spirits, since it too is by its order higher than all the other hierarchies, and nearer to God, placed immediately about him, that is, with no other standing between it and God. And therefore, because it is nearest to God, on that account the theophanies — that is, the divine apparitions, or manifestations, or illuminations first-operating, namely from the Creator into the creature, not through a creature (for those are the first operations of the divine illumination into the creature, or [rather] the creature; the second are through a creature into a creature): and the perfections too of the spiritual gifts are borne down more principally into that first hierarchy than into the others which follow and are subject: inasmuch as they conceive every participation of spiritual grace [1035C] only by its mediation. « Heating ones therefore are they named, both the thrones, and a fusion of wisdom, by a name manifesting their deiform dispositions. » Since the divine illuminations and perfections are borne down more principally into it, on that account the orders constituted in it — some, by conceiving and furnishing the fire of love, are named heating ones, like the seraphim; others, powerful by the judgment of truth, thrones; others, shining and illuminating by the knowledge of science, a fusion of wisdom, like the cherubim — are called by a name manifesting their deiform dispositions. For from the very name of the appellation is manifested the virtue of the deiform disposition, so that by it they may be known to be endowed with the divine likeness, by which they are known to be marked with a distinct [1035D] and singular appellation. For that in each order the distinction of the name marks a certain singular and proper disposition of the divine participation, he proves from the sentence set below, saying:

« For their ever-moving about the divine things, and unceasing, and hot, and sharp, and super-fervid — of an intent, and perhaps innermost, and ever-inflexible motion, and reductive of the things placed beneath, and active — the exemplative [property], as it were re-heating those things, and resuscitating them into a like heat, and fiery from heaven, and purgative by whole-burnt-offering, and unveiled, and inextinguishable, and having thus ever a light-form and illuminative property, persecutrix of every dark obscuring, and manifestatrix — this the naming, [1036A] or manifestation, of the seraphim teaches. » If I should say what I feel, first I confess this: that I have heard words either not spoken to a man, or not spoken by a man. For that they should even be spoken through a man seems to me so great a thing that nothing more could be given to a man. And perhaps, because these words were born from those which could be heard, they ought not to have been spoken. For he indeed who suggested or taught these things had reached even to the third heaven, and had entered into the paradise of God, and there had heard certain words concerning the Word, wholly secret, and next to silence, [reaching] even to those which the human ear could not touch: which no one would hear, until he knew. For within they were heard where they were spoken, and could not go forth outside where man was. Therefore by him who was within, and deeply within, they could be heard within and inwardly [1036B]; but to those who were outside they ought not say. Yet lest even those who were outside should be abandoned, if they were not called by him who was within: words were born from words, just as the words were born from the Word: from the words which had to be kept within, words which could be brought forth outside; from immense things, great ones; from hidden things, obscure ones; from impenetrable things, profound ones — which, though heard by us, whether they were understood, I do not know. These are the very words which the disciple of the master, and the master of disciples, set before us to be searched out, or rather to be wondered at. For the first disciple of the Word heard words from the Word, and he, made a teacher by other words, had a disciple, and made a teacher. When this at length descended to our hearing, the voice, as if from heaven, resounded upon the earth, and already filled our ears [1036C] with amazement; yet it had not yet illuminated our hearts with manifest truth. Therefore those who were men, and could not yet grasp divine things, said that a thunder had come to pass, because they were only dismayed, and not instructed. Others, illuminated a little, not yet consummated, thought it an angel; they did not understand it to be God. And so we too have heard the thunder of the heavenly voice, and have begun to wonder, not yet to be illuminated. Yet if our wonder be an awakening, by that same wonder we shall be converted, that by conversion we may be illuminated. And then those very words will be sweet, not only to be wondered at, but to be loved, when they begin to be heard and known, if only we shall be gracious toward them. [1036D] For if they are not loved, they are not understood; nor are they loved, if they are not tasted. What then? Why have we heard, if we do not understand; or how do we understand, if we do not love? I, for my part, answer: If I do not presume upon love, I do not depart from wonder. Perhaps by that same wonder I shall wake up to knowledge: and if I am less roused to knowledge, I shall be incited to love. And meanwhile love itself will be a refreshment, until from it there arises contemplation, through which illumination may come to be.

What is that [saying] of the angels — « the ever-moving about the divine things, and unceasing, and hot, and sharp, and super-fervid, of an ever-intent, and perhaps innermost, and ever-inflexible motion? » If we should say that this is love, perhaps we may seem to have said little, [1037A] to those not knowing what love is. For never does he say little who says love, unless perchance he means a small love. But this one did not wish to mean a small love, who said so many things about love. « Moving, he says, and unceasing, and hot, and sharp, and super-fervid. » Moving, because it is life; unceasing, because it is perpetual; hot, because it is love; sharp, because it is wisdom. Is this enough? He said life, he named it perpetual, he set down love, and he added wisdom. And all this is in one love, and it is one love. Do you wish to know that love is life? Hear that beloved one, and the lover, commending love. « He who does not love, he says, remains in death [n: (Joan. I)]. » Therefore love is life; and what kind of life? « Charity never falls away [n: (I Cor. XIII)]. » But if [1037B] charity never falls away, love is perpetual life. And what of love? Where shall we be able to show that hot and fervid quality in love? Where did love have fervor and heat; or rather, where was love without heat and fervor? Walking and loving, kindling and fervent, what did they say of Jesus, whom they heard, and did not recognize on the way? For they were walking and being moved, driven by the impatience of love, because if they stood still they would not love. For the moving quality belongs to love as does the hot, so that true love may not grow numb. They were walking therefore in the moving quality of love, and were burning in the hot, and were saying: « Was not our heart burning within us concerning Jesus, while he was speaking to us on the way? [n: (Luc. XXIV.)] » For because they were walking, they had the moving quality; and the hot, because they were burning; but the sharp [1037C] they did not have, because they did not recognize [him]. For this reason, because they did not have the sharp, they heard: « Foolish ones, and slow to believe in all the things that the prophets have spoken [n: (ibid.)]. » Therefore they were dull, and slow to know; but not lukewarm, or sluggish to love. Yet because they first loved, afterward they knew, so that the sharp might be in love just as the hot also. First the hot, afterward the sharp. Therefore he did not love the sharp and the hot, but the hot and the sharp: just as first the moving, afterward the unceasing, so that the moving may rouse to inquiry, the unceasing may confirm to perseverance; the hot, that it may vivify the sense; but the sharp, that it may penetrate to comprehension. For the sharp signifies a certain onrush of love, and the vehemence of an ardent desire, bearing [1037D] itself into the beloved, and entering, and penetrating, so that it may be there where the very thing which is loved is, with it, and in it, so that not only may the warmth be from it, but the sharp may pass over into it. For it could be hot, and, as it were, be warmed from afar: for which it would be enough to love one so absent, and not to see him present, or to possess him most present. But the love of the hierarchy was not perfect, nor much to be loved, unless it made the sharp for itself, and passed through all things, and penetrated, until it reached the beloved, nay rather went into the beloved. For if you do not go into the beloved, you still love outside, nor do you have the sharpness of love. But you have [only warmth], and, growing numb, you remain divided, and outside him, so that you are not made one [with him]. But love wishes to make you one with [1038A] him: and therefore it penetrates all things, and approaches as much as it can, unto him who is one.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

Consider now what keenness of love they had, of whom it was said: « Where the impulse of the spirit was, thither they went [n: (Ezech. I)]. » For that very impulse was keen, just as in a certain other place the liquid too is named as the keenness of love. And I think that it was the bride herself who was speaking; and it was not fitting that anything hard or harsh be brought to one who was fearful and timid. Therefore the liquid was named instead of the keen, in the blandishment of love. For the liquid too penetrates like the keen, and does not cease until it has reached the inmost parts. Therefore she says: « My soul melted when the beloved spoke; I sought him [n: (Cant. III)]. » For she sought him for this reason, that [1038B] she had been melted toward him. For unless she had been melted toward him, she would not have run after him; but she would have stood hard, and would not have entered. But now she has been melted, and has begun to run; yet she did not straightway find him, until she arrived. Therefore here too the unceasing was necessary, that she might enter, and penetrate, and say: « I held him, nor will I let him go, until I bring him, she says, into the house of my mother, and into the chamber of her that bore me [n: (Cant. V)]. I will bring him, she says, into the house of my mother, into the chamber of her that bore me. » Therefore he himself will enter in to you, that you may go in to him. For then you enter in to him, when he enters in to you. When his love enters your heart, and penetrates it, and his love reaches to the inmost part of your heart, then he himself enters into you, and you too enter [1038C] into yourself, that you may go in to him. Therefore do you bring him in to yourself; and bring him in to yourself in no such way that he should merely remain or subsist outside, near you, or in your gates, or in your courts; or before the door of your house, or even only in your house — because this is not much, nor is it great for a great love, unless it reach as far as the marriage-chamber, and enter the inner room, and penetrate to the inmost parts, and rest in your innermost places. I will say something further still: that even the chamber of him who begot perhaps she does not love, unless she be brought into the chamber of her that bore, where love is more tender and the blandishments sweeter, so that it may find in you nothing hard or rigid of manly fierceness. But let the whole be melted, and grow soft with the fire of love. For then nothing of hardness will withstand, so that charity may reach to the [1038D] inmost parts, and love may have the keenness to penetrate all things; this it was we had to say, concerning the keen of love and the liquid, that you may understand the force of love and affection, how great it is. If, however, this can be understood, since love surpasses knowledge, and is greater than understanding. For more is loved than is understood, and love enters and draws near where knowledge is outside. Nor is this to be wondered at: for love always presumes further, and always trusts; love brings itself in without hesitation. Therefore it has the keen, and the liquid that penetrates all things, and follows the impulse of its own ardent desire, unable to dissemble until it reach the beloved; and by that very fact thirsting still more to enter into him, and to be with him, and so near that, [1039A] if it could be, it might be the very same thing that he is. Is it not exceedingly keen, to penetrate all these things, and to enter into the inmost parts, so that it cannot be repelled by any power, until it reach where it loves? « Who, says the Apostle, shall separate us from the charity of Christ? Persecution, or famine, or the sword? [n: (Rom. VIII)] » and many other things, which could have been a hindrance, had love not withstood them. But love, because it had the keen, could not be held; but it passed through, and penetrated, escaping freely, and running to its own desire. If, then, such is the hot and the keen of love, of what sort do you think is that which follows, the « superfervent »? For that must have something more, which has been added to the increase of the preceding powers. And the very matter admonishes us to think something greater in that [1039B] which is superfervent, than in that which is hot and keen.

For you know how that which boils is cast beyond itself by a certain violence of its heat and burning, and is lifted above itself, and makes a great movement out of the underlying and invisible seething of the fervor conceived. And the heat of the hidden burning, which is within and moves, is not seen; but that which is moved is seen by it: and from that which is seen, we conceive and understand the great force, and the robust power, and the strong violence of that which lies hidden and is not seen. Who will be able worthily to bring the emulation of visible things to the majesty of invisible things? Spectacles are set before us, and there is shown to us fervor from heat and moisture, or rather in moisture from heat: and we see how the heat [1039C] without tumult gradually enters into the moisture, so that, having entered, it may powerfully and violently cast it out. It brings itself in invisibly, that it may manifestly raise it up, as though it would not have it there, the thing which it hastens to cast out with so vehement, as it were, an indignation of a certain impulse. The hot, therefore, is moved to the keen, then the keen is advanced to the superfervent. For that which was first keen, and liquid in love, able to penetrate other things that stand in its way, now becomes superfervent, and boiling, unable to stand still in itself. For the keen belongs to love, when by passing through it despises all things; but the superfervent, when by despising it forsakes even its own self. For he who desires this alone which he loves despises even his own self in comparison with it. For neither would he truly desire that alone, if he loved [1039D] even his own self along with it. But this a great and singular love alone can do: that for love of that which alone is loved, even he who loves should be despised, as it were, by his own self. It comes to pass, therefore, in a wondrous manner, that while through the fire of love he is lifted up into him who is above himself, by the force of love he begins to be driven out, and even to go out of himself. How, then, does he grow fervent, and how does he boil in heart, who through the fire of the supernal love conceived, while by thought and desire he is borne toward the desiring of him alone who is above, is cast beyond his own self, and is lifted above himself, and does not think of himself, while he loves him alone? Thus we understand the mobile, and the unceasing, and the hot, and the keen, and the superfervent of love.

[1040A]But how shall we be able to assign these things in those supercelestial natures, in which the same thing is life as is essence, because they have no other essence than life? What is that unceasing movement of theirs about divine things, and the hot, and the keen, and the superfervent? What are those divine things, about which they are unceasingly moved, and grow hot, and are sharpened, and are superfervent? For he who is in a circuit does not yet enter; because either he is lukewarm from the hot, or he is numb from the keen. If, then, they have the keen, how are they in a circuit? Perhaps because those divine things, of which Scripture has spoken, are within every creature, and so utterly secret and hidden, that even if they can be touched, they cannot be penetrated. But there are certain other divine things, which come into manifestation, and in some manner set themselves forth to knowledge, [1040B] either while they come forth inwardly to the mind, or while they proceed outwardly as far as the sense. For the Apostle intimates that certain divine things are utterly within, and hidden, and latent, but that certain things have gone forth outwardly, and have been manifested, saying: « What is known of God is manifest in them [n: (Rom. I)]. » For when he says, « what is known of God, » that is, the knowable concerning God, he plainly shows that of the things which are of God, and are in God, something is manifest, something hidden. And that indeed which is manifest can be touched through knowledge; but that which is utterly hidden can by no reasoning be penetrated. There are, therefore, certain divine things, and certain things of God set forth for manifestation, which according to something can be penetrated and comprehended; but certain things so deep, and hidden, [1040C] and exceedingly inward, and altogether impenetrable, that no intellect can search them out, nor any wisdom investigate them: concerning which it is a great thing, when it is granted to touch them, even if it be not granted to penetrate them; and when by penetrating one arrives at them, they nevertheless are not penetrated, but remain impenetrable and incomprehensible, in which this alone, which is outside, is shown to the arriving understanding for knowledge, and that which is always within is not opened for comprehension.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

Consider now and see whether the things which have been made visible by God do not instruct you concerning the invisible things of God. For the things which by reason alone are sometimes less investigated, are not seldom made knowable by the light of examples. See, therefore, what the sense of the flesh can do in [1040D] the world, that from it you may understand, as far as it can be understood, what the sense of the mind can do in God. When we touch the just visible world with the eye of the flesh, we perceive the things which are outside it; and to the things which lie hidden within, by that same sense we are not able to penetrate. And even if certain things which lay hidden are sometimes opened, still many others lie hidden which cannot be comprehended, either by their immensity, because they exceed the sense; or by their subtlety, because they escape the sense; or by their obscurity, because they do not admit the sense to themselves. So consider that the sense of the rational mind, that by which we perceive divine things, if ever it be admitted to touch God, perceives those things only which are, as it were, outside him; and the things which lie hidden within, secret and concealed, it does not comprehend. [1041A] But he said that it is outside him, and not outside us, for this reason: because everything that is in God is within to every creature; but yet, in comparison with those things which cannot at all be comprehended, that which even of the most secret and most subtle understanding is manifested concerning him is said in some manner to be to him, or in him, outside. Through the keen of love, therefore, they penetrate to him; and yet, through his incomprehensible majesty, they remain about him, so that they do not enter into the whole, even though they penetrate as far as something. But this very thing also must be considered, that they are said to be about him, and not in some one part. For they encompass with desire that which they do not penetrate with the intellect, so that they may leave nothing unconsidered of all the things that can be known, [1041B] always seeing, and always thirsting to see. Stable, that they may not depart; mobile, that they may unceasingly desire. In a circuit, because they do not enter into the whole of that which is. In a circuit, because they draw near immediately. In a circuit, because they survey by contemplation and love all that is knowable in him. Thus, therefore, the mobile of them about divine things, and the unceasing, and the hot, and the keen, and the superfervent of that unceasing, and intent, and perhaps inmost, and inflexible movement itself, can fittingly be understood. This, however, must not be passed over, that he named the movement of the invisible natures unceasing, and intent, and perhaps inmost, and inflexible, using the voice of one doubting in this one thing alone, when he said "perhaps," as though he asserted the rest without hesitation, and this one thing alone he would not venture to affirm except with the qualification [1041C] of doubt. The movement, therefore, of those spirits that draw near to the supreme divinity is called unceasing, and inmost, and inflexible; because, tending from itself toward God through the desire of love, it is both always mobile, so that it never rests in itself; and intent, that it may press on toward him; and inmost, that it may not flow out to outward things; and inflexible, that it may not turn aside to other things outside itself and beside him. Why, then, does he not say absolutely "inmost," but, as if doubting, and as if he knew not whether it were so, or whether it were so to be said, did he add "perhaps"? Perhaps because he judged that this alone was to be understood and called truly inmost, than which nothing is more inward. An inmost movement, therefore, there is not, except that which is either from the inmost, or is unto the inmost. Because, therefore, the divine nature [1041D] alone is within to every nature, it alone is inmost to every nature; whose movement, without movement toward the creature, is alone called inmost in reality, because it is from that than which nothing more inmost is found. But the movement of the creature toward the Creator, however secret and penetrating, cannot properly be called inmost; because it is from that which is outside, toward which it is: and when it is led to touch him who is inmost, the way is not opened to it as far as his inmost parts. Because, therefore, it comes in some respect and outwardly, going out from the creature, and subsists outwardly, not reaching to the penetrating of the inmost parts of the Creator, it ought by no means to be called inmost; although, however, for the reason that it is from an inner nature unto the inmost, it can fittingly be called inmost: on account of this he says well, "perhaps inmost," that he might intimate [1042A] that it could according to something be called inmost, which according to every mode was not inmost. For it can be called inmost by property, not by comparison. Inmost to that from which it is; but not to that toward which it is.

There follows: « And the exemplative of the reductive and active of the subjects. » In the enumeration above he expressed the power of true love toward God, whether how great it is, I know not; but I think, as great as it can be told. Now, in what follows, he shows the force and effect of the same love toward the neighbor. There he demonstrated its movement and its turning to higher things, by which they thirst after their Creator: but here he sets forth its movement, and its turning to lower and nearer things, by which they do not withdraw from one another. The movement, therefore, of love, which there toward higher things [1042B] is called intent, and inmost, and inflexible, here toward the subject and lower things is named the exemplative of the reductive and active. For that movement, which in the higher is contemplation, in the lower is operation. It tends to higher things, that it may rest in them; it tends to lower things, that it may bring them back to itself. Upward, therefore, charity is moved, that it may abide there; downward, that it may return. Therefore the movement of charity in the higher beings is called reductive and active toward the lower. Reductive in this, that, kindling those beings with the same fire of charity, it turns them to their Creator. Active in this, that, illumining them with the brightness received, it composes them to his will. It is reductive, therefore, of the subjects, because it draws them to higher things. Active, because it disposes them in lower things. Reductive, that they may tend to God. [1042C] Active, that they may walk according to God. Of such a reductive and active movement of the subjects, therefore — that is, which brings back the subjects to those things which are above them, and disposes them to acting in themselves — the exemplative is that form of love, in which by the example of the higher beings it is shown to the subjects with how great an affection of charity they ought both to walk according to God, and to pant after him. Or so that it be read thus, the exemplative of the subjects, that is, that which is set before the subjects as an example: "reductively" and "actively" being pronounced adverbially, the same sense remaining. See, therefore, how charity spreads itself out, desiring to embrace all things in those blessed spirits, and those near to God, as though burning from close at hand, and fervent the more. The fire of love [1042D] is indeed brought back to higher things, while, thirsting for its own good through the love of God, it is moved also to lower and subject beings, wishing to gather to itself partakers and companions of its good. Loving, therefore, they bestow upon the subjects the form of loving, and, burning in themselves, they kindle others also with the flame of love. On account of this, therefore, their love has been made an example to the subjects of tending to higher things, and of walking according to the higher things, as though rewarming those, namely the subjects, and re-arousing them into a like warmth, so that they may similarly burn, though not equally. But that he says « rewarming, and re-arousing, » it is not so to be understood as though, being first extinguished and dead, they are again kindled and made alive; but that through the love coming from above they are aroused again to the loving and seeking of those things which are above. [1043A] Through the fire of love, therefore, they are as it were rewarmed and re-aroused, in whom the kindled flame of love is brought back again to the loving of those things from which, in the first and principal lovers, it arose upon the following and subject lovers. All these things, therefore, the surname of the Seraphim, or its manifestation, teaches. For in that they are surnamed Seraphim, that is, "the enkindling," or "the warming," it is both a surname to them, and a manifestation to us; because both to them the surname is expressed in the word, and to us the property of the surname is manifested in the interpretation of the word, since they would not be manifested unless they were surnamed. For those who are known to themselves by contemplation become known to us by surname: and therefore to them, as regards the word, it is called a surname; [1043B] to us, as regards the interpretation of the word, it is called a manifestation. That very surname, therefore, or manifestation of the Seraphim teaches all these things, that is, namely, their mobile about divine things, and unceasing; and it teaches also the hot, and the keen, and the superfervent of their intent, and inmost, and inflexible movement; and it teaches also the exemplative of the subjects, reductively and actively, which is in them, and is furnished by them to the subjects for reduction and action, that they may be rewarmed and re-aroused into a warmth like the warmth and fervor of the higher beings. The surname of the Seraphim teaches also the « fiery from heaven, and the purgative by holocaust, and the unveiled, and the inextinguishable. » Which is in them first from heaven, or descending divinely into them: then from them, [1043C] that they may burn and enkindle, be purged and purge, be revealed and reveal.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

And that very surname of the Seraphim teaches also their light-formed and illuminative property, holding itself always thus: thus, that is, in one and the same mode, and remaining without variety and mutability, and the pursuer of all dark obscuration, so that it does not draw near to it; and the manifester, so that nothing lies hidden outside it: all these things, therefore, the surname or manifestation of the Seraphim teaches. And all these things, as we have said, are in one love, and are one love: which is given to them from above, and is derived through them to the subjects.
