Of which love the author distinguishes a threefold power in those highest spirits by this enumeration: above them, in them, and beneath [1043D] them. Above them, mobile; in them, vital; beneath them, operative. Above them, through desire; in them, through sense; beneath them, through affection. Above them, seeking; in them, perceiving; beneath them, gathering. Above them, in that which they desire; in them, in that which they perceive; beneath them, on behalf of those whom they draw along with themselves to that which they perceive in themselves, and to that which they desire above themselves. On account of such wondrous operations of love he said so many things about it: in which he would perhaps have said the whole, if the whole could have been said. But we fear both, [1044A] if we be either negligent or fastidious. It is hard for us, in a matter so sweet, to deny anything which we have received: and again it seems to us rash to add anything which we ought not. What, do you think, is love? When shall the whole be told? Behold, we have said the « mobile » that is theirs, « and the unceasing, and the hot, and the keen, and the superfervent, and the intent, and the inmost, and the inflexible, and the exemplative, and the reductive, and the active, and the rewarming, and the re-arousing: » and this seemed to be much, and perhaps enough: except that other wondrous things still followed, I know not whether more wondrous. « Fiery, » he says, « from heaven, and purgative by holocaust. » Two things are to be noted, that he named it fiery, and that same fiery from heaven. For the fiery too is one thing from the earth, but is not like that which is [1044B] fiery from heaven. For it burns, and consumes, and lays waste, and destroys; nor can there be any fellowship with it for another. For those who draw near to it are harmed; and if they touch it at all, they already begin to be consumed. But that which is fiery from heaven burns sweetly; and it kindles indeed, but does not consume; and if it consumes anything, yet not unto harm, but unto purgation: for it consumes this, which would harm, if it were not consumed. Therefore after « fiery from heaven » there follows « purgative by holocaust; » because that fiery thing purges, and purges the whole, and purges from the whole, not only from the corruption of evil, but also from the defect of good. For there were certain things which had contracted the corruption of evil; there were certain things which had not yet received the perfection of good: and [1044C] both were to be purged; the one from corruption, the other from imperfection. Those which were on earth were to be purged from corruption; those which were in heaven were to be purged from imperfection. The heavenly, because in their first condition they were not perfect; the earthly, because after their first condition they had been corrupted. The heavenly have been purged from imperfection, when they are consummated in glorification; the earthly have been purged from corruption, when they are freed from sin. Of the heavenly, therefore, the purgation was not the amendment of an evil which they did not have; but the consummation of a good which they had in lesser measure. But of the earthly the purgation was first amendment; afterward consummation. Such a purgation, therefore, we understand in that spiritual and heavenly nature. But even if anyone should understand in those beings a purgation, [1044D] not of the corruption which was in them, because they were always clean, but of the perfect cleanness, to which no corruption could be present, this too he will understand fittingly. That « fiery from heaven, » therefore, by which they are inflamed, that they may burn, and are purged either from imperfection unto consummation, or unto full cleanness against all corruption, is « purgative by holocaust, » that is, universally purgative, or wholly purgative, because it purges the whole and purges from the whole; whether that no corruption may be present, or that no perfection may be lacking. For a holocaust is that which is wholly kindled, and wholly burned up.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.*

[1045]

[1045A]Long leisures demand a new beginning. A little above we had entered upon the discourse concerning the surname of the angels, where the author shows why such surnames or appellations have been attributed to the several orders of the angels. And concerning the first, and highest, order indeed, which is surnamed Seraphim, we have pursued, according to our capacity, the account of that appellation which has been given by theology. Now it remains that we also consider the account which he renders of the surname of the following order. He had first said that the surname, or manifestation, of the Seraphim teaches « their mobile, and the unceasing, and the hot, and the keen, and the superfervent, » which were added after these: but now he brings in and says that the surname, [1045B] or manifestation, of the Cherubim teaches « their knowable, and the deiform, » etc. For he says: « But the Cherubim itself. » Two things are to be repeated from what precedes, "the surname" and "teaches." As if he should say: The very surname of the Cherubim teaches, that is, in that they are surnamed Cherubim, which is interpreted *fullness of knowledge,* there is taught and signified « their knowable, » that is, the knowledge or acquaintance and science which they have; and by this name there is signified also « their deiform, » that is, the vision of God which is in them, because through the light of wisdom implanted in them they clearly contemplate the majesty of their Creator. There is signified also « the receptive of the highest bestowal of lights: » that is, it is signified that they have received the lights divinely given most highly [1045C] and most perfectly. For in that they are signified, by their very surname, to have the fullness of knowledge, they are assuredly taught to have received the light of that same wisdom from elsewhere; because, according to the saying of the Apostle: « If they had not received it, they could not have had it at all [n: (I Cor. IV)]. » But in that they have it, reception is noted; in that they have the fullness, advance is noted. And the order is this, that first they receive the light of wisdom divinely given, and afterward, illumined by that same light, they see and know God, the author of the light. Well, therefore, are they illumined, who are so illumined that they see and know him by whom they are illumined. Many are illumined that they may see other things, and see not him himself through whom they see. But it is no great thing to see a work, if you [1045D] know not the craftsman. A thing made cannot beatify, if you do not reach to the beauty of the one who wrought it. Therefore the surname of the Cherubim teaches « their knowable, and the deiform, and the receptive of the highest streaming-in of light. » Note the unusual compositions in that he says "deiform," and "streaming-in of light." The surname of the Cherubim teaches [1046A] also, namely, their contemplative in the first working power of the divine beauty, and their repleteness of wise handing-down, and their communicative of the copious wisdom given for the outpouring unto the second beings. Distinguish thus: The surname of the Cherubim teaches the contemplative of the divine beauty in the first working power. For in that they are called Cherubim, that is, full of knowledge, it is shown that through the light of the wisdom given they contemplate the divine beauty; since assuredly, whatever they knew, they would not be full of wisdom, if they did not know the divine beauty, from which, and in which, all things are beautifully and reasonably ordered. Because, therefore, they are full of wisdom, they assuredly contemplate the divine beauty. Nor do they contemplate it in any way whatever, [1046B] but in the first working power, so that, namely, being first and principally illumined by God, they may illumine the rest that come after them. For the divine power works first, and principally, and of itself in those who are nearest; but afterward through them in the others who follow. This, therefore, the surname of the Cherubim teaches, namely that the contemplation of the divine beauty is in them through the first working power; because God works first in them, that afterward he may work through them. It teaches also the repleteness of the wise handing-down, that is, it teaches that they are replete with divine grace: which by the wise handing-down of the Creator is distributed in participation to some more, and to some less. A wondrous excellence of theirs, therefore, is shown, because of that good which the wisdom [1046C] of the Creator handed down unequally unto the beauty of the universe, they are proclaimed to have not a part but the fullness. It can also be distinguished otherwise, so that it be said that the surname of the Cherubim teaches their contemplative replete, that is, their contemplation replete with the divine beauty, and with the wise handing-down, in the first working power. And it can be understood, not unfittingly, by the divine beauty and the wise handing-down, that the divine wisdom effects a greater beauty in its works from this very thing, that it did not settle its gifts to be participated by all in one and the same mode. Of which beauty, indeed, and of which handing-down, those highest spirits are for this reason replete; because the gifts which were given in part to the lower and subject beings, they have merited [1046D] to possess according to the fullness.

There follows: « And the communicative of the wisdom given copiously for the outpouring unto the second beings. » Again it must be repeated from what precedes. The surname of the Cherubim teaches their communicative, that is, the communication, or participation, of the wisdom given copiously for the outpouring, [1047A] that is, that in communicating they participate not slightly, but copiously, in the wisdom given to them for the outpouring. Which outpouring, namely, was first made in them copiously, that through them it might afterward be made unto the second beings: inasmuch as the light of wisdom, which is first poured copiously into them from above, is afterward through them transfused unto the second, that is, the following orders, to be illumined. This fullness, therefore, and dignity, and excellence in the gift of wisdom, and in the light of truth, the surname of the Cherubim teaches.

There follows: « But it itself, of the highest and compacted seats, diligently to be exalted from all the ignominy of subjection, and bearing upward supramundanely unto the summit, and by every extremity ineffably unto the most sublime, and about the truly lofty placed with all its powers unchangeably [1047B] and stably: and receptive of the divine coming-upon in all impassibility and immateriality, and God-bearing, and in a servile manner opened unto the divine receptions. » After the surname of the Seraphim, and of the Cherubim, he passes to explaining the surname of the Thrones. As if he should say: As the surname of the Seraphim signifies the ardor of love, and the surname of the Cherubim the brightness of knowledge, so also the surname of the Thrones signifies the loftiness of dignity, for the reason that the invisible Founder, sitting in them, through them disposes all subject things by judging. For on account of this dignity and excellence of the divine judgment, which is exercised through them, he named the Thrones themselves the highest and compacted seats; the highest, [1047C] on account of their dignity; compacted, on account of their truth. For it is manifest that thrones are the seats of those who reign and judge. And to the one reigning belongs sublimity, but to the one judging belongs truth. And therefore he deservedly named the Thrones themselves the highest seats, because he who reigns in them has no superior; and compacted, because he who judges in them does not depart from the truth. What is "compacted"? Fitly and suitably joined together. See the joining of the seats of God. The joining of the seats of God is the fittingness of judgments. Every judgment infers one thing from another. From fault, punishment; from justice, glory; from merit, reward; from the quality of the work, the quality of the retribution. It finds fault, it adjudges punishment. It finds justice, it adjudges glory. Well is it joined, that seat is compacted. Well does it accord and [1047D] fitly cohere. Punishment to fault, glory to justice. If glory were joined to fault, and punishment to justice, they would not accord with one another, nor would the judgment have a compacted seat. The compaction, therefore, of the seats is the truth of the judgments. But it must be known that every lower judgment, when it comes into question or contradiction, is wont to take from a higher either testimony or confirmation. But the highest judgment, because it has not another above itself by which it may be confirmed; by right the seats of God are named not only compacted through truth, but also highest through dignity. But if we read not "compacted," but "raised up," which from the ambiguity of the Greek word can likewise be understood: this is signified, that those heavenly spirits, over whom God presides for judging, have merited to be singularly high [1048A] in glory, not through the loftiness of nature, but through the raising-up of grace. Let him say, therefore: « But it itself, of the highest and compacted seats, » that is, of the Thrones (again from what precedes "the surname" must be supplied) teaches this, namely that those same Thrones are exalted, or have been exalted, diligently, that is, perfectly, from all the ignominy of subjection, that is, from all ignominious subjection. For the more perfectly they are subject to the presiding God, the more truly they have merited to be raised above all other things through him, and in him. Because, therefore, thrones signify not subjection, but dominion, when the word of God names those heavenly spirits thrones, in that very thing he demonstrates them to be perfectly ruling, and free from all subjection. So, therefore, the surname of the Thrones teaches them to be exalted from all [1048B] the ignominy of subjection; it teaches also their bearing upward, that is, their raising-up unto the summit: and this supramundanely, that is, spiritually or invisibly, namely by a spiritual and invisible exaltation, and one transcending all mundane and visible loftiness. The surname of the Thrones, therefore, not only teaches them to be preferred to the lower beings through dominion; but also, through sublimation, to be exalted unto the summit, so that, namely, between them and him who is the highest there be nothing in the middle.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

There follows: « And by every extremity ineffably unto the most sublime: and about the truly lofty placed with all its powers unchangeably, and stably. [1048C]" Namely, the surname of the Thrones teaches the placed, that is, their placing, or their establishment, made unchangeably, and stably, with all its powers, that is, with every kind of power, and unshaken strength. Made, I say, ineffably far from all extremity, that is, beyond every end, unto the most sublime, and about the truly lofty. For where would the seat of God be, except where God sits? Where God dwells, where he reigns, where he sits, where he rests, where his thrones are, and his seats. See how far they are from us. How far is the highest majesty from all subjection, the eternal unchangeableness from all corruption. Where majesty is, there is a throne; where unchangeableness is, there is a seat. A throne signifies unchangeableness. Therefore in that very eternity, in that very unchangeableness, the seats of God [1048D] have been placed. And how far this is from all extremity, who can tell? What is extremity? An end: where an end is, there is extremity. An end at the top, an end at the bottom. In both the creature has an end. The end is at the bottom, where the falling-away ceases, lest that which is something go into nothing. The end is at the top, where the advancing sets a limit to itself, lest that which is great extend itself beyond measure. By how much, then, do those surpass the lowest things, who ineffably transcend the highest? We can still, not unfittingly, interpret those extremities in another mode. For all visible things are extremes; because, as to one ascending upward from the lowest there is a highest, than which nothing is higher, so to one coming forth outward from the inmost there is an extreme, than which nothing is more outward. The divine benches, therefore, as, in that they are called thrones, [1049A] they are shown to be excellently raised up, through dignity, in the summit, above the subjection of the lowest things, so also, in that they are called seats, they are demonstrated to be placed unchangeably, through stability, in the inmost, above all fluctuation of the extremes. And yet neither, raised up on high, can they be made equal to the ineffable loftiness under which they are; nor, gathered to the inner parts, can they be compared to the simple unity about which they are. On account of this, therefore, they are called seats, because they are both underneath, through the ineffable majesty, to him who presides above, and are in a circuit, through the unchangeable unity, to him who rests within. This, therefore, the surname of the Thrones teaches: and not only this, but it teaches also « the receptive of the divine coming-upon, » that is, that they receive the divinity coming upon them from above in all impassibility and [1049B] immateriality, that is, incorruptly and purely. For because they receive it in purity, they receive it immaterially; and again, because they receive it flowing in sweetly, without labor and weariness, they receive it impassibly. Those who advance to the knowledge of the hidden divinity through study and labor, these receive the divine coming-upon passibly. Again, those to whom the hidden divinity presses itself in to be known through outward signs and corporeal figures, to these it comes in some manner as it were materially. Because, therefore, those highest spirits, in perceiving the knowledge of the divinity, neither advance by study, nor are taught by material figures: rightly are they declared to receive the divine coming-upon both impassibly and immaterially.

[1049C]There follows: « And God-bearing, and in a servant's manner opened unto the divine receptions. » From what precedes it must again be supplied, "the surname of the Thrones teaches," namely their God-bearing, that is, that they bear God presiding over them; and "opened in a servant's manner unto the divine receptions," that is, that in a servant's manner, that is, obediently, they open themselves and voluntarily fit themselves, that they may receive into themselves God himself coming, inasmuch as the will subject to the divine operation may respond, and the sacred ministry may be proved to be not of constraint, but of love. A right order. First the benches of God are raised up through dignity; then they are placed on account of stability; afterward they receive God coming into themselves, and bear him. Lastly, because they are rational vehicles, that the work may be one of felicity, to the ministry they join desire, [1049D] and will.

There follows: « This indeed is the declaration of their names, as far as it concerns us. » He continues himself unto what follows. For, the surnames of the three orders having been told, he now passes to defining and setting forth their hierarchy, that is, their sacred power. « This indeed, » namely this which we have said above, « is the declaration of their names, » namely of the three orders. It is, I say, as far as it concerns us, that is, as far as it seems to us, or as far as it concerns us it is the declaration of their names, that is, for our sake, that they may be declared to us through names, not for their own sake, who can be known to themselves, and without names.

There follows: « But it must be told, what hierarchy [1050A] of theirs we deem it to be. » As if he should say: Thus far we have spoken concerning their surname; but now we ought to speak concerning their sacred power, of what sort we deem it to be. « For that the speculation of every hierarchy, depending ineffably upon a deiformity imitating God, is; and that every hierarchic action is divided into a sacred participation, and a handing-down of pure purgation, and of divine light, and of perfective knowledge: I think has already been sufficiently said by us. » The sense is this: I think it has already been sufficiently said by us, that the speculation of every hierarchy, that is, the general definition of the hierarchy, depends ineffably upon deiformity, that is, upon likeness to God: a likeness, I say, imitating God, that is, upon a likeness of the imitation of God. Enough, he says, [1050B] I think has already been said, what the hierarchy is, defined generally, namely deiformity, that is, a conformation or likeness to God, which is in those who imitate God. For above, in the third chapter, he defines the hierarchy universally thus. The hierarchy is likeness and unity to God, as far as is possible. On account of this, therefore, I think it has now been sufficiently said what the hierarchy is, namely as far as pertains to the general definition. Likewise I think it has now been sufficiently said, that every hierarchic action is divided, that is, that every hierarchic action is divided into a sacred participation, and a handing-down of pure purgation, and of divine light, and of perfective knowledge. For the action of every sacred power consists either in this, [1050C] that they participate from a higher; or in this, that they hand down to the lower purgation, and illumination, and perfection. Threefold is the grace, which they exercise in two modes, by receiving, and by imparting: first is purgation unto purity; then illumination unto truth; then perfection unto goodness. For this is the perfective knowledge, which perfects, and makes perfect, when from the habit of virtue truth is perceived. « But now let us worthily go on to tell of the most excellent intellects, how already according to them the hierarchy is manifested by the sacred words. » As if he should say: Since above the definition and the division of the hierarchy have been universally and sufficiently given by us; now let us go on worthily to tell of the most excellent intellects, that is, the highest spirits, [1050D] namely the Seraphim, the Cherubim, and the Thrones, in whom the first angelic hierarchy is ordered. Let us go on, I say, to tell how the hierarchy according to them, that is, how their hierarchy is manifested by the sacred words. For this the order of reason demands, that after the general definition and division of the hierarchy, the discourse descend to the special treatment of it. First indeed, concerning the first hierarchy, which consists in those three highest orders, it must be asked of what sort the sacred words manifest it to be. And lest perhaps anyone should think their sacred power to be diverse for this reason, that their orders are found to be differing, he says that in all three there is one and altogether like hierarchy, [1051A] inasmuch, namely, as they are all in the first place united to the highest and principal hierarchy, and immediately obtain from it the dignity of their sacred power.

For this is what he subjoins, saying: « In the first essences, which, placed after their substantifying divinity, and ordered as it were in its vestibules, are made to be above all invisible and visible power, it must be deemed that there is a proper and altogether uniform hierarchy. » The first essences are those highest and principal three orders of angels, which among all creatures are first, and, after their substantifying divinity, that is, that which makes them subsist, are first constituted, and ordered as it were in its vestibules, that is [1051B] so near, that whatever is beyond, is only in it, and is itself the divinity. In those first essences, therefore, which, so placed and ordered, are next to the divinity, superexcellent, and transgressing every made power, invisible and visible, that is, the power of every making, or creature, invisible and visible; in those essences, I say, it must be deemed that there is a hierarchy, that is, a sacred power; proper, that is, distinct, and differing from others in itself, and altogether uniform, that is, like and equal among themselves. For in that sacred power which they have, as they are more excellent than all others, so among themselves they exist altogether equal, so that each in that which they receive immediately from the highest divinity have not others superior; and in that which to the lower beings [1051C] they bestow out of the divine participation, all alike exist as first dispensers. In this, therefore, the hierarchy is one, and alike, in the three.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

There follows: « They are therefore to be reckoned pure: not as freed from unclean stains and defilements, nor as receptive of material phantasms, but as clean of all diminution, and higher; and, above every over-established temple, set on high above all things according to the most exalted chastity, with all their most God-formed virtues; and by their own proper motion through themselves, and by that same motion, according to the unturnable [character] of the order that loves God, ineffably received; and altogether knowing no contumely in things subordinate, but holding the incapable-of-falling and also unchangeable [1051D] most pure placement of their own God-formed specialty. » Above he said that in those three most excellent orders of angels there is one, and an altogether similar, hierarchy — that is, a sacred power; now, consequently, he adds that those things which pertain to hierarchical action — that is, to the operation of the sacred power — are perfectly present in them, namely purgation, illumination, perfection. These three every sacred power ordered under the supreme power exercises in two ways, namely by receiving from what is above and imparting to what is below. He says, therefore, that those first essences, in which the first hierarchy is ordered, have purgation, which they both receive from the divinity above, that they may be purged, and confer on those below, that they may purge. But they receive purgation not as though they were to be freed from some [1052A] uncleanness or corruption, but as to be perfected from all imperfection and diminution — this is what he says: They are to be reckoned pure, namely those first essences; pure, I say, yet not as though freed from certain unclean stains and defilements, since they never had any uncleanness or defilement; nor pure as though receptive of material phantasms, since they do not at all receive material phantasms, and therefore do not need to be purged of them, since they do not have them at all; but rather pure as clean, and estranged from all diminution and imperfection. Indeed, by unclean stains and material phantasms he has expressed two kinds of spiritual defilement: one which is in depraved desires, the other [1052B] which is in vain thoughts. For depraved desires defile the cleanness of the heart like mud, while vain thoughts, like a certain dust sprinkled over it, becloud its brightness. Since therefore those most pure essences of the highest spirits take on no corruption either in depraved desires or in vain thoughts, they consequently do not require to be cleansed either from unclean stains or from material phantasms. They are therefore to be understood as pure, not as purified from corruption, but as clean from imperfection; and not only estranged from imperfection, but also, through the excellence of their perfection, higher than all the rest that are perfect, and set on high above them.

[1052C]For this is what follows: « And higher » — supply: they are to be reckoned to be, those first essences — « and set on high above the over-established temple of all, » that is, above every rational creature in which God dwells, and which, through him dwelling within it, is confirmed toward the highest good. Above all these those first essences are set on high according to their most excellent chastity — that is, more excellent than the chastity of all the rest; a chastity, I say, existing in all their virtues, that is, in their virtues of every kind, the most God-formed, that is, drawing nearer to conformity with God than the virtues of others draw near. Thus, therefore, those first essences are to be reckoned both to be pure, and, through the excellence of their purity, higher than the rest, and set on high [1052D] above them. And they are also to be reckoned to have been ineffably received, for the participating of that purity in God, by their own motion through themselves — that is, without a mediator; and by that same motion — that is, ever straining without deviation toward the selfsame thing; according to the unturnable — that is, according to the unturnableness of the order that loves God. For those who never grow lukewarm in love are never bent aside or turned away from their straining.

There follows: « And in things subordinate knowing no contumely at all — supply: they are to be reckoned. For just as they participate the purity of God from above without diminution, so they offer it to be participated below without elation; in this very thing imitating God, « who gives to all abundantly, and does not upbraid [n: (Jac. I)]. » [1053A] For this reason, he says, « they are to be reckoned as knowing no contumely in things subordinate, but rather as holding the incapable-of-falling and also unchangeable placement of their own God-formed specialty. » By 'their own God-formed specialty' he understands the excellent and singular beauty of divine conformation, which is in those highest essences; in which they, most pure and most perfect, are so placed and grounded that they have a placement incapable of falling, over which a fall cannot hold sway; and rightly incapable of falling, inasmuch as it is unchangeable, or immutable. The sum, then, of this chapter is this: that those first and principal essences participate their purgation, or purity, without diminution, offer it to be participated without elation, and possess it uncorrupted without mutability. [1053B] And these things, indeed, have been said concerning their purgation.

Afterward he adds concerning illumination, saying: « And again, contemplative of sensible symbols, or speculative of intellectual things, not as brought back into the divine by the variety of sacred-writing theory; but as filled with the higher light of every immaterial knowledge, and, as far as is lawful, replete with the contemplation of the form-making and principal beauty, both super-essential and thrice-shining; and likewise made worthy by the communion of Jesus: they do not figure the deiform likeness formatively in sacredly-fashioned images; but as truly drawing near to him himself in the first participation of the knowledge of his deiform lights; and because what is like to God has been substantially bestowed on them. And they communicate [1053C] in this manner, as is possible, in his pre-operative power, in his deiform and human virtues. » All this has been said concerning illumination. As if he said: Not only are those first essences to be reckoned to have purgation and to be pure; but again — that is, further — they are to be reckoned to have illumination, and to be contemplative of sensible symbols, and speculative of intellectual things. Sensible symbols are material signs — whether in creatures, or in the Scriptures, or in the divine sacraments — set forth for the demonstration of invisible things; whose mystical signification and invisible truth those highest angelic spirits recognize by contemplating through divine illumination. They also behold, through that same illumination, intellectual things [1053D] — supply: symbols — that is, spiritual theophanies, that is, divine manifestations, through which the nature of the hidden and invisible divinity is manifested to them within. Or it can be read thus: They — namely the essences — are to be reckoned to be contemplative of sensible symbols, and speculative of intellectual things, and not (supply) of symbols. For outwardly, in sensible things, where there are material signs, there are symbols; but inwardly, in intellectual things, where there are no signs but truth, there are no symbols. Therefore in sensible sacred symbols they contemplate the signs of truth, but inwardly, in intellectual things, they behold the naked truth without signs. Therefore they are to be reckoned to be contemplative of sensible symbols, and likewise [1054A] speculative of intellectual things; yet not as brought back into the divine — that is, into the divine cognition — by variety, that is, by the manifold teaching of theory, that is, of divine Scripture; theory, I say, of the sacred-writing [kind], because, namely, it writes and speaks about divine and sacred things. For, instructed by the simple illumination of divine contemplation, not by the varied and manifold teaching of the Scriptures, they contemplate all the sacred figurations and sensible signs which, whether in the Old or in the New Testament — such as the tabernacle of the covenant, and the ark of the testament, and other things of this kind, which Scripture sets forth for the demonstration of invisible things; the visible appearances of creatures too, through which invisible things are demonstrated; the mystical revelations also made through sensible formations — all these, namely, [1054B] sacred symbols they contemplate. And not only these things that are outward, but intellectual things too, which shine within through pure and naked truth, they behold. But to the beholding of all these things, and to the divine cognition, they are not to be reckoned to have been brought back by the variety of sacred-writing theory — that is, by the manifold teaching of the divine Scriptures, which is necessary only for this: that the minds of men, estranged from God, may be brought back to the knowledge of truth, and, scattered through diverse things, may be gathered into one. Therefore those highest essences are not to be reckoned to have the cognition of all these things as though instructed through the teaching of the Scriptures, but rather as filled with a higher light — that is, with a more excellent cognition of every immaterial, that is spiritual, knowledge. For higher and [1054C] more worthy is the light of cognition that is poured in within through invisible inspiration than that which is possessed from without through the instruction of teaching. And so those highest essences are to be reckoned to have the cognition of things visible and invisible, inasmuch as they are filled with such a light; and also as replete with the contemplation of the form-making, principal, super-essential, and thrice-shining beauty. Replete, I say, as far as is lawful — that is, as is granted to the possibility of the creature. Now he signifies the divine beauty, which is form-making, because according to itself it formed all things made by itself; and is principal, because, though it is the form of all things, it itself received its form from no other; and is super-essential, because it not only goes before through [1054D] form, in that it is the exemplar of all things, but also transcends through essence, in that it is the creatress of all things.

There follows: « And of the thrice-shining » — namely beauty — « they are to be reckoned replete with the contemplation. » By 'thrice-shining beauty' he signifies that same divine beauty, which shines in three, though it itself is not triple, but one: Father, and Son, and Holy Spirit are three persons; but God is one, the Godhead one, the nature one, the essence one, the beauty one. You see the Father: you see that shining beauty in the Father; you see the Son: you see that shining beauty in the Son; you see the Holy Spirit: you see that shining beauty in the Holy Spirit. So it, as it were, shines thrice, because it shines in three, [1055A] though it itself is not triple; but it is one that shines. The Father is another in person: there it shines. The Son is another in person: there it shines. The Holy Spirit is another in person: there it shines. And yet, of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, as the Godhead is one and the nature one, so the beauty is one. Therefore, he says, « they are to be reckoned replete with the contemplation of the thrice-shining beauty. » What wonder, then, is it if those highest essences perfectly know the divine works, visible and invisible — they who are full of the contemplation of the Creator himself, as far, namely, as is possible for a creature.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

There follows: « And likewise made worthy by the communion of Jesus, » — supply: they are to be reckoned. Note [1055B] that there is a compound there, « made-worthy, » that is, made worthy. Not only, he says, did they merit to receive the contemplation and cognition of the divinity, but they were also made worthy — by the communion of that salvation which was accomplished in the humanity of Jesus, both by knowing and by participating. For they had communion with Jesus: the mystery of his incarnation — both by foretelling it before it came to pass, and by ministering when it came to pass, and, after it was accomplished, by rejoicing together with man, restored to eternity, in the same fellowship of blessedness. Afterward he adds, showing that they receive the divine contemplation without any figures, illumined immediately by the divinity itself. For just as above he demonstrated that they read and draw the cognition of all things visible and invisible not through outward teaching, [1055C] but through the inner [teaching] in the very wisdom of God; so now he demonstrates that they receive that same divine contemplation not through any intervening forms or images, but from the divinity itself, in the first place, immediately, nakedly, and purely. « They do not, he says, figure the deiform likeness formatively in sacredly-fashioned images; but rather they figure it as truly drawing near to it, » namely to the deiform likeness, or to the divinity, « drawing near, » I say, in the first participation of the knowledge « of his deiform lights, » that is, of his deifying illuminations. The deiform likeness is the divine contemplation itself; because, while, illumined through it, they are shining, they in a certain manner receive the likeness of the illuminating light itself. But in this [1055D] deiform likeness they do not figure themselves, nor do they acquire it for themselves through any sacred images formed and fashioned — that is, composed for the demonstration of spiritual things; as do men, who through visible and material demonstrations in the sacred discourse are instructed unto the cognition of invisible things. Therefore those highest essences are not illumined with the divine contemplation by means of such things. But, as truly drawing near to it — that is, drawing near to truth itself not through another, but through truth itself, since between them and truth there is nothing in between; and therefore they are drawing near in the first participation of knowledge, because in the first place they participate the knowledge of his deiform lights; because immediately they contemplate, and know, and recognize his deiform — that is, deifying — lights; [1056A] because those illumined by them through the divine likeness they in a certain manner make gods.

There follows: « And because what is like to God has been substantially bestowed on them. » For this reason too, he says, they are truly, and in the first participation, drawing near to the Divinity itself; because what is like to God has been bestowed on them — that is, because the likeness of God has been substantially bestowed on them. For that they are like to God they have not from another, or through another; but because they receive the divinity itself substantially, nakedly, and purely. Or, what is like to God has been bestowed on them most subordinately, because in the nearest place they are subordinate, so that they receive the likeness of God itself by first participation.

There follows: « They communicate in this manner, as is possible, in his pre-operative power, in his deiform and [1056B] human virtues. » He concludes what was said above. For because they are replete in this manner — that is, those highest essences [are replete] with the contemplation of the principal beauty — therefore they communicate in the deiform virtues; and because they are made worthy by the communion of Jesus, therefore they communicate in the human virtues; and because they participate in the first and nearest place, therefore they communicate in the pre-operative power of God, which, namely, first operates in them, and afterward operates through them. Or he calls 'the human virtues of Jesus' his virtues [shown] from his humanity — that is, shown from clemency or kindness; so that the sense is: Since, just as they communicate in his virtues in the contemplation of his majesty, so also they communicate in the co-operation of his kindness. They communicate with him through contemplation in his majesty; they communicate [1056C] with him through the ministry of co-operation in our redemption. Thus, therefore, they communicate in his deiform and human virtues; and this is as far as is possible for them, according to the measure of grace given. Thus far it has been said concerning their illumination. Now, in the third place, he adds concerning perfection.

There follows: « And likewise perfect, not as illumined unto analytic knowledge by the sacred variety, but as filled with the first and supereminent deification, according to the most excellent knowledge — as far as [is possible] in angels — of the divine works. » As if he said: Not only are they to be reckoned pure and illumined; but also perfect in the knowledge received from the habit of virtue, yet not as illumined, or instructed [1056D] [in] analytic knowledge or discipline — that is, resolutory — by the sacred variety, that is, by the manifold sacred teaching of Scripture. For they are not, like men, instructed outwardly by the multiplicity of speech, so that for them, through disputations and distinctions, the things that are hidden and intricate are resolved in knowledge and discipline; but rather they receive to be illumined through simple contemplation within, so that from their cognition nothing of the things that can be known is hidden. Thus, therefore, they are to be reckoned to be perfect, not as illumined unto a resolutive and explicable knowledge through the variety of the sacred discourse; but as filled with the first and supereminent deification — that is, with the divine illumination by which they are illumined before all the other celestial essences, according to the knowledge of the divine works, [1057A] a most excellent knowledge, as far, namely, as it can be excellent in angels.

« For not through other holy essences, but sanctified by the Divinity itself, they are extended immediately into it; they are placed, with a virtue and order supereminent above all, unto an altogether most chaste fortitude, and are led into the contemplation of the immaterial and invisible beauty, as far as is lawful; and unto the knowable reasons of the divine works, as the first essences and [those] around God, they are bent, and by the very Prince of perfection are most exaltedly sanctified. » He proves in sum that those essences receive the divine illumination before all others, and above all other celestial essences; because, not through other holy essences, but sanctified by the Divinity itself, they are extended immediately into it through love and [1057B] exaltation, according to the virtue and order — that is, the grace and dignity — which they have supereminent above all. And so, thus immediately joined to the Divinity, they receive from it alone both purgation, and illumination, and perfection — this is what he says: they are placed, that is, established, unto an altogether most chaste fortitude, or inflexibility — that is, a strong and inflexible and incorruptible chastity. They are also led, through illumination, as far as is lawful — that is, as is permitted or granted — into contemplation, unto the contemplating of the immaterial and invisible beauty of the Creator. They are also bent, as into soft wax, or informed, or instructed, or even perfected unto the knowable reasons of the divine works, as the first essences [1057C] and [those] standing in the nearest place around God, and which are most exaltedly sanctified by the very Prince of perfection — namely the Divinity.

There follows: « This, therefore, the theologians also openly declare: that the subordinate dispositions of the celestial essences are indeed instructed in the deiform sciences by those set over them in an ordered way; but that those higher than all are illumined unto teaching by the Divinity itself, as far as is lawful. » By authority he proves what he had said: namely, that the supreme orders of angels are illumined by the Divinity alone; but that the lower are instructed by the higher. For this the theologians — that is, the prophets, and those holy men who spoke about celestial and divine things — openly declare: that the subordinate dispositions — that is, the lower orders of the celestial essences — [1057D] are instructed in an ordered way, that is, beautifully and fittingly, unto the deiform sciences, by those set over them, that is, by the orders placed above; but that those which are higher than all are illumined unto teaching by the Divinity itself, as far as is lawful — that is, as is possible for them.

There follows: « For some of them they introduce as sacredly instructed by those before them, that the Lord of the celestial powers, and the King of glory, has been received into the heavens according to his humanity. » He sets forth an example where the lower celestial essences were instructed by the higher: namely, where the theologians introduce some of them as instructed by those before them, or by the higher, so that they might know that God, the Lord of the celestial powers, and the King of glory, was received into the heavens according to his humanity. For thus [1058A] it is written that, when the Savior, according to the flesh he had taken, was ascending into heaven, some of the angels, still ignorant of the exaltation of his humanity, and therefore marveling, and saying: « Who is this King of glory? » it was said by others, more fully illumined: The Lord of powers, he himself is the King of glory. » [n: (Psal. XXIII.)] In which it is proved that at times some are instructed by others.

There follows: « But some [they introduce as] inquiring of Jesus himself, and learning the knowledge of his divine action [performed] for us; and [they introduce] Jesus himself as immediately teaching them, and, bestowing it beforehand upon them, manifesting his human kindness. For I, he says, dispute justice, and the judgment of salvation. » This example is taken from Isaiah, to prove that the supreme orders [1058B] of angels are illumined by God alone unto the knowledge of truth. But some celestial essences alone the theologians themselves introduce as inquiring — that is, making inquiry of Jesus himself; and learning, not from another, but from him who is God, the knowledge of his divine action displayed for us. By the divine action of Jesus himself he means the passion and death which he underwent for us. Which action is therefore called divine, because it was possible for God alone that, through death, he should destroy the power of death. The knowledge of this action, then, they introduce the celestial essences inquiring of Jesus himself, where, beholding the garment of the assumed flesh made bloody with the blood of the passion, they say: « Who is this that comes from Edom, with dyed garments from Bosra? [n: (Isai. LXIII.)] » And thereafter: « Why [1058C] then is thy garment red, and thy raiment like theirs that tread in the winepress? [n: (ibid.)] » Thus, therefore, they introduce them as inquiring and desiring to learn. They also introduce Jesus himself as teaching them immediately, and through his very self, and manifesting to them his human — that is, clement — kindness, which he displayed to us. Which manifestation he confers on them, bestowing it beforehand — that is, granting before all others the knowledge of his operation. « I, » he says, « dispute justice, and the judgment of salvation [n: (ibid.)]. » Justice and the judgment of salvation — that is, of the Savior, or of salvation — signifies the redemption of the human race. In which there was both justice, inasmuch, namely, as the Maker called back his own creature from an alien domination; [1058D] and judgment, inasmuch as he powerfully cast out the devil, the invader of another's right, from the man whom he possessed. And he says that he disputes this justice and judgment for this reason: because that [work] which pertained to the sufferings of the assumed flesh he fulfilled with labor, and as it were with a certain struggle.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

There follows: « But I marvel that even the first of the celestial essences, and [those] alone at once supereminent above all in the divine illuminations, seek questions reverently, as though mediated. » This looks back to what is said, that the highest essences are immediately illumined and instructed by God; for the contrary seems to be the case, that in this passage they are found inquiring not only of their superior, but also of one another, and as it were wishing to learn. But it must be known that this questioning is not [1059A] an inquiry after teaching, but a profession of ignorance. For they question, showing that they do not know, and that they have need to be taught. But where all question, they assuredly show that they ought to be taught from elsewhere. They question, therefore, among themselves, [being] to be taught from above themselves. Because so mutual a questioning seems to be an inquiry of teaching and knowledge from one another, whereas it is established that they are instructed by their superior alone, therefore he says: I marvel that those first and principal of the celestial essences — that is, among the celestial essences, and so far supereminent above all the other celestial essences at once in the divine illuminations — I marvel, I say, that they seek questions, that is, that they make questions seeking knowledge, reverently, that is, humbly like the humble and lower, and as though not attending to the excellence of their own illumination; and [1059B] this is what he says, « as mediated, » that is, like those who have something in between themselves and God, and need to be instructed by others; or 'as mediated,' because they are not truly mediated, since they make no question to others; nor yet truly immediate, since they refer their question not to their own principle, but to one another.

There follows: « For indeed they do not thence ask: Why thy red garments? » He proves that they truly make their question 'as mediated,' because they direct their words not first to Jesus himself, but to one another, saying: « Who is this that comes from Edom, with dyed garments from Bosra? » For they do not, he says, thence ask — that is, they do not begin their questioning from him — 'Why thy red garments?' where they would direct their speech immediately to Jesus himself; [1059C] but first among themselves, as though mediately: « Who is this, » and the rest. Whence it is a marvel that those supreme essences, though they are first, and nearest to the Divinity, make their questions as though mediated.

There follows: « But among themselves they deliberate before asking, showing that they learn, and seek the deiform knowledge. » He now resolves the question, namely why those highest essences, though they are nearest to God, make their question among themselves; namely, because among themselves they deliberate about asking, lest perhaps by too eager a questioning they leap ahead of, or anticipate, that illumination which comes to be in them through the divine procession — that is, through the divine grace [1059D] proceeding to illumine them; showing also, by the very deliberation of their questioning, that they seek the deiform knowledge. For in that they ask, they signify that they seek knowledge; but in that they first confer among themselves, questioning and deliberating, they demonstrate that they do not dare to anticipate the divine procession into themselves.

For this is what follows: « But not leaping ahead of the illumination implanted through the divine procession. » For they seek knowledge, and therefore they ask, but not leaping ahead of the divine illumination, until it offers itself, proceeding of its own accord into them; therefore they deliberate among themselves, and do not first dare to question Jesus until he himself offers himself, saying: « I am he who speaks justice. » Then at last, [1060A] having taken confidence, they direct their question to him: « Why is thy raiment red, and thy garment like theirs that tread in the winepress? »

There follows: « Is not, then, the first hierarchy of the celestial intellects, sanctified by the very Prince of perfection in that it is extended immediately into it, purged, and illumined, and perfected — with a most holy purgation, with much light, with a fore-perfected consummation filling it proportionally? » The sense is this: Is not, then — 'num' for 'nonne' — the first hierarchy of the celestial intellects purged, and illumined, and perfected? Sanctified by the very Prince of perfection — that is, by the divinity itself, which is the prince and principle of all sanctification. Sanctified, I say, in that the divinity itself is extended immediately into it, [1060B] illuminating, or irradiating, or filling it proportionally — that is, by a participation differing from the illumination of the rest — with a most holy purgation, and with much light, and with a fore-perfected — that is, super-excellent — consummation or perfection. « It is purged, » I say, and « illumined, and perfected, that it may be pure from all lessening or imperfection, and full of the first light, » that is, of the [light] shining into it first before the others; and that it may be « perfect, participating the perfect knowledge and cognition first given to it. » But when he says « sanctified » — or, as perhaps it is more fittingly, though less usually, translated, 'made pontiff' by the very Prince of purgation — he signifies, as we said, the divinity itself, which in Greek is called teletarchia, that is, the prince of purgation, or [1060C] of sanctification.

There follows: « But, comprehending, this too I would perhaps not undeservedly have said. » As though it were asked what this purgation is, he answers that the taking-up of the divine knowledge in the rational soul is both purgation, and illumination, and perfection. Purgation, because it purges ignorance; illumination, because it illumines with divine cognition; perfection, because, by illuminating, it consummates by the knowledge of perfect teachings, or disciplines, according to the illumined habit — this is what he says: « Comprehending, » that is, briefly gathering into one the things said above; this too I would perhaps not undeservedly have said, that the taking-up of the divine knowledge is both purgation, and illumination, and perfection. [1060D] And he gives the reason why it is called purgation, and illumination, and perfection. « Purging, indeed, ignorance, inasmuch as the knowledge of perfect teachings is implanted according to order. » For this reason it is called purgation, inasmuch as it purges ignorance by implanted knowledge — that is, through the implanted knowledge of perfect teachings. Implanted, I say, according to order — that is, according to what the dignity and excellence of each order demands. « But illuminating by that very divine cognition, through which it also purges [the hierarchy] that does not contemplate beforehand. » For this reason it is called illumination, because it illumines each hierarchy with divine cognition, through which divine cognition, namely, it also purges that hierarchy. The hierarchy, I say, that does not contemplate before it is purged, as it is written: [1061A] « Blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God [n: (Matth. V)]. »

There follows: « Which it manifests through a higher illumination, and again perfecting, by that very light, according to habit, with the knowledge of the most lucid teachings. » Which hierarchy, namely, the very taking-up of the divine knowledge manifests through a higher illumination. For in this it declares it to be higher than all the rest: that it illuminates it with a higher and more excellent light of divine cognition.

There follows: « And again perfecting, by that very light, according to habit, with the knowledge of the most lucid teachings. » He does not distinguish illumination and perfection clearly enough, in that he seems to assign both to cognition and knowledge. Yet this [1061B] is the difference: that illumination properly pertains to that cognition which builds up knowledge; but perfection to that cognition which shows the form of good morals and the habit of the virtues. For there are certain things which are investigated only for the cognition of understanding; but certain things pertain especially to the understanding of doing. For the former, illumination is necessary; for the latter, perfection. For the cognition of truth does not make [a man] perfect, unless the habit of virtue follows. Therefore he calls 'most lucid teaching' that which consists in the habit of virtue, because experience is the mistress of understanding; and he knows virtue best who has learned it not by hearing alone, but also by tasting and by doing. In the experience and habit of virtue, the cognition of truth is perfected, which is begun in the illumination [1061C] of understanding alone. All of which, because it is acquired through the taking-up of the divine knowledge, is therefore fittingly called both purgation, and illumination, and perfection.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

There follows: « This, therefore, as far as regards our knowledge, is the first disposition of the celestial essences, standing in the encircling of God, and around God, immediately, and simply, and incessantly encircling his eternal knowledge, according to a most excellent — as far as [is possible] in angels — ever-mobile placement. » He infers from what was said above. As if he said: Since that hierarchy of the highest spirits is first and principally both purged, and illumined, and perfected by the Divinity itself; therefore it is the first and principal [1061D] disposition, or the principal order, of the celestial essences, standing immediately, both in the encircling of, and around, God. For it stands through the immutability of contemplation; but it encircles through the life-giving and unceasing desire of eternal love. Therefore incessantly, because it does not fail from love; therefore simply, because, encircling, it does not withdraw from unity. That unity in the midst is the simplicity of the Divinity, around which and in the encircling of which they are, inasmuch as they immediately draw near to it. In the encircling too, because to his ineffable and hidden incomprehensibility they too are in a certain manner outside, into which they do not at all penetrate. Yet they go about it and desire it; and they strive toward the inner things; and, drawing near by cognition and by love, they become nearest, according to the most excellent placement which [1062A] they have beside God; most excellent, I say, as far as [is possible] in angels — that is, in comparison with that [placement] which is found in all the other angels. And I call the placement 'ever-mobile,' because both by contemplation they do not withdraw, and by desire they ever draw near. For all these reasons, then, this disposition is principal among all the other celestial essences; principal, I say, as far as regards our knowledge — namely, as far as it can be comprehended through our knowledge. He further adds, in praise of this hierarchy, one thing after another, heaping up many lofty and sublime things worthy of divine wonder, and certain profound things, and [things] known only to the pure. Such, he says, is that disposition, or order, of the first hierarchy — nearest in placement, most ardent in desire and intention; [1062B] which, although it cannot penetrate to the whole of what belongs to God, [which is] incomprehensible, yet with eternal love does not cease to go about [it] as far as it can be grasped and comprehended by a creature. But lest perhaps it should seem a pursuit of toil, not of happiness, if it were always to go about [it] and never attain [it], always to seek and never to grasp, always to desire and never to taste, he adds the fruit of the inquiry and the effect of the desire, saying: « Seeing indeed many and blessed contemplations purely, and simple and immediate splendors, illumined, and filled with divine nourishment. [Filled] with many things, indeed, by the first given outpouring, and by the unity — sole, domestic, and unifying — of the divine refreshment; and made worthy by much communion with God, and co-operation with him, unto that [likeness], as is possible, by the likeness of good dispositions [1062C] and actions; and knowing many of the divine things super-positively, and made a partaker, according to what is lawful, of the divine knowledge and cognition. » He said, as best he could say it: Three stand about the Trinity — the Seraphim by love, the Cherubim by cognition, the Thrones by domination. Here is the whole Trinity. Domination in the Father, wisdom in the Son, love in the Holy Spirit. It seemed that he had set the Seraphim above, because charity is supereminent; but behold, we also see that the throne belongs to the Father, and the Thrones are joined with the Father, and love is given to the Holy Spirit. If you think the Thrones subordinate, because they are named after the Seraphim and Cherubim, then think them also set above, because they pertain to the Father, who is named before the Son and the Holy Spirit. But in [1062D] the Trinity there is no degree. Father, and Son, and Holy Spirit is one God. Unity cannot be lower than itself. Therefore that hierarchy of the highest spirits, which is formed first and principally by the highest and supereminent domination of the Divinity, as far as concerns the most excellent likeness by which it imitates the simple unity, has no degree in domination — although, nevertheless, according to that measure of participation and distribution of grace (by which a creature cannot be made equal to the Creator) it has a difference. Therefore the domination of the three is one, by which they are universally set before all things subordinate — after the supreme and supereminent domination of the Deity — [but] are not subordinated to one another. Therefore they all together hold the first place, and they stand about [him], [1063A] each in the nearest placement, so that one does not come between another by position, nor anticipate [another] by participation, nor surpass [another] by domination. Thus is this first disposition, standing in the encircling of God, and around God, immediately — thus properly. And what is the fruit of so great a familiarity?



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

Hear what follows: « Seeing indeed many and blessed contemplations purely, and simple and immediate splendors, illumined, and filled with divine nourishment. » Do not, then, marvel if they go about [it] thus, and do not suffer themselves to be drawn far off. There is something there that draws [them]; and what is this? You are waiting for it to be said what that is which so incites the affection of those who desire, and provokes their love. But how do you think it will be told by us, which is not penetrated by them? They themselves have not yet entered, but [1063B] stand in the encircling; and it is a great thing for them to draw near to it, that they may see, and touch, and taste, and experience what the sweetness of the good is — [the good] set forth to its lovers without obstacle; yet they neither enter nor penetrate so as to comprehend it, and to grasp even to the whole how great is the immensity of the hidden. How, then, do you think to set it forth outwardly, from which even those who are within remain outside? That good, then, cannot be told by us — [the good] which holds those blessed spirits immovable beside itself through the joy of eternal contemplation, that they may not flow away toward perishable things; and again, moving [them] by desire, draws them to itself, that they may seek it with unceasing love. But lest that be altogether passed over in silence which cannot be told at all, hear what it is that both holds them beside itself and draws them to itself. A certain good, and great above [1063C] all those goods that you know, quite other — not only in difference, but in supereminence; yet, that it may be conveyed to you and made known according to these goods that you know, as far as can be, let us say: that good is light and sweetness. Why light? Because it makes the eyes grow bright. Why sweetness? Because it refreshes. These are two great goods among us, and no others are found greater than these, nor any that work more toward our joy or our happiness: light and sweetness. The one is for illumination, the other for refreshment. If you are illumined and not satisfied, it is a great good, but not a full one. If you are satisfied and not illumined, it is again a great good, but not a perfect one. Refreshment makes pleasant what is within; illumination [1063D] presents as pleasant what is without; both are required for full joy. For if you are refreshed in the one and afflicted in the other, the gladness is not perfect, to which sadness is mixed. Seek, therefore, refreshment, that what is in you may be pleasant to you; seek also illumination, that what is outside you may be pleasant to you. Refreshment indeed would seem to be more necessary, just as the good which is in you is more properly your own than the good which is outside you. Nevertheless, how much pleasantness does the illumination of contemplation add? Sweet is the light, and delightful for the eyes to see the sun. Behold this world, which offers many spectacles of pleasantness? And all these things were made through the wisdom of God. All that you see went forth from there, where is the reason and cause of all things. If, then, it is so beautiful [1064A] to see the disposition and form of the work, how pleasant and delightful do you think it is to contemplate the wisdom of the craftsman? But do not think, because it is named one wisdom, [that it offers] a certain solitary and wearisome contemplation to those who see it: it is one, but not one in that way. For how do you think the wisdom of God is one? Perhaps as you call one essence, one species and one form, one place and one time: all these are one in number. What is [it] to be one in number? By distinction. What is [it] to be one in number? By smallness. What is [it] to be one in number? By imperfection. For thus you number this and that. For when you number, you say this and not that. What, therefore, is numbered by unity is separated by unity; and by that same unity it is proved to be diminished from all those from which it is shown, [1064B] by that very unity, to be distinct. Do you think the wisdom of God to be one in that way? Then you number it, and say, rightly, this and not that. If, then, it is [this] and not that, then it is not the whole; and there is something outside it which is not in it. If the whole is in it, it is the whole; how do you say [that] it is this and not that? Do not, then, number. Scripture tells you: « His wisdom is not number. All things that are from it were made in number, and weight, and measure. But it itself is not under number; » in which there is true unity without smallness, and universality without multiplicity; nor is it under weight, [being] inestimable; nor under measure, [being] incomprehensible. What, then, do you think it is to see the wisdom of God? When you see this world, how many things do you see in them? And all [1064C] this comes from there; and the whole is there, where the wisdom of God is. And why do I say 'all this'? For it would be too little a thing, were there in the wisdom of God nothing else [but this]. If God understands his own work, and does not understand himself, what wisdom is that? For what God is is more than what is made by God. If, then, he knows what he made, and does not know what he himself who made [it] is, he is ignorant of what is greater, and there is not perfect wisdom in him. If your heart can grasp and comprehend the things that you do and that you unfold in work — you dispose all things by reason dictating within, and all that you are about to do you first conceive in reason; and when they go forth into work, they do not withdraw from reason — how can it be that in the eternal reason of the Founder there is not everything [1064D] that has been made? Again, if it is a small thing for a rational soul to see and comprehend the things that are outside, unless it contemplate those things that are within — [things] far greater and more wonderful — and recognizes that what it itself is is greater than everything that is outside it: [then] the eternal craftsman cannot, in that wisdom by which he sees all that he himself made, be ignorant of what is greater than all things — which is he himself who made [them]. Consider, then, what that wisdom is, in which are all things that have been made, and — beyond all things and above all things — that which is greater than all things: he by whom all things were made. What kind of form do you think that is, in which there is such great beauty? When, therefore, you hear the wisdom of God named, think of all this: all this is in it; and to see it is to see all this. In it are seen all things that have been made; [1065A] and in it is seen he by whom all things were made. Therefore those highest spirits, who through the excellence of supereminent grace draw near immediately, are illumined by it to contemplate it, and in the contemplation of it they see both what was made through it, and what is in it, and him who through it made [all things], in whom it [itself] is. These, then, are the contemplations which the first hierarchy sees purely, immediately illumined unto the simple splendors — which contemplations are both many, inasmuch as all things are seen there; and blessed, because, with all things, he too who made all things is seen. For what is seen in him is not seen apart from him, because that which is seen is one, which is he himself; and in that one all things are seen, in which all things are one and one is all things. See how [1065B] many are the contemplations, where nothing of all things is hidden; how blessed the contemplations, where in the highest good all things are seen; where to see and to have, to love and to taste the good, is the same; where nothing is seen but truth, nothing is loved but goodness. For there are two things: cognition and love. The one pertains to illumination; the other to refreshment. Cognition illumines, love satisfies. Cognition of truth, love of goodness: in these blessedness consists — to know and to love the good. « Taste, says Scripture, and see, for the Lord is sweet [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]. » In « taste » is love; in « see » is cognition. These two he commends distinctly, namely cognition and love. « Seeing indeed many and blessed contemplations purely, and simple and immediate splendors, [1065C] illumined. » This has been said concerning illumination and cognition.

There follows: « Filled with divine nourishment. [Filled] with many things, indeed, by the first given outpouring, and by the unity — sole, domestic, and unifying — of the divine refreshment. » This has been said concerning love and refreshment. He commends both in many ways. First, cognition, in the many and blessed contemplations seen purely through the illumination of the simple splendors. What are the simple splendors? Pure illuminations. Through pure illuminations one comes to pure contemplations. Pure illumination is when the truth is conceived through itself; pure contemplation is when the truth is seen in itself. When the truth is learned through an underlying image or figure, [1065D] it is not pure illumination; when the truth is known in an underlying image or figure, it is not pure contemplation. For there can be neither simplicity except where there is [only] the one thing, nor purity except where there is the true. Simplicity in the one alone; purity in the true. Therefore those who contemplate immediately, and draw near to the truth through themselves, alone have simple illuminations and pure contemplations.



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

There follows: « Filled with divine nourishment. » What is the divine nourishment? The divine refreshment. And this refreshment, what is it but the love of God? Therefore, when he had said, « that surpassing angelic ordering filled with divine nourishment, » he added also, saying: « filled with the unity of the divine refreshment. » But what is the divine refreshment, if not the divine nourishment? [1066A] Therefore the unity of refreshment and the unity of nourishment is one refreshment and one nourishment. Therefore at the table of God only one dish is set. But do not despise it. The satiety is great. « I shall be satisfied, » says the Psalmist, « when thy glory shall appear [n: (Psal. XVI)]. » There are many things in this world, and all these cannot satisfy the heart of man. But there is one good with God, and when this alone is received, satiety is found. Therefore satiety is not in multitude, but in unity. When is there satiety? When the satiety is enough. Suppose now that of these many things which are in the world you have some few that you love, and you will see that it is not enough. Let more come: still you will not say, it is enough. Let all things be set before you; and you will find yourself still in need, and not yet having enough. In all these things, therefore, there [1066B] can be no satiety, where there can be no enough. But that one good will come; and there will be satiety, because there will be enough. Do not wonder. All these are many, but they are not much. That is one, and it is much. Therefore that sinful woman, because she had many things to be forgiven, loved not many things but much. She let go many things, and chose much. She was wise; she did not regard the heap, but weighed the value. In many things, little; in one thing, much. Therefore do not attend [sic: *atttendas*] to the number, but seek the fruit. There is one good, which God has prepared for you. Do not be afraid when you hear "one"; it is one good, but in that good is every good. He has prepared one refreshment, one food, one dish, one bread — but do not despise it. Hear what is written: « Having every taste, and [1066C] every delight of sweetness [n: (Sap. XV)]. » This is the divine nourishment, and the great outpouring, or abundant effusion, by which outpouring, namely, that blessed society, granted the first place, is filled. There is also the unity of the divine refreshment, by which unity alone — that is, singular and domestic and unifying — it has been made specially worthy. But this one and simple divine refreshment is called singular for this reason, that it is not received with any foreign delight or outside sweetness; domestic, because it is prepared only for friends and members of the household; unifying, because it makes one with itself all those to whom it grants itself to be taken and shared.

There follows: « And by much communion with God and cooperation: » — understood, the ordering is filled, or the [1066D] first hierarchy of the celestial essences. This, I say, has been made worthy of that communion and cooperation with God, as far as is possible for it; by the likeness of good dispositions, that is, of virtues and actions. For by good dispositions it has been made worthy of communion; by good actions, worthy of cooperation. And the order is right. First it is illumined by the brightness of knowledge; then it is refreshed by the sweetness of love, so that thus it may both commune with God in virtue and cooperate in action, as it is written: « If any one love me, he will keep my word; and my Father will love him, and we will come to him, and make our abode with him [n: (Joan. XIV)]. »

There follows: « And knowing many divine things in a supereminent way, and made a partaker of divine knowledge and cognition, [1067A] according to what is lawful. » That is, this hierarchy knows many of the divine things, that is, many things concerning the divine, or concerning God, in a supereminent way, that is, more excellently than the other hierarchies « and it has been made in participation, » that is, it has been made a partaker of divine knowledge and of divine cognition, according to what is lawful for it, that is, granted to it.

There follows: « Therefore theology has handed down its praises also to those who are on earth, in which the eminence of its most exalted illumination is wondrously made manifest. » As if he said: Because so great is the excellence of the hierarchy of the first essences, therefore theology, that is Holy Scripture, has handed down to those who are on earth, that is, to men, its praises or laudations. In which laudations, namely, [1067B] the eminence of its most exalted illumination is wondrously made manifest. As if he said: Because so great is its sublimity, therefore theology has made manifest to men its laudations, by which it praises God, so that through the excellence of the laudation the eminence of the illumination might be made manifest.
