There follows: « For some of it, speaking perceptibly, resound like the voice of waters: Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place. » As if he said: Truly excellent are the laudations of this hierarchy, which are made manifest by Holy Scripture, because some of it, that is certain of them, sounding perceptibly resound, or re-echo, or cry out like the voice of waters: Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place. Or it can be read thus. Theology has handed down its praises perceptibly, that is, [1067C] by the likeness of sensible and corporeal things, speaking of spiritual things, namely that « some of it. » That is, of its number « resound like the voice of waters. Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place. » This testimony is taken from Ezekiel, where the prophet records a voice of great commotion made behind him. Which voice, although it is not said there by whom it was uttered, yet this author testifies without ambiguity that it was made by the highest spirits of the celestial orders.

There follows: « But others cry back that most laudable and most holy theology: Holy, holy, holy: the Lord God of Sabaoth. All the earth is full of his glory [n: (Isa. VI)]. » This testimony is taken from Isaiah, where two seraphim, flying, are recorded to have proclaimed this theology, that is, this divine address or laudation, one to [1067D] another, back and forth.

There follows: « But these most exalted hymnologies of the celestial minds we have already, indeed, opened up as far as possible in those things which concern the divine praises. And it has been said of these, in those places, sufficiently as far as we are concerned. » These aforesaid hymnologies, that is, the divine laudations of the celestial minds, that is, of the celestial spirits, he says that he has opened up, or expounded, as far as was possible for him, in other writings, which were made by him concerning the divine praises; and concerning these hymnologies, namely, he testifies that it has been said sufficiently in those (understood) writings, as far as pertained to him, that is, to his capacity. But this [1068A] writing, which he asserts that he made concerning the divine praises, is nowhere found among these his writings which are with us. Yet from this statement we can know that he wrote concerning the divine praises, since in the book *On the Divine Names* he introduced certain things from the amatory hymns of Saint Hierotheus, in which something of the aforesaid praises is treated.

There follows: « Of which it suffices, for a reminder, to say only this much according to the present occasion: that this first ordering has been illumined in theological knowledge, as far as is lawful, by the divine goodness, through which, as a deiform hierarchy, it then handed itself down to others also. » As if he said: Of which, namely of the divine praises sufficiently [1068B] expounded in the aforementioned book, now, according to the present occasion, it suffices to say only this much for a reminder or admonition, namely that this first ordering, that is, the first hierarchy of angels, has been illumined in theological, that is divine, knowledge by the divine goodness itself. Note the construction in that he says, "illumined knowledge." For it is said thus: But I am instructed, I am taught a discipline. He says, therefore, that the first ordering has been illumined unto knowledge by the divine goodness alone; through which knowledge, namely, this ordering handed down itself; then, that is, consequently, to the other hierarchies following after it. « It handed down, » I say, « itself as a deiform hierarchy, » that is, having conformity and likeness to God, and imitating God in this, that, being illumined by him, [1068C] it illumines others, and sets forth itself, as it were, as a form and exemplar of divine conformity. This, therefore, now suffices to say concerning the aforesaid praises: that in them the first ordering of angels, praising its Creator, handed down itself as a form of praising to the other hierarchies following after, « introducing, » that is, suggesting to them, or insinuating, « that by speaking briefly. » For it introduced, or suggested, as it were something hidden, and manifest to few. And it suggested by speaking briefly the other thing which it suggested. And rightly was that suggested only in a hidden way which was invisible, and that spoken only briefly which was ineffable. For however much it were expounded unto knowledge, that would remain hidden which was impenetrable; [1068D] and however much it were extended through speech, it would be brief in relation to it, which was without end. Yet it suggested, and insinuated, and expounded, so that through it there might come to the knowledge of others that which it itself had perceived with no intermediary. What did it suggest?



## BOOK SEVEN. *Exposition of the remaining part of the seventh chapter of the divine Dionysius the Areopagite on the celestial hierarchy, the text of which has been set out above.* (cont.)

Hear: « That most holy, and supremely blessed, and altogether blessed divinity, it is lawful that it be blessed by those receiving God, as far as it is possible for it to be known and praised by the intellects. For they are, as it were, deiform divine places, of the divine repose, as the oracles say. » He expounds the aforementioned testimony taken from Ezekiel. « Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place. » This first hierarchy, he says, crying out, « Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his [1069A] place, » teaches that it is lawful — that is, just, or due, or worthy — that this most holy and supremely blessed and altogether blessed divinity be blessed by the intellects that receive God, and blessed to just such a degree as it is possible for it to be known and praised. It is just, he says, that this divinity, which is in itself ever most holy — ready, namely, to bestow, and not needing to receive — and which is supremely blessed, and altogether, or most strongly, blessed, that is, whose glory in itself is so great that it can neither be increased by another's benefit nor be further commended by another's praise. It is lawful, he says, that although it be in itself so perfect that it needs not to be blessed or praised, yet that the intellects — that is, rational hearts — should bless and praise it, as far, namely, as they can know it [1069B] and praise it according to the knowledge received. For although his glory be perfect of itself, yet we ought not to cease from blessing and praising him: we who, even if by blessing and praising we add no perfection to him, are nevertheless perfected by him. Or what he says can be understood otherwise: that it is just that the divinity, which is in itself supremely blessed, be blessed by the intellects that receive God. As if he said: Although the praise of God be in itself supremely perfect, yet this is by right assigned to his praise, that through grace he deigns to inhabit the hearts made by him. Thus, then, it is just that the divinity, in itself supremely blessed, be blessed also for this, and be blessed, that it offers itself to be received by the minds to be beatified, and deigns to inhabit them as its own [1069C] place. For it does not so dwell in them as though seeking a support, and as though about to fall if it had no prop. For God does not seek a place for himself, as though he could not exist unless he were contained; but he seeks, as it were, a receptacle into which to pour himself: which certainly could not be blessed, unless it were filled by him. Therefore it is added, above all his praise, that he who is supremely blessed in himself makes others blessed from himself. Let him be blessed by them, let him be blessed also for them. Let him be blessed by them, in so far as he is revealed to them to be known and praised; let him be blessed for them, in so far as he is made manifest in them.

There follows: « For they are, as it were, deiform divine places, of the divine repose, as the oracles say. » He gives the reason why, from that which has been said, « Blessed be the [1069D] glory of the Lord from his place, » he understands it to be signified that the glory ought to be blessed by the intellects that receive God: because, he says, these intellects that receive God, they themselves are the divine places. For "Theodocos" is said in Greek, that is, "receiver of God." By which name every pure mind, whether human or angelic, can be signified. For in these two God dwells and rests. They, then, are as it were deiform divine places. He gives, as it were, the reason why they are called divine places. For the cause is the same why they are places and why they are deiform. For from the fact that they receive light, they become a place of light. And again from the very fact that they receive light, they become shining, and exist conformed to the light. They are, then, divine places, inasmuch as [1070A] they are deiform, and have the likeness of God from the God who dwells within. Again, because they are divine places, it is clear that they are places of the divine repose, because they could not be divine places at all unless they were places of repose and peace, as the oracles say: « In peace is his place made [n: (Psal. LXXV)]. »

There follows: « And that it is a monad, and a unity three substantially: and, over the celestial essences down to the uttermost parts of the earth, extending its goodness into all things that are — a providence, as the super-principal principle of all essence, and the cause, and binding all things about, superessentially, with an immeasurable containment. » This he adds concerning the exposition of what follows, which had been taken from Isaiah. As if he said: Just as that first celestial hierarchy, crying out, [1070B] « Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place, » taught that God is to be blessed and glorified not only in himself, but also in his saints, so too, by proclaiming him thrice holy, and by adding not "lords" but "the Lord of Sabaoth," it signified that there are three persons in one Godhead — not three gods, but one God. This is what he says. And because it is a monad, and a unity three substantially, as if he said: Not only this, which was said above concerning the glory of the Lord to be blessed from his place, did this first celestial hierarchy introduce to the hierarchies following after it; but also this, that the monad and unity — that is, the divinity, which is truly one and a simple nature — is three persons substantially, that is, that the three persons are one essence or substance, and the one essence is three persons; [1070C] because God is one in essence, and three in persons. Truly, that "monad" and "unity," though they seem to be the same, are set down doubled, is done on account of the Greek: in which there are two names, "monas" and "henas": which, although they have one interpretation with us (for both sound "unity"), yet this difference seems to exist, that "monas" signifies rather that unity which is spoken of according to distinction, while "henas" signifies that which is marked according to simplicity. For everything that is simple is one. But not everything that is one is simple; since a thing is called one by collection, and one by composition, and one by likeness. All of which, because it is not the whole of what they are that is one, are not truly one, and borrow the name of unity by likeness only, not by property. [1070D] But to the divine nature, for which to be one and to be simple is the same, its whole being is truly to be one; and therefore rightly it had to be called not only "monas," but "henas" also; because its unity subsists ever in the true simplicity of the same and undivided substance. Let him say, then, that in the true Godhead the monad and unity are substantially; because, as the trinity of persons in God does not divide the unity of essence, so the unity of nature does not confound the trinity of persons. But the three are substantially one, of which three the substance is one.

There follows: « And, over the celestial essences down to the uttermost parts of the earth, extending its goodness into all things that are — a providence. » And this too, he says, [1071A] the first hierarchy insinuated to those following after it: that the true unity and supreme trinity extends, or makes to penetrate, its goodness from the supercelestial essences down to the uttermost parts of the earth, that is, from the highest things to the lowest; its goodness, I say, being a providence into all things, because it provides for all and leaves nothing foreign to itself, inasmuch as it is above the principal, or super-essential, principle of all essence, and the cause of all; because from it and unto it all things were made: and which is the binding-about of all things [1072A] — that is, the encirclement, because it binds about all things, and embraces, and contains them superessentially with an immeasurable containment, which comprehends the whole, and in it itself remains incomprehensible. Because, then, it is the principle from which all things were made, and the cause through which all things were made, and the binding-about containment in which all things subsist, therefore all the earth is full of his glory, since from that goodness all things are filled and nourished, that they may subsist in it, which were made through it.

## BOOK EIGHT.

[1071]

## TITLE OF CHAPTER VIII: *On the dominations, and the powers, and on their middle hierarchy.*



## THE TEXT.

[1071B]

*But now we must pass over into the middle ordering of the celestial intellects, exploring those dominations, as far as is possible, with supermundane eyes, and the truly powerful contemplations of the divine powers and virtues. For each appellation, among the essences above us, signifies their deiform properties, imitative of God. Therefore I judge that the manifesting naming of the holy dominations declares a certain absolute anagogy, free from all pedestrian diminishment, and in no way whatsoever universally inclined by any of the tyrannical dissimilitudes, a dominion liberally severe, set above all diminishing servitude, superior to every subjection, and remote [1071C] from every dissimilitude, and unceasingly desirous of dominion, and conforming, as far as is possible, both itself and the things that are after it, in the best and most beautiful way, to the likeness of that very virtue subsisting naturally, turned toward none of the things seen as one, but toward the properly* ὄν *universal, and made, as far as is possible for it, ever in participation of the Lordly deiformity. But that certain strong and unchangeable manliness of the holy virtues, in all their operations according to their deiformity, in no way feebly weakened toward any reception of the divine illuminations imparted to it, powerfully led back into the imitation of God, not abandoning by its own feebleness the deiform motion, but firmly bearing itself into the superessential [1071D] and power-making virtue, and made its image like to the virtue, according to what is permitted, and powerfully turned toward it indeed as toward the principal virtue: but toward the second things, giving [dans et] virtue, coming forth deiformly. But that ordination of the holy powers, equal in power to the divine dominations and virtues, well-ordered and unconfused about the divine receptions, and the ordered rank of the supermundane and intellectual power, not tyrannically [1072B] hurled down by its power-wielding virtues into the things that are lower, but powerfully led back into the divine things after the well-ordered ones, and leading back deiformly the things that are after it, and assimilated, as far as is lawful, to the power-making causal power, and revealing it, as is possible, to the angels in the ranks well-ordered through it by its power-wielding virtue. Having these deiform properties, the middle ordering of the celestial minds is indeed purged, and illumined, and perfected, as has been said, by the divine illuminations imparted to it in the second place through the first hierarchical ordering, and conveyed through that middle one by a second manifestation. And so, by the utterance passing from one angel [and] heard in another angel, we shall make a symbol of the perfection which is from afar above [all], and [1072C] hidden through the procession into the things that follow. For those wise concerning our sacred immolations say that the plenitudes of the divine things, shining through themselves, are more perfect than [those] through other contemplative participations. So too I judge that the immediate participation of the angelic orders first extended into God is more perfect than that accomplished through the mediation of the perfected. On which account, by our priestly tradition, the first intellects are named the perfective, light-bringing, and purgative virtues of the lower ones, as of those led back through them into the superessential principle of all, and made partakers of the mystical purgations, and illuminations, and perfections, according to what is lawful for them. For [sic: *Poc*] has been divinely promulgated altogether by the divine ordination: that the second things participate in the divine [1072D] illuminations through the first. And you will find this too expressed many times by the theologians. For when the divine and paternal humanity, correcting Israel nurturingly for its sacred salvation, and handing it over into correction to avenging and monstrous nations, by an all-kinds conveyance of things provided for unto the better, both dismissed the captivity and clemently led it back to its former beneficence, one of the theologians, Zachariah, saw one of the first — as I judge — and of the angels about God (for this angelic appellation is common [1073A] to all, as I said) learning from God himself concerning this the consoling, as has been said, words. But another of the subject angels coming forth to meet the first, as to the reception and perception of illumination, then instructed by him in the divine counsel as by a high priest, and turned to teach this to the theologian, namely that Jerusalem shall be fruitfully inhabited by a multitude of men [n: (Zach. I)]*. *But another of the theologians, Ezekiel [n: (Ezech. VIII)]*, *[says] that this too, he says, was most sacredly promulgated by the most glorious divinity established above the cherubim. For Israel itself, as has been said — the leader of the host, the humanity, conveying it unto the better through disciplines, divine justice being rendered to those who correct — justified the corrected. This [1073B] is taught: the first after the cherubim, girt about the loins with sapphire, who was clothed in the podere according to the hierarchic symbol. But the rest of the angels, who had axes, the divine ordination commands to be taught by the prior one concerning this divine judgment [n: (Ezech. X)]*. *For to him indeed it said to pass through the midst of Jerusalem, and to give a sign on the foreheads of the corrected men; to the others: Go out into the city after him, and strike, and spare not your eyes; but to all, upon whom the sign is, do not draw near [n: (Ezech. IX)]*. *What, perhaps, would one say of the angel saying to Daniel: The word went forth; or of that first one receiving fire from the midst of the cherubim [n: (Dan. IX)]*. *Or that [saying] of the same, which [is] more abundantly toward the showing of the angelic order, namely that [1073C] the cherub also puts fire into the hands of the one clothed in the holy stole [n: (Ezech. X)]*; *or of the one calling the most divine Gabriel, and saying to him: « Make him understand the vision [n: (Zach. I)]*; » *or whatever other things have been said by the sacred theologians concerning the deiform adornment of the celestial hierarchies. To which the ordination of our hierarchy, assimilated as far as is possible, will have the angelic beauty, in so far as it is formed in characters through it, and led back to the superessential of the whole hierarchy at once.*



## THE EXPOSITION.

The eighth chapter is on the dominations, and the virtues, and the powers, and on the middle hierarchy, which is theirs. For in the preceding chapter he treated [1073D] of the three first orders — the seraphim, and the cherubim, and the thrones — and of the hierarchy, that is, their sacred power, which in the celestial ordering is first and principal, and ordered immediately after God. Now, therefore, he proceeds consequently to treat of the three following orders, that is, the dominations, virtues, and powers; and of the hierarchy, that is, their sacred power, which in the celestial ordering is constituted second, or middle, that is, after the first and before the last. Of this hierarchy, then, he treats in the same manner as he does of the higher order: first by showing the cause or reason of the appellation which is attributed to the orders themselves of this hierarchy, then by expounding more fully the power of that sacred authority.

[1074A] « We must now, he says, pass over into the middle ordering of the celestial intellects: exploring those dominations, as far as is possible, with supermundane eyes, and the truly powerful contemplations of the divine powers and virtues. » Join it together: himself to the following statement, as if he said. After the exposition of the supreme hierarchy, the order demands that we pass over to the middle, that is, to the exposition of the middle hierarchy of the celestial intellects, that is, of the angelic spirits. Afterward he sets forth by name which are those orders of angels in which that middle sacred power consists: to whose exposition he says he ought to pass over, adding: « The dominations: exploring those, as far as is possible, with supermundane eyes, and the truly powerful [1074B] contemplations of the divine powers and virtues. We must pass over, he says, » he says, « as we explore in that » — namely hierarchy — « the dominations, and the truly powerful contemplations of the powers and virtues, » that is, both the powers and the virtues. For these are the three orders of the second hierarchy, whose power and offices he must diligently explore who desires to expound their sacred power. Therefore, he says, « with supermundane eyes, » that is, with spiritual ones, it must be explored. For these things are not seen with bodily eyes. « As we explore, » therefore, « those, » namely the celestial dominations, and « as we explore the contemplations, » that is, the speculations or contemplations « of the powers and virtues. » As to his saying that he will explore the speculations or contemplations [1074C] of the powers and virtues; either it is to be understood thus, as if he said, that through the speculation or contemplation had concerning them he will explore their dignity, or he testifies that he will explore and investigate their very contemplation, by which they contemplate God and perceive divine things. He calls them "truly powerful contemplations," or "powerful speculations," for this reason, that they explore those who are truly powerful. For by "powerful speculation" he understands "speculation of the powerful." He names them "truly powerful" for this reason, that they are powerful in goodness and in truth. For to be able to do evil is not truly to be able, but to be unable, as it is written: « Why dost thou glory in malice, thou that art mighty in iniquity [n: (Psal. LI)]: » Wherefore he also calls those [1074D] powers divine, because from God and in God they can do all that they can.

There follows: « For each appellation of the essences which are above us signifies their deiform properties, imitative of God. As if he said: Rightly have I named the powers and virtues divine, because their very appellation also teaches this. Just as each appellation of the essences which are above us, that is, the celestial ones, signifies their deiform properties: and they are deiform for this reason, that they are imitative of God. For that those celestial essences have conformity, they have it not from quality, but from imitation, because it is not nature that confers on them the likeness of God, but grace.

There follows: « Therefore I judge that the manifesting naming of the holy dominations declares a certain absolute [1075A] anagogy, free from all pedestrian diminishment; and in no way whatsoever universally inclined by any of the tyrannical dissimilitudes, a dominion liberally severe, set above all diminishing servitude: superior to every subjection, and remote from every dissimulation, and unceasingly desirous of dominion; and conforming, as far as is possible, both itself and the things that are after it, in the best and most beautiful way, to the likeness of that very virtue subsisting naturally; turned toward none of the things seen as vain, but toward the properly ὄν on universal, and made, according to what is possible for it, ever in participation of the Lordly deiformity. » All these things the naming of the dominations teaches; for in that they are called dominations, they are signified to have and to do all these things [1075B] which have been said of them. This is what he says: « I judge the manifesting naming of the holy dominations, » that is, the naming by which their power and excellence and dignity is manifested and declared: I judge, he says, that that naming declares all these things. All what things? Hear. « For they are many. » Therefore I said "all these things": because they are many with respect to us, but few with respect to them. « I judge, he says, that the naming of the holy dominations declares a certain absolute, » that is, free, and estranged from all depression, « anagogy, » that is, their excellence, or sublimity, or upward leading, by which they are raised to the highest, so that they are subjected to none below the highest. And I judge that this naming also [1075C] declares that this anagogy, or their excellence, is not universally inclined in any way — that is, that it is in no way at all inclined by any of the tyrannical dissimilitudes, that is, through any, or according to any, tyrannical dissimilitude, that is, according to any tyranny, which is wholly dissimilar from their dominion. As if he said: Just as by their universal excellence they are raised above all subjection, so toward the oppression of their subjects they are inclined by no tyranny.

There follows: « A dominion liberally severe; set above all diminishing servitude. » From what precedes (it is to be understood) "I judge the naming of the dominations to declare" their dominion "liberally severe," that is, kindly severe. Having severity [1075D] in power, liberality in kindness. Severe, because power is exercised from the superior; yet liberty is not taken away from the inferior. Therefore he calls it a dominion liberally severe, set above all diminishing servitude, that is, not subject to anyone unto a servitude which would diminish liberty, but superior to every subjection, and remote from every dissimilitude, that is, from all inferiority or imperfection which would make it dissimilar to its Creator, and unceasingly desirous of dominion, that is, looking toward — or tending toward, or clinging to — the Creator through likeness of dominion and conformity, and conforming both itself and the things that are after it, so that each, as far as is possible for it, may [1076A] imitate the divine virtue in the best and most beautiful way. Therefore he says, « I judge their naming to declare their dominion conforming itself, and the things that are after it, to the divine virtue; and turned toward none of the things seen as vain, but toward the properly on universal. » He puts "of those seeing" (videntium) for what he ought to have said, "of things seen" (visorum), by the most usual manner of the Greeks; since it is derived from the verb δοκοῦμαι, that is, "I see" or "I am seen," because it is common: so that the sense is: I judge it to declare her turned not toward any of those things which seem vain, that is, which, considered in themselves, appear lowly and transitory. But rather turned toward the properly on universal, that is, turned toward that which is properly and universally; namely toward God, to whom being is both proper, because he subsists through himself, and universal, [1076B] because he grants being to all. But it must be known that ὄν on among the Greeks, when it is written with ὄντῶν μικρὸν, through omicron — that is, a short or small "o" — is neuter, and is interpreted *that which is.* But when ὤν is written with ω τὸν μέγα, that is, a long or great "ω," it is masculine, and is interpreted *who is.* "On," therefore, is the Creator of all himself, and the principle, who is, and that which is properly in himself, and universal toward all: toward which principle he says this dominion is turned, and made in participation, that is, made a partaker of the Lordly deiformity, that is, of the divine likeness, which it retains always, that is, unchangeably: made, I say, according to what is possible for it. Thus far he has shown what the naming of the dominations signifies; now he passes on to show what the naming [1076C] of the virtues signifies.



## THE EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « But that certain strong and unchangeable manliness of the holy virtues, in all their operations according to their deiformity, in no way feebly weakened toward any reception of the divine illuminations imparted to them, powerfully led back into the imitation of God: not abandoning by its own feebleness the deiform motion, but firmly bearing itself into the superessential and power-making virtue, and made its image like to the virtue, according to what is permitted, and powerfully turned toward it indeed as toward the principal virtue. But toward the second virtue giving and coming forth deiformly. » That, he says, of the holy virtues (understood: the appellation) [1076D] I judge to declare a certain strong and unchangeable manliness in all their operations: a manliness, I say, imparted to them according to their deiformity, because in this they are like, or conformed, to God, that they have merited to receive a strong and unchangeable manliness, or virtue, for the doing of all things. For the divine virtue is both strong for acting, so that it is not broken by the difficulty of any work, and unchangeable for persevering, so that it does not grow faint or be weakened by the length of duration. In this, therefore, the virtues are deiform, and therefore they are alike in that for the doing of all things they have a strong and unchangeable vigor. Nor do they only have a manliness strong and unchangeable for doing all things beneath them, but they have also a manliness or virtue not feebly weakened [1077A] toward that reception of the divine illuminations imparted to it. For they are strong in the execution of works, and strong also in the reception of gifts, because their virtue, even if it be weak when compared to the divine virtue, is nevertheless not weak when conformed to the divine. It is weak by its condition for grasping the whole that is proper to the Creator; but it is not weak by corruption for receiving the whole that is owed to a creature. It is not, therefore, feebly weakened: for even if it does not have a virtue equal to the Creator, it nevertheless has a strength sufficient for a creature. Therefore it has a manliness strong and unchangeable for the execution of things to be done, and not weakened for the reception of gifts; and powerfully led back, that is, turned or [1077B] reformed in the imitation of God, that is, that it may imitate God; led back indeed through illuminations, into imitation through operations. And they have a manliness not abandoning the deiform motion by any feebleness of their own. He calls it "deiform motion," that these celestial virtues are moved unceasingly, that they may imitate God; either from above, namely by desiring to receive what he has, that they may be the same with him; or downward, by doing what he does, that they may not depart from him. And so they have a manliness not abandoning the deiform motion; but through that deiform motion firmly bearing or exalting itself into the superessential and power-making virtue of the Creator: and through this made an image like to the virtue of the Creator himself, according to what is permitted, that is, according to what [1077C] is granted to them, or possible. For in this, that their manliness is raised through the deiform motion into the superessential and power-making virtue of the Creator, it is proven to become the image and likeness of that same divine virtue. Therefore, he says, I judge their manliness to be made an image like to the divine virtue, and turned toward that divine virtue, inasmuch as toward the principal virtue; but toward the second things giving virtue, and coming forth deiformly. Thus, he says, the appellation of the virtues teaches that their manliness is turned toward the higher virtue of God, that from it, as from the first, it may receive deiformity; and it teaches also that it comes forth or proceeds toward the second things, that is, toward the subsequent orders, that it may come forth to them deiformly, giving them virtue, and in that virtue [1077D] conformity to God, so that it both receives that deiformity from the superior and bestows it on the inferiors: receiving through gift, bestowing through example.

There follows: « But that of the holy powers, equal in power to the divine dominations and virtues, well-ordered and unconfused about the divine receptions by its ordination, and the ordered rank of the supermundane and intellectual power, not tyrannically hurled down by power-wielding virtues into the things that are lower, but powerfully led back into the divine things after the well-ordered ones: and leading back deiformly the things after it, and assimilated, as far as is lawful, to the power-making causal power: and revealing it, as is possible, to the angels in the ranks well-ordered through it by its power-wielding [1078A] virtue. » After the appellation of the virtues he passes on to the appellation of the powers. But that of the holy powers (understood: the naming) I judge to declare a certain ordination of theirs, equal in power to the divine dominations and virtues, that is, an ordination — I say — of equal power with the dominations and virtues, well-ordered and unconfused about the divine receptions, that is, about the divine gifts to be received, because the gifts of God, which it receives for the adornment of spiritual beauty, it guards without confusion, humbly persisting in its own degree and order. I judge also that this naming of the powers declares the ordered rank, that is, the ordination of the supermundane and intellectual power: a power, I say, not tyrannically hurled down into the things [1078B] that are lower, with power-wielding virtues, that is, with violent force. But rather a power led back, or powerfully turned, into the divine things after the well-ordered ones, that is, after the virtues, which are ordered before it: and a power, I say, leading back deiformly, that is, according to the divine likeness, the things that are after it, that is, those orders of angels which follow after it. For just as it itself is turned to the likeness of God by the superiors meditating [cj: *meditantibus*; read *mediantibus*, "mediating"], so, with it mediating, the things that are lower are called back to the same likeness of God. There follows further: The powers assimilated, as far as is lawful, that is, either permitted or granted, to the power-making, causal power; because it can do all things, and is the cause of all. And a power, I say, revealing, namely that divine [1078C] power, as far as it is possible to reveal it to the angels. Revealing how? By its power-wielding virtue. Where revealing? In the ranks well-ordered through it, that is, in the subsequent orders, which it adorns, by revealing to them the divine power by its own example.

There follows: « Having these deiform properties, the middle ordering of the celestial minds is indeed purged, and illumined, and perfected, as has been said, by the divine illuminations imparted to it in the second place through the first hierarchical ordering, and conveyed through that middle one by a second manifestation. » The middle ordering of the celestial minds, he says — of which, namely, treatment has thus far been made, which consists of dominations and virtues and powers: this, having the deiform properties, namely those [1078D] which we said above, is purged and illumined and perfected, just as was said above concerning the first hierarchy; purged, I say, by the divine illuminations imparted to it in the second (understood: place) through the first hierarchical ordering. The first hierarchical ordering consists of the three first orders, that is, seraphim, cherubim, and thrones: which in the first place receives the divine illuminations from the divinity itself: which illuminations afterward being conveyed by a second manifestation, through that middle one, to the second hierarchy, this second hierarchy is in the second place purged, and illumined, and perfected, through those same divine illuminations.

There follows: « And so, by the utterance passing [1079A] from one angel [and] heard in another angel, we shall make a symbol of the perfection which is from afar above [all] perfect, and hidden through the procession into the things that follow. » By example and authority he proves what he had said, namely that the middle ordering of angels is illumined by the first, or through the first, that is, what he says: We shall make a symbol, that is an example, or a demonstration, of the perfection, namely the divine perfection perfect from afar above all things: and hidden, through the procession, into the things that follow; because, although in itself it is hidden and invisible, yet through the procession it goes forth and is made manifest in the things that follow, that is, in those who are immediately after it in the first place, or in those who follow after them. Of this kind of divine perfection, I say — perfect in itself and hidden, and yet, through the bestowal of grace, proceeding into the things that follow [1079B] — we shall make a symbol: the utterance, that is, the saying or word brought forth by one angel, and afterward passing into another angel, and heard by him. For in this, that one angel spoke, another heard, one brought forth the teaching, another received it: a symbol has been made, that is a demonstration or argument, that the divine perfection proceeds, to be participated, through the prior and superior ones to the following and inferior ones. « For those wise concerning our sacred immolations say that the plenitudes of the divine things, shining through themselves, are more perfect than [those] through other contemplative participations. » As if he said: It is no wonder if among the angels one taught and another was taught, since one is more learned than another. For those who are wise concerning our sacred immolations, [1079C] that is, concerning our sacrifices or sacraments, they say that the plenitudes of the divine things, that is, those angels who are full of divine gifts, or illuminations, and shine through themselves, are more perfect than the contemplative participations through others, that is, than those participating by contemplative participations through others. For he put the genitive for the ablative. Those who are wise, he says, or skilled in our sacraments, testify that those angelic spirits who receive the divine contemplation immediately through themselves are more perfect than the rest, who, with the superiors mediating, participate in the same contemplation.

There follows: « So too I judge that the immediate participation of the angelic orders first extended [1079D] into God is more perfect through the mediation of the perfected. » Because, he says, the wise testify this, therefore I too judge that the immediate participation of the angelic orders first extended into God — that is, those angelic orders which, in the first place and principally extended into God, participate in him immediately — is more perfect, that is, that they are more perfect in participation than the perfected who participate through mediation, that is, than the perfected participating through mediation, that is, that they are more perfect than those who are made perfect by others mediating.

There follows: « On which account, by our priestly tradition, the first intellects are named the perfective, light-bringing, and purgative virtues of the lower ones; as of those led back through themselves into the superessential principle of all, [1080A] and made partakers of the mystical purgations, and illuminations, and perfections, according to what is lawful for them. » As if he said: Because the superior spirits are more perfect than the inferior and following ones, therefore by our priestly tradition the first intellects, that is, the supreme orders of angels, are called the perfective, and light-bringing, and purgative virtues of the inferiors, because through them the inferiors are perfected and purged and illumined: they are named the virtues of the inferiors, inasmuch as these are led back through themselves, that is, through their own selves, into the superessential principle of all, that is, God; and the inferiors, I say, made in participation, because they have been made partakers, as far as is lawful for them, of the mystical purgations, and illuminations, and perfections. [1080B] Through those superiors, therefore, by whom they are purged, and illumined, and perfected.

There follows: « For this has been divinely promulgated altogether by the divine ordination: that the second things participate through the first in the divine illuminations: this, he says, has been divinely promulgated, » or made manifest, « altogether, » that is, universally, concerning the divine ordination — that the second things everywhere participate in the divine illuminations through the first.

There follows: « But you will find this too expressed many times by the theologians: » this, he says — that I say the second things participate in the divine illuminations through the first — you will find expressed, that is, manifestly magnified and marked in the Scriptures, namely the sacred ones: by the theologians, that is, those who have reasoned [1080C] concerning divine things and written.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « For when the divine and paternal humanity, in a nurturing way correcting Israel for the sake of its holy salvation, and handing it over for correction to avenging and savage nations, by a universal translation of the things provided-for unto the better: both dismissed the captivity, and mercifully led it back to its former beneficence: one of the theologians, Zechariah, saw one of the first angels, as I suppose, and of those about God (for this angelic surname is, as I have said, common to all), learning from God himself, concerning this matter, consoling words, as has been said. But another of the subject angels coming to meet the first, as if unto the reception and perception of illumination; [1080D] then instructed by him in the divine counsel as by a high priest: and this angel, being turned, teaches the theologian, that Jerusalem is fruitfully inhabited by a multitude of men. » He proves by an example what he had said. In the prophet Zechariah it is read that an angel, having received a command from God, went forth, and gave a command to another angel meeting him, that he should run and announce to the prophet that Jerusalem was as yet inhabited without walls. For this is what he says: « When the divine and paternal humanity, » that is, the paternal mercy of God, « in a nurturing way correcting, » that is, in the manner of a foster-father who scourges his little son for this end, that he may chastise him, correcting Israel with the scourge of the Babylonian captivity for the sake of its holy salvation, namely so that, while for a time he outwardly dispersed it in bodily fashion, he might inwardly [1081A] save it by restoring it unto holiness. And so thus the mercy of God, in a fatherly way correcting Israel and handing it over unto correction — that is, handing it over to the nations that it might be corrected, namely to the Chaldeans: nations, I say, both avenging and savage; because they both avenged the injury done to God, and yet did this not out of zeal for justice but out of the fury of cruelty. Handing over, I say, by a translation; namely that they might be carried over from their homeland into exile, from their own land into a foreign land, from Jerusalem into Babylon, from the vision of peace into the captivity of confusion: by a translation, I say, that is universal, that is, general, of all the things provided-for unto the better — that is, unto the salvation of the predestined. For indeed through this captivity and leading-back, which was done bodily in one people, [1081B] there was shown forth a figure of all the elect, and of those to be freed from the captivity of sin. When therefore the divine mercy, in a fatherly way handing over Israel by such a translation, afterward both dismissed the captivity, and mercifully led Israel itself back to its former beneficence. Then « one of the theologians, namely Zechariah, saw one of the first angels, » that is, one of the first angels, that is, one of the excellent and superior angels, and of those set about God. He says, « one of the first, as I suppose: » for theology does not say this, that that one was of the first. But I suppose that that one was of the first, because he did not receive a command from another prior to him, but gave a command to the second. Therefore, he says, « I suppose » that that one was of the first angels. How [1081C] of the first angels, since the angels are not first, seeing that only those who are last and lowest are named angels? Not so, he says, do I mean 'angel.' But according to this, that this angelic surname is sometimes common to all, as was said above, I now call the supreme orders angels. And so thus one of the theologians, Zechariah, saw one of the first angels learning from God himself consoling words, namely concerning this matter of the captivity and the leading-back of Israel, as was said in the prophecy, namely of Zechariah himself. He saw also another of the subject angels coming forth to meet the first angel, that is, going out, or coming before, that is, running on ahead before he is called, as one ready and [1081D] devoted of himself unto the reception and perception of his illumination, which he was about to receive from him. Then Zechariah himself saw that second angel instructed in the counsel by the first angel himself as by a high priest, namely as by a superior, and one giving holy things: and he saw also that same second angel afterward turned, teaching the theologian this — that is, turned away from the first angel, to whom he was attending, toward the theologian, that is, toward Zechariah himself, that he might teach him this, namely the divine counsel which he himself had learned from the first angel: but the first angel from God — and this is the counsel which he himself announced, « that fruitfully, » that is, abundantly, « Jerusalem shall be inhabited » by a multitude of men. Another translation says: « Without walls [1082A] shall Jerusalem be inhabited. » Under the same sense assuredly, in that after the slaughter of the captivity that people would, by divine grace, be multiplied to such a degree that the multitude of those dwelling in Jerusalem could not be enclosed within the circuit of its walls.

There follows: « But the other of the theologians, Ezekiel: and, he says, this was most sacredly promulgated to the cherubim by that same most glorious divinity, established firmly above. » By the testimony of the other theologian he proves what he said, namely that the divine counsels, immediately received by the first angels, are then carried through the lower angels even unto the minds of the theologians — that is, what he says: « The other of the theologians, Ezekiel, » namely that this divine counsel was most sacredly promulgated to the cherubim by the most glorious divinity itself, established firmly above [1082B]: this, he says. Ezekiel says that the most glorious divinity itself, established firmly above in majesty, in eternity, most sacredly promulgated or revealed its counsel to the cherubim, from whom it was afterward manifested more fully to the following orders? « For humanity itself — as has been said, the leader of the army — translating Israel unto the better through chastisements, is answered by divine Justice to those who correct, [and] justified the corrected. » Humanity, he says, that is, mercy, the leader of the army, or the mistress of the exercise, translating Israel itself unto the better through disciplines, condemned the guilty, justified the corrected; and this, namely the judgment, is answered out of divine justice to the correcting angels, who had been sent to punish: because for this reason the divine mercy brought in the scourges, that [1082C] it might condemn the reprobate, and justify the corrected. Or the divine justice itself is answered to those who correct, in that which is just, that the reprobate be condemned, and the corrected be justified. Or, to the correcting Chaldeans, that is, the persecutors through whom God scourged and corrected his people, it is answered out of divine justice, that they may receive as they have deserved; but the divine mercy justified the corrected Israelites. « This is taught to the first after the cherubim, girded about the loins with sapphire, who was clothed with the *podere* according to the hierarchical symbol: » this, he says, divine counsel of condemnation and justification « is taught to the one set first after the cherubim, who was clothed with the *podere* according to the hierarchical symbol. » For the fact that after the cherubim a certain one [1082D] appeared girded about the loins with sapphire, and clothed with the *podere*, was a hierarchical symbol, that is, a hierarchical figure, because the hierarchy uses visible figures for the demonstration of invisible things. And not only is he who was first after the cherubim taught the divine counsel, but also the rest who follow. For this is what he subjoins: But the divine ordinance commands that the rest of the angels, who had axes, be taught concerning this the divine judgment by the one prior to them. Namely concerning this counsel, or concerning this work, the divine ordinance commands the cherubim themselves « that the rest of the angels be taught by him » who was first after the cherubim, so that in that order the divine precept might first descend into the cherubim, then through the cherubim to him who was next, clothed with the *podere*, through whom it might at last come to the following [1083A] angels, who were made ready with axes.

There follows: « For to him indeed he said to pass through the midst of Jerusalem, and to give a sign upon the foreheads of the corrected men: » — either 'to him,' that is, the divine ordinance said to the cherubim, that he should command the one following him to pass through the midst of Jerusalem, etc. Or 'to him,' that is, to the one following after the cherubim, through the cherubim itself he « said to pass through the midst of Jerusalem, » etc. And not only did he give the precept to that one through the cherubim, but also to the others following after him — this is what is added: « To the others: Go forth into the city after him, and strike; and do not spare your eyes. But to all upon whom is the sign, draw not near. » But all these things, which have been taken from the prophetic visions, [1083B] avail for this, that it may be shown that the first and highest angels are instructed by God himself, the second by the first, the third by the second.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « What perhaps might someone say of the angel saying to Daniel: The word went forth; or of that first one himself receiving fire from the midst of the cherubim? or that saying of the same, which is more abundantly for the showing of the angelic order; namely that the cherubim also sends fire into the hands of the one clothed with the holy stole; or of the one calling the most divine Gabriel, and saying to him: Make him understand the vision. Or whatsoever other things have been said by the sacred theologians concerning the deiform adornment of the celestial hierarchies, unto which the ordination of our hierarchy, assimilated according to what is possible, will have the angelic beauty, as far as in figures, [1083C] being formed through it and led back to the superessential at once of every hierarchy. » In these prophetic testimonies, as we said above, he intends to prove this alone: that among the angels some teach others, and the lower receive precept and doctrine from the higher; or also when men are instructed by the angels, and the lower receive knowledge from the higher. For what might one — that is, someone — say of the angel speaking to Daniel, except that the lower is instructed by the higher, and receives doctrine? Or again, what might someone say of that first one, mentioned above after the cherubim, clothed with the *podere*, and receiving fire from the midst of the cherubim, except this likewise, that one is shown to receive from another that which [1083D] he cannot have of himself? Or what else also is that saying of the same — namely of the vision, or of the example — which is more abundantly, or more copiously, for the showing of the angelic order, that is, for showing that among the angels some superior orders are set over others, as I say, that the cherubim sends fire into the hands of the one clothed with the holy stole, except that manifestly a dispensation is demonstrated to be made through [1084A] one into another? That testimony, indeed, in the vision of Ezekiel, from which this is taken, is rather conjectured than expounded; because from the fact that that first one after the cherubim, clothed with the holy stole, or *podere*, is bidden to receive fire from the midst of the cherubim, he is believed, not unfittingly, to have received that same fire from the cherubim itself. Concerning this, therefore, what will anyone be able to say, except that it is manifestly shown that the divine bounty passes through some unto others? Or again, of the angel calling the most divine Gabriel, and saying to him: Make him understand the word [visionem]: what will anyone be able to say, except that, according to the ordination of the same disposition, the divine precept descends through some unto others; and those who are superior [1084B] command to the inferior the things to be done, even as they insinuate the things to be known? Concerning these things therefore, or also concerning all the others, whatsoever other things have been said by the sacred theologians concerning the deiform adornment of the celestial hierarchies, what will anyone be able to say, except what has been said above? Unto which adornment of the celestial hierarchies, namely, the ordination of our — that is, of the human, or Ecclesiastical — hierarchy being assimilated, as far as is possible for it (namely, to be assimilated), will have from that very likeness the angelic beauty, as far as in figures, that is, in figures and likenesses. For as much as it now has in the figure of likeness, so much will it afterward have in the loftiness of glorification. Now indeed it has the character and the sign in figure; then it will have the reality and the truth in glory. Certain men have tried to interpret these characters [1084C] in a strange way; setting before those who contemplate, in that future glory of the supernal blessedness, certain phantasms and images of errors in place of God, asserting that God is never visible to anyone in his own substance. But we do not await blessedness in the contemplation of figures, to whom the very truth has been promised. Therefore the disposition of our hierarchy, formed unto the angelic adornment, as much as it now has of likeness in characters and figure, so much will it afterward have in the glory of beauty. Now indeed it is formed through it: that is, both according to it, namely the angelic adornment, and by the mediation of that same angelic adornment, led back to the superessential at once of every hierarchy — that is, [1084D] restored unto the likeness of God, who is the superessential of every hierarchy; because from him every hierarchy has not only form, but also substance; because he himself not only instituted every hierarchy, but also created it, so that it might be after him in likeness, under him in condition.

## BOOK NINE.

[1083]

## TITLE OF CHAPTER IX: *On the principalities, archangels, and angels, and on their last hierarchy.*



## THE TEXT.

[1083D]

*The adornment remaining to us for contemplation, [1084D] concluding the angelic hierarchies disposed by the deiform principalities, archangels, and angels. And first indeed [1085A] I judge it necessary to speak, according to what is impossible for me, the manifestations of their sacred surnames. For it manifests, indeed, that deiform principal eductive of the celestial principalities, together with the sacred order and the most becoming principal powers, and that they are universally converted unto the superprincipal principle, and lead others hierarchically, and are, as far as possible, formed unto that very principial principle, and that it manifests his superessential ordination and the adornment of the principal powers. But that of the holy archangels is indeed of equal power with the celestial principalities. For there is, both of them and of the angels, as I have said, one hierarchy and disposition. Nevertheless, since indeed there is no hierarchy not having also first, and middle, and last powers, the [1085B] holy order of the archangels is received into the communicative hierarchical middle of the extremes. For it communicates with the most sacred principalities, and with the holy angels. With the former indeed, because it is principally converted unto the superessential principle, and is, as far as possible, reformed unto it, and unifies the angels according to its well-adorned, and ordered, and invisible leadings. But with the latter, because it is to them also a prophetic order, receiving the divine illuminations hierarchically through the first powers, and announcing them deiformly to the angels, and manifesting them through the angels to us according to the sacred analogy of each of those divinely illumined. For the angels themselves, as we have said before, completively consummate all the dispositions of the celestial minds, according to what is to be consummated: inasmuch as, in the celestial essences, [1085C] having the angelic property, and more fittingly named angels among us than the prior ones, in so far as, concerning that which is more manifest, there is to them a hierarchy, and more of the circum-adornment. For it is to be judged that the most excellent disposition, as itself firstly and orderly approaching the hidden one, secretly, forming [formantem], ordains the second. But the second, which is completed by the holy dominations, and virtues, and powers, presides over that hierarchy which is of the principalities, and archangels, and angels. The first hierarchy indeed more manifestly; but that which is after it, more hiddenly. But the manifestative disposition of the principalities, archangels, and angels commands the human hierarchies through the consequent things, so that there be through order an ascent unto God, and conversion, [1085D] and communication, and unity. And indeed also, from God, best imparted to all the hierarchical ones, and communicatively supervening, and a procession with most sacred adornment. Thence theology distributes our hierarchy to the angels, naming Michael the prince of the people of the Jews [n: (Dan. X)]*, *and others, diverse, of the nations. For the Most High established the bounds of the nations according to the number of the angels of God [n: (Deut. XXXII)]*. *But if anyone should say, and how was the people of the Hebrews alone led back into the divine illuminations? it must be answered that the right dominations of the angels are not to be accused of the error of the other nations unto non-existent gods, but those very ones, by their own inflexions, falling away from that right leading-back which is unto the divine, by their own love, and by pride [1086A] of the things divinely seen by them, and, in a co-rational manner, unto worship. This is reported that the people of the Hebrews itself also suffered. For thou hast repelled the knowledge of God, he says, and hast gone forth after thy own heart [n: (Ose. IV; Jer. II)]*. *For neither do we have a coerced life; nor are the divine lights of provident illumination revealed through the proper power of those who provide, but the dissimilarity of the intellectual visions makes the donation of light, out of the superfull paternal goodness, either wholly not participated, not distributed unto their reformation, or makes the participations differing — small or great, obscure or clear — of the one and simple, and ever holding itself in the same mode, and superexpended fontal ray. Then, because also to the other nations, out of which we also have looked back unto that, [there is] prepared unto tradition and open the [ray] of divine light, and the great, [1086B] and copious sea, no alien gods indeed ruled: but the one principle of all, and unto it the angels, exercising the priesthood according to each several nation, led back their followers. Melchizedek is to be understood as a high priest, existing most friendly to the Lord — not of the non-existent, but of the truly existent, most high God. For thus simply the theosophists themselves called Melchizedek not only the friend of God, but also priest [n: (Gen. XIV)]*. *Or, that to the wise they might openly signify, that he converted not only out of them unto that God who truly is. But still also to others, as a high priest, he brings forth those things which are of the leading-back unto the true and sole divinity. And this, moreover, we shall admonish thy most-priestly understanding: that both with the angel ruling over Pharaoh among the Egyptians themselves [1086C] [n: (Gen. XLI)]*, *and with the prince of the Babylonians having his own presiding one — with all providence, both provident and empowered of domination, distributed according to the divisions — and for those nations there were established leaders, ministers of the true God, by the manifestation of the vision of the formations from the angels; the [visions] revealed continually by the angels, from God through the angels, to the holy men Joseph and Daniel [n: (Dan. II)]*. *For there is one principle and providence of all. And in no way is it to be judged that the Jews indeed spoke of the divinity fully; but that the angels specially, or equally honorably, or oppositely, or that certain gods, one over another, rule the other nations. But that saying also is to be received according to the sacred understanding itself, not as though God, together with other gods, or angels, contending, suffered our leadership — the principate in Israel, and the leadership of the nation Israel. [1086D] But as, that same one and lofty providence at once salutarily distributing all men by the restorative hand-leadings of their proper angels, Israel almost alone beyond all being converted unto the illumination and knowledge of the man of God. Whence theology, signifying that he possessed Israel unto the service of the true God, said, He became the portion of the Lord [n: (Psal. CXXXIV; Deut. XXXII; Isa. XIX)]*. *But showing that he also distributed, man by man, to the rest of the nations, [and] to a certain one of the holy angels, Michael, for the knowing through him of the one principle of all, said that he led the Jewish people [n: (Dan. X)]*. *openly teaching us that there is one providence of all, superessentially set above at once all the invisible and visible powers. [1087A] And all the angels ruling throughout the several nations extend their several powers unto him, as unto their proper principle, following him by an essential will*



## EXPOSITION.

« The adornment remaining to us for contemplation, concluding the angelic hierarchies disposed by the deiform principalities, archangels, and angels. » The words are plain. The adornment, he says, concluding the angelic hierarchies, disposed by the deiform principalities, archangels, and angels, is remaining to us — that is, it now remains for us to contemplate or consider the adornment, that is, the beautiful disposition of the angelic hierarchy, which is, as it were, the conclusion of the two preceding, and the end, in which three orders are disposed, that is, principalities, angels, and archangels.

[1087B]There follows: « And first indeed I judge it necessary to speak, according to what is possible for me, the manifestations of their holy surnames. » I, he says, judge it to be necessary that I first speak the manifestations of their surnames, so that through the interpretation of the surnames their properties may be manifested. This therefore he judges must first be expounded: why in this last hierarchy some are named principalities, others archangels, others angels, because from the very distinct surname the properties of each are manifested. This is what he adds: « For it manifests, indeed, that deiform principal eductive of the celestial principalities, together with the sacred order and the most becoming principal powers, [1087C] and that they are universally converted unto the superprincipal principle, and lead others hierarchically; and that they are, as far as possible, formed unto that very principial principle, and that it manifests his superessential ordination, and the adornment of the principal powers. » He expounds what the several surnames in this last hierarchy signify: and first he treats of the surname of the principalities, showing that this very surname manifests that principal eductive, that is, that principal leadership which they have, deiformly, that is, [in] the likeness of God; because just as God is the one and highest prince of all, so they themselves are the princes and leaders of the subject angels and men. This [1087D] leadership, therefore, the surname itself manifests together with the sacred order and the most becoming principal powers, because they have that leadership both from the sacred order and from the principal powers. For that they are leaders and princes of others, they have both from this, that they have been ordained to this by office, and from this, that by their principal powers they are more excellent than the rest.

There follows: « And that they are universally converted unto the superprincipal principle; » this also, he says, the surname of the principalities manifests, namely those powers which they themselves have: that they are universally converted unto the superprincipal principle, that is, God. And their surname also signifies that they lead other powers, or the powers of others, unto that very [1088A] principial principle, [and], as far as is possible, that those powers are formed unto it, or conformed to it: to lead, I say, hierarchically, that is, according to the manner and measure of their own precedence. For their own powers they lead unto their principle by universally converting; the powers of others they lead unto their principle by hierarchically preceding; because they have to lead only in so far as they ought to precede or preside. Therefore they lead hierarchically, because in that in which they are not set above, they cannot rightly be leaders. This therefore their surname manifests: namely that they have a principate in the likeness of God, from office and from power; and that they convert both their own powers unto their principle, and lead the powers of others. Their surname also manifests the adornment [1088B] of the principal powers themselves — that is, the disposition of the principalities themselves manifests the superessential ordination of that first principle. For from the very surname of the principalities it is intimated that in their precedence the first Principate is manifested, because by ruling they both imitate it and manifest it.

There follows: « But that of the holy archangels is indeed of equal power with the celestial principalities. For there is, both of them and of the angels, as I have said, one hierarchy and disposition. » That surname (it is understood) « of the holy archangels is of equal power, » that is, signifies an equal power, or an equal potency, « with the celestial principalities; » because there is one hierarchy and one disposition of the three orders, [1088C] that is, of the angels, and of them, namely the archangels, and of the principalities. And although from this — that the hierarchy is one — there is also one and equal power of the three: nevertheless, because there is no hierarchy which does not have first, and middle, and last orders, therefore the archangels, set in the middle place between the principalities, who are first, and the angels, who are last, participate in the property of both. This is what he says: « Nevertheless, since indeed there is no hierarchy not having also first, and middle, and last powers, the holy order of the archangels is received communicatively into the middle of the extremes of the hierarchy. » Since, he says, there is no hierarchy which does not have first, and middle, and last powers: [1088D] therefore « the holy order of the archangels is received communicatively into the middle of the extremes of the hierarchy: » that is, it is received so as to be in the middle of the extremes, communicating with the hierarchy, that is, with their sacred power; or it is received into the hierarchical middle, that is, for the possessing of the hierarchical middle, which is communicative of the extremes — this is what follows: « For it communicates both with the most sacred principalities, and with the holy angels. » But in what it communicates with each, he shows subsequently, saying: « With the former indeed, because it is principally converted unto the superessential principle, and is, as far as possible, reformed unto it; and it unifies the angels according to its well-adorned, and ordered, and invisible leadings. » In this, he says, the order of the archangels participates with [1089A] the principalities in the same power, because « it is converted unto its superessential principle, and is reformed unto it, » as far as is possible for it: and because it also unifies — that is, gathers unto the unity of that same principle — by leading the angels after itself according to invisible and ordered leadings, by which it has to lead those subject and following ones. In this, therefore, the order of the archangels communicates with the power of the principalities: that in the likeness, or imitation, of them it both converts itself unto its first principle, and gathers those following it. And not only with the principalities set above, but also with the angels established below, does the order of the archangels itself communicate. This is what he adds: « But with the latter, because it is to them also a prophetic order, receiving the divine illuminations hierarchically through the first [1089B] powers: and announcing them deiformly to the angels: and manifesting them through the angels to us according to the sacred analogy of each of those divinely illumined. » With the former, he says, that is, with the principalities, the order of the archangels communicates, as we said above. But with the latter, that is, with the angels, it communicates in this: that it is to them also a prophetic order — that is, an order prophetic, and ordained for prophesying — prophesying to them, as to inferiors, the divine secrets, receiving indeed the divine illuminations through the first powers, that is, through the principalities, who are prior and superior: itself receiving, I say, « hierarchically, » that is, according to what the hierarchical order and disposition requires, namely that the inferior receive their illuminations from the superior. « Receiving, » [1089C] I say, « and announcing them, » namely the illuminations which it receives from the superiors, to the inferior angels: but announcing deiformly, so that in this — that it receives from the superiors — it may keep the hierarchical disposition; but in this — that it imparts to the inferiors — it may not depart from conformity to God, who, the highest of all, bestows his gifts upon all subject things to be participated. For to receive illumination from a superior is a hierarchical thing, but to give to an inferior is a divine thing. And through this the archangelic order, established in the middle according to the disposition of the hierarchy, participates both with the superiors and with the inferiors — receiving the divine illuminations from the superior principalities, and announcing them to the inferior angels: and through the angels [1089D] announcing to us, as though to the last and lowest, those same divine illuminations according to the sacred analogy of each of those divinely illumined, that is, according to the manner and measure of the capacity of each of us for perceiving that same divine illumination; or, announcing to us through the angels according to the sacred analogy of each of them, namely of the angels divinely illumined — that is, they announce the divine illumination through each one only in so far as each is fit to receive it in himself, and to announce it to others through himself.

There follows: « For the angels themselves, as we have said before, completively consummate all the dispositions of the celestial minds, according to what is to be [1090A] consummated. » As if he should say: Fittingly does the archangelic order announce the divine illuminations to us through the angels, because the angels themselves are last in the celestial disposition, and nearest to men, and in them are completed, or terminated and consummated, all the dispositions or orders of the celestial minds, that is, of the angels. Thus therefore the angels consummate all the celestial orders, according to what is to be consummated, so that it may be understood concerning them; or, that it may be said more expressly, according as they themselves, namely the orders of the angels, are to be consummated. But in what way the angels themselves perfect, or consummate, the celestial orders, he subjoins, saying: « Inasmuch as, in the celestial essences, having the angelic property, and more fittingly named angels among us than the prior ones, in so far as, [1090B] concerning that which is more manifest, there is to them a hierarchy, and more of the circum-adornment. » It is not, he says, wondrous if the celestial orders are terminated in them, because among all the celestial essences it is their property to announce the divine secrets to us; and in this they have the angelic property, that it is their property to be messengers to us; and therefore they are named angels more fittingly among not [cj: *non*; read *nos*, "us"] than the prior or superior ones, namely in so far as there is to them a hierarchy, that is, a power or office concerning that which is more manifest to us, and in so far also as their adornment, or disposition, or order is more 'circum-,' that is, is more circumscribable and intelligible to us, approaching visible things because of their ministry. Therefore to them belongs more the circum-adornment — that is, a knowable or comprehensible [1090C] order — because it is nearest to us.

There follows: « For it is to be judged that the most excellent disposition, as itself firstly approaching to order the hidden one, secretly forming [forma], ordains the second. But the second, which is completed by the holy dominations, and virtues, and powers, presides over that hierarchy which is of the principalities, and archangels, and angels. The first hierarchy indeed more manifestly, but that which is after it, more hiddenly. But the manifestative disposition of the principalities, and archangels, and angels commands the human hierarchies through the consequent things, so that there be through order an ascent unto God, and conversion, and communication, and unity. » It is to be judged, [1090D] he says, that the most excellent disposition — that is, the supreme hierarchy, as firstly, that is, principally, approaching the hidden one, that is, God — ordains the second, secretly, that is, hiddenly, or incomprehensibly forming it. But the second hierarchy, which is completed by the holy dominations, and virtues, and powers, is to be judged to preside over that — namely the hierarchy which is of the principalities, and archangels, and angels; and the first hierarchy, that is, the supreme, is to be judged to perceive the divine illuminations more manifestly, or to preside more manifestly over the third; but that which is after it, that is, the second, to preside, namely, more hiddenly. But the disposition — that is, the hierarchy of the principalities, and archangels, and angels — [1091A] the manifestative, that is, the known and notifying one, is to be judged to command the human hierarchies through the consequent things, that is, consequently, after a precept has been given to it by the superiors: so that thus there may be through order an ascent unto God, and conversion, and communication, and unity — that is, so that human minds, turned through the lower hierarchy unto the middle, and from the middle unto the supreme, may ascend unto that one thing to be communicated which God is.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « And indeed also, from God, best imparted to all the hierarchical ones, and communicatively supervening, and a procession with most sacred adornment. » By such an order, he says, the one good is communicated by all, proceeding from God into all the hierarchical orders, so that it may adorn them, supervening upon them and imparted [1091B] through communication. This is what he says: *And indeed also,* that is, even so, there comes to be from God a procession of that highest good, namely, best imparted to all the hierarchical — it is understood, orders: upon them « supervening communicatively, » that is, unto communicating, and with most sacred adornment. For in this is the great adornment of the divine disposition: that, although all participate in the one good, yet it comes forth to be communicated not in one mode, but some perceive it through others, and in turn offer it to be communicated to others after them. Just as, among those spirits, the first hierarchy offers the divine illuminations to be communicated to the second, the second to the third, the third to ours, that is, to the human.
