This is what he subsequently adds, saying: « Thence theology distributes our hierarchy [1091C] to the angels, naming Michael the prince of the people of the Jews, and others, diverse, of the nations. » Thence, he says, that is, for this reason — because the inferior are ruled by the superior — theology, that is, Holy Scripture, distributes our hierarchy to the angels to be ruled, naming Michael the prince of the people of the Jews, and naming other diverse angels as princes of the nations, namely: as in Daniel we read the prince of the Persians, and the prince of the Greeks named. And not only in those testimonies is it proved that the angels have rule over men, but Scripture also testifies elsewhere, saying: « The Most High established the bounds of the nations according to the number of the angels of God [n: (Dan. X)]. » The bounds — that is, the divisions, or distributions, of the nations — the Most High established according to the number [1091D] of the angels of God: because he fitted the number of the nations to the number of the angels, so that individual angels might preside over individual nations; although, because according to another translation there not 'angels' but 'sons of God' are named, something else seems to be signified.

There follows: « But if anyone should say: And how was the people of the Hebrews alone led back into the divine illuminations? It must be answered that the right dominations of the angels are not to be accused of the error of the other nations unto non-existent gods, but those very ones, by their own inflexions, falling away from that right leading-back which is unto the divine, by their own love, and by pride — [pride] of the things divinely seen by them, and, in a co-rational manner, unto worship. » As if he should say: It seems indeed wondrous. If [1092A] anyone, he says, wonders why the good angels suffered those nations over which they presided to fall away from the knowledge of the true God, it must be answered that this was not done by the fault of the angels, and that the right dominations of the angels ought not to be accused for the error of the other nations, which were not of Israel, unto non-existent gods — that is, because by erring they worshipped those who were not gods; but that those very ones, by their own inflexions, that is, by their own will, falling away from the right leading-back, that is, from the knowledge by which man is led back unto the divine, so that they believed [to be] gods those who were not gods; those very ones, I say, are to be accused, who by their own will, in so far as they were of free choice, slipped from the truth into error, and this out of their own love, because they loved themselves more than God, and sought their own glory, [1092B] and were therefore proud; and out of the pride of the things divinely seen by them, that is, of those things which were divinely revealed to them, and which were revealed to them also in a co-rational manner unto worship, namely the divine — that is, from which they might reasonably or probably have understood in what manner they ought to worship God. For as the Apostle says: « When they had known God, they glorified him not as God; but they became vain in their thoughts. For saying themselves to be wise [behold, pride], they were made fools, and their senseless heart was darkened [behold, the error unto non-existent gods], because they served the creature rather than the Creator, who is blessed for ever [n: (Rom. I)]. » Or, 'divinely and co-rationally' [means] with God recompensing — it is understood, to them: that is, wisely and worthily [1092C] unto worship; that is, as was fitting for the worshippers of false [gods], namely gods seen by them — that is, who seemed to them gods, and were not. For in this God recompensed them wisely and reasonably: that, as the Apostle says, because they approved not to have God in [their] knowledge, God delivered them up unto a reprobate sense, that they should do the things which are not fitting, in their very selves. For they were to be punished in their very selves, who had sinned in their very selves.

There follows: « This is reported that the people of the Hebrews itself also suffered — namely this, that it withdrew from the worship of God, just as the other nations also had withdrawn unto the cult of idols; or this, that is, a like divine vengeance, because it likewise withdrew from the worship of God, and cast off the knowledge of God. For the divine voice says to Israel itself [1092D]: « Thou hast repelled the knowledge of God: and hast gone forth after thy own heart. » Namely, God being forsaken, who is worshipped within by a pure mind. Thou hast gone forth outward through the love of visible things, whither thy own heart — that is, thy desire and depraved will — was drawing thee. In all which the power of free choice appears, because without coercion man — whether aided unto good, or permitted unto evil — is inclined by his own will. This is what follows: « For neither do we have a coerced life. » For the will can be given free to a rational creature, but it cannot be coerced at all. And just as it is not in our power that the gift of divine illumination be offered to us, so it is only in our power that, once offered, it be received. For sometimes it is offered even when we are unwilling, [1093A] but it is never received except when we are willing. And when we are willing, we are willing indeed from God, because a good will is the gift of God. But when we are unwilling, we are unwilling from ourselves, because to be unwilling is nothing other than not to will: which is a desertion of the good unto non-being, and nothing, which was made without it. And so « the divine lights of provident illumination, » because through them God providently illumines, are indeed not « revealed through the proper power of providence: » that is, of those who, having received them, become provident; or 'of the things provided-for,' that is, of those who have been provided for, or predestined by God to receive them. It is to be noted that where we have *revelantur* ('are revealed'), another text has *obcaecantur* ('are blinded'); which, although they are different, yet pertain to establishing the same truth: namely that it may be shown [1093B] that whether the divine lights be revealed, that is, manifested, or blinded, that is, hidden and concealed, this stands not in the power of those receiving, but in the choice of the giver. But nevertheless, although the manifestation or concealment itself is not in their power, yet the reception never comes to be except with their will, because a rational good is that which cannot be perceived except by one who knows and wills. Therefore either it comes about, by a depraved will, that they are wholly repelled, or, by a difference of good will, that they are participated dissimilarly. This is what he says: « But of the intellectual visions, » that is, of the spiritual eyes, namely of the rational minds, « the dissimilarity makes the donation of light, » coming namely « out of the superfull, » that is, out of the exceedingly full and eminently full paternal [1093C] goodness; that donation, I say, the dissimilarity itself makes either wholly non-participating, and not distributed unto the reformation of those visions: « or it makes the participations differing, that is, small or great, obscure or clear, of the one fontal ray, and simple, and ever holding itself in the same mode, and superexpanded. » For the ray of divine illumination, descending from the fount of the good, in itself one and simple, and ever holding itself in the same mode, is superexpanded upon all rational minds, and is participated by them differently, not according to its own simplicity, but according to their diversity. For either they are depraved, and wholly repel it, and cause that it be in no way participated, nor [1093D] distributed unto their reformation; or they are dissimilarly good, and receive it differently.

There follows: « Then, because also to the other nations — out of which we also have looked back unto that — [there was], prepared unto tradition and open to all, the great and copious sea of the divine light; no alien gods indeed ruled, but the one principle of all; and unto it the angels, exercising the priesthood according to each several nation, led back their followers. » As if he should say: That good angels were set over the nations can be proved not only from this, that the Most High is said to have established the bounds of the peoples according to the number of the angels of God, but also from this, that over those nations no alien gods ruled from the beginning, but one God. For it is altogether probable [1094A] that the good Lord established in his commonwealth good ministers under himself: by whose ministry this too was afterward done, that we, who have believed out of the nations, looked back through faith unto God, and unto the fullness of his illumination, being led back through them: which assuredly they would not have done, if they had not been good. This is what he says. Then also from this it can be proved that good angels were set over the nations: namely because to those other nations, which were not of Israel — out of which nations we also, who afterward believed, looked back, turned through faith unto that great and copious sea of the divine light, which is prepared and open to all unto tradition, or bestowal, because it bestows itself and hands itself over to all who will and desire to receive it. Over those nations, namely, [1094B] no alien gods ruled, although this might seem [so], because they worshipped idols, and adored false gods. But one God ruled over them, and had dominion, because just as there is one principle of all, from whom are all things, so there is one Lord and ruler, under whose power all things are established. Who, as he is good in himself, so established good ministers under himself, as rulers and leaders, [namely] angels, and those angels led us back, following through faith, unto that very principle of ours. They led back, I say, according as they were exercising the priesthood — that is, the sacred precedence — according to each several nation; that is, each one, out of that nation in which he exercised the priesthood, that is, the sacred power, converted unto the faith those who believed, and looked back unto their principle.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

[1094C]There follows: « Melchizedek, it is to be understood, being the highest priest, most beloved by the Lord of those that exist — nay, not of those that exist, but of the truly existing most high God. » Treating of the ministry and office of the angels, he suddenly introduces a narrative about Melchizedek: not because, as certain men have supposed, he holds that Melchizedek himself was an angel, or that on this account his priesthood is to be commemorated among the ministries of the angels, but that he may show that it was not only by the work and ministry of angels, but also of the saints and of men pleasing to God, and set over other men in the things that were divine, that unbelievers from among the Gentiles were converted to the faith and worship of the true God. According to this manner, then, [1094D] by which the angels, from the office of their prelacy, exercised the priesthood among the Gentiles, converting them to the faith of the true God, it is to be understood that Melchizedek was the highest priest, being most beloved by the Lord of all that exist — nay rather, of those that do not exist, but of the truly existing most high God. The letter is intricate. But the sense is this. Since he had said that Melchizedek was most beloved by the Lord of all that exist, as though correcting his own statement — because he had called those who truly are not "existing" — he adds "of those not existing," according to what is written: « All the nations are before him as though they were not [n: (Isa. XL)]. » And, as though it were asked who then exists, he adds: « The most high God alone, who has true existence. » But that he does not say "the truly existing most high [1095A] God," « but of the truly existing most high God, this was done because of the likeness of the preceding word, so that by the same case it might answer to "of those not existing," « if of the truly existing most high God. » Or it may be read thus. It is to be understood that Melchizedek, being most beloved by the Lord of all that exist, was a priest not of any existing things whatsoever, but of the most high God who truly exists, as it is written: « For he was a priest of the most high God [n: (Gen. XIV)]. »

And there follows: « For indeed thus simply the theosophers themselves called Melchizedek not a friend of God, but also a priest. » Rightly, he says, did I say that Melchizedek is to be understood a priest, since the theosophers, that is, those who write of divine things, called him not thus simply a friend of God only, but also a priest. And why [1095B] they called him a priest he adds, saying: « Was it that they might signify openly to the wise that he converted not only from among them into him who truly is God? But moreover also others, that as highest priest he may lead them forth by that leading-back which is unto the true and sole divinity. » That Melchizedek, he says, who was not of the Jews, they called not only a friend of God, but also a priest of God — did they do this for this reason? Assuredly they did this for this reason: that they might openly signify to the wise that he converted not only from among them, that is, from Israel, who alone then seemed to be led back and converted to divine knowledge, into God himself, that is, to the faith and knowledge of that same God who truly is, but that he would also lead forth many from other nations out of the darkness of ignorance, namely by that leading-back which is unto the true [1095C] and sole divinity, that is, unto the knowledge of God. Not that Melchizedek himself is read to have converted any from Israel, which was not yet a people: unless perhaps we take Israel in the earlier fathers, of whom some perhaps through Melchizedek himself, in his own time, were led to the knowledge of God. Or so that it be read thus, namely that for this reason he was called not only a friend of God, but also a priest, because he converted — understand: the ignorant and those who knew not — unto God himself, in whom truly one must believe. And as though someone should object that Melchizedek himself, who was not of Israel but a foreigner, converted no one to the faith of God, since Israel alone knew God, he answers that not only from Israel were men converted to the faith of God, but also from other nations: which conversion of the others, namely, was accomplished through Melchizedek himself. Who then indeed, that is, when he blessed Abraham, was called a priest of the most high God, though he was not of Israel, that it might be shown that he was to lead forth many unto God, and that God had both a priest and a people not only in Israel, but also among the Gentiles. Therefore he does not say "he led forth," but « he shall lead forth, » he says. Signifying indeed something future to that time when these things were said — when either Melchizedek himself afterward by himself converted men from the Gentiles unto God, or Christ, of whom he was a type, led forth many, not only from Israel but also from all nations, through faith, unto his Church out of the darkness of unbelief.

There follows: « And this also we shall bring to the notice of thy highest [1096A] priestly understanding: that — the angel ruling over Pharaoh among the Egyptians themselves, and its own president presiding over the prince of the Babylonians, by all providence both provident of dominion and endowed with power, distributed according to the divisions — for those nations also the ministers of the true God were established as leaders of the formations by the manifestation of the vision from the angels, the angels forthwith revealing to the holy men Joseph and Daniel from God through the angels. » He adds yet another authority, from which he wishes to prove that good angels were set by God over the Gentiles: namely, that among the Egyptians the angel shows a vision to Pharaoh, and through it enjoins upon him a precaution concerning the coming barrenness; the same vision being then made manifest through Joseph; and likewise upon the prince of the Babylonians, namely Nebuchadnezzar, his own president, that is, his own [1096B] appointed one, namely his own angel, forms a vision, and afterward makes it manifest by unlocking it through Daniel. In all which it appears that God has both providence, and dominion, and power over all things, according to which, through those visions, it was discerned, or distinguished, or distributed: which visions were first revealed to the angels by God, and then forthwith through the angels to the holy men Joseph and Daniel. This is what he says: O Timothy, not only the higher things, but this also shall we bring to the notice of thy highest priestly understanding, that is, thy understanding — thou who art the highest priest — we shall bring this to thy notice, that, the angel ruling over Pharaoh among the Egyptians, and its own president ruling over the prince of the Babylonians, that is, the angel who was set over [1096C] him; by this, I say, that the angels are read to have ruled over them, there was distributed the provident and powerful quality of the divine providence and dominion according to those, namely, visions — that is, it was shown that God distributes his providence and power for the governing of the several nations; and it was also shown that for those nations the ministers of the true God were established as leaders; and how it was shown, this he adds: by the manifestation, namely, of the vision of the formations by the angels, that is, of the vision in which were displayed the formations, or figures, made by the angels in the minds of the prophets, to signify things to come — such as were the cows and the ears of grain which Pharaoh saw [n: (Gen. XLI)], and the tree and the statue which Nebuchadnezzar saw [n: (Dan. II)]. By the manifestation, therefore, of such a vision was displayed the divine providence and power, disposing the nations through the angels; the vision, I say, first revealed to the angels by God, and then forthwith revealed through the angels to the holy men, namely Joseph and Daniel, from God.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « For one is the principle and providence of all; and by no means is it to be supposed that the divinity indeed led the Jews fully, but that the angels, either specially, or equally honorably, or as opposites, or certain other gods, ruled over the other nations. » The repetition proves what he said above, that one is the principle and providence of all, that is, one God, from whom are all things, and by whom all things are ruled; and therefore by no means [1097A] is it to be supposed that the divinity led the Jews, that is, that it furnished leadership to the Jews fully, or absolutely, that is, through itself, namely without the intervening ministry of angels; but that over the other nations the angels ruled specially, that is, particularly, or with a less excellent power; or equally honorably, that is, with a power equal and like to the divine dominion; or as opposites (understand: with opposing sovereignties), just as the apostate angel is read to have said that he would set his seat toward the north and would be like to the Most High in power [n: (Isa. XIV)]. Or, again, it is not to be supposed that certain other gods ruled over the other nations, as though all things were not subject to the power and dominion of the one and true God. It is not, he says, so to be supposed, as though the true God had power [1097B] over the Jews alone, and over the other nations either angels, or certain other gods, ruled with a power either equal or unequal, yet contrary and foreign to the divine dominion. Or let it be read thus: By no means is it to be supposed that the Divinity, through itself, without an interposed subject angelic power, furnished leadership to the Jews; but that the angels ruled over the other nations specially, that is, particularly, namely among nations of a power and dignity inferior to that of the Jews themselves; or equally honorably, that is, among nations, namely, equally powerful and honorable; or even among opposing nations, which, namely, were opposed and contrary to the Jews themselves, assailing and oppressing them. It is not, he says, so to be supposed — namely that God for this reason led the Jews through himself [1097C] as though they were more honorable than all the other nations, and left the other nations, as inferior and baser, to the angelic dominion — since in other peoples many are not doubted to have been not only equally honorable, but even stronger and more powerful, oppressing the Jews themselves by their power and strength. For what has been said: « When the Most High divided the nations, he set the bounds of the peoples according to the number of the angels of God; but the Lord's portion is his people, Jacob the cord of his inheritance; » is not to be so understood, as though he had given the other nations to be ruled by angels, retaining Israel alone to himself, that is, to his own providence and governance — as if either Israel itself, without angels, or the angels, without him, [1097D] disposed the remaining nations; since both Michael is read to be set over Israel, and God is recorded in the sacred eloquence to possess and govern all nations. Not thus, therefore, is what is written to be understood: « But that eloquence, » in which, namely, this is said to be taken, « is according to the sacred understanding itself, » that is, according to such an understanding as does not disagree with the sacred, that is, with the truth. And this is the sacred understanding: that we understand this saying not as if, that is, as though, God dividing our leadership, that is, the leadership of the human race, with certain other gods or angels — dividing it, I say, into the principate of Israel and into the leadership of the nation, God being content, namely, with the principate over Israel, that is, retaining it to himself, so that [1098A] he alone should be to that people prince and leader. Not, I say, is it so to be understood. But so as, that is, as though, the exalted providence itself, or the providence of the Exalted One, which is one and at once of all, because it provides for all — that providence, I say, salutarily distributing all men by the restorative hand-leadings of their own angels, that is, by leaderships, by which as blind men in the darkness of ignorance they were led by the hand, and restored to the light of truth; and Israel alone being converted to the illumination and knowledge of the true God almost beyond all nations. For on this account Israel alone is read to have been received by God into a portion, and led; because it alone then, out of all nations, was called back to the knowledge of God, and established in the divine worship. « Whence, he says, theology, signifying that he, [1098B] that is God, » « possessed Israel unto the service of the true God, » says: « It became the portion of the Lord, » namely Israel itself. Thus, therefore, Israel was his portion, which was so possessed by him that it possessed and had him.

There follows: « But showing that he distributed even it, man by man, to the other nations, he said that Michael led the Jewish people, unto a certain one of the holy angels, that through him the one principle of all might be known. » In this, he says, that Holy Scripture said Michael led the Jewish people, it plainly shows that he, that is God, distributed also among the other nations, namely, leadership man by man, that is, one by one, to a certain one, that is, to some one of the holy angels — or, to say it more expressly, one nation to one, or each nation [1098C] to each, or the several to the several: distributed it, I say, that through him, namely the angel, that is, that each nation, through its own angel, might know the one principle of all, from whom are all things, and under whom all are ruled; so that, in order that Holy Scripture might show this, he says, it said that Michael was the leader of the Jewish people, whom God had specially taken up to be guarded under his own providence and governance. For, in saying this, it plainly taught us that there is one providence which presides over all in governing, under which the angels ruling over the several nations extend, or promote, those same nations that follow them by essential will, that is, by free choice naturally implanted, [1098D] into their own proper principle, namely by suggesting and helping; Scripture, he says, said that Michael led the Jewish people, whose leader it had elsewhere recorded him to be from God. In this « plainly teaching us that there is one providence of all, superessentially set above, » that is, presiding not only potentially, but even naturally, over all invisible and visible virtues at once, namely of angels and of men. For this is what stands as good in both. Showing also « that all the angels under that providence, ruling over the several nations, extend each, » or the several virtues of those nations, namely, into (understand: God) himself, « as into their own proper principle: » the virtues, I say, « following [1099A] by essential will, » that is, by free natural choice. For the angels arouse human minds to love God [1100A] by suggesting virtues, not compelled by necessity, but « following by will. »

## TITLE OF CHAPTER X. *A recapitulation of the ordering of the angelic synagogue.*



## THE TEXT.

[1099A]

*Thus, therefore, is the most honorable disposition of the minds about God connected, ordered from the perfective illumination, immediately ascending into it, more hidden: and by the more manifest illumination of the divinity it is purged, and illuminated, and perfected. More hidden indeed [1099B] as being more invisible, and more simplified and unified. But more manifest as given before, and shining first, and more universal, and having form more poured out into it, as is fitting. But from it again proportionally the second, and from the second the third, and from the third the hierarchy according to us, according to that same law of the well-adorning ordination, in the divine harmony and analogy unto the whole adornment of all good at once, is led back to the sovereign principle and consummation of the hierarchy. And all are manifesters, even the angels of those who are before them. They themselves indeed the most honorable of God who moves them, but proportionally the rest of those moved by God. For the superessential harmony of all foresaw for each of the rational and intellectual ones, with sacred adornment [1099C] and ordered leading, as much as each order of the hierarchies itself is sacredly and becomingly placed. And we see every hierarchy divided into first, and middle, and last virtues. But it also distinguished the very disposition — speaking specially, through each one — by those divine harmonies themselves. For which reason the theologians say that the most divine seraphim themselves cry one to another [n: (Isai. IV)]*, *openly declaring this, as I suppose: that the first themselves hand on the theological utterances to the second. But I would add perhaps, and this not incongruously, that each celestial and human mind, according to itself also, has special first, and middle, and last ordinations and virtues, manifested proportionally through each of the hierarchic illuminations, unto the aforesaid proper anagogies: [1099D] through which each is made, insofar as this itself is both lawful and possible, in participation of the most superunknown purgation, of the fullest light, of the fore-perfected perfection. For nothing is perfect through itself, needing the universal perfection, save only the truly most perfect and fore-perfect.*



## EXPOSITION.

The title of the tenth chapter is: A recapitulation of the ordering of the angelic synagogue. For here is briefly repeated what was said above, namely, the ordered synagogue, that is, the congregation, or angelic multitude, in all its first, and second, and third members. And the chapter thus begins:

« Thus, therefore, is the most honorable disposition of the minds about God connected, ordered from the perfective illumination, immediately ascending into it, [1100A] more hidden and more manifest; and by the illumination of the divinity it is purged, and illuminated, and perfected. » He speaks of the first hierarchy, which consists of three orders — seraphim, cherubim, and thrones. This disposition, then, or ordination, of the minds, that is, of the spirits, the most honorable or most excellent of all, [1100B] is connected about God, that is, both joined among themselves in concord, and immediately associated to God; thus ordered from the perfective illumination, that is, the divine, which alone perfectly shines and illuminates. For whosoever are illuminated by it are indeed, in shining, made like to it, and in illuminating others, imitate it. Yet neither in that they shine are they made equal to it, nor in that they illuminate are they compared to it. Therefore this hierarchy, which alone is illuminated by it immediately, is singularly ordered from the perfective illumination. Others, indeed, who receive the lower illuminations mediately, are also fittingly disposed and ordered lower down. But this hierarchy, which is ordered from the perfective, or teletarchic, and divine illumination, which is the sovereign [1100C] of sanctification, that is, the first and greatest and singular cause of sanctification — or, to say it more expressly, is made pontiff, that is, is raised into the pontifical order, or dignity — is fittingly set above all in dignity, namely by immediately ascending into that illumination, being more hidden, and more manifest, and by that same illumination of the divinity being purged, and illuminated, and perfected. But how the first hierarchy, from the fact that it receives the divine illumination immediately, is both more hidden and more manifest, he sets forth in the following words, saying: « More hidden indeed as being more invisible, and more simplified and vivified. But more manifest as that which, given before, shines, and shines first, and is more universal, and has form [1100D] more poured out into it, as is fitting. » It is more hidden, he says, in that it is more invisible, and more simplified, and unified. In this it is more hidden, that it is more invisible, and nearer to the simplicity and unity of the Godhead. But it is more manifest, inasmuch as it shines from the light given to it before, that is, before it was given to others: therefore it shines first, and more excellently. It is also more manifest because it is more universal, in which one likeness stands in many, since that is more known which is more common and suits more. And whence it is more universal and more alike, he consequently shows in turn, namely, because the form, that is, the divine illumination, from which it is formed so as to shine, is more, that is, more expressly, or more abundantly, poured out into it than into the other hierarchies below it. But the sense is this: because, namely, the divine illumination is poured first into this hierarchy, therefore [1101A] it is itself more perfectly illuminated. And by how much the more perfectly they who are in it, the spirits, receive the one illumination, by so much the more perfectly and clearly do they, shining in that one form of one light, become one; and what in them is diverse by nature and manifoldly distinguished, when the one form of clarity comes upon all, does not appear unlike. Thus, therefore, this hierarchy, because it shines first, shines more perfectly; and because it shines more perfectly, it shines more universally, or more alike, or more expressly; because it shines more expressly, it shines more manifestly. That, then, which is essentially more hidden, is formally more manifest; and that which is subtler by nature is clearer by grace.

There follows: « But from it again proportionally the second, and from the second the third, and from the third the hierarchy according to [1101B] us, according to that same law of the well-adorning ordination, in the divine harmony and analogy, unto the whole adornment of all good at once, is led back to the sovereign principle and consummation of the hierarchy. » From it, he says, the first hierarchy, is led back or converted the second, and by the second the third is converted, and by the third is converted that hierarchy, namely, which is according to us. For the first angelic hierarchy converts the second, the second the third, the third angelic converts the human. But how it converts he shows, saying: Unto the sovereign principle, and the consummation of the whole good adornment at once, that is, unto God, who is the principle and perfection of every good and beautiful disposition. And it leads back hierarchically, that is, according to the office, or [1101C] ordination, or law of the hierarchies, in which it is ordained that the higher, according to their manner and virtue, by illuminating and suggesting to the lower, convert them to their principle. And they convert according to that same law of the well-adorning ordination, that is, of the divine ordination, which adorns all things: whose law and institution it is, that some be illuminated by others, and converted in divine harmony and analogy, that is, in the concord and proportion conferred by God upon the universe. For in this, that the participation of the good is transfused through some to others proportionally, the concord in the universe is perfected and consummated. But that he says, "is again led back," is to be so understood as by a second leading-back. For the first leading-back is when [1101D] the first, illuminated by God himself, are converted unto God himself. The second, when the second are converted by the first, or the third by the second. « And all are manifesters, even the angels of those who are before them. » All the celestial spirits, he says, are manifesters, and angels, that is, messengers, of those who are before them. For what they receive from those preceding, they announce and manifest to those following after them.

There follows: « They themselves indeed the most honorable of God who moves them, but proportionally the rest of those moved by God. » He had said that all are messengers of those preceding to those following: now he subdistinguishes, that they themselves indeed, the most honorable, that is, the most excellent spirits, who have God alone before them, are messengers of God himself moving them by inspiration, that through them his secrets [1102A] he may reveal to those following. The rest, who follow after them, are messengers of those same, who are moved by God. Messengers, I say, they are proportionally, that is: the second, of the first, and of God through the first; the third, of the second, and of the first through the second; but of God, through the second and the first; and in this manner the rest that follow after this. But how the several proportionally reveal the divine secrets, he sets forth in the following words, showing that to each is given only so much power as befits the order and place in which it is set. This is what he says: « For the superessential harmony of all foresaw for each of the rational and intellectual ones, with sacred adornment and ordered leading, as much as each order of the hierarchies itself is sacredly and becomingly placed. » [1102B] The superessential harmony, he says, that is, the divine providence, which disposed all things concordantly, foresaw only so much, namely of virtue or power, for each of the rational or intellectual ones, with sacred adornment and ordered leading, namely sacredly adorning and orderly leading; foresaw only so much, I say, as each order of the hierarchies is sacredly and becomingly placed — this, as much as befits the holiness and becomingness of the order of each. Concerning which order in each hierarchy he adds distinctions, saying: « And we see every hierarchy divided into first, and middle, and last virtues, or orders. » And not only each hierarchy, but also each order through the several hierarchies. This is what follows: « But it also distinguished the very [1102C] disposition — speaking specially through each one — by those divine harmonies themselves. » The divine providence itself, he says, distinguished, or distinguished apart, the very disposition, namely each one, that is, the order, by the divine harmonies, so that, namely, there might be distinction, and there might be no discrepancy. And this is to be said specially through the several hierarchies: the several orders have special distinctions.

There follows: « For which reason the theologians themselves say that the most divine seraphim themselves cry one to another: openly, as I openly suppose, declaring that the first themselves hand on the theological utterances to the second. » For which reason — namely, because the several orders are distinct within themselves — therefore the theologians themselves say that the seraphim themselves, [1102D] who are most divine, that is, nearest to the divinity, cry one to another, as is read in Isaiah [n: (Isai. VI)]. In this, namely, that they say they cry one to another, openly declaring that the theological, that is, divine utterances, they who are first in that order hand on to those who are constituted second in the same order. In which it is clear that one and the same order has first and second. But if this is truly taken of the first and highest order, of those following it can by no means be doubted.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « But I would add perhaps, and this not incongruously, that each celestial and human mind, according to itself also, has special first, and middle, and last ordinations and virtues, assigned through each of the hierarchic [1103A] illuminations, manifested proportionally unto its proper anagogies: through which each is made, insofar as this itself is both lawful and possible, in participation of the most superunknown purgation, and of the fullest light, and of the fore-perfected perfection. » The sense is this: that not only is the universal hierarchy divided into a first, and a middle, and a last hierarchy, and the several hierarchies into first, and middle, and last orders, and the several orders into first, and middle, and last spirits; but even the several angelic, or human, spirits themselves are, within themselves, virtually distinct, containing first, and middle, and last virtues: through which, by their own anagogies, that is, upward-leadings, or ascensions, from the lowest to the middle, and from the middle to the highest, ascending proportionally according to the hierarchic [1103B] illuminations, they are made partakers, as far as is possible for them, of the divine purgation, which is most unknown, or most hidden, because it purges inwardly; and of the divine light, which is fullest, because it puts all darkness to flight; and of the divine perfection, which is fore-perfected, because it is prior to all in eternity, and higher in dignity, and fuller in truth. But he calls the hierarchic illuminations proportional for this reason, because in the sacred powers, where some are constituted higher, others lower, they do not all perceive the divine illuminations in one manner. For to the higher and more capacious, more is bestowed in the gifts of grace; to the lower, according to the measure of their capacity, less is granted to be participated. According to this manner, then, by which, whether in the several [1103C] hierarchies to the diverse orders, and to the diverse minds, the divine illuminations are differently bestowed, each mind also within itself, in those same illuminations, growing by a reasonable difference from lesser to greater, is advanced. This is what he says: « But I would add this also, not incongruously, » as if he should say: Not only concerning the several hierarchies or orders can I fittingly say that they have first, and middle, and last orders, or spirits; but this also perhaps I can, not incongruously, add to what has been said above, namely, that each celestial and human mind, according to itself, or in itself, has special, that is, proper, first, and middle, and last ordinations and virtues, that is, virtues ordained in the first, and middle, and last place within it. To [1103D] what end it has differing virtues, he adds, [1104A] namely, unto its proper anagogies — that each, namely, may have its own proper ascensions, and advancements unto the better, as it is written: « He hath disposed ascensions in his heart [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]; » and again: « They go from virtue to virtue: and the God of gods shall be seen in Sion. » And unto these anagogies, that is, ascensions, are disposed the virtues, some lower, some higher, so that, by ascending from the lower to the higher, they may at length be made partakers of the highest good, which is the God of gods in Sion. Each spirit, he says, has its own proper virtues, first, and middle, and last, unto its proper anagogies, that is, ascensions, to be made. Anagogies, I say, already spoken of, that is, which we have already spoken of above, namely, through each order of the hierarchic illuminations: [1104B] because, when we showed how, in the distribution of the hierarchies, the distinct orders perceive differing illuminations, we also demonstrated in what manner among them the lower ascend proportionally, that is, by a reasonable difference of advancement, through the higher unto the highest. Therefore he said the anagogies of the hierarchic illuminations manifested proportionally, or manifested in proportions, or from the preceding treatment, in which this was demonstrated, that those ascensions are made proportionally; or manifested proportionally in this, because by how much the more the ascension grows, by so much the more the knowledge grows. On account of these ascensions, therefore, the grades of virtues are disposed, by which ascensions, namely, each thing, that is, each mind ascending [1104C] through them, is made in participation, that is, is made partaker of the most superunknown purgation, and of the fullest light, and of the fore-perfected perfection. Partaker, I say, it is made "as this itself," that is, that it may be made partaker, is both lawful and possible: lawful, as to the dignity of the good to be participated; possible, as to its own capacity.

There follows: « For nothing is perfect through itself, needing the universal perfection, save the truly most perfect, and fore-perfect. » Rightly, he says, did I call the divine perfection fore-perfected; because all things that are perfect are perfect from it; because nothing is perfect through itself, and there is nothing which is not through itself imperfect, and which is not through itself needy of the invisible, or of every perfection, save that highest good: [1104D] which is both truly most perfect, because nothing is lacking to it; and fore-perfect, because its fullness is eternal.

## TITLE OF CHAPTER XI. *Why all the celestial essences are commonly named celestial virtues.*



## THE TEXT.

[1103D]

*But these things being defined, it behooves to understand that which is worthy: for what cause we are wont to call all the angelic essences alike celestial virtues. For it is not to be said, as in the angels, that the very disposition of the holy virtues is the last of all. And indeed the dispositions of the superposed essences partake the holy and comely illumination of the last; [1104D] but the last in no wise the illumination of the first. And for whose sake are all the divine intellects indeed named celestial virtues; but the seraphim, and thrones, and dominations in no wise. For the extremes are partaken of by the most excellent, in the universal properties. For the angels themselves, and before the angels the archangels, and the principalities, and the powers, ordered after the virtues by theology itself, are often commonly called by us, together with the other [1105A] holy essences, celestial virtues. But we say that, using commonly in all the naming of celestial virtues, we do not introduce a certain confusion of the properties of each disposition; but, because they are divided into three according to themselves by a supermundane reason — all the divine intellects — into essence, and virtue, and operation, when we call all of them at once, or certain of them unobservantly, either celestial essences, or celestial virtues, it is to be supposed that we signify periphrastically those of whom the discourse is, from that essence or virtue which is through each of them. For neither is it easy for us now to annex the superposed property of the holy virtues, already distinguished by us, altogether to the lesser essences, in a turning-about of the unconfused ordination of the angelic adornments. For according to the reason often rightly [1105B] rendered by us, the superfirmed dispositions indeed have abundantly the sacred properties even of the lesser; but the last do not have the superposed universalities of the greater, the illuminations first appearing particularly into them, distributed to them proportionally through the first.*



## EXPOSITION.

The title of the eleventh chapter is: Why all the celestial essences are commonly called virtues. This is the question: since only one order among the celestial spirits is called virtues, why is this name commonly attributed to all. And he says:

« But these things being defined, it behooves to understand that which is worthy: for what cause we are wont to call all the angelic essences alike celestial virtues. » [1105C] This indeed is worthy of inquiry; because it is not established that the cause here is the one which we render when we call all the celestial essences angels: namely, that the order of the angels is the last, and therefore its name is assumed to the higher orders, because its property is partaken by them; since all the higher, just as they partake all the properties of the lower, so also fittingly sometimes assume their name. But here we cannot say likewise, when we name all the celestial spirits virtues; because, since certain orders are lower than the virtues themselves, they, just as they do not partake in the property of the higher, so also it seems that they ought not to partake in the name. This is what he says: « For it is not [1105D] to be said, as in the angels, namely, that the disposition of the holy virtues itself is the last of all. » For if this were true, it would be no wonder if all the other orders partook their name, because they would partake their property as being higher. This is what follows: « And indeed of the last essences, » that is, of the last spirits, « the dispositions of the superposed essences partake the holy and comely illumination; but the last, » understand: dispositions, « in no wise, » namely, partake the illumination of the first essences.

There follows: « And for whose sake are all the divine intellects indeed named celestial virtues; but the seraphim, and thrones, and dominations in no wise. » [1106A] It is worthy, he says, to understand for what — understand: thing — cause all the divine intellects are named celestial virtues, whereas the seraphim, and thrones, and dominations are in no wise named. For that they are named angels is no wonder, because the angels are last, and their property is universally partaken by the higher. This is what he says: « For the extremes, » understand: essences, such as are the angels, « are partaken of by the most excellent virtues » in all their properties; but not conversely do the properties of the higher come universally to the participation of the lower. Therefore it is a wonder how the angels, and those who are before the angels, the archangels, and the principalities, and the powers, assume the name of virtues, since they do not partake the property; because « after the virtues [1106B] they are ordered by theology itself, » that is, they are reported to be ordered after the virtues by the divine Scripture. And yet « together with the other holy essences they are called by us celestial virtues. »

There follows: « But we say that, using commonly in all the naming of celestial virtues, we do not introduce a certain confusion of the properties of each disposition. » Now he resolves the question, why the name of virtues is attributed to the lower orders. « We say, he says, that we, using the naming of celestial virtues, commonly also for all, do not bring in any confusion of the properties of each disposition, » that is, by this, that we make the name common, we do not confound the property of distinction. But rather, when [1106C] we name the lower orders virtues, we signify not the property of that order, but the virtue common to all. For all the divine intellects are divided into three, not among themselves, one, namely, in relation to another, but each according to itself, or in itself, « by a supermundane reason, » that is, spiritual. For that they are divided is not made from a consideration of parts, where there is not simplicity of essence, but there is a spiritual distinction, where not the whole is discerned into parts, but the nature into properties. Afterward he adds into what three each spirit is divided within itself, namely « into essence, and virtue, and operation. » For in every spirit these three are. First, essence, in which it subsists; then virtue, according to which it is strong; then operation, [1106D] through which it effects. « When therefore we call all together, » namely spirits, « or certain of them, unobservantly, » that is, indistinctly or commonly, « either celestial essences, or celestial virtues, it is to be supposed that we signify those, of whom the discourse is, periphrastically, » that is, by circumlocution, « from that essence, or virtue, which is through each of them, » that is, which is common to all, not from that which is properly singular, which discerns the dignity of its order. But why he is unwilling to attribute the singular property of one order to the other subject orders, he shows, saying: « For neither, he says, is it easy for us to annex the superposed property of the holy virtues, already distinguished by us, » that is, properly and singularly attributed to them, « to the lesser essences, » or to bestow it, [1107A] « in a turning-about of the unconfused ordination of the angelic adornments; » because if I should do this, I should turn about, or pervert, the ordination of the angelic adornments, which is unconfused. But how the ordination of the angelic adornments, that is, of the angelic orders beautifully or comely disposed, is unconfused, he shows, namely, because they keep their own distinction, and do not mingle themselves with the higher, in whose properties they have no part. « The superfirmed dispositions indeed, » that is, the higher orders, « have abundantly, » or fully, « even the sacred properties of the lesser; but the last do not have the superposed universalities of the greater. » A little above we already said how, there, where God is all in all, anything proper can belong to any one, which [1107B] is not common to all. Here too the author has not altogether kept silence how this is to be understood, in that he says: « They have abundantly. » In this, then, is the property to be understood, which befits the higher, [1108A] does not befit the lower; that the higher have abundantly whatever the lower have; but the lower do not have abundantly whatever the higher have. For what can be so great there, which does not pass into the communion of all — there, where charity is common, which is greater than all? Therefore all things are of all. But this alone is proper there, that it is abundantly to any one what is not to all; because the higher virtues indeed have universally and abundantly those of the lower; but the lower do not equally have the virtues of the higher universally, that is, according to all fullness, of the divine illuminations, « only particularly first appearing into them. » Not first appearing into them, but first particularly into them. For into others, that is, [1108B] into the higher, they first appear universally, and then afterward through those first ones they are distributed proportionally, that is, according to proportion, not according to all its fullness.

## TITLE OF CHAPTER XII. *Why hierarchies according to men are called angels.*



## THE TEXT.

[1107B]

*But this also is inquired by those studiously beholding the intelligible oracles. For if the participants of the more excellent universalities of the virtues are not the last, for what cause is the highest priest according to us named an angel of God almighty by the oracles? [n: (Malach. II; Apoc. II.)] But there is not a contrary reason, as I suppose, to the things before defined. For we say that [1107C] by the universal and superposed virtue of the greater adornments the last are left behind. For they partake the middle and proportional according to the one conjunctive society of all at once: such as is that the order of the holy cherubim partakes wisdom and a higher knowledge. But under them the dispositions of the essences do indeed themselves also partake wisdom and knowledge, particular, however, in relation to those, and subject. And indeed to be altogether in participation of wisdom and of knowledge is common to all deiform intellects. But attentively, and firstly, or secondly, or below, is by no means common; but as for each one before, its proper analogy is defined. And this, of all the divine minds, one perhaps not erring will define. For as the first have more abundantly the holy and beautiful [1107D] properties of the lesser, so the last have those of the first; not however alike, but below. Nothing therefore, as I suppose, is inordinate, if theology also calls the highest priest according to us an angel, according to the proper virtue of the angels partaking of the prophetic property, and, unto a manifestative likeness of them, extended as far as is possible for men. But thou wilt find that theology calls both gods, and the celestial essences above us, and those among us most beloved of God, and wonderful men. And indeed the divine secret, superessentially at once remote from all, and set above, and none of those things which are from it can properly, or at all, be named like to it. Nevertheless whatsoever, both of the intellectual [1108B] and of the rational, is universally converted to the unity of it, and whatever virtue, and is unceasingly extended to its divine illuminations, as far as is possible, according to virtue — if it is just to say it — is made worthy by divine imitation, and by a divine univocation.*



## EXPOSITION.

The title of the twelfth chapter is: Why the hierarchies according to men are called angels. The sense of it is: [1108C] Why those who are hierarchies, that is, the sacred princes according to men or among men — such as are the highest pontiffs, or any priests and the other ministers of the word of God — why, namely, those are called angels, since it was said above that the lower, just as they do not partake the properties of the higher, so also ought not to assume their names. For if the order of the angels, because it is lower than the virtues, cannot partake their name according to their property, neither can men (because they are undoubtedly lower than the angels) usurp their name according to their property. But this question is resolved by that which we said above: that the gifts of spiritual grace, which pass into the communion of all, [1108D] also bring their appellation into the participation of all; and although, by the fact that they are possessed singularly through excellence by certain ones, they make a distinct appellation, yet by the fact that in some manner they are common, they also sometimes, according to the requirement of the causes, bring their names to a common appellation.

« But this also is inquired by those who studiously behold the intelligible oracles. » By those studiously beholding, that is, by the studious inspectors of the intelligible oracles, that is, of the divine oracles — which are intelligible, that is, spiritual and profound, made concerning intelligible things — is inquired, I say, this, namely, why men set over others are called angels. [1109A] But why this is inquired he subjoins the reason: « For if the last, that is, the lower orders, are not partaking of the virtues of the higher universalities, » that is, of the properties which are in the higher universally, or, to say it more expressly, universally, that is, fully and perfectly: « for what cause then is the priest, who according to us » or among us, namely men, « is the highest, named by the oracles an angel of God almighty? » For if he does not have the singularity of the property, why does he assume the expression of the appellation? It seems contrary that a name of distinction should be brought to communion. But yet, he says, « this reason, » or assertion, « is not contrary, as I suppose, to the things before defined, » that is, to those which were defined before, namely concerning the distinction of the several ones. For in [1109B] this, that we concede such a communion of appellation to the many, we do not take away the distinction of the several ones. « For we truly say that the last, » or lowest, namely orders, « are left behind by the universal and superposed virtue of the greater adornments, » that is, of the more excellent orders, so that, namely, they do not partake the virtues of the higher according to that universality, or totality, or fullness by which those partake. And yet they can sometimes fittingly partake their name; because, although they do not partake their virtue according to an equal fullness, they nevertheless partake it according to a like imitation.

« For they partake, he says, the middle and the proportional, » understand: virtue — that is, they partake [1109C] their virtue, and though not according to the highest participation, in which they would be equal, yet they partake according to a middle, or moderate and lower, participation, in which they can be alike; but this participation is made not according to the singular perfection of each one, but « according to the one and conjunctive society of all at once, » that is, not according to that which is singular to each one, but according to that which is common to all. And because it is one, that is, common to all, in it all are at once joined and associated. And so, according to that which is common to all, the singular names are drawn to a common signification; because, as we said, even that which through excellence [1109D] is proper to certain ones is, through participation, common to all. « Such as is that the order of the holy cherubim partakes wisdom, and a higher knowledge, » etc. By the subjoined example he proves what he said: that that which certain ones alone excellently possess comes, according to a lower communion, into the participation of all; such as is this, that the order of the holy cherubim partakes wisdom and a higher knowledge. « But the dispositions of the essences, » that is, the orders of spirits which are under the cherubim themselves, « do indeed themselves also partake wisdom and knowledge; but particular, however, and subject in relation to those, » that is, not so perfectly nor so excellently as they. And truly not the cherubim alone partake wisdom and knowledge, but also others lower; [1110A] because « to be altogether, » that is, universally, « in the participation of wisdom and knowledge is common to all deiform intellects, » that is, to all spirits conformable and like to God through the reason implanted in them. To partake wisdom in some manner is indeed common to all. But « to partake it attentively and firstly, » that is, expressly and principally, « or secondly, or even to partake it below, is by no means common to all. » For those who do not partake firstly partake secondly, or below, that is, they partake more lowly and more imperfectly; but they do not also partake firstly; because whoever have perfection have also the beginning; but not whoever have the beginning can straightway have perfection. « But » they have each thing only [1110B] « as it is defined to each one, » or dispensed, or as some one thing is determinately assigned, « according to its proper analogy, » that is, according to the rule, or manner, or proper measure, which was predestined and foreseen for it before all things in the eternal providence of God. « But this, » namely that the lower partake the virtues of the higher not according to fullness, yet in some manner, perhaps « someone will define, » or affirm, « of all the divine minds, » that is, of the rational spirits, « not erring » in this, because it is true. « For indeed as the first, » and the higher orders, « have more abundantly the holy and beautiful properties of the lesser, so the last themselves and the lesser have those properties which are of the former: not however alike, but below, » that is, more imperfectly than they. Therefore, [1110C] he says, « as I suppose, nothing is inordinate, » understand: was or is, « if theology calls the highest priest according to us an angel, » that is, him who according to us men discharges the highest priesthood, or calls him an angel according to us, because, namely, he announces to us the word of God; and therefore an angel, because « partaking according to his proper virtue, » that is, according to the grace given to him from his proper office: « partaking, » I say, « of the prophetic property » of the angels, because it belongs to him to prophesy, and to announce the word of God as the angels announce. And him, I say, also « extended, » or advanced, or exalted, unto the manifestative likeness of them, namely of the angels, because in this he is like the angels, that he manifests [1110D] or reveals the secrets of God for the instruction of his subjects. Nor is it a wonder if men are called angels, since angels and men alike have been called gods.

This is what he says: « But thou wilt find that theology calls even gods the celestial and higher essences themselves, » that is, the angels, and not only those, but « also certain ones » among us, that is, men of our kind, namely « wonderful, and most beloved of God. » But why men are called angels, or men gods, he subjoins the cause: namely, because the divinity, although it is secret and remote, according to the excellence of its nature, from every creature, is nevertheless in some manner conceived and partaken by the rational minds which turn themselves through virtue to its unity and illumination; and therefore, inasmuch as they are [1111A] partakers of the divinity, they can in some manner not unfittingly be called gods.

This is what he says: « And indeed the divine secret, » that is, the secret of the divinity, « both is remote and is set above, superessentially, at once above all things; and none of those which are from it, » understand: created, « can at all and properly be named like to it; » because the Creator and the creature cannot expressly be alike. « Nevertheless whatsoever virtue of the intellectual and the rational, » that is, of the angels, « and, » to say it universally, « whatsoever [1112A] virtue, » whether of angels or of men, « is converted to the unity of it, » namely of the divinity; and whatsoever virtue is unceasingly extended to receive the divine illuminations of that same Deity, that virtue is made worthy of divine imitation through likeness, and of divine univocation through appellation: made worthy, I say, is that virtue according to virtue, that is, worthy in so far as it is virtue. Yet only if it is just to say this, that any virtue, however great, can be worthy of « divine imitation, and divine univocation. »

## TITLE OF CHAPTER XIII. *Why the prophet Isaiah is said to have been purged by a seraph.*





## THE TEXT.

[1111B]

*Come, let us consider this also according to our power: for what reason one of the seraphim is said by the theologians to have been sent [n: (Isai. VI)]. For someone may answer that it is not one of the subordinate angels, but a certain one of the greatest and innermost essences, that purges the priest. Some therefore say that, according to the definition of the society of all the intellects already rendered before, the oracle does not name one of the first minds about God to have come unto the purgation of the theologian; but that a certain one of the angels excelling us, performing the sacrifice of the prophet's purgation, was called seraph by equivocation, on account of the fiery and celestial washing away of the aforesaid sins, and the raising up of the purged one into the divine obedience. And they say the oracle said 'one of the seraphim' simply, [1111C] not one of those set about God, but of the purgative virtues excelling us. But another furnished me a certain not too unfitting apology concerning this kind of state. For he says that the great angel who then was, forming the vision to teach the theologian divine things, referred his own purgative sacrifice unto God, and after God unto the pre-operative hierarchy. And is not this reason then truthful? For he who said this says: How the divine virtue, coming into all things, fills them, and immeasurably penetrates through all, and again is invisible to all — not only as being superessentially remote from all, but also as secretly letting into all things its provident operations. But nevertheless it also shines above proportionally in all the intellectual beings, and, bringing its own [1111D] illumination into the most precious essences, through those as the first ones distributes itself in well-ordered fashion into others established under them, according to the contemplative commensuration of each disposition. Which, that I may say it more openly, also through examples proper — though deficient in relation to God, remote from all, yet more manifest to us. The distributions of the solar ray, well distributed into the first matter, fill it, the most lucid of all, and through it more manifestly it declares its own splendors. But approaching grosser matters it has a dimmer distributive over-appearance, from the matters to be illuminated in relation to the distributive habit of illumination in point of opportuneness; and a little afterward, from this, it is contracted to what is almost perfectly undistributed. Again the heat of fire distributes itself more into the more capacious things, and into those well suited and easy [1112B] to its own likeness. But toward the essences contrary to its re-formings, it manifests no hidden trace of its primal operation. And this the more, because to those things which are not cognate it is admitted through the things possessing what is opportune to it — first, inasmuch as it makes things easily movable ignited from the fiery ones, and through these heating proportionally either water, or something else of the things not easily kindled. According to this reason then of natural ordination, the supernatural ordination itself of every good adornment, visible and invisible, fittingly manifests to the most exalted essences the brightness of illumination first appearing, as in the most copious outpourings; and through those the essences which are after partake the divine ray. For these first ones, knowing God, and supereminently desiring the divine [1112C] virtue, and to become pre-operative, as far as is possible, with a virtue like to God, have been made worthy also in action; and they themselves extend deiformly the essences after them unto a like virtue, as virtue, copiously delivering to them from the brightness supervening upon them, and those again to their subjects; and through each one the first delivers to that which is after it. Does not the water itself also proportionally reach into all? God then is at once, to all the illuminated, the principle of illuminating — by nature indeed, and truly and properly, as the essence of light, and causal of its very being and seeing. Then, deiformly and God-likely remaining superposed, transferring through itself the divine lights after it into each one, into that. Therefore the essences of all the remaining ones at once [1112D] marvel at the most excellent disposition of the celestial minds, as, according to what is consequent, the principle after God of all sacred and divine knowledge and of divine imitation — the divine illumination being distributed, as it were, through those into all, and into us. Wherefore they also refer every sacred and God-like operation unto God as the causal one; then unto the first deiform intellects as the first operators of divine things, and masters. Does not then the first disposition of the holy angels have, more than all together, the fiery property, and the poured-out tradition of the divine wisdom, and the mystical property of the most exalted knowledge of the divine illuminations, and, bearing the sedent property, signifying the divine reception. But the dispositions themselves of the subordinate essences do indeed partake the fiery, the wise, and the knowing receptive virtue of God — but below, and looking to the first [1113A] ones, and through them, as made worthy by divine imitation to be pre-operative, brought back into what is possible of deiformity. The said holy properties, therefore, of which the participation comes about through the first ones for those subsisting after them, they place back in those very ones, after God, as in hierarchies. He says therefore these things, saying [n: (ibid.)]: That the vision descending from her was received by him for the theologian, through one of the holy and blessed angels ruling over us, and that he referred back his illuminative leading-by-the-hand into that holy contemplation before. And he saw the most exalted essences sitting above — as far as is to be said in symbols — placed after God, and about God, and with God; and, more sublime than all these themselves in a super-arcane way, the super-principal summit super-placed in the midst of the super-firmed virtues. The theologian himself therefore learned by the visions [1113B] that, according to every superessential super-excellence, the divine is incomparably super-placed above every invisible and visible virtue of all things. And that it is remote from all as universal, nor like even to the first essences of the things that are. Moreover, that it is itself the principle of all, and the substance-making cause, and, in secret singularity, the immutable foundation of the things that are — from which being, and well-being, is even to the supremely established virtues themselves. Then he was taught the same deiform virtues of the most holy seraphim; by their sacred surname indeed — which is the fiery, concerning which we shall speak a little later, as far as is possible for us — to introduce, into the deiform, the anagogies of the fiery virtue. But by the sacred formation of the expanded wings, the absolute and highest extension into the divine, in the first, and in the middle, [1113C] and in the last intellects. But the intellectual theologian, seeing also their manifold and multiform character, and that the vision was distinguished by wings beneath the feet, and it beneath the faces, and its ever-motion in the midst of the wings, was brought back to the invisible knowledge of the things which were seen, the many-wayed and many-seeing virtue of the highest intellects being manifested to him; and by their sacred awe, which they have supermundanely against the proud, and bold, and impossible scrutiny of higher things and of lower; and, in the commensuration of the actions imitating God, the unceasing and high-flying character of their ever-motion. But he was also instructed in that divine and much precious hymnody, the angel forming the vision according to virtue for the theologian himself, and [1113D] delivering his own sacred knowledge. He taught him therefore this also, that purgation is, for those purged however much, the participation, as far as is possible, of the divine brightness itself, unknown. But this brightness, from the remote causes themselves of the divinity, by which it perfects all sacred intellects with superessential occultation, is somehow more manifest to the highest virtues about itself, and more manifests and distributes itself. Then to the second, or the last, or our intellectual virtues — as far as each one has come forth from it according to the deiform — thus it conducts its manifest illumination unto the praising of the unknown of its own occultation. But it shines through the several ones, to the second through the first. And if it behooves to say it briefly, it is first led from the hidden to the manifest through the first virtues. This therefore the theologian learned from the light-[1114A]leading angel, namely purgation, and that all the divine operations, shining back through the first essences, are distributed into all the rest, according to the analogy of each one unto the deifying participations. Wherefore he consequently placed back the fierily purgative property in the seraphim themselves, after God. Nothing therefore is inordinate, if a seraph is said to purge the theologian. For thus God purges all, of whose whole purgation he is the cause. But let us more closely use an example: just as he who according to us is the highest priest, purging or illuminating through his ministers or priests, is himself said to purge and to illuminate — the orders purged through him referring back through themselves unto him their own sacred operations; so also the angel accomplishing the purgation of the theologian places back his own purgative knowledge and virtue [1114B] unto God as the causal one, then unto the seraph itself as the highest priest first acting: as perhaps someone, with angelic reverence, teaching the purged one, might say that the principle of the purgation to be accomplished in thee, before me, is indeed the exalted one, and essence, and creator, and causal, bringing the first essences to be present, and containing them about himself in their collocation; and keeping them unturnable and free from falling; and moving himself into the first participations of his own provident operations; for this, teaching me these things, he says the mission of the seraph itself manifests. But the highest priest, and after God the leader himself, the adornment of the excelling essences, from whom I have been deiformly instructed to purge, he therefore it is who through me purges thee — he through whom the very cause and artificer of the whole purgation brought forth from the [1114C] hidden even into us his own provident actions. These things indeed he taught me; but to thee I deliver them, and I would grant to thy intellectual and discerning knowledge either to absolve one part of the said causes from doubt, and to honor the same before the other as having what is consequent, and reasonable, and equally true; or, from thyself — which is truly nearer to the truth — to find it, or to learn it from another; God, namely, giving it, and the angels first receiving it, and to us, the friends of the angels, revealing it, through his more lovable contemplation.*



## EXPOSITION.

« Come, let us consider this also according to virtue. » Come now, he says, O Timothy, after the other things which have been said, [1114D] let us consider this also according to our virtue and possibility, « for what reason, namely, it is said by the theologians that one of the seraphim was sent » — in Isaiah, namely, when, the coal having been taken up from the altar with the tongs, he purged the lips of the prophet [n: (Isa. VII)]. And it is necessary that we inspect this question, and discuss it, because it can become doubtful whether by 'seraph' here some one of the higher order is to be understood, on account of the property of the name; or one of the lower, on account of the property of administration, because it is proper to the weak and the lowest to administer our hierarchy. Therefore the question is to be inspected, that the ambiguity of the estimation may be taken away. For perhaps « someone may answer that it is not one of the subordinate ones, » that is, some one of the Angels, « that purges the priest, » [1115A] namely Isaiah; « but a certain one of the innermost and greatest essences, » that is, the supreme spirits. But this again can be doubtful to someone, for the reason that the supreme orders are not sent to dispense, or administer, exterior things.

Therefore « certain ones say that, according to the definition of the society of all the intellects already rendered before » by us, that is, according to that which we defined above — they say that all spirits have among themselves a certain society of properties and names — that the oracle « does not name, » that is, « does not » say « that some one of the first minds came unto the purgation of the theologian, » that is, of the first spirits about God, understand: established. But they say « that a certain one rather of those excelling us, » who [1115B] are set only over us men, « performing the sacrifice of the prophet's purgation, » that is, making it holy, and offering as it were a sacrifice pleasing to God, « was called seraph, by equivocation, » that is, of the name, not by the excellence of the property. But by what likeness an angel of the lowest order was called seraph, he subjoins: « On account of the fiery and celestial washing away, » or cleansing, « of the » sins « aforesaid » already above, of the prophet himself, and on account of the « raising up » also, or vivification, of that same one « purged into the divine obedience; » because just as it belongs to fire to purge rust by burning, and to vivify dead and extinguished things by heating, so by that divine fire he was both purged from sins, and inflamed [1115C] to the love of God. And because this was done by the ministry of an angel, certain ones « say » that the sacred « oracle » « said 'one of the seraphim' simply, not one » of the virtues « set about God, » but rather one « of the purgative virtues excelling us, » that is, one of the angels set over us, that is, over men — yet him called 'seraph' on account of the likeness of the action.

There follows: « But another furnished me a certain not too unfitting apology concerning this kind of state. » Certain ones, he says, hold that the angel who purged the prophet was of the supreme spirits, on account of this, that he was called seraph. But others say that he was of the lower ones, and called seraph only on account of the likeness of the action: [1115D] thus each one defining probably according to his own opinion. But a certain « other, » agreeing wholly neither with those nor with these, « furnished me a certain apology, » that is, an excuse, or satisfaction, or defense, « not too unfitting, » concerning this kind of state, that is, definition, by which the state of the matter is described; or concerning this kind of instance, that is, question, which in some manner presses, and is importunate until it is resolved.
