But what satisfaction or defense he himself furnished concerning this question, he subjoins: « For he says (understand: he who furnishes the apology) that that great angel, who then was forming the vision for teaching the theologian divine things, » that is, in the mind of the theologian, whom that angel was bound to teach divine things through that vision [1116A] — namely, his own purgative sacrifice, that is, the operation by which he then properly and singularly purged the lips of the prophet, and made them holy, that operation of his he referred back « unto God, » that is, attributed to God, « and after God referred it back unto the pre-operative hierarchy, » that is, unto the hierarchy in which and through which God first, that is, before all the others, operates. But he attributed his action to that hierarchy for this reason, that from it, after God, he principally received that he should do this. And this is the reason why the seraph is said to be the highest, namely because the angel who did this, just as he principally received it, after God, from the seraph, so also, after God, attributed it to the seraph itself. This reason therefore he rendered concerning this kind of question. « And is not this reason then [1116B] truthful? » These are the words of the author, who seems to accommodate his own estimation to this opinion.

This reason seems to be truthful because that man, « who said this, » says, showing « how the divine virtue, coming into all things, fills all » — and not only comes, but also penetrates, and passes through all, immeasurably crossing over them, « and is invisible to all. » And again this is invisible, thus transcending all without end, as it is invisible to all, subsisting before all without beginning. Or « again it is invisible » through immensity, as it is invisible through eternity, because just as temporal intelligence does not grasp eternity, so a circumscribable nature does not comprehend immensity. Or again « it is invisible, » that is, not only in itself, according as [1116C] it is essentially and ineffably remote from all, but also to us, according as it is incomprehensibly hidden from all. In itself therefore it is invisible, and in us it is invisible, and in both ways it remains incomprehensible. This is what he says: « Because it is not only invisible in itself, as being superessentially remote from all, but » it is also invisible in us, « as secretly letting, » or causing to penetrate, « into all things its provident operations. » Thus therefore the divine virtue is both invisible in itself as remote, and invisible in us as hidden, through operation, because according to its providence it operates in us. « But nevertheless, » although it is thus invisible, it shines above, pouring itself out [1116D] unto manifestation « to all the intellectual beings, » that is, to the rational minds. « It shines above, » I say, « proportionally, » that is, to some more, to others less, namely according to the measure and capacity of each one. And though it shines above commonly to all, yet excellently « bringing its own illumination into the most precious essences, » that is, into the subtlest and most excellent spirits: « through those » essences, namely, « as the first ones » it afterward distributes itself in well-adorned fashion « into others, » namely essences, « established » « under those » first ones, that is, made firm, or collocated, or disposed. « It distributes, » I say, « according to the contemplative commensuration of each disposition, » that is, according as each disposition, or order, is capable of that contemplation according to its proper [1117A] measure: « which, » understand: distribution of the divine illumination, arriving thus differently according to the measure of the receivers, « that I may say it more openly, also through examples proper; » proper, I say, « though » that is, although, yet « deficient in relation to God, remote from all; » that is, deficient in comparison with God, who is remote from all things, and can properly be demonstrated by no likeness. Therefore deficient, and not proper. « Nevertheless » proper, because « more manifest to us. » Not proper to the divine majesty, proper to human possibility; not proper as it is in him, proper as it can be shown to us. That therefore I may show, through examples not proper to God but proper to us, the distribution of the divine illumination to be dispensed, [1117B] I set forth this likeness. « The distributions of the solar ray, well distributed into the first matter, fill it, the most lucid of all, and through it it more manifestly declares its own splendors. » The distributions, he says, or outpourings of the solar ray, well distributed into the first matter, that is, coming into that matter which is first, that is, best and purest, and most apt for illumination; coming, I say, to such a matter, they fill it, finding in no part any obstacle to the light, and by filling they make it more lucid, most lucid of all; and thus through it the ray itself more manifestly declares its own splendors, because the more perfectly it radiates, the more perfectly it will be illuminated; and the more perfectly it is poured into it, the more brightly [1117C] it is poured back from it. « But approaching grosser matters, » that is, the grosser matters, and those which are less apt for illumination, there « it has a dimmer distributive over-appearance. » For just as, when it pours itself into them, it is received imperfectly, so also, when it pours itself out through them, over this it appears dimly from without: which comes from « the inopportuneness of the matters to be illuminated; » the inopportuneness harms, and is a hindrance to « the distributive habit of illumination, » that is, to this, that the illumination itself be either distributed, or possessed. Thus therefore in pure and polished bodies it shines more perfectly, in the grosser and coarser ones it is lessened; and a little afterward, descending to others still less apt, from this defect, [1117D] that is, after this defect, it is contracted almost to the perfectly undistributed, that is, to this, that it be in no way distributed. For from the subtlest matters, by which it is perfectly comprehended, descending to the less apt, by which it is imperfectly received, it comes at last to others so dreggy, and gross, and dull, that they almost wholly repel the light, and can receive nothing of the illumination. In this manner the spiritual light, coming to the rational minds, perfectly illuminates those which it finds pure and purified, but those which it finds less apt it illumines with a lesser brightness, and, so to speak, with a dimmer light. But those which it meets as wholly contrary and dreggy, it leaves altogether free from its participation and communion. Thus through the likeness of visible things the truth of invisible things is demonstrated. [1118A] Not only in light, but also in material heat, is there an image of the invisible truth. For heat itself more kindles those bodies which it finds more apt for its reception, but others less, until at last, meeting things wholly contrary and dissimilar, it shows in them no effect of its operation.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

This is what he says: « Again the heat of fire, » as if he said: Not only in the splendor of light, but also in the heat of fire, can the aforesaid likeness be seen. « For the heat of fire distributes itself more into the more capacious ones » — understand: bodies — and into those which are well suited to its likeness, so that, namely, they may be heated by the heat, because between the hot and heat there stands a likeness, as between the white and whiteness, and the good and goodness, and, in short, of every [1118B] property to its own affection. But coming, namely the heat, « to the essences contrary to its re-formings, » because, namely, they resist by a contrary quality the heating through which they ought to have been re-formed unto the likeness of heat, in them the heat itself « manifests no trace, » or at least « hidden, » or obscure, « of its primal operation; » that is, it shows there no effect, in which it could even faintly be recognized that it has there at least some beginning of operation. In this manner the rational substances either receive the fire of celestial love, if they have been apt, and dwelling-places prepared and suited to its reception, or they wholly repel it, if they disagree by a contrary quality, or cannot [1118C] be kindled. I desire that my soul repel not, by any dregginess of earthly filth, the light of the inner brightness, that it shut not out, by any coldness of malice, the heat of holy devotion, but that it grow bright and grow warm, re-formed from heaven unto the divine likeness. O what an essence, joined to the superessential good! A blessed nature, full of the supernatural good! Happily has it been made, which merits thus to be refreshed. If this be granted to me, I have nothing further to complain of concerning all these things which have passed before. The creature shows the craftsman, and the form of wonderful works commends the beauty of the author. There is one good and one beauty, and the good itself is the very beauty. The highest good and the highest beauty, and in the highest good every good, one good, and in the highest beauty every beauty, [1118D] one beauty. But the visible nature could not contain all things in that one; and by God many good things were made, that they might show forth one highest good, and likewise many beautiful things, that they might demonstrate the image of one highest beauty. But many lives too were established in those whose condition merited to have something more, beyond those which were made indeed good and beautiful, and yet cannot receive this good which is life. And all these lives emulate one highest life: each in its own kind, in that which it is, and inasmuch as it is from her, and according to her who alone is true. But of every corporeal life there are two principles, heat and moisture: the one, namely moisture, furnishes the nourishment of life; the other, that is, heat, makes life to feel. Without heat [1119A] that which subsists does not live; without moisture that which lives does not subsist. Therefore these two, by a rational tempering, pour themselves into all things to be vivified, so that from them each life may receive the measure of its own capacity, as much as is fitting, to the end that the lower lives may advance, through the steps of their increments, unto the image of the highest life. For the first step of corporeal life is sense-making; the second, entering through sense, imagination; the third, through the imagination of the things conceived, memory; the fourth, according to the passible application of sense, a certain foresight without the discernment of understanding. In which there is indeed as it were an image of reason, but there is no reason. According to this, certain brute animals too seem craftier than others of their kind, and, by a certain readiness as it were of sense, imitating the foresight of a rational [1119B] mind. Which nevertheless is proved to be rather a passion of sense than an operation of the understanding. But in all these the corporeal life imitates the spiritual life. First, namely, in that it feels; secondly, in that it conceives sense; thirdly, in that it retains the things conceived; fourthly, in that, whether in things imagined or in things felt, through the passion of sense, according to a certain likeness of reason, it bends itself either to seeking or to fleeing. And so in all these the corporeal lives hold the image of the spiritual lives, and through them as intermediaries emulate, as far as they can, the highest life. For the spiritual lives themselves too all receive from the highest life that they are lives, partaking the spiritual heat and moisture descending thence, whereby they may be nourished and vivified that they may live. [1119C] These two, spiritually conceived, perfect the spiritual life: the spiritual moisture nourishing through joy; and there stands no life which has not its own heat and moisture nourishing and making to feel, that it may be able to subsist in that which is life; and that which has these more is deservedly called more a life. As there are many lives, so there are many participations, and every participation is from one, and every life from one; and so the highest good, diffusing itself into all things, constitutes every life, and by forming leads back every life unto the highest life.

There follows: « And this the more. » As if he said: Not only is this an argument of the invisible truth, that some bodies receive corporeal light and material heat, while some [1119D] wholly repel them, but this also, that through those which are more warm the other less warm ones are kindled: this, he says, is a still further argument. Further after the first, not further above the first: this is an argument of the invisible truth. Because the heat itself of material fire is admitted, and poured into those bodies, namely, « which are not cognate » to it, that is, apt to receive it: poured, I say, into them « through others having what is opportune, » that is, which stand opportunely toward it. « First, » they receive it, « inasmuch as it makes things easily movable ignited from the fiery ones. » For the heat itself, from the fiery things which, more warm, are ignited, makes others, which are secondarily and less heated, which yet themselves become ignited from the fiery ones for this reason, that they are easily movable [1120A] and apt for the reception of heat. And so from the fiery things first and principally warm it makes things ignited, and through these ignited things, namely afterward, it heats still colder and more remote ones, that is, either water, or something else, that is, « something other of the things not easily catching fire, » that is, of those which do not easily catch fire. « It heats, » I say, « proportionally, » that is, each thing according to its manner, and its measure, and its capacity for receiving heat.

There follows: « According to this then, » etc. He adapts the likeness. « According to this, he says, reason, » that is, likeness « of natural ordination, » that is, of the ordination of natural and visible things, « the [sic: *supernatur alis*] ordination itself also, » that is, the [1120B] distribution of divine grace, which is « the ordination of every good adornment, » that is, of every order well and comelily disposed, whether « visible » as in men, or « invisible » as in angels; that, I say, supernatural ordination fittingly manifests first « the brightness of illumination, » that is, of manifestation, or revelation, « to the most exalted essences, » that is, to the supreme spirits: that « brightness, » I say, « first appearing » in them, inasmuch as « in the most copious outpourings, » that is, as it befits it to appear when it copiously pours itself out, or to those into whom it has most copiously poured itself. « And through those, » namely the most exalted essences, those essences « which are after » them « partake the divine ray, » that is, the divine brightness. « For these, » [1120C] namely the most exalted essences, « are the first knowing God, and supereminently desiring the divine virtue. » And because they principally know and love, they have been made worthy « to become pre-operative, as far as is possible, with virtue and action like to God; » that is, that before all the others, first after God, they may operate into all subject things, by bestowing the divine virtue; and in that very thing become like to God, because the virtue which they receive from above they expend upon their subjects. For they « extend the essences which are after them, or subject to them, unto a like virtue, » namely to their own virtue, inasmuch as it is a virtue holding itself deiformly, because in that they do this, by their virtue they imitate God, « copiously delivering to them, » namely to the subject essences, « from the brightness supervening upon them. And those, » [1120D] namely the subject essences, « again » deliver « to their own subjects; » « and » thus « through each one, » the outpouring of the divine gift running on, « the first delivers to that which is after it, » and so onward unto the end.

There follows: « The water itself, » etc. After the likeness which he set forth concerning fire according to brightness and heat, he sets forth again another likeness concerning water. « The water itself, he says, does it not proportionally reach into all? » The moisture itself of water, which diffuses itself through all the parts of earthly bodies, proportionally fills all, moistening some more, some less, according to this, namely, that each one is more or less capable of moistening. In this example therefore is fittingly demonstrated, likewise, the distribution of divine grace, coming forth to all in no equal [1121A] manner.

There follows: « It is therefore, » etc. Since, he says, just as one visible light illuminates many things, and from that light many shining things shine, so the invisible light illumines all things invisibly shining. « Therefore God is the principle of illuminating, » or of illumination, « at once to all the illuminated. » Nor is it a wonder: since he too is light, and not light in any manner whatsoever, but by nature, that is, naturally, and truly, and properly. Naturally, because from himself; and truly, because in himself; and properly, because through himself. Naturally, because from that which he is he is light; and truly, because that very thing which he is is light; and properly, because through that which he is he is light. For that is natural which is not taken from elsewhere, [1121B] that is truly which is essentially possessed, that is properly which is not conferred through another. And so thus God is light: and I say too little when I say that he is light, inasmuch as he is the essence of the light itself. For the rest, who shine, are not the essence of light, but its effect. They therefore, in that they are lights, are from light; but he is light from that which he is. « And it is also causal of being and seeing, that is, of the essence and vision of the light itself; » as if it were said thus: God is the essence of light, and is also the cause of that essence, because that which is light, is essentially; and that which is essentially, is from itself. Thus therefore he is the cause of the essence of light: and not only of the essence of light, but also of the vision itself, or manifestation, of the light, because he has from himself not only that he is essentially light [1121C] shining by nature, but this also, that he temporally shines, illuminating by grace.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « Then, deiformly and God-likely remaining superposed, » etc. In God, he says, the light itself is nothing other than the essence itself, to him for whom to be and to be light are the same, who from his very self is the principle of illuminating, because those who first shine he illuminates from his very self. But then, that is, thereafter, after him, namely God, each thing « remaining superposed deiformly and God-likely, » that is, according to the conformation and likeness of God — understand: it becomes the principle of illuminating after itself, « transferring through itself the divine lights into that, » namely into what is constituted after itself. The sense is this: That God, who is superposed to all, illuminates through himself those who are nearest; [1121D] and thus he becomes the principle of illumination, illuminating the first shining ones; then each one of those first shining ones, superposed to all the other subjects according to the conformity and likeness of God, becomes the principle of illumination to each one of the subjects, transferring through himself the divine lights into that, namely the subject. But that he put 'superposed' in the neuter, in place of what he should have said, 'superposed' in the masculine, namely order, or angel, was done after the manner of Scripture, especially when unknown and wonderful things are treated of, when it is said 'that,' as if it were said, 'whatever that is.' But that he subjoined 'to each one after itself,' this seems said as though that superposed thing, whatever it is, being one, is the principle of illumination to the several [1122A] subjects, so that it may furnish a light not as many to many, but as one to many. But in that which after this he again added, « transferring the divine lights into that, » it seems to minister illumination not as one to many, but as one to one. Whence it is plain that, according to the manner of Scripture, whether number be put alternately for number, or gender for gender, the truth of the same understanding is not changed.

There follows: « Therefore the most exalted disposition of the celestial minds. » He infers from the aforesaid, as if he said: Since the first disposition has received the divine things, and furnishes illumination to all its subjects; « therefore the essences of all the remaining ones marvel at the disposition, » that is, the spirits of all the remaining orders [1122B] contemplate with admiration and astonishment « that most exalted disposition of the celestial minds itself, the principle after God, according to what is consequent. » For thus it is consequent, that they should know God as the first principle; then also should venerate those who are first after God, as in their own manner a principle unto the things that follow, through whom the divine grace descends to those who are subject. And this is what follows: « They marvel that these are the principle of all sacred and divine knowledge and of divine imitation: » which, namely, flow to the subjects through them, as the divine illumination distributed through them into all the other celestial virtues, and not only into them, but also into us; namely men, who receive the divine illuminations by their mediation. And because all the remaining celestial [1122C] essences are illuminated by the mediation of the first ones, therefore whatever sacred thing they can perform, and like to God, this indeed they first attribute to God, who is the cause of all; then to those first spirits, by whose operating and mediation they receive the divine grace. This is what he says: « Wherefore, » namely, because through those illuminated by God the rest are illuminated, therefore the rest themselves « refer every sacred and God-like operation of theirs unto God as the causal one, » because he is the first cause of all; « then they refer it unto the first intellects, » that is, the supreme spirits, who are deiform, or conformable to God, in this, that they bestow the spiritual gifts: not however as God, who is the first cause from whom they are, but [1122D] as the first effects, through whom they are. Therefore they refer their good to those, through whom it is, « as through the first operators, and masters of divine things, » namely of gifts. Operators indeed in that they move by effecting, but masters in that they preside by directing.

There follows: « Does not then the first, » etc. *Num* stands for *nonne*. « Does not then, he says, the first disposition of the holy angels, » which is so greatly more sublime than all the rest, « have more than all » the highest and « fiery property, » that is, charity, as in the seraphim; « and the poured-out, » that is, abundant « tradition of the divine wisdom, and the mystical of the most exalted knowledge of the divine illuminations, » that is, the mystical, or hidden, or profound, most exalted [1123A] knowledge, which it perceives from the divine illuminations, as in the cherubim; and the sedent property, as in the thrones, bearing, namely, God, and « signifying the divine reception: » because in that they are called thrones, it is openly signified that they receive God sitting and resting in them. The sense is: Does not the first hierarchy, more excellently and more abundantly than all the rest, have both the fire of charity, and the light of knowledge, and the judgment of discernment? It has assuredly all these more than all the rest. « But the » dispositions themselves « of the subordinate essences, » that is, of the subject orders, do indeed themselves also partake the « fiery virtue, » that is, charity, as the seraphim; the wise and knowing « virtue, » that is, knowledge, as the [1123B] cherubim; and the virtue receptive of God, as the thrones: « they partake, » I say; but « below, » that is, more imperfectly than the first ones: « and looking to the first ones, » that is, imitating the first ones, « and through them, » namely the first ones, brought back into that of deiformity which is possible for them, that is, brought back to as much deiformity as is possible for them; through them, namely the first ones, I say, « made worthily, » that is, made worthy, « by divine imitation, » that is, that they may imitate God, « pre-operative, » that is, operating, before and more excellently than all the others, from him and according to him.

There follows: « The said holy properties therefore, » etc. Since, he says, through the first ones the subsequent ones receive the gifts of graces, therefore the holy properties already said above — such as was to set the prophet's lips on fire with a coal, [1123C] which seems to be proper to the seraphim, and to teach knowledge, which seems proper to the cherubim. Such properties therefore, « of which the participation comes about through the first essences, » the other essences « subsisting after them, » these properties, I say, « place back in them, » that is, attribute to those first ones, namely through whom they partake them: « as in hierarchs, » that is, in princes, and superiors, so that they may refer them to those from whom they received them. « They place back, » I say, « in them after God: » because they first attribute them to God, then to those who are nearest after God. But that he says that the subsequent essences become the participation of the properties of the first ones, the manner of speaking is such as if he said: They become partakers [1123D] of the properties of the first ones, because that which the first possess through property, the second share through participation.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « He says therefore these things, saying, » etc. He, he says, of whom I spoke above, who furnished me a not unfitting apology. He, saying these things which have thus far been said concerning the divine illumination descending through some to others, says that the aforesaid vision — about which the question arose concerning the purification of the prophet's lips — « was received by the theologian himself, » namely Isaiah, « through one of the holy and blessed » angels « ruling over us, » that is, over men, « descending from that, » understand: the first hierarchy. For he says this, that that vision which Isaiah saw descended from the first hierarchy, and was brought to the prophet himself [1124A] through one angel of the last order of them, who are the lowest among the angels and preside over us alone: and therefore he says that that angel « before its own illuminative hand-leading, » that is, that revelation by which he himself led the prophet by the hand and instructed him, and by which he himself had before been illuminated by the first hierarchy: « had reposed in, » that is, had referred to « that holy contemplation, » that is, to the first hierarchy itself, from which he contemplated that he had had what he had, and had done what he did. « He sees also, » Isaiah himself, « sitting above, » that is, sitting on high and aloft, « the most exalted essences, placed after God, and about God, and with God, as far as, in symbols, » that is, in figures and likenesses, it « is to be said. » For all this is symbolic and figurative, [1124B] that God, according to a corporeal likeness, is said to sit upon a lofty throne; and the seraphim are reported to stand about him and beside him. And Isaiah himself sees also a summit more sublime than « all these, » namely than the most exalted essences of the blessed spirits; « even super-secretly, » that is, in a most excellent manner: « the summit, » I say, « above the principal, » that is, God, who is the highest and principal of all, superplaced « in the midst of the superfirmed virtues. » For in the midst of the virtues, that is, of the holy spirits, who are firmed and established by an eternal placing about God above all things, God himself, the highest and principal, is seen sitting upon a throne lofty and elevated. « In these visions therefore the theologian himself learned, » namely Isaiah this, « that the divine, » that is, the divinity, [1124C] « is superplaced incomparably above every superessential superexcellence, above every invisible and visible virtue, » that is, that the divinity incomparably transcends, according to every mode of excelling, every virtue, visible and invisible. And no wonder, if the divine itself transcends all things: « and yet, » that is, assuredly, « because it is removed from all, » namely by excellence, inasmuch as that which is universal is removed from those things which are all in part, and so far removed and excelling that « it is not like » « even to the first essences of those » which are, but far distant and transcending. But that he says « to the first essences of those which are, » it is as if he should say, to the essences of those who are first, or to the essences which [1124D] are first, that is, to the supreme spirits of the angels. Nor did he learn only that the divine is removed from all, and like to none. « But still, » or moreover, he learned this also, that it « is the principle of all, » because from it are all things; and the sanctifying cause, because through it all things subsist; and the immutable foundation of those things which are, by a secret singularity, because all things persevere subsisting in it: which, containing all things by its secret and hidden divinity, as a unity gathers together the universe, and as a singularity binds together multiplicity. From which both being and well-being belongs even to those most highly fortified virtues, that is, to those spirits who are firmed and established in the highest virtue and felicity: who have from it not only that they are, but also that they are blessed. All this therefore he learned in [1125A] that vision concerning the excellence of the divinity. « Then, » he also learned, and « was taught, the same deiform virtues of the most holy seraphim, » or, to speak more expressly, of the same most holy seraphim, « the deiform virtues, » or the same virtues which he had learned to be in God, he learned also to be in the seraphim out of conformity to God; because, what God has by nature, they have by grace, being conformed to God. But which and what sort of virtues, or also from what he learned the virtues to be in the seraphim, he subjoins, saying: « From their sacred surname indeed, which, » that is, which surname, « is fiery. » Because seraphim is interpreted *burning,* or *succeeding,* [cj: *succedens*; read *succendens*, "kindling"] and signifies fiery: « concerning which » fiery thing, he says, we « shall a little later, as far as it will be possible for us, introduce the anagogies, » [1125B] that is, the upward-leadings « of the fiery virtue, » namely of the charity of the seraphim themselves, « into the deiform: » that is, into a thing so worthy and altogether like to God. Or thus: From this sacred surname, I say, the prophet himself learned the anagogies, that is, the upward-leadings, or ascents, or advancements of the fiery virtue, that is, of the charity of the seraphim themselves. « But in the expanded sacred formation of the wings, » that is, in the sacred formation of the expanded wings, the prophet himself learned « an absolute and most lofty extension into the divine, » which is « in the first, and in the middle, and in the last intellects. » Through the surname therefore, which sounds "fiery," he learned the fiery virtue; that is, charity ascending to the heights by desire; but through the expansion of the wings, he learned knowledge stretching itself by subtlety [1125C] into far-off things, and extending itself even unto the divine to be grasped, in the first, and middle, and last intellects, that is, in the spirits first, and middle, and last in that order: or in the first intellects by which they grasp the divine above themselves, the middle by which they grasp the divine in themselves, the last by which they grasp the divine below themselves. But the extension itself, or the stretching-forth of knowledge, is both absolute, because, diffusing itself unto immensity, it is enwrapped by no darkness of ignorance; and most lofty, because, pressing on to the highest things, it is weighed down by no infirmity. And not only from these things which have been said did he recognize the virtue of their love and knowledge, « but also, seeing their manifold and multiform character, the intellectual theologian himself » [1125D] Isaiah, « and seeing also that vision distinguished by wings beneath the feet, and it distinguished by wings beneath the faces: and seeing also the ever-moved, » that is, that which ever is, in the middle wings: through all these things « was led back, » and instructed « to the invisible knowledge of the things which were seen. » For through this, that he saw the distinction of the wings which downward beneath the feet; and of the wings which beneath the faces of the one sitting were stretched upward; and of the wings which in the middle were ever moved: through this, namely, he himself saw the manifold virtue of them, which is both multiplied in operation, signified through the motion, and multiform in knowledge and love, signified through the stretching-forth. And in all these things he was led back and admonished, that he might invisibly recognize what he saw according to the appearance of visible things: [1126A] « the manifold-wayed and manifold-seeing virtue of those most lofty intellects being manifested to him through all these things. » Which virtue, namely, is both manifold-wayed, inasmuch as it is moved in manifold ways by desiring; and manifold-seeing, inasmuch as it is beatified in manifold ways by possessing. Manifold-wayed in that by which it presses on by searching; manifold-seeing in that which it finds by penetrating. Many are the ways by which one goes to the one good: therefore rightly manifold-wayed. But why manifold-seeing, since it is one that is seen, unless because in the one all things are seen? Thus therefore the prophet was led back to the knowledge of truth, the manifold-wayed and manifold-seeing virtue of the most lofty intellects being shown to him through sacred images. There being shown to him also « their sacred dread, » or reverence, « which they have supermundanely, » that is, impassibly and [1126B] purely, « against a proud and audacious and impossible searching-out of higher and lower things, » that is, lest they should proudly, and audaciously, and impossibly search out, beyond the measure of their possibility, the secrets of God, which are higher than they by majesty, and lower by profundity. For this reason indeed they veil the head, that they may profess the higher things to be covered from them: for this reason they veil the feet, that they may testify the lower and deeper things to be impenetrable. On this account they dread to be proud and audacious toward that which is impossible to be searched out. Proud toward the heights, audacious toward the depths; proud, lest they be too much lifted up; audacious, lest they be cast down headlong. In this therefore they have a sacred dread, because it is a sacred thing to fear that which, if presumed, would do harm; and they have it supermundanely, because they dread without passion and affliction. For they fear, [1126C] and are not affected; they tremble, and are not shaken. They are afraid, yet secure, and without any trouble, or corruption of their rest, they are wary of passing, toward the incomprehensible majesty, beyond the measure of their possibility. To the knowledge of all these things the prophet was led back, seeing the aforesaid things, in the extension of the wings. And seeing also the unceasing and ever-high-flying character, namely of the persevering motion in the commotion of the wings: which commotion consists in the commensuration of actions imitating God, that is, in their actions by which they imitate God, commensurating themselves to their possibility, so that they presume to do nothing beyond reason and measure.

There follows: « But he was instructed also in that divine and much [1126D] precious hymnody. » As if he should say: Not only in the habit of the seraphim did he recognize their virtue; but also in their voice he learned the hymnody, that is, the divine praise, « the angel forming the vision for the theologian himself according to the virtue » of his capacity, « and handing on his own sacred knowledge: » because what the angel himself knew, through this sacred vision he made the theologian to know. « He taught therefore » the angel himself « him, » namely the theologian, in this, that he purged his lips — of him who already seemed just — with a coal taken from the altar, « because the very participation of the unknown divine brightness, received as far as is possible, is a purgation to however perfect ones. » Because however much anyone has been purged, he becomes more purged when that is received.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

[1127A]There follows: « But these things, » etc. He said that the perception of the divine brightness is a purgation: now consequently he shows that this brightness, proceeding from the hidden depth of the highest divinity, unto the perfecting and illuminating of all the sacred intellects, first manifests itself to the most lofty virtues placed more openly about it: and he shows how it is in its own and true being; then after the first ones it shows itself to the second; and afterward to the last; and at the very last also to our human intellects, and so, descending, it conducts its illumination to each virtue, according as each has been made deiform by it, this is what he says: « This, » namely « brightness proceeding from the remote causes of the divinity, going forth from which divinity it perfects all the sacred intellects, » that [1127B] is, the rational minds, illuminating them « by a superessential hiddenness, » that is, by a very hidden and secret inspiration: proceeding, I say, from those remote causes unto manifestation, it first becomes more manifest as it is, that is, according to its true being, to the most lofty virtues placed about it: and it manifests, « and distributes itself to those more » than to others; « and then » after those « it manifests itself to the second, » « then to the last, » lastly also « to our intellectual virtues, » that is, to the rational minds; « and so, » descending from the first even to the last, « it conducts its illumination through the several virtues: so, » that is, manifest in the several ones to just so great a degree as each virtue exists from that same brightness according to the deiform, that is, according to [1127C] conformity to God. For it illuminates each virtue the more, the more it fits it to deiformity. But why this brightness proceeds to manifestation he subjoins: « Unto the praising of the unknown thing of its own hiddenness. » For we perceive something of it for this reason, that in that, and through that which is hidden, and unknown, and altogether incomprehensible in it, we may praise it.

There follows: « But it shines through the several ones, to the second through the first. » For everywhere it shines to the second through the first, or through the first ones. « And if it behooves to say it briefly, » this can be said in sum, that « the first is led from the hidden to the manifest through the first virtues. » For that which is in itself is altogether hidden; and it then [1127D] first begins to be seen when it first begins to be had. For neither is it ever seen by anyone, except by him by whom it is had. « This therefore the theologian learned from the light-leading angel. » From the angel that is leader of the brightness he learned this. A new composition, "light-leading," as if he should say "light-leader." From him therefore the theologian learned this. What is this? this, namely, is what he learned: « That the purgation, and all the divine operations shining back through the first essences, are distributed through them into all the rest, according to the analogy of each of the rest, » that is, the mode and measure, « unto the deifying participations. Wherefore also he reposed the fierily-purgative property » — that angel, that is, attributed it — « to the seraphim themselves consequently after God: » and therefore « nothing inordinate » is said « if the seraphim is said to purge [1128A] the theologian. For so also God purges all, of whose whole purgation, » or of whose purgation of all, « he is the cause; » and not only God, but rather in this example it can be seen how not only he through whom something is done, but also he from whom it is done, is said to do it. Because « we shall use an example more nearly at hand. » Thus the seraphim is said to purge him whom the inferior angel purges, the virtue and mandate having been received from the seraphim: « Just as, according to us, the highest priest, purging or illuminating through his ministers, or priests, is himself said to purge and to illuminate, the orders purged through him reposing their own sacred operations upon him, » that is, attributing them to him of themselves. For what he works through them, is through them attributed to him. « So also his own purgative knowledge [1128B] and virtue, the angel himself perfecting the purgation of the theologian, reposes upon God indeed as upon the causal one, then upon the seraphim itself as upon the highest priest that first acts. » So therefore the angel attributed what he did, first to God, then to the seraphim, through whom he did it. « As perhaps someone would say, » that is, for example: « If anyone with angelic reverence, » that is, under the person of an angel, which is to be revered: « teaching » the prophet « to be purged » by him, « should say » to him: O Isaiah, « of the purgation to be perfected in thee, » namely through me, « I am not the principle, but before me the principle is exalted indeed, » that is, God, who is both « essence, and Creator, and causal » of all and bringing the first essences to be present; « and containing them about himself by a placing, » that [1128C] is, by stable firmness; « and preserving them unchangeable, and lacking a fall, » that is, lest they be together turned from themselves, and fall; « and moving himself into the first ones of the participation of his own provident operations, » that is, of the operations which he does according to providence; because everything that he does in time, he disposed in eternal providence. The proper operation of God is when he works through his very self without a mediating creature, from which, doubtless, the first participation comes; because they receive him without a medium who become the first effects of his operation. For the first operation of God is when he moves himself from himself; the first participation, when he presents himself through himself. And that indeed is the most lofty creature, unto which the first operation is, [1128D] and in which is the first participation through it, then descending to the things that follow. In this manner the subject angel attributed what he did to the superior seraphim, from whom he received the power to be able to do this. « For this, » that he received from another the power to do it, he says, « teaching me to manifest these things, » or to signify « the mission of the seraphim itself. » He, he says, who, furnishing an apology for all these aforesaid things, taught me, himself says that the mission of the seraphim signifies this, namely that what he did he had assuredly received to do from him from whom he came. Therefore fittingly the angel himself can say to the prophet: « I am not the principle of the purgation which I work in thee. But the exalted and first principle before me is » God, who is Creator and cause of all. Then, moreover, that « adornment [1129A] of the excelling essences, » that is, the order of the highest spirits, which is « after God the priest, and leader » bestowing divine things, and leading back to God: « from which » order « I also was instructed to purge deiformly, » even as now I do in thee. « It, » namely the order, « is that which purges thee through me, » on this account, because it taught me to purge: « through which, » namely order, the very cause and worker of the whole purgation (understand: the divinity) « brings forth its provident actions from the hidden even into us » lowest ones, namely the angels. If the angel working the purgation should say these and words like these to the prophet, it would not be unfitting: whence it is plain that when the seraphim is said to fly to the prophet, and is said to purge the prophet, it can be understood not unfittingly that this operation [1129B] was indeed administered through the subject angel, but attributed to the superior one, from whom it was.

There follows: « These things indeed he taught me: » they are the words of the author returning to his subject. He, he says, who furnished me the apology, taught me all these aforesaid things. « But I, » O Timothy! « hand on to thee » all things either said by me, or approved, but holding to either side. « But to thy intellectual and discretive knowledge, » that is, to thy discretion: « I would grant that the one part of the said causes be released, » namely by thee, « from doubt, » that is, to be held as ratified and undoubted: « and that the same be honored before the other as having what is consequent, and reasonable, and equally true. » This I grant both to thy will, and to thy discretion, and to thy knowledge, that thou choose the one part of these opinions, doubt being removed, [1130A] and honor it more than the other as true and reasonable. « Or, » if this pleases not, I grant thee « to find from thyself what is truly nearer to the true. Or, » if that still pleases not, I grant thee « to learn from another, God, namely, giving it, » whether thou recognize it in this or in any other way: « yet the angels first » receiving the very knowledge of truth « from God, » and afterward, through the angels, revealing it to us holy men, the friends of the angels — that is, receiving it for this, that they may reveal it to us. Or thus. And I grant thee to reveal to us « through its more lovable contemplation, » the angels, and the friends of the angels, first receiving, namely, the very knowledge of truth. A wonderful word. The angels receive from God, and men from the angels; and men themselves in turn, [1130B] taught by the angels, teach other men; and the doctrine of truth comes through some into others, and many seem to be masters and teachers of truth; and yet no one is taught, because he is not illuminated from the contemplation of it. Other masters apply the ministry; there is one master, who alone furnishes the understanding. He can teach without the others; the others cannot without him. Thou givest me the word. But what is a word without intelligence? Through the word indeed intelligence comes. But he places intelligence in the heart who illuminates within, not he who sounds without. His unction teaches us concerning all things. Thou speakest without; but within thou dost not anoint. Therefore thy erudition is indeed lovable to me. But more lovable is the contemplation of him; because even this, which in thy erudition is lovable, is nothing except from the contemplation of him.



## TITLE OF CHAPTER XIV. *What the handed-down angelic number signifies.*



## THE TEXT.

[1129C]

*What the handed-down angelic number signifies. And this too is worthy, as I judge, of intellectual cognition, because the tradition of the oracles concerning the angels says there to be « a thousand times a thousand, » and « ten thousand ten thousand times [n: (Dan. VII)]*, *according to us the most sublime of numbers, revolving them into itself, and multiplying them, and through this openly signifying the innumerable ordinations of the celestial essences. For many are the blessed hosts of the supermundane intellects, surpassing the weak and constricted commensuration of material numbers according to us, and defined by the gnostic supermundane and celestial intelligence and knowledge alone, according to the very health most richly bestowed on them of the divine and manifold knowledge, sapiential of all things at once which are — of the principle superessentially [1129D] subsisting, and of the substance-making cause, and of the containing virtue, and of the environing consummation.*

## EXPOSITION.

« What the angelic number handed down signifies, » that is, given, or defined by the Holy Scriptures? Not only, he says, the aforesaid things; but « this too is worthy of intellectual cognition, » namely, why a certain and defined number is attributed to the angelic spirits: [1130C] « because the tradition » of the divine « words concerning the angels says there to be a thousand times a thousand, and ten thousand times ten thousand » angels, « revolving the most sublime of numbers according to us, » that is, the numbers which according to us are most sublime. Revolving, I say, into itself, that is, in the utterance of the words — the words which are contained in it — revolving them into words, and multiplying some through others, just as thousands are revolved into "a thousand times" and ten thousands into "ten thousand times" by multiplication. Which he would have said more plainly as "revolving the numbers into themselves," although this was nevertheless said more expressly, because not the numbers, but the names of the numbers, placed into themselves by multiplication, are revolved. If therefore the tradition of the words, signifying the multitude of the celestial spirits, says there to be a thousand times a thousand, and ten thousand [1130D] times ten thousand angels, revolving into itself the most sublime numbers according to us, or comparing them among themselves, and multiplying some by others. « And through this openly signifying that the ordinations of the celestial essences are innumerable. » For in that he set down the greatest numbers, he plainly intimates that he ought still to have said more, if he had been able to say more. Truly it must be known that this, which he says — a thousand times a thousand, and ten thousand times ten thousand — our translation does not contain in this manner; [1131A] but a thousand of thousands, and ten thousand times a hundred thousand, which nevertheless pertains to the same truth. Therefore, wishing to signify infinite numbers, he set down the greatest finite numbers, because « many are the blessed hosts, » that is, the blessed multitudes warring for God, « of the supermundane intellects, surpassing the weak and constricted commensuration of material numbers according to us, and defined by the sole gnostic, that is, cognitive knowledge; or, namely, by the divine cognition, which knows all things, and alone knows the number of those supernal virtues, and alone discerns them, and fully knows them according to that health, that is, the purity, or integrity, of their simple and incorruptible nature, bestowed on them most richly and most abundantly by the supermundane and [1131B] celestial sapiential intelligence and knowledge of the divine and manifold knowledge — of the principle of all things which are superessentially and existing, and of the substance-making cause of all, and of the virtue containing all things, and of the consummation environing all things. » The sense is such: that the wisdom of God alone, which is gnostic, that is, cognitive of all things, definitely and perfectly knows the number [1132A] of those celestial essences in that purity and integrity in which it created and formed them. Which knowledge, namely, is both celestial, and divine, and manifold. Celestial, by purity; divine, by truth; manifold, by incomprehensibility. And though it be such in itself, it is also sapiential in its work, making all things wisely, and establishing nothing worthy of reproof. And this such wisdom, through which all things were created, and all things are known, is the wisdom of him who is the principle of all things which are (because from him are all things), subsisting superessentially through himself, namely perfect, and needing nothing; and who is the substance-making cause of all, because he makes all things to subsist, that they may be; and is the virtue containing all things, that they may remain in that which they are; and is the consummation [1132B] environing all things, lest they flow apart, or fall away from that whereby they thus are. It is no wonder therefore, if those spirits whose number is incomprehensible to us can be discerned through so great a knowledge, and the knowledge of so great a one: through which all things, when they were not, were created; and now, lest the things made revert to nothing, subsist in it.

## BOOK TEN.

## TITLE OF CHAPTER XV. *What are the formative images of the angelic virtues, and what next, that is, and the rest?*

## THE TEXT.

[1131C]



## THE TEXT. (cont.)

*Come, on then, let us say what remains, sending back, if it seems good, our intellectual eye, intent about the sublime contemplations by an angelic vigor, to the divided and much-parted breadth of the multiform variety of the angelic specifications, descending again to them, as being inconsequent, analytically reflected back into the simplicity of the celestial minds. But let one thing be foreknown by thee: how the sacred discretions of the formed images sometimes signify the same dispositions of the celestial essences as ordaining, and again as ordained, and the last as ordaining, and the first as ordained, and the same, as has been said, as having first, and middle, and last virtues, no inordinate reasoning being introduced, according to the manner of such unlockings. [1131D] For if we should say that certain ones are ordained by the prior ones, then that the prior ones themselves ordain the same, and again ordain of the last ones, and are ordained by those very ones that were ordained, truly the interposed matter would be commingled with much inordination and confusion. But if we say that the same both ordain and are ordained, yet not that they ordain and are ordained by the same, but that they severally are indeed ordained by the prior ones, and ordain the last: not inconsequently perhaps would someone say that the sacred-made forms in the oracles can sometimes be beautifully and truly encompassed about by first, and middle, and last virtues. And that they can therefore be extended upward convertibly, and be firmly enwrapped about themselves, being keepers of their own virtues, [1132C] and, by a sociable procession toward the things that come, be providently in participation of virtue — this one will not falsely conjoin to all the celestial essences, although to some superplacedly and universally, as has often been said, but to others particularly and subjectly. But one must begin with reason, and inquire in the first discretion of the forms, for what cause theology is found honoring the fiery sacred description almost beyond all. Thou wilt find it therefore not only conforming fiery wheels, but also fiery animals, and men flashing as it were with fire, and setting about those celestial essences heaps of coals of fire, and rivers blazing with fire with an immeasurable sound [n: (Ezech. I; Dan. VII; IV Reg. II)]*. *But it says also that thrones are fiery, and that the most exalted seraphim themselves signify by their surname that they are burning after a heavenly manner: and [1132D] it distributes to them the property and operation of fire, and altogether, upward and downward, it selectively honors the fiery making of the forms. Therefore I judge the fiery to signify the most deiform thing of the celestial minds. For the holy theologians themselves [n: (Hebr. X)] often describe the superessential and formless essence in fire, as having (if it is lawful to say) many images of the divine property, as far as is possible in things invisible. For fire perceptible — thus indeed must it be said — comes clearly in all things and through all things, and is removed from all; luminous at once, and as it were hidden; itself unknown through itself, no matter lying by in which it manifests its own action, and immeasurable and invisible, through itself [1133A] at once potent over all, and, whatsoever things are done among them, movable to its own action, delivering itself to all that in any way draw near, renovative, a guardianship of nature, illuminative by veiled-about splendors, incomprehensible, clear, discrete, leaping back, bearing upward, acutely passing, exalted, not to receive the contumely of diminution, ever-moved with a motion through itself, moving another, comprehending, uncomprehended, not needing another, latently growing of itself, and, to the materials it has taken up, manifesting its own magnitude, active, potent, present to all at once invisibly, when neglected it is thought not to be, but it is rubbed into being, as by a certain vengeance, connaturally and properly it suddenly shines back forth, and again incomprehensibly impalpable, not lessened, in all its most rich [1133B] deliveries of itself. And perhaps someone will find many other beautiful properties of fire, as images, though insensible, of the divine operation. Knowing this therefore, the theosophers conform the celestial essences out of fire, signifying their deiform and, as far as possible, God-imitable character. But they also describe them as human-formed, on account of the intellectual nature, and by having upward the intuitive virtues, and the straightness and brightness of the figure, and, according to nature, the principal and regal quality, and, according to sense, the least indeed as compared with the remaining virtues of the irrational animals. Yet potent over all, by virtue according to the magnitude of intellect, and by continuity according to rational knowledge, and, according to the nature of the soul, free and most potent. It is possible also, as I judge, through the several members of our corporeal manifold [1133C] partition to find the celestial virtues, saying that the sight-virtues indeed signify their most clear regard toward the divine luminaries, and again the tender and liquid and not-repercussed, but acutely movable, and pure, and full reception, impassibly, of the divine illuminations. But the discretive virtues of the smells signify that reception, as far as possible, of the above-intellect distribution of the sweetly-smelling thing, and the discernment through knowledge of those things which are not so, and the utterly-fleeing from them. But the virtues of the ears, that partaking and gnostic reception of the divine inspiration. And the tasting virtues, that reception of the fullness of the invisible foods, and of the divine and nourishing advancements. But the touching virtues, the discernment through knowledge of what is fitting or hurtful. Then the eyelids [1133D] and eyebrows, the keeping of the divine visions and of intelligence. And youthful and adult age, that property of the ever-renewing vital virtue. But the teeth, the dividing of the imparted nourishing perfection. For each intellectual essence divides by provident virtue the uniform intelligence bestowed on it by a more divine one, and multiplies it unto the leading analogy of the lower. But the shoulders, and arms, and likewise hands, the making, and operative, and active power. But the heart to be a symbol of deiform life, sowing its own vital virtue deiformly into those things which were pre-understood. The breasts again to signify the hard and keeping power, as of the life-giving distribution from the heart placed beneath; but the back, the continuous holding at once of all the fertile virtues. But the feet, [1134A] the movable, and swift, and running power of the motion ever going into the divine; on which account theology figured also the feet of the holy intellects as winged [n: (Ezech. I)]*. *For the winged signifies anagogic velocity, and the celestial active power of the upward journey, and the removed-from-all-that-is-lowly by bearing upward. But the very lightness of the wings signifies nothing earthly, but the whole that is cleanly and without weight ascending on high. The naked also, and unshod [n: (Gen. XVIII, XIX)]*, *and the loosed, and the let-down, and the immeasurable, and the pure, is assimilative, by the setting-away of those things which are outside, and to the divine simplicity as far as possible. But since again the simple and much-varied wisdom both clothes the naked, and gives them certain vessels to carry about, come, let us open up, as far as is possible [1134B] for us, the sacred vestments and the instruments of the celestial minds. For the bright and fiery garment [n: (Apoc. I)]*, *I judge to signify the deiform, according to the image of fire and its brightness, on account of the reposes in heaven, where the light must be said to be altogether invisible, or intellectually illuminating, or intellectually illuminated. But the priestly vestment signifies the leading toward the divine and mystical contemplations, and the vow of the whole life. The girdles also signify the keeping of their fecund virtues, and the habit that gathers them to be turned unitedly into itself, and to be carried round about itself circularly with ease, lacking a fall, from the likeness of nature. The rods also signify the regal and principal power, and that which defines all things straight. But the darts, and axes, the separative power of dissimilitudes, and the acumen of the discerning virtues, both efficacious and full of action. The geometric and tectonic vessels [1134C] signify the founding, and building, and perfecting power, and whatsoever else belongs to the providence of the seconds that lead back and convert. But there is a time when the instruments too, those things which have been done by the holy angels, are symbols unto us of the divine judgments; some declaring the correcting discipline, or the punishing justice; but others, liberty from anguish, or the end of discipline, or the resumption of the prior beneficence, or the addition of other goods, small or great, sensible or invisible [n: (Ezech. X; Judic. VI; II Machab. V; Ezech. XXXI; Apoc. III; Zach. VIII)]*. *And altogether perhaps a perspicacious mind would not doubt to unite beautifully the visible things to the invisible. That they are named winds also, signifies their velocity, and their effect arriving almost without delay upon all, [1134D] and the transporting motion from above into the things that are below, and upward into the things that are below, and the wind as raising the seconds to a higher loftiness, and moving the first to a communicative and provident procession of the lesser [n: (Gen. XXVIII; Joan. I)]*. *But perhaps someone will say that the windy surname of the aerial spirit signifies also the deiform character of the celestial ones: for this too has an image and form of the divine operation, as in the symbolic theology it is demonstrated through many things by a four-line adjudication, according to the moving, and generating, and swift, and potent capacity of nature, and the unknown to us, and invisible hiding-place of the moving principles and consummations. « For thou knowest not, he says, whence it comes, and whither it goes (Joan. III [1135A] Isai. V)*. » *But theology forms also about them the appearance of a cloud, signifying through this that the sacred intellects, supermundanely over-filled indeed with hidden light, receive the first manifestation pompously, and distribute the same copiously, lucidly and proportionally, into the things that are second: and that there subsists in them a genital, and vivific, and active, and perfective power, according to the intellectual conception of showers, receiving the bosom that calls forth into vital births by moist rains. Theology also sets about the celestial essences the appearance of brass, and of electrum, and of many-colored stones [n: (Isai. XXXV; Apoc. I; Dan. II)]*. *The electrum indeed signifies as it were the gold-formed, and at once the silvery: an incorruptible, as in gold, and ample, and not lessened, and uncontaminated splendor; and an open, as in silver, [1135B] and light-formed and celestial clarity. But in brass, according to the traditions handed down, either the fiery or the gold-formed is to be attributed. But the many-colored kinds of stones must be thought to signify: either, as it were, the white ones, the light-formed; or, as it were, the ruddy ones, the gold-formed; or, as it were, the pallid ones, the youthful and new; and through the several kinds thou wilt find an anagogic adjudication of the typic images. But since indeed I judge these things to have been sufficiently said by us according to our virtue, we must pass over to the sacred unlocking of the sacred-figured bestial formation of the celestial minds. For the lion must be thought to signify the principal, and robust, and untamed power, and the hidden power of the ineffable divinity, as a virtue, assimilative of the intellectual traces by a veiling-about and, mystically perhaps, by a pompous garment, its journey being restored into itself according to the divine [1135C] illumination. But the form of the ox signifies the firm and new, and that which recalls [renovans] the intellectual furrows into the reception of the celestial and generating showers, and the keeping and most strong power. Thence the form of the eagle signifies the regal, and high-bearing, and swift-flying, and, unto potent nourishment, the sharp, and sober, and agile, and well-contrived power, and, unto the copious and much-lucent ray of the divine sun, the power contemplative by desire, immediately, rightly and inflexibly, in the sound gazes of the speculative virtues. But that form of the horses signifies the obedient, and bridle-able; and the white indeed signifies the truly lucid, and, as it were, most greatly most-akin to the divine light. But of those which are black, the hidden; the red indeed the fiery, and active; and of those commingled of white and black, the power conjunctive of the extremes [1135D] with a perfective virtue, and connecting the first to the seconds, and the seconds to the first, convertibly and providently. But if we should not be limiting the commensuration of the discourse, we should perhaps not incongruously have united, part by part, the properties of the said animals, and all their corporeal conformations, to the celestial virtues according to unlike likenesses. The furious indeed to their intellectual fortitude, of which fury is the lowest image; but concupiscence again to the divine love: and, to be said summarily, referring at once all the senses, and the manifold parts of the irrational animals, unto the immaterial intelligences and uniform virtues of the celestial essence. But for the wise there suffice not only these very things, but the adjudication of one significative [1136A] image, unto the declaration, in like manner, of the neighboring ones. And this too must be inspected, that rivers were said to be, and wheels, and chariots connected with the celestial essences [n: (Dan. VII; Ezech. I, X)]*. *For the rivers signify the fiery divine advancements, giving to them a copious and not-failing affluence, and nourishing them with a vivific fecundity. But the chariots, the conjunctive society of likenesses [n: (IV Reg. I; Ezech. I)]*, *But the wheels, since indeed they are winged, going unconvertibly into the things that are before the sight, by a straight and just way signify the virtue of their outgoing operation into the same way without bending, and rightly cut, all of them at once by an intellectual rotation supermundanely directed. But it is possible also, by another anagogy, to adjudge the imaginary description of the intellectual wheels. For there was cried out [1136B] to them, as the theologian says, gel, gel, gel [n: (Ezech. X)]*. *But this signifies, according to the Hebrew word, revolutions and revelations. For the fiery and deiform wheels have revolutions indeed about the very same best thing by an ever-movable motion; but revelations, by the manifestation of secrets, and the circular-leading-back of the things subjected, and by the deductive perfection of high illuminations into the things which are subjected. But there remains to us the discourse in explanation concerning the joy of the celestial dispositions. For they are altogether not receivers of that passible delight which is according to us. But they are said to rejoice together with God [n: (Luc. XVI)] at the finding of the lost, according to the deiform banqueting, and at the deiformity of those returning into God in providence and in salvation, and the gladness of incorruption, and that ineffable beneficence, [1136C] in the participation of which holy men too have often been made partakers through the deific advents, from above, of the divine illuminations. So many things have been said by me concerning the holy formations, deficient indeed as regards a diligent manifestation of them; but perfect, as I judge, for our not remaining lowlily in figurative phantasies. But if thou shouldest say this also, why then we have not made mention of all the angelic virtues, or operations, or images in the oracles, we answer the truth: that of those whose supermundane knowledge we have been ignorant, we desire in them another light-leader to teach us [n: (Job XII)]*. *But certain things, as being equally potent with the things said, we have passed over, providing for the commensuration of the discourse, and honoring by silence the secret that is above us.*



## EXPOSITION.

[1136D]

The title of the fifteenth and last chapter is: What are the formative images of the angelic virtues, and what next, that is, and the rest? For thus far he has treated of the invisible properties of the angels: which are, namely, those gifts of the virtues, the invisible operations in them. Now consequently he begins to treat of the visible formations: which the sacred word attributes to them by a mystical signification according to the images and likenesses of corporeal things. "Come, on, speak, ho!" is the voice of one exhorting either himself, or also Timothy, to whom he speaks: « Ho, let us say what remains. » What is it that remains? This, namely, that we, « if it so seems, » send back to thee, or relax, [1137A] « our intellectual eye, » hitherto « intent about the sublime contemplations » held by beholding « concerning the angelic vigor; » and, the eye being turned back, that we descend « to the divided and much-parted breadth of the multiform variety of the angelic specifications, » that is, to the multiform variety, or the variety of many forms, which is in the angelic specifications, or formations: in which variety, because there is an ample multitude, or a manifold amplitude, in place of simplicity the divided is found, and in place of unity the much-parted. Thus therefore let us say what remains, sending back our eye from the invisible things, and descending to the visible. « Again in them, » namely descending, and fixing our consideration, and yet « he reflected » again, or those images being led back [1137B] « analytically, » that is, resolutively, « into the simplicity of the celestial minds: as » of themselves « in things consequent, » that is, in things unfitting. And the sense is: we, descending through contemplation from the invisible things to the visible, and fixing our consideration namely in the visible things themselves, let us again reflect them back to the simplicity of the invisible things, by resolving and expounding, or showing how, through that which is discerned as visible, that which cannot be seen is signified. Otherwise the visible formation would be inconsequent and unfitting, if in that simple nature this were believed to be by the truth of essence, which is attributed to it as manifold in signification according to the various species of corporeal things. As therefore we descend [1137C] by representing the simple natures through visible things, so we ascend, and, as it were resolutively, reflect ourselves back to the divine consideration, when, by expounding the same forms spiritually, we declare them to be understood mystically. We descend, when we fit the multiplicity of corporeal formations to the simplicity of the celestial natures through signification: we ascend, when we resolve the same multiplicity of visible figurations, by expounding it, unto the understanding of the simple truth.

There follows: « But one thing, etc. » O Timothy, before we treat of the visible formations of the angels, let one thing be foreknown by thee, that thou first know this. What is that? this, namely, that thou know it. « How the sacred discretions of the formed [1137D] images, » that is, how the Holy Scriptures, distinguished in the images which they form concerning them, the angels, « signify and represent the same dispositions, » that is, the orders « of the celestial essences. » Sometimes the lower ones as « ordaining, and again » the same as « ordained » by the higher: wherein they signify in a certain way that these are middle, and that they have lower ones whom they may ordain, and higher ones by whom they may be ordained. « And again sometimes they signify even the last dispositions as ordaining, and the first as ordained: » which nevertheless seems a wonder, since neither do those lower ones have any whom they may ordain, nor do these higher ones have any by whom they may be ordained. Wherein nevertheless it is given to be understood that the supreme ones, although they have no other angels above themselves by whom they may be ordained, yet have God, by whom they are ordained [1138A] and sanctified. And the last ones again, although they have no angels beneath themselves whom they may ordain, yet have men, who are ordained, and disposed, and sanctified by them. Or certainly in this, that the supreme ones are introduced as ordained, and the last as ordaining, it is signified that among them some differ from others: and there are among the higher ones certain lower ones, who are nevertheless ordained unto something by others established in the same order; and among the lower ones certain higher ones, who ordain unto something others placed in the same order. And this is what follows: That the very words signify the same dispositions, namely, as having first, and middle, and last virtues. As has already been said above, that each disposition has distinction in the persons, and each person in [1138B] the properties, so, he says, the discretions of the sacred formations represent the first, and middle, and last spirits in the same order, and the first, and middle, and last virtues in the same person; « yet no » « inordinate reasoning being introduced in this, according to the manner of such unlockings, » that is, expositions. For if it is understood in this manner, as we have expounded above, there is nothing unfitting in this representation. And I well say that in this representation, by which the same ones are introduced both as ordaining and as ordained, there is no unfittingness, because there is no confusion, or transposition, of the right disposition there, since they are introduced as ordained by some and as ordaining others. But if they were said to be ordaining and [1138C] ordained in respect of the same ones, it would be unfitting, this is what he says: « If indeed we should say that certain ones, » understand: dispositions, namely the middle ones, « are ordained by the prior ones; » and then that those « prior ones ordain, » or are ordainers, « of the same, » namely of the middle ones, « and again, » through those middle ones, « ordain, » or are ordainers, « of the last ones: » if, he says, we should say that those prior ordainers of the middle and last ones, in a reversed order, « are ordained by those very ones that were ordained: truly the interposed matter would be commingled with much inordination and confusion; » because it would not be orderly that some should be ordained by those whom they ordain. « But if we say that the same ones, » in a different respect, « ordain and are ordained; yet not that they are ordainers of the same ones » by whom they are ordained, « or that they are ordained by the same ones » whom [1138D] they ordain; « but that they severally, » that is, remaining distinct without confusion of reciprocation, « are indeed ordained by the prior ones, but ordain the last ones, » there is nothing unfitting. And according to this perhaps « not inconsequently, » that is, not unfittingly, « someone would say that the sacred-made forms which are in the words can sometimes both beautifully, and truly, » that is, fittingly and truthfully, « be encompassed about, » that is, be fitted, « the same forms, to first, and middle, and last virtues. »

There follows: « And upward therefore, etc. » As if he should say: Since someone can truthfully say that the same forms are fittingly fitted to first, and middle, and last virtues. Therefore that very one who shall have said this will not falsely conjoin, that is, will commonly [1139A] attribute to all the celestial essences: both to be extended upward through the love of God, and to be firmed in themselves through the keeping of themselves, and to advance below themselves through the love of the neighbor — this is what he says: « To be extended upward convertibly [ *convertibiliter* ], » namely through the love of God, « and to be firmly enwrapped about themselves, » that is, to be constrained and encompassed: constrained, lest the good flow out; encompassed, lest the evil flow in. Wherein, namely, because they are enwrapped about themselves, « they exist as keepers of their own virtues, » that is, they keep their own virtues. This therefore, that is, to be extended upward, and to be enwrapped in themselves, « and that they are in participation of virtue, » understand: prepared, « providently, » that is, intently or devotedly, « toward the things that come, by an associable procession. » These three things therefore someone will commonly and truly [1139B] attribute to all « the celestial essences, » namely that they are extended upward through the love of God, and that they are enwrapped about themselves through the keeping of themselves, and that they are prepared providently, and intently, and devotedly to impart their virtue toward the things that come to them, proceeding by a social love, and freely offering themselves unto social love. All these things therefore can be commonly attributed to all: « although, » that is, though, « to some indeed superplacedly and universally, » that is, they belong excellently and fully, as « has already often been said; but to others particularly and subjectly. »
