There follows: « But one must begin with reason, » etc. As if he should say: Since we must treat of the angelic formations, therefore we must in the first place begin [1139C] with reason, that is, rationally, « and inquire in the first discretion » of the very « forms, » why « theology is found honoring the fiery sacred description, » that is, formation, in which the species of fire is set forth for signification, « almost beyond all the other descriptions. » For rarely is any visible species found (if indeed it is found) having a more excellent demonstration toward the invisible things. « Thou wilt find it therefore, » namely theology, « not only forming fiery wheels » for the signification of those invisible things, « but also fiery animals, and men flashing as it were with fire; » and thou wilt find that theology also « sets heaps of coals of fire about those celestial essences, » and also describes « rivers blazing with fire with an immeasurable [1139D] sound. » And not only this: « But it says also that thrones are fiery, » namely theology itself, that they are in heaven; and it says also « that the most exalted seraphim themselves signify by their surname that they are burning after a heavenly manner, and it distributes » also « to them, » the seraphim, « the property and operation of fire. And, » to say it briefly, « altogether, upward, and downward, everywhere it selectively honors, » that is, specially, the fiery making of the forms.

There follows: « Therefore the fiery, » etc. As if he should say: Since theology so greatly venerates the fiery formation: « Therefore, » I judge « the fiery to signify the most deiform thing of the celestial minds, » that is, that virtue which is most deiform in them. And rightly, because « the theologians themselves often describe in [1140A] fire, » that is, in the species and figure of fire, even the very « superessential and formless essence » of the divinity: which is both superessential by majesty, and formless by incomprehensibility. They describe, I say, in fire « as having many images of the divine property, as far as, in » « visible » things, an image of the divine property can be had. Yet I say thus, « if it is lawful to say it, » that the divine property has some likeness, the property which is very incomprehensible and invisible.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « For fire, » etc. Now he enters upon describing the properties of fire, in which he shows its nature, and quality, and wonderful power, and demonstrates its excellent signification. « For fire, » he says, « is sensible. So indeed [1140B] it must be said, » namely that it is sensible. He adds this for this reason: because to some, perhaps, it could seem — on the ground that the nature of fire is perceived by sense only in subjacent matter — that fire is not a body at all. He says therefore that fire is truly sensible, and a body, though far more excellent and subtle than all other corporeal things, and nearest to the spiritual nature, and by this demonstrating its effect far more unlike all others. « For fire in all things, and through all things comes clearly, and » yet « is removed from all things. » It is near by presence; it is far by excellence. It is in all things because it makes itself visible through illumination, and it is far from all things because it does not offer itself perceptible through a distinct perception of its substance. [1140C] This is what follows: And « it is at once bright, and as it were hidden. » For it is bright, because it is perceived in subjacent matter; hidden, because in its own essence it is not seen corporeally. « It is unknown » itself « through itself, matter not lying by; » because without subjacent matter, as has been said, it cannot be perceived: « in which » matter, namely, « it manifests its proper action. » From which in a certain way it seems to be more incorporeal; because apart from subjacent matter it does not come to sense. Whence fire seems in a certain way to be midway between the invisible and the visible? inasmuch as it approaches those, incorporeal, and is corporeal inasmuch as it draws near to these. It is also immeasurable, because it grows out [1140D] into the infinite, and as far as the subjacent matter suffices, it itself does not fail; and it is invisible, because in its pure substance, outside subjacent matter, it is not seen. It is also « through itself potent over all things at once, » namely things, « and potent » also « over all things, whatsoever in them, » namely in things, « come to pass unto proper action. For in fire indeed is the first corporeal motion, which moves all other things, and is itself not moved by another. Therefore whatever is done in all things is not proper to those in which it comes to pass, but rather to that itself through which it comes to pass.

There follows: « Movable, giving itself to all things drawing near in whatsoever manner. » For because it is moved of itself by its own motion, it diffuses itself into all things, moving each according as it draws near to it, [1141A] that is, according as it is found more or less movable. It is also « renovative » of all things, because it renews things growing old, lest they wholly fail and go into nothing. For we see through the several years how fire renews nature, when those things which had grown old with the winter's cold, warmed by the spring's heat, revive. In this therefore fire itself is « the guardian » of « nature; » because it guards nature, lest it wholly cease to be, if it always suffered defect and were never repaired. It is also « illuminative, » because it illuminates all things, « with splendors veiled about. » For its splendors flash forth without unto illumination, but are veiled about when, drawn back again to the secret of their nature, they hide themselves. Therefore, then, it is « incomprehensible, » because outwardly indeed [1141B] poured forth it is perceived by sense, but inwardly withdrawn it is not comprehended. It is also « clear, » manifestly appearing, and « distinct, » distinguishing diverse things. And it is « resilient, » because it is fed downward, and is moved upward — laying hold of lower things by its ardor, but from its own lightness rebounding to the heights.

Therefore there follows: « Bearing upward, » because, lacking [sic: *carons*] weight, it seeks the highest things, « and going acutely, » because by the motion of its proper power it penetrates all things, « and lofty, » because it stands eminent above the universe; because whatever is corporeal besides itself is by nature beneath itself: « Not about to receive the affront of diminution; » because, just as when it is poured forth it is not increased, if [cj: *si*; read *sic*, "so"] when it is received it is not diminished, neither, in that [1141C] it seems to receive increase, taking glory, nor, in that it is thought to be diminished, having suffered affront, because what it is in itself it is always whole. It is also « always moved, » that is, having motion, and it is « moved through itself, » because the motion which it has is not from another; and in that it moves itself, it moves all other things having motion; and therefore it is « moving another, » and is itself « comprehending » though « incomprehended. » For changing all matter by its own power, by using and consuming it it draws it into itself, and is held by no body, since, that which it burns up being consumed, it returns into itself. It is therefore « not needing another, » because through the subjacent matter, in which it burns, it is not so much held that it may be, as held that it may be there; and if it seem to grow from [1141D] it, it does not receive from it the substance of increase, although from it it takes in it the cause of growing, because it becomes not greater in itself, but rather in it. Therefore he says that it is « latently growing from itself, » because, as to the manifest, it seems to grow from the subjacent matter itself; but in the truth of the thing it grows from itself, not that it be greater in itself, but that it be more in the matter itself: therefore it receives increase indeed from itself, when it seems to grow into the subjacent things, « manifesting only its own magnitude unto those matters, » in which it is; that is, it so grows in the matter which it takes up, that is, lays hold of, that in it indeed it appears greater, but nevertheless in itself it is not greater. For it does not there [1142A] receive magnitude, but shows it, manifesting there sometimes what in itself it has always. It is also « active, » and « potent, » because it acts powerfully, and is moved, destroying all things standing in the way; and it is « at once present to all things invisibly. » For in certain things it is only seen, but it is held present to all things invisibly: which appears from this, that sometimes by the striking and collision of bodies without attrition it is struck out even thence where it seemed not to be. « Neglected » indeed, and not moved, or remaining quiet, « it is thought not to be; but by attrition » or rubbing, provoked and set in motion, « as though by a certain vengeance it suddenly shines forth » leaping out, and raging upon those things which it has seized: which nevertheless it does « connaturally and properly, » that is, out of its own power and potency. [1142B] « And again incomprehensibly impalpable, » when it slides back, it remains incomprehensible to sense: « not lessened » by extinction, just as neither increased by kindling. All of which it does « in all » things, « by its most rich deliveries; » because whatever from itself it delivers to the subjacent things unto some power, it has not the less in itself, ever persisting full. These properties of fire, then, I have enumerated; and perhaps « someone will find many other properties of fire, beautiful » — namely, likenesses — « of the divine operation: as, » that is, just as « in sensible images, » namely, the likeness of it can be. And therefore « the theosophists, knowing this, » that is, so great an excellence of fire, « conform, » or figure, « the celestial essences out of fire: signifying » by this « their deiform, [1142C] and God-imitable, » quality which is in them, « as far as is possible » to be had. And not only do they conform them out of fire, « but they also describe them humaniform, » that is, in human form, « on account of the intellectual, » or rational, in them — that is, their rationality — since man alone among all visible things is found rational. Likewise in human form they describe them, on account of « the intuitive, » that is, contemplative, « virtues » of them, which they have by holding themselves upward, just as man alone among all living things has his countenance erect toward things above: « and » they also describe them in human form on account of the « rectitude » of the human « figure » itself, that is, the uprightness, because the human stature is erect, not prone like that of other [1142D] living things; « and » on account of « the luculent, » that is, the clear and evident, without [cj: *sine*; read *sive*, "or"] the distinguishing mark of the human figure itself; because from its very erectness it affords a more evident and more comely aspect; « and » on account of « the principal, and regal, » which is given to man himself « according to nature, » that he may have the first place and rule over the other creatures; and on account of « the least according to sense as regards the remaining virtues of the irrational animals, » that is, because man is less strong in corporeal sense than the other living things, which in the power of perceiving received a greater strength than he. On account of the least indeed in sense, and on account of the Omnipotent in power, or on account of « potent » with every power « according to the greatness of the intellect and according to rational knowledge remaining in continuity, » [1143A] « and on account of the free, and most potent, » which is in him « according to the nature of the soul. » For on account of this the angel is described in human form; because man, by how much he is less strong in sense, where he is surpassed by the beasts, by so much is he more potent in reason, and intelligence, and the freedom of natural choice, where he is likened to the angels.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

« But there is, » etc. As if he should say: Not only on account of these things which I have said is the human form fittingly adapted to the angels by signification, but also it « is, » that is, it happens, possible « to find the celestial virtues through the several parts of our corporeal manifold partition. » For in the several parts of the human body, through mystical signification, spiritual virtues can be found; because all things which have been visibly made have some [1143B] signification unto the invisible. Thus those who consider rightly can weigh the invisible from the visible. Whence it happens to find this, « saying, » that is, they who say, that « that clearest gaze, » which is given to man « toward the divine luminaries, » such as the sun, and the moon, and the stars, « signifies the conspective virtues, » that is, the contemplative, which are in the rational. Likewise saying that that same gaze of the exterior sight further signifies a certain « reception » of « the divine illuminations, tender, » that is, easily perceiving, and liquid, that is, clearly seeing through, « and not beaten back, » that is, effectually comprehending, « but acutely mobile, » because not beaten back; « pure, » because liquid; « fully impassibly, » because tender. [1143C] So indeed speaking of corporeal sight: « But the smell, » they say, signifies the « discretive virtues » of corporeal things, « that receptive of the distribution, » or bestowal, or effusion, of spiritual grace, « sweetly smelling above the intellect. » Receptive, I say, as far as it is possible to be receptive; « and of those things which are not so, » that is, of those which do not smell sweetly, « discretive by knowledge, and altogether refugitive. » That is, they say that smell signifies specially that force or virtue of the soul which spiritually discerns between those things which spiritually smell well, and those which do not; and desires the well-smelling, but flees the ill-smelling. « But the virtues of the ears » they say signify « that » which is in the soul « participant; [1143D] and gnostic, » that is, cognitive and receptive of the divine inspiration. « The gustative, » etc. He says the same, that is, they say that the gustative virtues signify « the fulness of the invisible and divine foods, » by which the celestial essences are refreshed; signify also « that receptive of the nourishing promotions, that is, that which receives for them the nourishing promotions, that is, the nurturing increases. But the tactive virtues » they say signify that which is in the soul, « discretive by knowledge of the fitting, or the harmful. Then the eyelids, and the eyebrows » they say signify « that custoditive of the intelligence of the divine visions; but the youthful and the adult age » they say signify « that » which is in the soul « of the vital virtue ever renewing » it. « But the teeth, » [1144A] by which food is divided, and broken small, and passed over into the body to be nourished, they say signify « the divisive of the nourishing perfection put into, » or engrafted in, the rational spirit itself. « For each intellectual essence divides and multiplies, by a provident virtue, the uniform intelligence given to it by a diviner essence » as it were whole food: breaking small, and adapting it « to the leading analogy of the inferior, » that is, to the measure by which it may be conveyed to the participation of the inferior knowledge. For unless it broke small to measure that which it receives great, it could by no means pass over to the participation of the inferior intelligence. « But the shoulders, and the arms, and likewise the hands » they say signify « the factive, and operative, and active. But the heart [1144B] » they say « is a symbol, » that is, a figure, « of the deiform life, » sowing, or scattering, or diffusing, « its own vital virtue into those things which have been foreknown: » just as the heart diffuses vital motion to the other members. Therefore they say that the heart is, in God, or in the rational spirit, that virtue from which vital grace is diffused into others, who have been foreseen by God to receive it. For it is given only to those who have been foreseen to receive it, and predestined unto life. « The breasts again » they say « signify the hard, and the firm; and the custoditive » of that « life-giving distribution proceeding from the heart, » inasmuch as « from a thing placed beneath, » because the heart is placed beneath the breast, that by it it may be fortified and guarded. « But the backs, » they say, signify « the continuous, » [1144C] that is, the continuation « of all the fertile virtues; » because, just as in the back spine is coupled to spine, so in the virtues one coheres to another. « But the feet » they say signify « the mobile, and swift, and cursive of the motion ever going into the divine. On account of which, » because they have swift motion, « theology has figured the feet of the holy intellects as winged. » And rightly, because « the winged signifies the anagogic, » that is, upward-leading, « swiftness; » and it signifies also « the celestial active, » that is, the celestial action, or impulse, or promotion of the journey, which is upward: and it signifies also « the removed, » that is, the removal, « from all that is lowly, » that is, from lowliness, or abjection, or depression: [1144D] which « removed » is in them « by the bearing upward, » because in that they are borne upward, they are removed from all things which are in the depth. But the very lightness of the wings signifies that there is nothing earthly or heavy in them, « but the whole ascending on high purely, and without weight. The naked, too, and the unshod, » which is attributed to them, signifies « the lowered » of them, « and the loosed, and the immeasurable, and the pure from » all « apposition of those things which are without; » that is, that the angels are described naked and unshod signifies that they are lowered, that is, loosed, and absolved, and pure, « from » all « apposition, » or conjunction, « of those things which are without, » that is, of visible things, and by this immeasurable. For by how much the less they are constrained by corporeal mingling, or apposition, [1145A] by so much the more they are, through the purity of intelligence, immeasurably dilated. It signifies also « the naked, and unshod, assimilative of them to the divine simplicity, as far as is possible, » because, pure from corporeal admixture, they do not subsist out of a double substance. By which sentence they are struck who say that the angels have bodies. For the author testifies that they are pure from all apposition of the things which are without, and that through their naked and pure essence, in which they subsist, they imitate the divine simplicity.

There follows: « But since again, » etc. As if he should say: I have said that divine Scripture, in that it describes the angels as naked, signifies their purity and simplicity. But since again that very wisdom, which is both simple by truth, and much varied [1145B] through the diversity of formations — « since, » I say, that same such « wisdom both again clothes the naked, and gives them certain vessels, » that is, instruments, « to carry about: come, » that is, up now, « let us open, according as it is possible for us, the sacred garments of the celestial spirits, and their organs, » that is, instruments; because, as has been said, they are described both with clothing, and certain vessels, or organs, are attributed to them to be carried about in that mystical description. And I well say let us open what these signify, because all are significative. First, then, « the bright and fiery garment, » which is attributed to them, « I esteem to signify the deiform, » which is in them, « according to the image of fire, » which is the likeness of deiformity; « and the luculent, that is, the clear, I esteem to be attributed to them, on account of the reposes, » which are [1145C] « in heaven, where light is, » but not any light whatever, but « altogether invisible » (for so it must be said, that that light which is there is altogether invisible), « either intellectually illuminating, » as in God, « or intellectually illuminated, » as in the angels. « But the sacerdotal garment » I esteem signifies « the leading, » or leadership, or direction, « to the divine and mystical contemplations, » and signifies « the vow of the whole life. » For in the sacerdotal garment is signified that they are leaders unto the divine, mystical contemplations, that is, unto the divine and mystical contemplations, instructing their subjects unto knowledge, and that they dedicate their life to God, exhorting, and confirming the devotion of a good work. « The girdles, too, » they say signify « the custoditive of those fecund virtues: » [1145D] and signify « the habit gathering them, » namely the virtues, « to be converted unitedly into itself, and to be carried about circularly around itself, with a faculty free from falling, from the likeness of nature. » The sense is: that the girdle, which surrounds and constrains the loins and the navel, in which is the seminative of propagation, signifies the guardianship of the fecund virtues; which guardianship surrounds those virtues, and guards, or constrains them, lest in their multiplication they be relaxed beyond the bounds of justice, or flow apart. It is also signified in the girdle itself, in that by surrounding the body after the manner of a circle it is turned into itself, and united: namely, that the habit of the virtues, gathering those virtues into one, lest they flow apart or recede from the unity of concord; [1146A] namely, that that habit, I say, is bent about circularly around itself through the coherence of all things and through the mutual consideration of those virtues: bent about, I say, with ease, because there is made an easy progression from virtue to virtue on account of the likeness and concord: which ease is free from falling, from the likeness of nature. For the progression of the virtues is not the corruption, but the reparation, of nature. « The rods, too, » or scepters, they say signify « the regal and principal, and defining all things right; » just as a rod is straight, and a royal bearing. « But the darts, and the axes, » by which whole things are wont to be cut, or divided, or severed, they say signify « the separative of dissimilitudes, » that is, that force which separates dissimilitudes, and discerns between good and evil; [1146B] « and » they say it signifies that same « acumen of the discerning virtues, » that is, subtlety, namely in inspecting; « and the efficacious, and the actuous, » that is, the efficacy of acting by perfecting: But « the geometric » « and tectonic vessels, » that is, the instruments of measuring and building, they say signify « the fundative, and edificative, and perfective, and whatsoever else there are of the providence reducing and converting the second things. » For the providence of the first and higher things, which converts and reduces unto the better the second and lower things, founds, and builds, and perfects in them a spiritual edifice. Of which work the signs are the geometric and tectonic vessels, formed in the vision according to a corporeal appearance.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « But there is, etc. » As if he should say: Those [1146C] things which are attributed to the angels according to the form of visible things are not only signs of their invisible virtue, but signify also sometimes those things which are done in us and about us. This is what he says: « There is, » that is, it happens, « when those organs, » that is, instruments, « which are enacted, » that is, exhibited or presented, « by the holy angels, are symbols, » that is, signs and demonstrations « of the divine judgments, » which are exercised upon us or toward us. « Some, » namely organs, by signification « declaring the corrective discipline, » namely of God, as the rod, which seems to strike, « or » declaring « the punishing justice, » as the axes and swords. « But others, » organs declaring « liberty, » or liberation from distress, « or the end of discipline, » that is, [1146D] of correction, and punishment, « or resumption, » that is, recuperation « of the prior beneficence, or the apposition of other gifts » after the prior ones given, « small or great, sensible, » as in corporeal goods, « or invisible, » as in spiritual. « And, that » I may say it universally, « perchance a perspicacious mind would not doubt at all, that is, in all things, beautifully to unite, » that is, to adapt by signification, « the visible to the invisible, » so that, namely, it would leave nothing of all visible things which it would not demonstrate to have some signification unto the invisible. And rightly: because even « that » — namely, the angels — « are named winds, » that is, that even this, that the angels themselves are named winds, « signifies their [1147A] swiftness, and their effect reaching into almost all things without delay. » And it signifies also their transvective motion « from above into those things which are below, and » again from those « which are below into those which are above. » But what that motion may convey hither and thither he consequently adds. « A motion, » I say, « erecting the second things, » that is, the lower unto the higher loftiness, « and moving the first things, » that is, the higher, « to a communicative and provident procession of the lesser, » that is, that they proceed to the lesser, that is, the lower, to provide for them, and to communicate to them their virtue.

There follows: « But perhaps someone will say, » etc. He had said above that the angels are named winds on account of their swiftness; now he subjoins that perhaps [1147B] someone will be able to say that they are named winds on account of the divine likeness; because the wind too, according to something, has the form and image of the divine operation, as in another theology, concerning the quaternary dijudication, that is, concerning the signification of the four elements by several examples, or arguments, he testifies that he has demonstrated: which work is not had among us. « Perhaps someone will say, he says, that is, some one, that the windy denomination of the aerial spirit signifies » not only swiftness, but also « the deiform, » that is, the deiformity of the celestial spirits. « For it has even this » (understand: element) in itself « both the image, and the form, of the divine operation, » as, that is, just as by several examples, namely, it is demonstrated « in the symbolic theology, » that is, in the theology [1147C] which is demonstrated concerning symbols, that is, concerning figures and visible likenesses: I say « by the tetrastic, » that is, quaternary « dijudication, » where by the four elements severally it is shown how the visible are both likenesses and signs of the invisible. According to that dijudication, then, it was demonstrated how this element, that is, the wind, has both the image and the form of the divine operation. But wherein it has this image and form, he subjoins: « According to the motive capacity of its nature, and generative, and swift, and potent, » because the moved air subjects motion and increase to things, and running swiftly from without impels potently, or violently, « and according to the unknown to us, [1147D] and invisible lurking-place of the moving principles and consummations. » For the wind moved comes forth to sense; but the beginning and end of its motion sense does not apprehend. Whence it is written: « Who produces the winds out of his treasuries [n: (Psal. CXXXIV)]. » For to produce the winds out of treasuries is to bring forth to sense, by manifest agitation, the breathings of the winds out of hidden and latent causes. According to this likeness also the Creator Spirit, hiddenly presiding over all things and remaining invisible, moves and promotes all things by his virtue; and the things which are moved are seen, but he who moves is not seen; potent in doing, swift in perfecting; whose works indeed are discerned without, but the unknown and invisible lurking-place of the first causes and [1148A] the principles moving those very works unto effect is not penetrated. Likewise also the unknown lurking-place of the consummations is not penetrated; because, when anything is moved unto effect, we discern indeed that which comes to pass; but whence it so comes to pass, or unto what it comes to pass, we do not see. This is that, in the vision of the seraphim, they cover the head and the feet of him sitting on the throne, because we discern the middle things of the divine operation, which are done in time; but the things which were first before the times, and which shall be last after the times, we cannot investigate, because the lurking-place of the moving principles, and of the consummations, is invisible to us and unknown. Likewise also when the Spirit comes to the heart, it is felt indeed in that it is perceived. « But whence » it « comes, or whither it goes, » is not investigated. Whence [1148B] it comes, is the beginning; whither it goes, is the consummation and end. « But thou knowest not, » he says, « whence it comes, or whither it goes, » because the lurking-place both of the beginning and of the end is at once unknown and invisible. Therefore thou knowest not whence it comes, because thou findest no merit in thee for which thou mightest say thyself to be the grace. Thou knowest not whither it goes, because from the gift received thou dost not understand unto what faith thou mayest attain. According to these things, therefore, every wind has the image and form of the divine operation.

There follows: « But also of the cloud, » etc. Not only, he says, does theology name the angels winds, « but it also forms about the appearance of a cloud, or adapts it to them: by calling them clouds, signifying by this the sacred intellects super-filled supermundanely, » that is, spiritually, « with the occult light, » that is, invisible charity: those [1148C] I say « receiving pompously, » that is, excellently, « the first manifestation » of that light in its first appearing, because it is first shown to them, and excellently perceived by them: therefore they themselves are « receiving the first manifestation of it pompously, » and gloriously; and they are also distributing that same, namely the manifestation, « into those which are second after them; distributing it to them lucidly, » that is, clearly, « and proportionally, » that is, according to the measure of each one. So also the clouds first receive the light of the sun, then diffuse it, resplendent from themselves, into the things below. And therefore also the angels are called clouds, because « in them subsists the general, and vivific, and active, and perfective, according to the intellectual conception [1148D] of » spiritual « showers. » As the clouds, which by the effusion of showers, making the earth fruitful, give to the things born of it the force of generating, and of vivification, and of the increase of perfection. So then they are called clouds, on account of such a conception of showers — I say « evoking, » or provoking, or eliciting, « unto vital births » the receiving bosom, that is, the earth, which receives the showers in its bosom: « evoking, I say, by moist rains, » that is, through moist rains. « For through the rains it provokes the earth by nourishing to bring forth vital births.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « It itself also, » etc. As if he should say: Not only in the appearance of a cloud does theology itself describe the angels, « but it also puts about the appearance of air, and of electrum, and of many-colored stones about them itself by » signification [1149A] « on the celestial essences. » And rightly. For « electrum » indeed, inasmuch as « gold-formed, and at once silvern, signifies, » inasmuch as in gold, the splendor « imputrescible, and at once ample, and not diminished, and uncontaminated, » as gold does not at any time rust, but has a « splendor » pure, and perpetual. « And » it signifies also, inasmuch as « in silver, brightness open, and light-formed, and celestial, » as silver is bright and shining. So then electrum, according to each appearance, namely of gold and of silver, signifies the bright and the fulgid, that is, the clear and the fiery, because the supernal wisdom, which shines in those spirits, is both clear through knowledge, and fiery through love. This, then, through the appearance of electrum is signified in the supernal spirits. « But in the air there is to be attributed » [1149B] to them « either the fiery or the gold-formed, according to the reasons » already « delivered, » namely in the exposition of electrum, where according to something a similar appearance is found. « But the many-colored appearances of stones it is to be esteemed signify either the light-formed, as it were the white ones, » that is, as the white appearances, namely, signify, « or the gold-formed, as it were the red ones, or the youthful and new, as it were the pallid ones. » And, that I may say it briefly, « through the several appearances thou wilt find an anagogic, that is, spiritual, dijudication of the typic, » that is, figurative, images. « But since indeed I judge these things to have been said by us sufficiently according to our virtue, therefore there must be a passing over » — it is now — « into the sacred unlocking, » that is, exposition, « of the sacredly-figured bestial formation of the celestial spirits, [1149C] namely that we may expound the bestial forms which are sacredly figured to the celestial spirits for signifying them. For the lion is to be reckoned to signify the principal, and robust, and indomitable, » which subsists in them spiritually, and to signify also « the assimilative of the intellectual footprints » going « to the hidden, » that is, secret, « of the ineffable divinity. » Which footprints, as, that is, as the virtue, that is, the spiritual thing, is able, imitate the footprints of the lion, because, just as the lion wipes out his footprints with his tail and in a certain way reforms and restores the level ground which he had impressed with his foot, that his going may be hidden, so the invisible step of the celestial spirits is in a certain way figured, « its journey restored into itself, » that is, brought back to [1149D] the prior state, and reduced to the level, the signs of the footprints being wiped out, « by a veiling-about, that is, through a veiling-about; and, that so it may perhaps rather be mystically said, it restores in a journey with a pompous garment, » that is, a distinguished, or excellent, or splendid and glorious garment coming to them « according to the divine illumination. » For to those going inward to contemplate the secret light of the divinity, from the supervening splendor the footprints are wiped out; because everything which before adhered through knowledge is wiped from the memory, when to the souls desiring, the desired brightness is manifested. Hence it is that Paul says: « One thing — forgetting the things indeed which are behind, I stretch myself unto those things which are before. » As therefore to go with the footprints wiped out is, the thought of all [1150A] [sic: *imagitionum*] being wiped from the mind, to set forth with the mind to contemplate the [sic: *incircumpscritum*] light. For there indeed the intellect, while it is surrounded with the splendor of the supervening brightness, as though covered by a certain garment, whatever besides that light is in itself, even that it cannot see. All these things, then, it is to be esteemed the form of the lion signifies. « But that of the ox » (understand: the form) it is to be esteemed signifies « the firm and new, » and recalling or renewing, « and repairing the intellectual furrows unto the reception of the celestial and generative showers; and » it is to be esteemed to signify also « the custoditive and most strong. » For because the ox firmly fixes its foot, it signifies the firmness and stability of immutability. But in this, that furrowing the earth it recalls it, and renews it unto the reception [1150B] of the celestial showers by which it is made fruitful for generating, it signifies the interior newness, by which the mind is tempered and adapted to the reception of the celestial grace, which, being made fruitful, brings forth the germs of the virtues. In this, then, that the ox is eminent by the strength of its neck, and bearing the yoke keeps guard, is signified the guardianship of the mind, by which in every act it perseveres in its own guardianship without defect. « That of the eagle thereafter » (understand: the form) it is to be esteemed signifies « the regal, » because it is preeminent over the other birds; « and the high-bearing, » because by flight it bears itself on high; « and the swift-flying, » because it flies swiftly; « and, unto potent nourishment, acute and sober, » because flying in the height it acutely descries its nourishment, soberly desires it, potently seizes it. [1150C] The form of the eagle also signifies « the agile, » because it is expeditious for moving itself; « and » it signifies « the well-contrived, » because in its motion it fittingly coadapts itself; « and » it signifies also the contemplative « unto the ray of the divine sun copious, and much-shining, » that is, much shining, one word. « Contemplative, » I say, « by desire, » that is, through the desire « of the inspective virtues, » that is, of those beholding within, or the interior things: and I say the contemplation « with sound gazes, and immediately, and rightly, and inflexibly, » just as the eagle with unbeaten eyes beholds the ray of the sun. All these things, then, in the contemplative spirits, it is to be esteemed the form of the eagle signifies. « But that of horses » (understand: the form) it is to be esteemed signifies « obedient, and bridleable, [1150D] and white indeed, » that is, the white form is to be esteemed to signify « truly lucid, and as it were most cognate to the divine light. But of those, » namely of horses, « which are black, » the form is to be esteemed to signify « the hidden, » that is, the occult, namely of the divine incomprehensibility, as the black color is dark, and repels the sight. « But the red » color it is to be esteemed signifies « the fiery and active, » because it intimates the fervor of a good will, which is exercised in work. « Of the mingled ones, too » (understand: horses), « from white and black » color, it is to be esteemed to signify « the conjunctive of the extremes with a perfective virtue: » which associates through [1151A] charity « the first to the second, and the second to the first, convertibly, and consequently connecting » them, namely that they be connected to one another through mutual love and providence. This, therefore, can be understood in the aforesaid formations. And perhaps, « if we did not terminate the commensuration of the discourse, » that is, if it were not this that we wish to terminate the discourse by such measure, and not to go further through the several things to be expounded: and unless we also terminated all the properties, « and all the corporeal conformations » of the aforesaid animals « through their parts » already said — that is, unless by this, that we have already said of them in part, we wish to signify by one likeness whatever can universally be said of them, whether in the interior properties, or in the exterior conformations: [1151B] unless this, I say, be that through the several things by expounding we are unwilling to go, « perhaps not incongruously we should have united to the celestial virtues, » even the furious, « according to dissimilar likenesses, » as was said above, that some dissimilitudes are like, but some are dissimilar. According to this manner, then, not incongruously we should have adapted « the furious » to the celestial virtues, for signifying « their intellectual fortitude: » « of which » fortitude, namely, « the fury is the lowest image, » because, namely, through the contrary and from afar it signifies. « But the concupiscence » of the irrational, not incongruously we should have adapted « unto the divine love, » for signifying it. « And, that we may say it universally, » as it is to be said summarily, fittingly we should have adapted « at once [1151C] both all the senses, and all the manifold parts of the irrational animals, » namely to those spiritual beings by signification — the senses and the parts, I say, « reducing » us by signifying « into the immaterial, » that is, spiritual « intelligences, and uniform virtues of the essence of the celestial; » because through those things which are corporeally and materially described in the senses and members of the irrational animals we are stirred up to the immaterial, that is, spiritual intelligence of those virtues which uniformly and purely are in the essence of the spiritual beings. Many other things could be said, « but there suffice to the wise not only these things themselves » which have been said, « but » also « the dijudication of one significative image unto the declaration of the neighboring, » that is, similar images, « to be made in like manner. » And therefore [1151D] « this too is to be inspected, » namely that the angels themselves « are said to have been called rivers, and wheels and chariots connected, » that is, adapted by signification « to the celestial essences, » or it is to be inspected that in the celestial essences they were said, that is, named, to be rivers, and wheels and chariots connected, that is, coupled and conjoined to one another. For « the fiery rivers signify the divine promotions, » that is, the promotions or effusions of divine grace, « giving » to those celestial essences « a copious, » that is, « not failing affluence, and nourishing them » with a life-giving fecundity. But the chariots, » in which many things coupled are rolled together, signify « the conjunctive society of the likenesses » which are in them, through [1152A] which they are moved concordantly by one assent unto all things. « But the wheels, » since they are indeed « winged, » that is, agile; « and since they are going inconvertibly, » that is, without retrogradation, and inflexibly, that is, without deviation, « into those things which are before the sight, » that is, the anterior things, signify « the virtue of the operation of them, namely of the essences: of the operation, I say, going forth by a right and just way, » by the intellectual rotation of all those essences at once, « supermundanely, » that is, spiritually « directed » into the same right way, that they may at once and concordantly go the proposed journey, without bending, that is, without deviation, « and rightly, » that is, without retrogradation. This, then, the wheels signify. And not only this, but « there is also, » that is, it happens, or is fitting, « by another [1152B] anagogy, » that is, spiritual interpretation, « to dijudicate the imaginary description of the intellectual wheels. For it was cried out to them, » namely the angels, « as the theologian says, » that is, the prophet: « Gel, gel, gel. » For Ezekiel says that, the wheels appearing and running, himself hearing, the angel called them voluble. In this, then, was cried out and shouted gel, whether to them, or by them. « But this signifies, » that is, gel, « according to the Hebrew word, revolutions and revelations. » The cause of each interpretation he subjoins: « For the fiery and Deiform wheels have indeed revolutions about the same best thing, with a motion ever movable, » that is, by a motion ever movable. Even as a circle for this reason is revolved into itself, because around one and [1152C] the same thing itself, that is, the center, it is ever moved; so the spiritual wheels as it were are revolved into themselves, while about that same thing which is best, that is, God, as though about their center, they are moved by ever surrounding it by contemplation. And therefore they are moved with an ever-movable motion, because both the desire is inextinguishable, and that which is desired is incomprehensible. On account of all these things the spiritual wheels have revolutions. « But they have revelations » in the manifestation « of the secret things, and in the circum-reduction of the subjects, » because to the subjects indeed they descend, illuminating them: but again they return, reducing them through contemplation to the higher things; and so they have revelations in « the deductive perfection of the high illuminations into those things which are subject; » because the illuminations [1152D] which they receive from on high they lead down unto the perfection of the subjects, through which they lead back those same subjects again to the higher things.



## EXPOSITION. (cont.)

There follows: « But there remains for us, » etc. After the explanation of the material figures and descriptions, he turns again to the interior things, about to contemplate last of all the joys of the angels. And well is the discourse concerning the celestial ones terminated in joy, because joy is the end of all celestial things. « There remains for us » a « discourse unto the explanation concerning the joy of the celestial dispositions, » because this alone now remains, that we make some discourse, for the sake of explanation, concerning the joy of the celestial ones. And well did he say that there remains a discourse not simply concerning joy, but concerning the joy of the celestial ones [1153A], because the celestial dispositions « are not at all receivers of that passible delectation which is according to us, » but « are said to rejoice together with God, » that is, to rejoice with a joy like the divine joy, at the « finding of the lost, » as when, the lost sheep being brought back, and the drachma found, they are invited to rejoice together. And « according to that deiform exultation » which, at the return of the prodigal son, was figured and perfected in the fatted calf. Or it can be read thus: They are said to rejoice together with God at the finding of the lost, not affected by any temporal and suddenly, passibly arisen gladness, but « according to a deiform feasting, » that is, the delectation and pleasantness which they impassibly enjoy, imitating the eternal joy of God. And [1153B] « they are said to rejoice together with God » according to deiformity, « in the providence and salvation of those returning unto God, » because after the likeness of God they provide for the salvation of those returning to God. They are said also to rejoice together with God according to « the gladness of incorruption, » that is, the incorrupt, or fully and perfectly sweet gladness, to which no bitterness is mingled; and according to that « ineffable beneficence » of divine grace, through which, an interior sweetness being poured forth, it refreshes the holy hearts, « in the participation of which, » that is, of which, namely, sweetness or beneficence, « holy men » also, that is, even holy men, « have been made partakers, through the deific advents of the divine illuminations, » coming, namely, from above. « So much by me, » etc. [1153C] He forms a clause in which by a brief recapitulation he establishes the end of the narration. « So much, » he says, « has been said by me concerning the sacred formations: » said, I say, « deficient from a diligent manifestation, » that is, from a diligent manifestation « of them, » namely the formations; because I confess that those things which have been said by me are less than sufficient unto the diligent and perfect manifestation of the sacred formations. But nevertheless, although unto this they are not perfect, they are yet « perfect, » « as I esteem, » unto another thing, namely « unto our not remaining lowlily in the figurative [1154A] phantasies, » that is, unto this, that they may instruct us how (at least altogether) we may not remain « lowlily, » that is, abjectly, « in the figurative phantasies, » believing nothing else to be than what we find written according to the formation of visible things. But rather, being instructed by the figurative things, let us ascend to the knowledge of those things which are true. « But if thou also shalt say this, » thou, some one, or thou, Timothy, « how » we « have not made » memory, or « mention, of all the angelic virtues » and « operations » and « images » which « are found in the oracles: » then, that is, after those things which we have said, « we answer the truth, » that is, « that » which is true; and that in those very things, namely virtues, or operations, or images, « of which the supermundane, » [1154B] that is, the too-lofty, « knowledge we have been ignorant, we desire another leader of light teaching » us, that is, that he may teach us, and lead us to the light of knowledge. « But certain things, as being equipotent to the things said, » that is, equal and not greater to those which we have said, « we have passed over; » yet « providing for the commensuration of the discourse, » that is, providing that our discourse should not pass its measure, and that we should say too much, if we said even this whole which we could say. Likewise also « honoring the secret » which is « above us » « with silence, » that is, by being silent, or through silence. For silence honors the secret, because to that which is kept silent a greater reverence is sometimes shown. And [1154C] some things which could be said must sometimes be covered, that there may always remain over what is to be sought within, and lest it grow cheap if the whole be expounded.

These things indeed upon the hierarchy of blessed Dionysius, according to the possibility of our understanding, I have presumed. But I fear lest, adding my own things to his, I be convicted of having overlaid a marble wall with mud. Therefore let the presumption be turned aside, let devotion implore. But it brings me solace, that of him whom he undertook to expound, in whom wisdom transcends, holiness condescends.
